Pillar 6E -> Human Development and Sustainable Development Goals (SDG) - Pillar 6E1 -> Human Development - Pillar 6E2 -> Sustainable Development Goals (SDG) - Microeconomics vs Macroeconomics - Microeconomics - Definition: - Study of individual units of the economy such as consumers, firms, and specific markets. - Scope: - Focuses on how decisions are made by individuals or firms regarding resource allocation. - Analyzes price determination, consumer behavior, and firm production. - Core Concepts: - Demand & Supply - Utility (Total and Marginal) - Elasticity (Price, Income, Cross) - Cost and Revenue Analysis - Market Structures: - Perfect Competition - Monopoly - Oligopoly - Monopolistic Competition - Price Mechanism - Consumer and Producer Equilibrium - Examples: - How much should a shopkeeper charge for a product? - How many units will a firm produce to maximize profit? - What will happen to demand for apples if price of bananas falls? - Applications: - Business pricing strategies - Taxation on specific goods (like tobacco or luxury cars) - Market intervention policies (e.g., price caps) - Macroeconomics - Definition: - Study of the economy as a whole, including aggregates like national income, employment, inflation, etc. - Scope: - Focuses on overall economic indicators and large-scale policy-making. - Core Concepts: - National Income (GDP, GNP, NNP) - Aggregate Demand and Aggregate Supply - Inflation & Deflation - Unemployment - Fiscal Policy (Government Spending & Taxation) - Monetary Policy (Repo Rate, CRR, SLR) - Balance of Payments and Exchange Rate - Business Cycles (Boom, Recession) - Examples: - What is India's GDP growth rate in FY25? - What will be the effect of increasing interest rates on inflation? - How does fiscal deficit affect private investment? - Applications: - Budget formulation by governments - RBI's monetary policy for inflation targeting - Employment generation programs (e.g., MGNREGA) - Key Differences -
Aspect Microeconomics Macroeconomics
Unit of Study Individual (Consumer/Firm/Market) Aggregate Economy
Focus Area Price, Output, Resource Allocation GDP, Inflation, Employment, Fiscal & Monetary Policy
Policy Usage Product-specific tax/subsidy, Market regulation National Budgeting, RBI Rate Decisions
Examples Impact of a tax on cigarette sales Effect of repo rate hike on inflation
- Interrelationship - Micro and macroeconomics are interlinked. - A rise in macro-level inflation (macro) may reduce real income of consumers (micro). - Policy-level changes like taxation (macro) affect firm pricing (micro). - Micro-economics - Definition: - Microeconomics studies individual economic units—consumers, households, firms, and industries—and their decision-making processes regarding the allocation of scarce resources. It focuses on price determination, market interactions, and resource distribution in specific markets. - Scope: - Analyzes consumer choices, production decisions, cost structures, market structures, and factor pricing, addressing how prices and quantities are determined through supply and demand interactions. - Fundamental Concepts - Scarcity and Choice: - Definition: - Scarcity occurs because resources (land, labor, capital, time) are limited relative to unlimited human wants, necessitating choices about resource allocation. - Key Concepts: - Opportunity Cost: - - The value of the next best alternative foregone when a choice is made. It’s about what you miss out on by choosing one thing over another. - Example: - A farmer using land for wheat instead of sugarcane sacrifices the potential profit from sugarcane. - Production Possibility Frontier (PPF): - - A curve showing the maximum possible output combinations of two goods given fixed resources and technology. - Points inside PPF: - Inefficient resource use. - Points on PPF: - Full resource utilization. - Points outside PPF: - Unattainable with current resources. - Example: - Producing only food grains sacrifices industrial goods; PPF illustrates trade-offs. - Marginal Opportunity Cost: - - The amount of one good that must be given up to produce one more unit of another. - Example: - Producing more cars reduces food production at an increasing rate due to resource constraints. - Central Problems of an Economy: - Definition: - Due to scarcity, every economy faces three core questions: What to produce? How to produce? For whom to produce? - Explanation: - What to produce: - Deciding which goods/services to prioritize (e.g., consumer goods like food vs. capital goods like machinery). - How to produce: - Choosing production techniques (e.g., labor-intensive handloom vs. capital-intensive textile machines). - For whom to produce: - Determining distribution (e.g., market-driven allocation vs. subsidies for the poor). - Economic Systems: - Market Economy: - Decisions driven by supply and demand (e.g., private telecom sector). - Planned Economy: - Central authority makes decisions (e.g., historical Five-Year Plans). - Mixed Economy: - Combines market and government intervention (e.g., India’s public-private model). - Example: - India prioritizes food security (what), uses cooperative farming (how), and distributes via PDS to the poor (for whom). - Demand Analysis - Definition: - Demand is the quantity of a good or service consumers are willing and able to buy at various prices, holding other factors constant (ceteris paribus). - Law of Demand: - - As the price of a good decreases, quantity demanded increases, and vice versa, resulting in a downward-sloping demand curve. - Example: - If rice price falls from Rs. 50/kg to Rs. 30/kg, demand rises from 2 kg to 5 kg per household. - Factors Affecting Demand: - Price of the good: - Primary determinant (inverse relationship). - Income: - Normal goods (demand rises with income, e.g., smartphones) - Inferior goods (demand falls, e.g., coarse grains) - Prices of related goods: - Substitutes: - Rise in price of one increases demand for the other (e.g., tea demand rises if coffee price increases). - Because they replace each other, if one becomes more expensive, people switch to the other. - Complements: - Rise in price of one decreases demand for the other (e.g., higher petrol prices reduce car demand). - Because complements are used together, if one becomes more expensive, people buy less of it, and since they are used together, they also buy less of the other. - Tastes and preferences: - Influenced by culture or advertising (e.g., demand for organic food due to health trends). - Expectations: - Anticipation of price changes (e.g., buying more fuel before a price hike). - Population: - Larger population increases demand (e.g., growing demand for housing in urban India). - Demand Schedule and Curve: - Demand Schedule: - A table showing quantities demanded at different prices (e.g., \( P = 100 \), \( Q = 5 \); \( P = 80 \), \( Q = 8 \)). - Demand Curve: - Graphical representation of the schedule, downward-sloping. - - Types of Demand: - Individual Demand: - Demand by a single consumer. - Market Demand: - Sum of individual demands (e.g., total demand for wheat in a village). - Elasticity of Demand: - Definition: - The degree of responsiveness of quantity demanded to a change in: - Price of the good, - Income of the consumer, - Price of related goods, - Advertising and other factors. - It is always measured in percentage terms. - Price Elasticity of Demand (PED): - Formula: - \( \text{PED} = \frac{\% \Delta Q_d}{\% \Delta P} \) - Types of elasticity: - Perfectly elastic (PED = ∞): - Demand changes infinitely with no change in price (theoretical). - Elastic (PED > 1): - Large demand change with small price change (e.g., luxury cars). - Unitary (PED = 1): - Proportional change. - Inelastic (0 < PED < 1): - Small demand change with large price change (e.g., salt). - Perfectly inelastic (PED = 0): - No change in demand regardless of price (e.g., life-saving drugs). - Example: - Price rises from Rs. 10 to Rs. 12 (20%), quantity falls from 100 to 90 (10%): - \( \text{PED} = \frac{-10\%}{20\%} = -0.5 \text{ (inelastic)} \) - Determinants: - Substitutes, necessity, income proportion, time period. - Income Elasticity of Demand (IED): - Formula: - \( \text{IED} = \frac{\% \Delta Q_d}{\% \Delta \text{Income}} \) - Positive for normal goods; negative for inferior goods. - Types of elasticity: - Elastic (IED > 1): - Large change in demand with small change in income (e.g., luxury goods). - Unitary (IED = 1): - Proportionate change in demand with change in income. - Inelastic (0 < IED < 1): - Small change in demand with large change in income (e.g., necessities). - Perfectly inelastic (IED = 0): - No change in demand despite change in income (e.g., salt). - Negatively elastic (IED < 0): - Demand falls as income rises (e.g., inferior goods). - Example: - Income rises 10%, demand rises 15%: - \( \text{IED} = \frac{15\%}{10\%} = 1.5 \text{ (normal good)} \) - Cross Elasticity of Demand (CED): - Formula: - \( \text{CED} = \frac{\% \Delta Q_d \text{ of Good X}}{\% \Delta P \text{ of Good Y}} \) - Positive for substitutes; negative for complements. - Types of elasticity: - Elastic (CED > 1): - Large increase in demand for one good with small increase in price of another (e.g., close substitutes like Coke and Pepsi). - Unitary (CED = 1): - Proportionate change in demand of one good with change in price of another. - Inelastic (0 < CED < 1): - Small increase in demand for one good with increase in price of another (e.g., weak substitutes like tea and coffee). - Perfectly inelastic (CED = 0): - No change in demand despite change in price of another good (unrelated goods). - Negatively elastic (CED < 0): - Demand for one good falls as price of another rises (complementary goods like car and petrol). - Strongly negatively elastic (CED ≪ 0): - Very large fall in demand with small price change (joint goods like left and right shoes). - Example: - Coffee price rises 10%, tea demand rises 5%: - \( \text{CED} = \frac{5\%}{10\%} = 0.5 \) - Advertising Elasticity (AED) - Formula: - \( \text{AED} = \frac{\% \Delta Q_d}{\% \Delta \text{Advertising Expenditure}} \) - Types of elasticity: - Elastic (AED > 1): - Large increase in demand with small increase in advertising expenditure. - Unitary (AED = 1): - Proportionate change in demand with change in advertising. - Inelastic (0 < AED < 1): - Small increase in demand despite large increase in advertising. - Perfectly inelastic (AED = 0): - No change in demand despite advertising. - Applications: - Tax Impact: - Inelastic goods (e.g., fuel) yield higher tax revenue. - Subsidy Impact: - Elastic demand (e.g., solar panels) responds more to subsidies. - Example: - High GST on luxury goods (elastic demand) reduces consumption more than on essentials (inelastic). - Supply Analysis - Definition: - Supply is the quantity of a good or service producers are willing and able to offer at various prices, ceteris paribus. - Law of Supply: - - As the price of a good increases, quantity supplied increases, resulting in an upward-sloping supply curve. - Example: - If wheat price rises from Rs. 20/kg to Rs. 50/kg, supply increases from 50 kg to 100 kg. - Factors Affecting Supply: - Price of the good: - Primary determinant (direct relationship). - Input prices: - Higher costs (e.g., fertilizers) reduce supply. - Technology: - Advances (e.g., high-yield seeds) increase supply. - Prices of related goods: - Higher price of one reduces supply of another (e.g., shifting from wheat to sugarcane). - Government policies: - Taxes reduce supply; subsidies increase it. - Expectations: - Anticipated price rises reduce current supply (e.g., hoarding crops). - Supply Schedule and Curve: - Supply Schedule: - A table showing quantities supplied at different prices (e.g., \( P = 100 \), \( Q = 10 \); \( P = 80 \), \( Q = 8 \)). - Supply Curve: - - Graphical representation of the schedule, upward-sloping. - Elasticity of Supply: - Price Elasticity of Supply (PES) - Measures responsiveness of quantity supplied to price. - Formula: - \( \text{PES} = \frac{\% \Delta Q_s}{\% \Delta P} \) - Types of elasticity: - Perfectly Elastic (PES = ∞): - Infinite change in supply with no change in price (theoretical). - Elastic (PES > 1): - Large increase in supply with small increase in price (e.g., manufactured goods). - Unitary (PES = 1): - Proportionate change in supply with change in price. - Inelastic (0 < PES < 1): - Small increase in supply with large increase in price (e.g., agricultural goods in short run). - Perfectly Inelastic (PES = 0): - No change in supply despite change in price (e.g., fixed land). - Example: - Price rises from Rs. 10 to Rs. 12 (20%), quantity supplied rises from 100 to 120 (20%): - \( \text{PES} = \frac{20\%}{20\%} = 1 \) - Income Elasticity of Supply (YES) - Measures responsiveness of quantity supplied to income of producers. - Formula: - \( \text{YES} = \frac{\% \Delta Q_s}{\% \Delta \text{Income}} \) - Types of elasticity: - Elastic (YES > 1): - Large increase in supply with small increase in income. - Unitary (YES = 1): - Proportionate change in supply with change in income. - Inelastic (0 < YES < 1): - Small increase in supply with large increase in income. - Perfectly inelastic (YES = 0): - No change in supply despite change in income. - Negatively elastic (YES < 0): - Supply falls as income rises (producers prefer leisure over production). - Cross Elasticity of Supply (XES) - Measures how supply of one good responds to price of another good. - Formula: - \( \text{XES} = \frac{\% \Delta Q_s \text{ of Good X}}{\% \Delta P \text{ of Good Y}} \) - Types of elasticity: - Elastic (XES > 1): - Large increase in supply of one good with small increase in price of another (competitive supply). - Unitary (XES = 1): - Proportionate change in supply of one good with change in price of another. - Inelastic (0 < XES < 1): - Small increase in supply of one good with increase in price of another (weakly competitive goods). - Perfectly Inelastic (XES = 0): - No change in supply despite change in price of another good (unrelated goods). - Negatively elastic (XES < 0): - Supply of one good falls as price of another rises (joint supply, e.g., petrol and diesel). - Strongly negatively elastic (XES ≪ 0): - Very large fall in supply with small price change (strong joint products). - Technological Elasticity of Supply (TES) - Measures how supply changes due to technology change. - Formula: - \( \text{TES} = \frac{\% \Delta Q_s}{\% \Delta \text{Technology}} \) - Types of elasticity: - Elastic (CES > 1): - Large increase in supply with small increase in cost of production. - This is what happened in green revolution with small increase in fertilizers + HYY leading to large supply. - Unitary (CES = 1): - Proportionate change in supply with change in cost. - Inelastic (0 < CES < 1): - Small increase in supply with large increase in cost. - Perfectly Inelastic (CES = 0): - No change in supply despite change in cost. - Negative (CES < 0): - Supply falls as cost falls (rare, abnormal cases). - Determinants: - Time period, production flexibility, input availability. - Applications: - Fod security: - Minimum Support Price (MSP) for crops increases supply by ensuring a minimum price, but may lead to surplus stocks with FCI. - Market Equilibrium - Definition: - The point where quantity demanded equals quantity supplied, determining equilibrium price and quantity. - Mechanism: - - At equilibrium, the market clears with no surplus or shortage. - Example: - If demand for rice = supply at \( P = 30 \)/kg, \( Q = 50 \) kg, the market is in equilibrium. - Disequilibrium: - Surplus: - Price above equilibrium (e.g., MSP > market price) leads to excess supply (e.g., unsold food grains). - Shortage: - Price below equilibrium (e.g., subsidized medicines) leads to excess demand (e.g., stockouts). - Shifts in Equilibrium: - Demand Shift: - Increase (e.g., income rise) shifts demand curve right, raising price and quantity. - - Supply Shift: - Increase (e.g., better technology) shifts supply curve right, lowering price and increasing quantity. - - Example: - A drought shifts the supply curve left, raising food prices and reducing quantity. - Price Controls: - Controls - Price Ceiling: - Maximum price below equilibrium (e.g., on essential drugs), causing shortages. - Price Floor: - Minimum price above equilibrium (e.g., MSP), causing surpluses. - Additional note - In standard economic theory, only one exists at a time. - They are not a price “range”, they are one-sided restrictions. - Consumer Behavior and Demand - Definition: - The study of how consumers allocate income to maximize utility (satisfaction) from goods and services, given budget constraints. - Utility: - Meaning: - Satisfaction or want-satisfying power derived from consumption of a good or service. - Types based on measurement: - Cardinal Utility: - Assumes utility can be measured numerically (in hypothetical units called utils). - Utility is absolute and comparable. - Ordinal Utility: - Assumes utility cannot be measured, only ranked. Consumer can say which bundle is preferred, but not by how much. - Analyzed using indifference curve analysis. - Example: - Consumer prefers Bundle A over B, and B over C. - Types based on quantity: - Total Utility (TU): - Total satisfaction obtained from consuming a given quantity of a good. - Marginal Utility (MU): - Additional satisfaction obtained from consuming one more unit of a good. - Law of Diminishing Marginal Utility: - Marginal utility from each successive unit of a good decreases as consumption increases. - Example: - First slice of pizza gives 20 utils, second 15 utils, fifth 5 utils. - Average Utility (AU): - Utility per unit of good consumed. - AU = TU / Quantity. - Budget line - Budget line shows all combinations of two goods the consumer can afford with given income and prices. - Indifference curve - A curve showing different combinations of two goods that give the consumer the same level of satisfaction (utility). - - Highest indifference curve - The best possible satisfaction level the consumer can reach within the budget. - The highest one that still touches the budget line. - Tangent to the curve - Tangent means the budget line just touches the indifference curve at exactly one point. They have the same slope at that point. - At that point: - The consumer is spending all income. - They are on the highest possible satisfaction level they can afford. - And their personal trade-off (MRS) exactly matches the market trade-off (prices). - Consumer Equilibrium: - Meaning: - A state where the consumer allocates income in such a way that maximum utility is obtained. - Under Cardinal Utility (Marshallian approach): - Also called law of Equi-Marginal Utility. - Achieved when consumer spends income so that marginal utility per rupee is equal for all goods. - \( \frac{\text{MU}_x}{P_x} = \frac{\text{MU}_y}{P_y} \) - Example: - MU of rice = 20 utils, \( P = 10 \); MU of wheat = 30 utils, \( P = 15 \): - \( \frac{20}{10} = 2 = \frac{30}{15} \) - Under Ordinal Utility (Indifference Curve approach): - Achieved where the budget line is tangent to the highest indifference curve. - Marginal Rate of Substitution (MRS): - Rate at which a consumer substitutes one good for another while maintaining utility: - \( \text{MRS} = -\frac{\Delta Y}{\Delta X} = \frac{\text{MU}_x}{\text{MU}_y} \) - Example: - Giving up 2 units of clothing for 1 unit of food, MRS = 2. - Budget Constraint: - Shows affordable combinations: - \( \text{Income} = P_x Q_x + P_y Q_y \) - Example: - Income = Rs. 100, \( P_x = 10 \), \( P_y = 20 \): - \( 10Q_x + 20Q_y = 100 \implies Q_x + 2Q_y = 10 \) - Consumer Surplus: - Definition: - The difference between what a consumer is willing to pay for a good (as shown by the demand curve) and what they actually pay (market price). - In other words, extra satisfaction or benefit consumers get because they pay less than what they were prepared to pay. - On a demand-supply graph, consumer surplus is the area below the demand curve and above the equilibrium price. - Example: - Willing to pay Rs. 500 for a phone, actual price Rs. 300, surplus = Rs. 200. - Substitution and Income Effects: - When price of a good falls: - Substitution effect: - Consumer buys more of the cheaper good instead of others. - It is the change in consumption due to relative price change. - Income effect: - Consumer’s real income increases, so they can buy more goods. - It is the change in consumption due to change in purchasing power. - Example: - Price of tea falls: - Substitution effect: - Switch from coffee to tea. - Income effect: - With money saved, consume more goods (including more tea). - Revealed Preference Theory: - Meaning: - What a consumer chooses reveals what they prefer. - Example: - If a consumer chooses vegetables over grains when both are affordable, it reveals a preference for vegetables. - Production and Costs - Definition: - Production transforms inputs (land, labor, capital, entrepreneurship) into outputs. Costs are expenses incurred in production. - Production Function: - Shows output as a function of inputs: - \( Q = f(L, K) \) - Law of Diminishing Returns: - Adding more variable input to a fixed input eventually reduces marginal output. - Example: - More workers on a fixed farm increase output initially, then less due to overcrowding. - Returns to Scale (Long Run): - - Increasing Returns to Scale (IRS): - Doubling inputs more than doubles output (e.g., large-scale steel production). - Constant Returns to Scale (CRS): - Doubling inputs doubles output (e.g., some agricultural processes). - Decreasing Returns to Scale (DRS): - Doubling inputs less than doubles output (e.g., overexpanded firms). - Types of costs: - Based on variability: - Fixed Costs (FC): - Do not vary with output (e.g., rent). - Variable Costs (VC): - Vary with output (e.g., raw materials). - Based on quantity: - Total Cost (TC): - The total expenditure incurred in producing a given quantity of output. - \( \text{TC} = \text{FC} + \text{VC} \) - Average Cost (AC): - It is the cost per unit of output. - \( \text{AC} = \frac{\text{TC}}{Q} \) - Average Fixed Cost (AFC): - \( \text{AFC} = \frac{\text{FC}}{Q} \) - Average Variable Cost (AVC): - \( \text{AVC} = \frac{\text{VC}}{Q} \) - Marginal Cost (MC): - The additional cost of producing one more unit of output. - \( \text{MC} = \frac{\Delta \text{TC}}{\Delta Q} \) - Example: - Total cost to produce 10 units = ₹500. - Total cost to produce 11 units = ₹560. - Change in total cost (ΔTC) = 560 − 500 = 60. - Change in quantity (ΔQ) = 11 − 10 = 1. - Marginal Cost: - \( \text{MC} = \frac{\Delta TC}{\Delta Q} = \frac{60}{1} = 60 \) - Interpretation: - The 11th unit costs ₹60 to produce. - Economies and Diseconomies of Scale: - Economies: - Per-unit costs decrease with output (e.g., bulk buying in manufacturing). - Diseconomies: - Per-unit costs increase at high output (e.g., coordination issues). - Revenue and profit - Revenue: - Total Revenue (TR): - Total income from selling output. - \( \text{TR} = P \times Q \) - Average Revenue (AR): - It is the revenue per unit sold. - \( \text{AR} = \frac{\text{TR}}{Q} = P \) - Marginal Revenue (MR): - The additional revenue earned by selling one more unit of output. - \( \text{MR} = \frac{\Delta \text{TR}}{\Delta Q} = P \) - Example: - Total revenue from selling 10 units = ₹500. - Total revenue from selling 11 units = ₹550. - Change in total revenue (ΔTR) = 550 − 500 = 50. - Change in quantity (ΔQ) = 11 − 10 = 1. - Marginal Revenue: - \( \text{MR} = \frac{\Delta TR}{\Delta Q} = \frac{50}{1} = 50 \) - Interpretation: - The 11th unit earns ₹50 in revenue. - Profit Maximization: - Definition: - A firm should produce up to the level where the extra money earned from the last unit equals the extra cost of producing it. - So: - If MR > MC - You’re earning more than you’re spending, you should produce more. - If MR < MC - You’re spending more than you’re earning, you should produce less. - If MR = MC - The last unit adds zero extra profit. This is the best point, perfect balance. - This is the best point where you stop and not incur losses. - Profit equilibrium: - In long run, \( P = \text{minimum AC} \), earning normal profits. - Market Structures - Definition: - The organization of a market based on number of firms, product differentiation, entry barriers, and pricing power. - Perfect Competition: - Many buyers/sellers, identical products, no entry/exit barriers, perfect information, firms are price takers. - Monopoly: - Single seller, unique product, high entry barriers, price maker. - Example: - British East India Company - Monopolistic Competition: - Many firms, differentiated products, low entry barriers, some pricing power. - Example: - Smartphone market. - Oligopoly: - Few dominant firms, interdependent pricing, high entry barriers. - Price rigidity due to firms matching price cuts but not increases. - Types - Collusive: - Firms cooperate (e.g., cartel-like behavior). - Non-Collusive: - Firms compete (e.g., price wars). - Example: - Telecom sector (Jio, Airtel) with price wars. - Factor Pricing - Definition: - Determination of prices for factors of production (land, labor, capital, entrepreneurship) via demand and supply in factor markets. - Rent: - Payment for land, based on scarcity and productivity. - Ricardian Theory: - Rent arises from differential fertility; better land earns higher rent. - Example: - High land prices in Mumbai due to scarcity. - Wages: - Payment for labor, based on demand (marginal productivity) and supply (labor force, skills). - Example: - MGNREGA provides minimum wages for rural labor. - Interest: - Payment for capital, determined by loanable funds market (supply: savings; demand: investment). - Example: - RBI’s repo rate influences borrowing costs. - Profit: - Reward for entrepreneurship, residual income after paying other factors. - Example: - Startup profits from innovation in digital payments. - Analysis of market failures and government policy - Market Failure - Core Definition: - A state where the free market fails to allocate resources at an optimal or socially desirable level. - Primary Drivers: - Externalities (Spillover Effects): - Negative: - Production or consumption that imposes costs on uninvolved parties (e.g., industrial pollution). - Positive: - Actions that provide uncompensated benefits to society (e.g., workforce training or vaccinations). - Public Goods (Non-Excludable & Non-Rivalrous): - Services that private firms cannot profitably provide because users cannot be excluded from benefiting (e.g., national defense, street lighting). - Information Asymmetry: - Imbalance of knowledge between buyers and sellers leading to market distortions (e.g., "adverse selection" in insurance or "moral hazard" in finance). - Market Concentration (Monopoly Power): - When a single entity controls supply, leading to inflated prices and reduced innovation (e.g., pharmaceutical patent abuse). - Government Intervention - Fiscal Instruments (Taxes and Subsidies): - Corrective Taxes: - Levied to discourage harmful activities (e.g., Carbon taxes). - Subsidies: - Financial support to encourage beneficial activities (e.g., renewable energy grants). - Price Regulation: - Price Ceilings: - Legally mandated maximum prices to ensure affordability (e.g., rent control). - Price Floors: - Minimum price guarantees to protect producers (e.g., Minimum Support Price for agriculture). - Additional note - In standard economic theory, only one exists at a time. - They are not a price “range”, they are one-sided restrictions. - Regulatory Oversight: - Enforcement of antitrust laws and competition policies to prevent monopolistic behavior and protect consumers. - Direct Public Provision: - State-funded supply of essential infrastructure and services that the private sector under-produces (e.g., public highways and emergency services). - Demand–Supply in Public Policy: - Tax Incidence: - Meaning: - Tax incidence refers to who actually bears the burden of a tax (buyers or sellers), regardless of who the government officially taxes. - Core principle: - The side of the market that is more inelastic bears more tax burden. - Explanation: - When a tax is imposed: - Cost of production increases. - Supply curve shifts left. - Price rises and quantity falls. - Cases: - Inelastic demand, elastic supply: - Buyers bear more burden. - Example: - Fuel or medicines (buyers cannot avoid). - Elastic demand, inelastic supply: - Sellers bear more burden. - Example: - Electronics or fashion goods. - Subsidies: - Meaning: - Subsidy is financial assistance given by the government to reduce production or consumption cost. - Explanation: - Subsidy lowers cost. - Supply curve shifts right. - Price falls and quantity increases. - Example: - Fertilizer subsidy reduces cost for farmers, increasing agricultural output. - Role of Elasticity in Policy: - Price Elasticity: - Inelastic goods generate more tax revenue. - Example: - Fuel, alcohol, tobacco. - Cross Elasticity: - Helps identify substitute goods. - Example: - Sugar price rise increases jaggery demand. - Income Elasticity: - Shows how demand changes with income. - Example: - Income rise increases car demand, reduces demand for coarse grains. - Pillar 1A1: Money and Payment Systems - Definition: - An economy is a system of production, distribution, and consumption of goods and services within a region, driven by interactions among individuals, businesses, and governments. Money and payment systems are critical for facilitating transactions, enabling trade, investment, and financial inclusion in modern economies. - Context: - Money and payment systems have evolved from barter to digital platforms, addressing inefficiencies and supporting India’s economic growth by promoting efficiency, transparency, and access to financial services. - Significance for India: - India’s shift to a digital economy, through initiatives like UPI and CBDC, aligns with goals of financial inclusion, reducing black​ money, and enhancing global competitiveness, crucial for a developing economy aiming for self-reliance (Atmanirbhar Bharat). - Barter System and Its Disadvantages - Definition: - The barter system is a method of exchange where goods or services are traded directly without using money as a medium. - Historical Context: - Originated with Mesopotamian tribes around 6000 BCE, serving as a foundational trade mechanism before monetary systems. - Disadvantages: - Double Coincidence of Wants: - Both parties must desire each other’s goods or services, making trade cumbersome and often unfeasible (e.g., a farmer with grain needing shoes must find a shoemaker needing grain). - High Search and Transaction Costs: - Locating a willing trading partner and negotiating terms requires significant time and resources, hindering efficient trade. - Storage Challenges: - Perishable goods, such as food or livestock, deteriorate quickly, leading to loss of value and impracticality for long-term trade. - Hinders Specialization: - The system discourages division of labor and specialization, limiting economic efficiency and productivity growth by restricting individuals to produce only what they can directly trade. - Functions of Money - Definition: - Money is a universally accepted medium that facilitates economic transactions by overcoming the limitations of the barter system, serving as a cornerstone of modern economies. - Functions: - Medium of Exchange: - Enables indirect trade, eliminating the need for double coincidence by allowing goods and services to be exchanged through money (e.g., buying groceries with rupees). - Unit of Account: - Provides a standardized measure to express the value of goods, services, and assets, facilitating price comparison and accounting (e.g., pricing a car at Rs. 10 lakh). - Store of Value: - Retains purchasing power over time, enabling savings for future use, though subject to inflation risks (e.g., saving Rs. 5000 for emergencies). - Standard of Deferred Payment: - Supports credit transactions by serving as a measure for settling debts over time, critical for loans and mortgages (e.g., repaying a loan in installments). - Evolution and Types of Money - Definition: - Money has evolved from physical commodities to digital forms, reflecting advancements in trade, technology, and economic systems, enhancing transaction efficiency and accessibility. - Types of Money: - Commodity Money: - Objects with intrinsic value used as money (e.g., grains, livestock). - Metallic Money: - Coins made from precious metals with standardized value. - Paper/Token/Representative/Fiat Money: - Currency valued by government backing rather than intrinsic worth. - Bank Money/Deposit Money: - Funds in bank accounts, accessible via cheques or digital transfers. - crypto​currency: - Digital or virtual currency secured by crypto​graphic technology. - Additional Subtypes: - Electronic Money: - Stored in digital wallets or prepaid cards (e.g., Paytm wallet, AmazonPay). - Central Bank Digital Currency (CBDC): - Digital fiat currency issued by central banks, combining fiat reliability with digital efficiency. - Significance: - The evolution reflects India’s transition to a digital economy, with systems like UPI and CBDC reducing cash dependency and promoting financial inclusion. - Money types in detail: - Commodity Money - Definition: - Commodity money consists of objects with intrinsic value used as a medium of exchange due to their inherent worth, accepted in trade for their utility or rarity. - Examples: - Iron nails, bear pelts, cocoa beans, whale teeth, gold nuggets, used historically in various cultures. - Challenges: - Perishability: - Items like food or livestock degrade quickly, reducing their utility as a reliable medium of exchange. - Non-Uniformity: - Variations in quality and quantity (e.g., size of pelts or purity of gold) complicate standardization and fair trade. - Non-Acceptance: - Foreign traders may not value specific commodities (e.g., cocoa beans irrelevant in non-agricultural regions), limiting international trade. - Metallic Money - Definition: - Metallic money refers to coins made from precious metals like gold or silver, standardized by weight and purity to ensure trust and uniformity in transactions. - Historical Context: - Traders and kings stamped gold nuggets to certify purity and weight, fostering trust (e.g., Lydian coins, 7th century BCE). - In India, Indo-Greek, Kushana, and Gupta kings issued gold coins; Gupta coins were notable for artistic designs (e.g., king playing veena, standing with wife). - Delhi Sultanate introduced silver tanka; Sher Shah Suri’s rupiyah (1540s) and Akbar’s muhr were significant silver and gold coins, standardizing trade. - Note: - Refer to Indian coinage history in standard history texts for detailed study, including numismatic evolution and economic impact. - Intrinsic Value - Definition: - Intrinsic value is the inherent worth of the material used in money, independent of its nominal or face value. - Precious metals - Have intrinsic value; can be melted for use in jewelry or weapons (e.g., gold coins used for ornaments). - Paper currency - Lack intrinsic value; paper has no significant alternative use (e.g., cannot be repurposed like metal). - Full-Bodied vs. Token Coins - Full-bodied coins: - Intrinsic value matches or exceeds face value. - Example: - Gupta coin (~8 gm gold), worth its nominal value. - Challenges: - Debasement: - Reducing metal content in coins, often during weak governance (e.g., Aurangzeb, Roman emperors), eroding trust and causing inflation. - Token coins: - Intrinsic value is less than face value, relying on legal backing. - Example: - 50 paisa coin (~4 gm steel), negligible material value. - Challenges: - Tughlaq’s failed bronze token experiment (1330s) — public rejected low-value coins. - Modern Indian coins made of cupronickel to deter melting: - Higher melting point - Alloy nature (harder to extract pure metals) - Lower resale value vs pure copper/nickel - Coinage Act 2011 prohibits coin melting to maintain circulation and prevent disruption. - Fiat Money - Definition: - Fiat money is currency without intrinsic value, deriving its value from government decree or legal backing, accepted due to trust in the issuing authority. - Conditions to meet simultaneously: - Must exists as physical coins/notes, virtual coins, or digital coins capable of measuring value. - Must be Issued by a government, monarch, or central bank (e.g., RBI in India). - Examples in India: - Coins (50 paisa to Rs. 20) issued under Coinage Act 2011 (e.g., Rs. 20 coin is a 12-sided dodecagon for tactile identification). - Rs. 1 note, signed by the Finance Secretary, issued by Government of India, excludes “I promise to pay bearer” clause. - It is issued by the Government of India, not the Reserve Bank of India (RBI). All other currency notes (Rs. 2 and above) are issued by the RBI under the RBI Act, 1934. - Since it’s issued directly by the government, no promise is needed — the note is legal tender by sovereign declaration - RBI issues other currency notes (Rs. 2 to Rs. 2000) under RBI Act 1934, signed by the RBI Governor with “I promise to pay bearer.” - Significance: - Fiat money ensures universal acceptability within India, supporting economic stability and transactions. - Legal Tender - Definition: - Legal tender is currency legally mandated for acceptance in settling all debts and transactions within a country, ensuring universal acceptability and trust. - Conditions to meet simultaneously: - It must qualifies as fiat money (issued by government or central bank). - It must be legally valid for all debts & transactions throughout the country. Other party can't refuse to accept. -
Item Physical/Digital Form? Issued by Central Bank/Govt? Fiat Money? Legal Tender in India?
Indian Rupee: Coins, Notes, CBDC Yes Yes Yes Yes

- We use cheque / credit card to pay fees. So, why are they not fiat money/legal tender? - These are not legal tender because these are payment instruments, not money themselves. They represent an instruction to transfer money, but not money in hand. - Limited vs. Unlimited Legal Tender - Definition: - Limited legal tender can be refused beyond a specified limit, while unlimited legal tender must be accepted for any amount, as per RBI regulations. - Limited Legal Tender - Recipient can refuse payment beyond a specified limit. - Coinage Act 2011: - Coins Rs. 1 and above: - Acceptable up to Rs. 1000 per transaction. - Coins 50 paisa: - Acceptable up to Rs. 10 (20 coins). - Coins below 50 paisa withdrawn in 2011 (e.g., 10 paisa, 25 paisa no longer valid). - Rs. 1 paper note: - Limited legal tender, rarely used. - Unlimited Legal Tender - No restriction on acceptance amount. - RBI Act 1934, Section 26: - All bank notes (Rs. 2 to Rs. 2000) are unlimited legal tender. - Cannot be refused for settling any debt, ensuring universal acceptability. - Note: - Finance Act 2017 restricts cash transactions to Rs. 2 lakh to curb tax evasion; higher amounts require non-cash methods like cheques or NEFT. - RBI’s App for the Visually Impaired - Definition: - Tools developed by the Reserve Bank of India to assist visually impaired individuals in identifying currency notes, promoting financial inclusion and accessibility. - Context: - Supports approximately 80 lakh blind individuals in India, ensuring equitable access to financial transactions. - Features: - Old Gandhi Series Notes: - Include tactile shapes for identification (e.g., square for Rs. 50, triangle for Rs. 100) to aid visually impaired users. - New Gandhi Series Notes: - Feature bleed lines (raised lines) and raised Gandhi printing for tactile recognition. - Mobile Aided Note Identifier (MANI) App (Launched January 2020): - Scans currency notes (even if half-folded) using a mobile camera. - Provides audio output in Hindi and English, identifying denomination, works offline for accessibility. - Cannot verify note authenticity (genuine vs. counterfeit), limiting its use for fraud detection. - Offers vibration-based notifications for deaf-blind users, enhancing inclusivity. - Significance: - Aligns with India’s commitment to inclusive financial systems, ensuring all citizens can participate in the economy. - Currency-Related General Knowledge - Definition - Currency is a system of money used in a particular country or region as a medium of exchange for goods and services. - Indian Currency Notes: - Feature 17 languages (15 on reverse, Hindi and English on obverse) from the 8th Schedule of the Constitution (22 languages), reflecting India’s linguistic diversity. - Why are only 17 languages featured on Indian currency notes, even though the 8th Schedule of the Constitution lists 22 languages? - Space constraint - It is physically not feasible to fit all 22 languages on a currency note without clutter. The design must remain readable and secure. - The 17 languages still represent the diverse linguistic heritage of India, though not exhaustively. - Design Practicality - Including all 22 would require shrinking font size, which affects legibility and aesthetics. - Not a Constitutional Requirement - There’s no constitutional mandate to feature all 22 languages on currency. It’s a design and practical decision by RBI. - Rupee Symbol (₹): - Designed by D. Udaya Kumar (a Professor IIT Guwahati) in 2010, selected via a Department of Economic Affairs competition, combining Devanagari “Ra” and Roman “R.” - Lion Emblem: - Derived from the Sarnath Pillar, Uttar Pradesh, symbolizing India’s historical and cultural heritage. - Slogan “Satyamev Jayate”: - From Mundaka Upanishad, meaning “Truth Alone Triumphs,” adopted as India’s national motto, printed on currency notes. - Numismatics Museum: - Located in Kolkata (RBI Mint), showcases the evolution of coins and trade in India, valuable for understanding monetary history. - Additional Facts: - Currency notes include security features like watermarks, security threads, and micro-text to prevent counterfeiting. - RBI’s clean note policy ensures damaged notes are replaced to maintain quality in circulation. - “I Promise to Pay the Bearer” Clause - Definition: - A clause on currency notes indicating the RBI’s obligation to provide value equivalent to the note’s denomination upon demand, reinforcing trust in fiat money. - Historical Context: - Colonial Era: - Notes were convertible to gold or silver coins/bars under the gold standard (e.g., 1 US Dollar ~ 14 grams gold, 1 British Pound = ~73 grams of gold). - Modern Era: - Convertible to other bank notes or token coins of equal face value (e.g., Rs. 2000 = 4 x Rs. 500 notes). - Features: - Notes are zero-interest, anonymous bearer bonds, not adjusted for inflation or linked to precious metals. - Clause appears on RBI-issued notes (Rs. 2 to Rs. 2000), except Rs. 1 note issued by the Government of India. - Significance: - Enhances public confidence in fiat currency by guaranteeing value, critical for economic stability. - Currency Note vs. Promissory Note - Definition: - A promissory note is a legal instrument promising payment between parties, while currency notes, despite similarities, are distinct as legal tender. - - Perspectives: - Academic: - Currency notes resemble promissory notes due to the “I promise to pay” clause, implying an obligation by the RBI. - Legal: - Under the Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881, currency notes are not promissory notes, as they are legal tender with universal acceptability, not negotiable instruments. - Why currency notes are not promisory notes? - A Promissory Note is a promise to pay money. A Currency Note is the money. You cannot "pay" money with the exact same money. - Comparison: -
Feature Currency Notes Promissory Notes
Definition Official legal tender issued by a central authority Written promise by one party to pay a certain sum to another
Issuer Central bank (e.g., RBI in India) or Government Individuals, firms, or institutions
Legal Tender? Yes No (unless accepted by parties or endorsed by a court)
Regulated by RBI Act, 1934 and Coinage Act, 2011 Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881
Transferability Freely transferable (used in circulation) Transferable only through endorsement and delivery
Enforceability Cannot sue RBI for payment – trust-based Legally enforceable in court if not honored
Example ₹500 note issued by RBI "I promise to pay ₹10,000 to Raj on 1st Sept 2025 – signed by Ravi". It doesn't include include check.
Contains Promise? Contains a general promise ("I promise to pay...") Contains a specific promise to a specific person
Signed by RBI Governor (or Finance Secretary for ₹1 note) Person/institution making the promise
- Significance: - Clarifies the legal status of currency in financial transactions, relevant for UPSC understanding of monetary systems. - Denomination of Fiat Money - Definition: - Denomination refers to the face value assigned to a unit of currency (e.g., Rs. 10, Rs. 100, Rs. 2000). - It reflects the amount printed on a note or coin and determines its purchasing power in the economy. - Issuance: - Coins: - Issued by the Government of India under the Coinage Act, 2011. - Denominations: Rs. 1, Rs. 2, Rs. 5, Rs. 10, and smaller paise coins (now largely discontinued). - Banknotes: - Issued by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) under the RBI Act, 1934. - Denominations currently in circulation: Rs. 2, Rs. 5, Rs. 10, Rs. 20, Rs. 50, Rs. 100, Rs. 200, Rs. 500, Rs. 2000. - Importance: - Facilitates ease of transaction across various price levels. - Allows efficient pricing, accounting, and monetary control. - Affects currency management (e.g., cost of printing, risk of hoarding or counterfeiting). - Changes in Denominations: - New denominations may be introduced to meet evolving transactional needs. - Older denominations may be withdrawn or demonetized to: - Combat black​ money or counterfeiting. - Rationalize the currency structure. - Promote digital payments or clean note policy. - Demonetization of Fiat Money - Definition: - Demonetization is the process of stripping a currency unit of its legal tender status, rendering it invalid for transactions, often to curb black​ money or counterfeiting. - Historical Instances in India: - 1946: - High-denomination notes (Rs. 1000, Rs. 10000) demonetized post-World War II to address black​ money. - 1978: - Rs. 1000, Rs. 5000, Rs. 10000 notes demonetized to target unaccounted wealth. - 8 November 2016: - Rs. 500 and Rs. 1000 notes of Mahatma Gandhi series demonetized. - 2016 Demonetization: - Initiated by Finance Ministry’s Department of Economic Affairs via gazette notification on 8 November 2016. - Objectives: - Curb black​ money, counterfeit currency, and terror​ financing. - Promote digital payments and formalize the economy. - Specified Bank Notes (Cessation of Liabilities) Act 2017: - RBI not obligated to honor “I promise to pay” for demonetized Rs. 500 and Rs. 1000 notes. - Holding banned notes is illegal, except for research, numismatics, or museum purposes with RBI permission. - Impact: - Disrupted cash-based transactions, boosted digital payments (e.g., UPI adoption surged). - Supreme Court (2023) upheld demonetization’s legality, citing economic policy prerogative (refer to Pillar #2: Taxation and black​ money). - Significance: - Highlights India’s efforts to combat black​ money and transition to a digital economy, a key UPSC topic. - 2000 Note: Withdrawal but Continued Legal Tender - Definition: - Withdrawal is the phased removal of a currency note from circulation without revoking its legal tender status, aimed at reducing its usage over time. - Timeline: - November 2016: - RBI introduced Rs. 2000 note post-demonetization to address currency shortages in the economy. - 2018–19: - Printing of Rs. 2000 notes stopped as lower denominations (e.g., Rs. 100, Rs. 200, Rs. 500) became sufficient. - May 2023: - RBI ordered exchange of Rs. 2000 notes with other denominations at banks, encouraging their return. - Status (July 2025): - Remains fiat money and legal tender, not demonetized, but withdrawn from active circulation. - Can still be used for transactions, though rarely accepted due to limited circulation. - Reasons for Withdrawal: - Temporary measure to address 2016 currency shortage; lower denominations now adequate. - Low usage in daily transactions due to high denomination, inconvenient for small purchases. - Aligns with RBI’s clean note policy to ensure quality banknotes in circulation. - Lifespan of Rs. 2000 notes (printed 2016–17) is 4–5 years; paper quality may deteriorate by 2022–23, prompting replacement. - There is no mention of fighting black​ money/corruption/fake notes by ISI-Pakistan. - Significance: - Reflects RBI’s strategy to optimize currency circulation and promote digital transactions, a key aspect of India’s monetary policy. - Redenomination of Fiat Money - Definition: - Redenomination refers to the process of changing the face value of existing currency units by introducing a new unit of money. - It typically involves removing zeros from the currency (e.g., 1000 old units become 1 new unit) without altering the actual value of money held. - It is purely a notional or accounting change — it does not affect purchasing power. - Purpose: - Simplify accounting and pricing structures in high-inflation economies. - Restore confidence in the national currency. - Facilitate everyday transactions by making numbers easier to read and interpret. - Improve the international image of the currency. - Psychological reset to promote economic stability and reforms. - Key Characteristics: - Usually implemented after hyperinflation or prolonged inflation. - Accompanied by a clear conversion ratio (e.g., 1000:1). - May include new coinage, new note designs, and dual circulation period. - Redenomination ≠ Devaluation: - Redenomination is a technical accounting adjustment with no change in actual value. - Devaluation reduces the external value of a currency (in terms of foreign exchange). - Global Examples: - Turkey (2005): - Removed 6 zeros; 1 new lira = 1,000,000 old lira. - Zimbabwe (multiple times): - Redenominated several times due to hyperinflation. - Russia (1998): - 1 new ruble = 1000 old rubles. - Brazil (1986 onwards): - Multiple redenominations to control inflation and reset currency structure. - India’s Position: - India has never undertaken a formal redenomination of the rupee. - Demonetization (e.g., 2016) involved withdrawal of certain denominations but not a change in unit value. - Rupee remains structurally stable with no need for redenomination due to controlled inflation and policy continuity. - Challenges: - Public confusion or panic during transition period. - Cost of reprinting currency, recalibrating machines, updating accounting systems. - Requires strong macroeconomic stability and policy credibility to succeed. - Significance: - Important for understanding how nations stabilize their monetary systems. - A key topic in monetary economics, especially in the context of inflation control, currency reforms, and public confidence. - Relevant in comparative case studies for UPSC and other exams. - Bank Money and Deposit Money - Definition: - Bank money - Bank money is "imaginary" money created by commercial banks through the process of lending. It does not exist as physical cash in a vault but as an entry in a ledger. - Deposit money - Deposit money refers to funds held in bank accounts, accessible via cheques, debit cards, or digital transfers, forming a significant part of the money supply (M1, M2 in monetary aggregates). - Types of Bank Accounts: - Savings Account: - For individuals, offers interest (e.g., 3–4% p.a.), suitable for regular transactions and savings, with withdrawal limits. - Current Account: - For businesses, no interest, supports high-volume transactions, offers overdraft facilities for cash flow management. - An Overdraft Facility allows a bank account holder to withdraw money even if the account balance is zero or insufficient. The bank "honors" the payment, and the account goes into a negative balance. - Fixed Deposit Account: - Locks funds for a fixed term (e.g., 1–5 years), offers higher interest (e.g., 6–7% p.a.), ideal for long-term savings. - A Fixed Deposit (FD) creates a separate account — distinct from your regular savings or current account. When you open an FD, the bank creates a new account specifically for that deposit. - Recurring Deposit Account: - Allows regular deposits (e.g., monthly) with fixed tenure, earns interest similar to fixed deposits, encourages disciplined saving. - Non-Resident External (NRE) Account: - For Non-Resident Indians (NRIs), fully repatriable, tax-free interest, used for foreign earnings (e.g., salaries abroad). - Non-Resident Ordinary (NRO) Account: - For NRIs, non-repatriable (with limits), taxable interest, used for India-based income (e.g., rent, dividends). - Salary Account: - For employees, linked to employer payroll, often zero-balance with benefits like free transactions. - Senior Citizen Account: - For individuals above 60, offers higher interest rates (e.g., 0.5% above regular savings) and additional benefits like priority banking. - Significance: - Bank accounts facilitate financial inclusion, enabling savings, credit access, and digital transactions, critical for India’s economic growth. - Paper Orders: Cheque and Demand Draft - Definition: - Paper orders are written instructions to transfer funds from a bank account, governed by the Viceroy Ripon's Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881, ensuring legal enforceability. - Cheque: - Definition: - A cheque is a written order by an account holder (drawer) instructing their bank (drawee) to pay a specified amount to a recipient (payee). - Three parties involved: - 1. Drawer (Sender) - "To draw" originally meant to draw (write) a bill or order of payment. - The drawer is the person who creates (draws) the payment instrument — such as a cheque or bill of exchange. - 2. Drawee (Bank) - The person or institution that is being ordered to pay is the drawee. - 3. Payee (Recipient) - Payee simply means the one who will be paid. - Process: - 1. Drawer issues the cheque, specifying payee and amount, and signs it. - 2. Payee presents the cheque to their bank, which forwards it for clearance via the Cheque Truncation System (CTS). - 3. Funds are transferred after verification, typically within 1–3 days (T+1 cycle post-2024). - Types: - Stale Cheque: - Invalid if not deposited within 3 months of issuance, ensuring timely clearance. - Post-Dated Cheque: - Depositable only after a specified future date, used for deferred payments (e.g., loan EMIs). - Open/Bearer Cheque: - Payable to anyone without crossing; crossing (two parallel lines) restricts payment to bank accounts for security. - Codes: - IFSC (Indian Financial System Code): - 11-digit alphanumeric code (e.g., SBIN0001234) identifying bank branches for electronic transfers. - MICR (Magnetic Ink Character Recognition): - 9-digit code printed in magnetic ink for automated cheque clearance, ensuring accuracy. - Use Cases: - Payments for goods, services, or bills, widely used in business and personal transactions. - Example: - Paying rent or settling supplier invoices. - Bounced check - A bounced cheque (or dishonoured cheque) is a cheque that cannot be processed by the bank because it fails to meet certain conditions, most commonly due to insufficient funds in the account. - Common reasons why a cheque bounces: - Insufficient funds - Signature mismatch - Stale cheque - Post-dated cheque - Overwriting or errors - Account closed or frozen - Significance: - Offers a paper-based, secure payment method, though declining with digital payment adoption. - Demand Draft: - Definition: - A demand draft is a prepaid instrument issued by a bank, guaranteeing payment to the payee upon presentation, ensuring secure and reliable transactions. - Process: - 1. Customer requests a draft from their bank, paying the amount upfront plus a nominal fee (e.g., Rs. 50–200). - 2. Bank issues the draft, drawable at a specified branch or correspondent bank, backed by pre-paid funds. - 3. Payee presents the draft to their bank for clearance, typically within 1–3 days, with guaranteed payment. - 4. If expired (e.g., after 3 months), drafts can be revalidated by the issuing bank. - Use Cases: - Secure payments for education fees (e.g., university admissions), property purchases, or government applications. - Preferred for inter-city or inter-bank transactions where cheques may risk non-payment (e.g., bounced cheques). - Example: - Paying for a competitive exam application or property registration. - Features: - Safer than cheques as funds are pre-paid, eliminating the risk of bouncing. - Issued in favor of a specific payee, reducing fraud risks. - Can be canceled or reissued if lost, with proper documentation. - Significance: - Widely used in India for high-value, secure transactions, especially where digital payments are not feasible, critical for UPSC understanding of payment systems. - Overdraft - Definition: - An overdraft is a credit facility allowing account holders to withdraw funds beyond their account balance, treated as a short-term loan with interest, supporting immediate financial needs. - Process: - Pre-approved by banks based on account holder’s creditworthiness, account type, or relationship (e.g., current account holders). - Funds withdrawn are repaid with interest, typically higher than regular loans (e.g., 10–15% p.a.), on demand or per agreement. - Features: - Used for short-term expenses, such as business cash flow or emergency personal needs. - Example: - Pradhan Mantri Jan-Dhan Yojana offers up to Rs. 10,000 overdraft for eligible account holders, promoting financial inclusion (refer to Pillar #1D: Financial Inclusion). - Comparison with Loans: - Overdrafts are flexible, short-term, and for smaller amounts; loans are for long-term, high-value needs (e.g., home or car loans). - Significance: - Enhances liquidity for businesses and individuals, supporting economic activity and financial inclusion in India’s banking system. - Cheque Truncation System (CTS) and Positive Pay Mechanism - Check Truncation System - Definition: - Cheque Truncation System (CTS) is a digital cheque clearance system that transmits scanned cheque images online, replacing physical cheque movement for faster processing. - Developed by: - Reserve Bank of India (RBI) - Supported by: - Institute for Development and Research in Banking Technology (IDRBT) for the pilot and technical backend. NPCI later provided operational and infrastructure support, but did not develop CTS. - Launched in: - 2010 (first in New Delhi, then extended pan-India). - Purpose: - To eliminate the physical movement of cheques and speed up the clearing process using scanned images. - Positive Pay Mechanism - Definition: - A fraud prevention system that cross-verifies key cheque details (e.g., date, amount, payee) provided by the issuer (drawer) against the actual cheque presented for clearing. - Developed by: - Reserve Bank of India (RBI). - It is a bank-level implementation, based on RBI instructions (RBI Circular: Sep 25, 2020). - Launched in: - Effective from January 1, 2021. - Purpose: - To prevent cheque-related fraud by requiring the drawer (issuer) to submit key details of the cheque (e.g., date, amount, beneficiary) to the bank for verification before the cheque is cleared. -
Mechanism Benefits
Cheque Truncation System (CTS)
  • Faster clearance than physical delivery (within hours, T+1 cycle post-2024).
  • Reduces logistics costs and risks of loss or damage during transport.
  • Enhances efficiency in banking operations, supporting India’s digital banking ecosystem.
Positive Pay Mechanism
  • Requires drawer to submit cheque details (e.g., amount, date, payee) to bank for verification before clearance.
  • Prevents fraud, such as amount tampering or unauthorized issuance.
  • Mandatory for high-value cheques (e.g., above Rs. 50,000, varies by bank), enhancing security.
- Significance: - CTS and Positive Pay strengthen India’s banking system by improving efficiency and reducing fraud, aligning with digital payment goals. - Electronic Orders and Digital Payments - Definition: - Digital payments refer to fund transfers done electronically via online platforms, mobile apps, cards, or other digital channels. These are regulated under the Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007, which empowers the Reserve Bank of India to oversee and authorize all payment and settlement systems in India. - Types: - Online Banking: - Transactions via internet banking portals (e.g., fund transfers, bill payments). - Mobile Payments: - Via apps like Unified Payments Interface (UPI) or digital wallets (e.g., Paytm, Google Pay). - Card Payments: - Using debit/credit cards at Point of Sale (PoS) terminals or online platforms. - Significance: - Drives India’s cashless economy, reduces black​ money, enhances transparency, and promotes financial inclusion, especially in rural areas. - Core Banking Solution (CBS) - Definition: - Core Banking Solution (CBS) is centralized banking software enabling seamless operations across bank branches, supporting branch-less banking and digital services. - Examples: - Finacle (Infosys), BaNCS (TCS), E-Kuber (RBI’s proprietary platform). - Process: - Integrates customer accounts, transactions, and services on a centralized server. - Enables real-time access to account details across branches, ATMs, and online platforms. - Benefits: - Facilitates services like mobile banking, ATMs, and online transfers. - Centralizes customer data for efficient management and fraud detection. - Enhances customer experience with seamless banking access nationwide. - Significance: - Critical for India’s banking modernization, supporting digital payments and financial inclusion. - Digital Payment Systems - NEFT, RTGS, IMPS - Definition: - National Electronic Funds Transfer (NEFT), Real Time Gross Settlement (RTGS), and Immediate Payment Service (IMPS) are electronic fund transfer systems managed by RBI (NEFT, RTGS) and NPCI (IMPS). -
Type Developed By Transfer Amount Target Customers Settlement Mechanism Timing Fees Who Can Provide This Facility

- Significance: - These systems form the backbone of India’s digital payment infrastructure, supporting economic transactions and financial inclusion. - Lightweight Payment and Settlement System (LPSS) - Definition: - A backup digital payment system developed by the RBI for use during emergencies like wars or natural disasters, when regular systems like NEFT, RTGS, IMPS, or UPI are disrupted due to connectivity issues. - Features: - Operates with minimal staff and infrastructure, ensuring continuity of payments. - Supports temporary transactions in low-connectivity areas (e.g., rural regions during floods). - Uses offline or low-bandwidth technologies for resilience. - Significance: - Enhances India’s payment system robustness, ensuring financial access during crises, a key aspect of economic resilience for UPSC. - Beneficiary Account Name Look-Up Facility (2024) - Definition: - A facility allowing senders to verify the beneficiary’s name before initiating NEFT or RTGS transfers, reducing errors and fraud. - Process: - Sender inputs beneficiary’s account number and IFSC code; system displays the registered name for confirmation. - Implemented post-October 2024 for enhanced security. - Benefits: - Prevents transfers to incorrect accounts due to typographical errors. - Reduces fraud risks (e.g., phishing scams redirecting funds). - Build users' confidence in digital transaction - Significance: - Strengthens trust in digital payments, critical for India’s cashless economy drive. - National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI) - Definition: - NPCI is a not-for-profit company under the Companies Act, 2013, established in 2008 to develop cost-effective, inclusive payment solutions for India. - Background: - Founded by 10 banks with Rs. 100 crore capital; non-banks (e.g., Paytm, PhonePe) added as shareholders in 2020. - Operates under RBI oversight, aligning with India’s financial inclusion goals. - Objectives: - Develops technologies like UPI, RuPay, and IMPS to streamline payments. - Promotes financial inclusion by expanding banking access to rural and underserved populations. - Significance: - NPCI’s innovations have transformed India’s payment ecosystem, making it a global leader in digital transactions (e.g., UPI’s 100 billion+ annual transactions). - Unified Payments Interface (UPI) - Definition: - Unified Payments Interface (UPI) is a real-time payment system developed by NPCI, enabling instant fund transfers via mobile apps, revolutionizing retail payments. - Features: - QR code scanning for merchant payments (e.g., at shops, street vendors). - Direct bank account linkage, eliminating wallet storage, ensuring funds remain in banks. - Supports push (sender-initiated) and pull (recipient-initiated) transactions. - Examples: - BHIM, SBI Yono, AxisPay, Google Pay, PhonePe. - Process: - Users link bank accounts to UPI apps using a Virtual Payment Address (VPA, e.g., name@upi). - Transactions are authenticated via UPI PIN or biometrics, completed instantly. - International Reach: - Available in Bhutan, Nepal, Singapore, Mauritius, Sri Lanka, etc., via NPCI agreements, enabling Indian tourists to use UPI abroad. - Transaction Limits: - Normal transactions: - Rs. 1 lakh/day. - Capital markets, credit card payments, loans: - Rs. 2 lakh/day. - G-Sec, IPO, medical/educational fees, tax payments: - Rs. 5 lakh/day (post-August 2024). - Significance: - UPI’s low-cost, instant fund transfer system has played a pivotal role in advancing India’s cashless economy. According to the ACI Worldwide report (2023), India accounted for over 50% of global real-time digital payment transactions, making it the global leader in this domain. - UPI - Push vs. Pull Transactions - Push Transactions: - Initiated by the sender (e.g., sending Rs. 500 to a friend via UPI). - Pull Transactions: - Initiated by the recipient (e.g., utility company deducting bill payments automatically). - Significance: - Enables flexible payment models, supporting both individual and automated transactions. - UPI Delegated Payments (2024) - Definition: - Allows primary account holders (e.g., parents) to create secondary UPI accounts with spending limits for others (e.g., children). - Process: - Primary user sets up a delegated account via UPI app, specifying transaction limits (e.g., Rs. 5000/month). - Secondary user transacts within limits, enhancing controlled access. - Significance: - Promotes financial literacy and safety, especially for minors or dependents, aligning with financial inclusion goals. - UPI Transaction Limits- UPI123, UPI Lite (2024) -

- UPI Lite: - On-device wallet for small transactions without PIN, reducing server load. - UPI Lite-X - UPI Lite-X extends UPI lite with offline payment capabilities. - Offline via Near Field Communication (NFC); sync within 4 days; payments in low-connectivity areas - UPI 123Pay: - Enables UPI payments on feature phones via IVR or USSD (*99#), promoting rural access. - Conversational Payments - Definition: - UPI payments initiated via AI-powered voice commands, similar to Siri or Alexa, integrated into UPI apps. - Process: - Users issue voice commands (e.g., “Pay Rs. 500 to Ravi”) via UPI app, authenticated via PIN or biometrics. - Benefits: - Enhances accessibility for visually impaired or less tech-savvy users. - Speeds up transactions with natural language processing. - Pre-Sanctioned Credit Line - Definition: - A pre-sanctioned credit line is a pre-approved borrowing limit offered by banks, allowing account holders to access funds without applying for a loan, repayable with interest. - UPI Overdraft (April 2023): - Definition: - Enables UPI payments even with insufficient account balance, using a pre-approved overdraft, repaid later with interest. - Process: - Banks offer overdraft limits based on creditworthiness (e.g., Rs. 5000–10,000 for PMJDY accounts). - Users transact via UPI, with overdrawn amounts deducted from future deposits. - Features: - Supports “Buy Now, Pay Later” (BNPL) model, popular for retail purchases. - Promotes financial inclusion by providing small loans to rural users, reducing reliance on high-interest moneylenders. - Example: - A small vendor uses UPI overdraft to buy inventory, repaying when sales revenue is deposited. - Significance: - Enhances credit access for underserved populations, supporting India’s financial inclusion goals. - Interoperable Card-less Cash Withdrawal (ICCW) - Definition: - ICCW allows cash withdrawal from ATMs using UPI without a physical debit card, enhancing convenience and security. - Process: - User selects “Card-less Withdrawal” on ATM, scans UPI QR code, and authenticates via PIN. - Cash is dispensed, debited from the linked bank account or overdraft. - Benefits: - Reduces risks of card cloning or theft. - Enables access for users without cards, especially in rural areas. - Significance: - Strengthens India’s digital payment ecosystem, aligning with cashless economy objectives. - UPI for Cash Deposits/Withdrawals - Definition: - UPI enables cash deposits and withdrawals at ATMs or Cash Deposit Machines (CDMs) using QR codes, reducing reliance on physical cards. - Features: - Cash Withdrawals: - Scan QR code at ATMs or coin-dispensing machines, authenticate via UPI PIN. - Cash Deposits: - Deposit cash into CDMs by scanning UPI QR code, credited to linked account. - Example: - A user deposits Rs. 5000 into their account via a CDM using PhonePe’s UPI interface. - Benefits: - Enhances accessibility for cash-based transactions in a digital framework. - Reduces dependency on bank branches or ATMs with card readers. - Significance: - Bridges cash and digital economies, critical for India’s semi-urban and rural populations. - BHIM App - Definition: - Bharat Interface for Money (BHIM) is NPCI’s UPI-based mobile app for seamless bank-to-bank transfers, promoting digital payments. - Features: - Supports smartphones and feature phones via *99# USSD code for low-tech users. - Uses three-factor authentication (e.g., UPI PIN, mobile number, bank account) for security. - Funds remain in bank accounts, earning interest, unlike wallet-based apps. - No card-related fees (e.g., Merchant Discount Rate), making it cost-effective. - Process: - Users link bank accounts, create a VPA (e.g., name@bhim), and transact via QR codes or mobile numbers. - Significance: - A flagship initiative for India’s cashless economy, widely adopted for its simplicity and accessibility. - RuPay Card Payment Gateway - Definition: - RuPay is NPCI’s card payment scheme, launched in 2012, competing with global networks like Visa and Mastercard. - Features: - Operates via ATMs, Point of Sale (PoS) terminals, and online payment gateways. - Offers lower transaction fees than competitors, benefiting merchants and users. - Free RuPay debit cards provided with PMJDY accounts (refer to Pillar #1D: Financial Inclusion). - International agreements enable use in Singapore, Japan, UAE, etc., for limited global transactions. - Process: - Users swipe RuPay cards at PoS or enter details online, authenticated via PIN or OTP. - Significance: - Reduces India’s reliance on foreign card networks, promoting self-reliance in payment systems. - Credit Card Network Portability (2024) - Definition: - Allows customers to choose or switch credit card networks (e.g., RuPay, Visa, Mastercard) for better offers or compatibility. -
Before 2024 After 2024
  • Customers locked to one network (e.g., SBI Visa card).
  • New customers choose preferred network at card issuance.
  • Existing customers switch networks at card renewal or via bank request.
- Significance: - Enhances consumer choice and competition, reducing costs and improving service quality in India’s credit card market. - NPCI Initiatives - Definition: - NPCI initiatives are technology-driven payment solutions developed to enhance India’s financial infrastructure, promoting efficiency and inclusion. -
Initiative Description
Bharat Bill Payment System (BBPS) - Facilitates utility bill payments (e.g., electricity, gas, water) via a single platform, accessible through UPI, cards, or net banking.
Unified Presentment Management System (UPMS) - Launched by Bharat BillPay Ltd. (2022), manages bill presentation and payments, enhancing BBPS efficiency.
National Financial Switch (NFS) - Powers ATM network operations, enabling inter-bank ATM transactions nationwide.
Cheque Truncation System (CTS) - Digital cheque clearance system for faster, secure processing (see 1.11.3).
National Electronic Toll Collection (NETC) - Supports FASTag for automated toll collection on highways.
PAI Chatbot - AI-based chatbot for resolving queries on NPCI products (e.g., UPI, RuPay).
DigiSaathi Helpline (1800-891-3333) - 24/7 helpline for resolving digital payment issues, enhancing user support.
DakPay (2020) - Payment app by India Post Payments Bank, offering UPI, bill payments, and banking services, especially in rural areas.
- Significance: - NPCI’s initiatives have positioned India as a global leader in digital payments, supporting economic formalization and inclusion. - NACH-ABPS- Hybrid / Mixed Mode in MGNREGA - Definition: - NACH (National Automated Clearing House) and ABPS (Aadhaar Based Payment System) are digital payment technologies developed by NPCI. - In MGNREGA, both methods are used for disbursing wages to workers. This dual system is called Hybrid or Mixed Payment Mode. - Background: - Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act (MGNREGA), 2005 is a rural development scheme by the Ministry of Rural Development. - Guarantees 100 days of wage employment per year to rural households. - Current Status: - Both NACH and ABPS are presently used for wage disbursement. - Government plans to shift entirely to ABPS mode. - Transition delayed due to operational and technical challenges. - ABPS Exemptions: - Some poor individuals without Aadhaar cards are exempted from ABPS requirement. - Controversy: - Opposition alleges the government is "weaponizing technology" to deny wages to the poor lacking Aadhaar. - Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT)- E-RUPI - Definition: - E-RUPI is a prepaid, cashless electronic voucher (delivered via SMS or QR code) developed by NPCI using UPI for targeted delivery of benefits and subsidies. - Supported by: - Ministry of Finance (Department of Financial Services). - Ministry of Health (National Health Authority). - Partner banks and NPCI. - Process: - Issued by banks or authorized entities to beneficiaries, specifying purpose (e.g., medical expenses). - Beneficiary scans QR code or uses SMS code at designated merchants to redeem benefits. - Funds are transferred directly to the merchant’s account, ensuring targeted usage. - If funds were assigned for buying books then they can't be used to buy gutka or liquor - Uses: - Government Schemes: - Food for mother, child, poor families under various schemes - Medicines for TB, Corona etc - Fertilizer subsidies for farmers - (e.g., PM Kisan, Ayushman Bharat). - Private Sector: - Even private sector companies can issue it for - 1. Employees e.g. Gym membership, golf club membership, hotel tourism travel. - 2. Use it for Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) programs - What is Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR)? - Big companies have to spend a portion of their profit for social service - Expanded Scope (2023): -

- Significance: - Ensures leak-proof delivery of subsidies, reduces misuse, and promotes digital payments, a key UPSC topic for welfare schemes. - QR Codes - Definition: - Quick Response (QR) codes are 2D barcodes storing payment information, enabling quick and secure digital transactions via mobile apps. - Background: - Developed in Japan (1990s) by Denso Wave, widely adopted in India for digital payments. - Types: -
Type Description
Proprietary QR Code
  • Exclusive to specific apps (e.g., Paytm); not interoperable.
  • RBI banned new proprietary codes to promote standardization.
UPI QR Code
  • Generated by NPCI’s UPI.
  • Scannable by all apps developed using UPI technology (e.g., BHIM, PhonePe).
Bharat QR Code
  • Developed by NPCI with Visa/Mastercard.
  • Interoperable with UPI and card apps.
- Mandate: - From 2022, apps must use UPI QR or Bharat QR for interoperability, ensuring seamless payments across platforms. - Significance: - QR codes have revolutionized retail payments in India, enabling small merchants and street vendors to accept digital payments, supporting the cashless economy. - Credit and Debit Cards (Plastic Money) - Definition: - Plastic money refers to credit and debit cards used for electronic payments at PoS terminals, ATMs, or online platforms, reducing cash dependency. -
