- Card Tokenization (October 2022) - Definition: - Tokenization replaces sensitive card details (e.g., 16-digit number) with a unique token to enhance security for online and PoS transactions. - Process: - Tokens generated via card issuer’s website/app, linked to user’s device or merchant. - During payment, token is used instead of card details, reducing hacking risks. - Benefits: - Protects against data breaches by not sharing actual card details. - Simplifies recurring payments (e.g., subscriptions) with secure tokens. - Significance: - Enhances trust in digital payments, critical for India’s growing e-commerce sector. - CVC-Less Transactions - Definition: - Post-tokenization, Cardholder Verification Code (CVC) may not be required for tokenized online transactions, improving user convenience. - Process: - Tokenized cards use device authentication (e.g., OTP) instead of CVC for payments. - Benefits: - Faster checkout process for online shopping. - Maintains security through tokenization and alternate authentication. - Significance: - Balances convenience and security, supporting India’s digital payment adoption. - Cards and Technologies for Transport/Travel - Definition: - Specialized cards and technologies designed for transport-related payments (e.g., tolls, metro fares) and travel services, enhancing mobility and convenience. - National Common Mobility Card (NCMC, 2019) - Definition: - NCMC is a multi-purpose card for transport and retail payments under the “One Nation One Card” initiative, promoting seamless mobility. - Developed by: - Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs, NPCI, Bharat Electronics Limited, and banks. - It's 3 components: - 1. NCMC Card: - Debit/credit/prepaid card used for metro, bus, railways, tolls, parking, and retail shopping. - 2. SWEEKAR: - Automatic Fare Collection System for efficient ticketing. - 3. SWAGAT: - Automated gates at transport stations for quick access. - Process: - Card is tapped at compatible terminals (e.g., metro gates, PoS), deducting fares or payments instantly. - Significance: - Integrates transport and retail payments, reducing cash use and enhancing urban mobility in India. - SBI Nation First Transit Card (2023) - Definition: - A prepaid card by State Bank of India for transport and retail payments, compatible with NCMC infrastructure. - Uses: - Toll payments, parking fees, public transport fares, and retail shopping. - Process: - Rechargeable via UPI, net banking, or bank branches; tapped at terminals for payments. - Significance: - Supports India’s smart mobility initiatives, aligning with digital payment goals. - FASTag/NETC (2017) - Definition: - FASTag is a prepaid, rechargeable Radio-frequency Identication (RFID) tag for automated toll collection on highways, using NPCI’s National Electronic Toll Collection (NETC) technology. - Developed by: - National Highways Authority of India (NHAI) with NPCI support. - Features: - Launched with MyFASTag and FASTag Partner apps for recharging and management. - Mandatory for vehicles since 2021, affixed on windshields for automatic toll deduction. - Process: - RFID reader at toll plaza scans FASTag, deducts toll from linked account or wallet. - Benefits: - Reduces congestion at toll plazas, saving time and fuel. - Enables tracking of stolen vehicles via RFID data. - Future: - Satellite-based toll collection planned for 2025, using GPS for distance-based tolling (refer to Pillar #5: Infrastructure). - Significance: - Transforms India’s highway infrastructure, promoting efficiency and digital payments. - Merchant Discount Rate (MDR) - Definition: - Merchant Discount Rate (MDR) is the fee merchants pay to banks for accepting card or UPI transactions, shared among the card issuer, acquiring bank, and payment gateway. - The term Merchant Discount Rate (MDR) does sound a bit misleading at first glance, since it's a fee charged to the merchant, not a discount given to them. - It is named from the perspective of the bank, which discounts a small portion of the transaction before passing the rest to the merchant - Challenges: - High MDR reduces merchant profits, discouraging PoS adoption, especially for small businesses. - Initiatives: - RBI (2017) - Caps MDR to a percentage of transaction value (e.g., 0.4–0.9% for cards, varies by transaction size). - MEITY (2021) - Subsidizes MDR up to Rs. 2000 for RuPay debit cards and UPI transactions to encourage adoption. - MDR Status: - RuPay Debit Cards and UPI: - Zero MDR, making them cost-effective for merchants. - So, if a banker promotes Rupay debit card / UPI App, banker will not earn MDR fees. So bankers promote MasterCard & Visacard to their customers and merchants (instead of Rupay/UPI). - RuPay Credit Cards, Visa/Mastercard: - Non-zero MDR, impacting merchant profitability. - RBI remarks - MDR subsidy game not financially sustainable, better charge some fees on UPI/Rupay debit card also. - Significance: - MDR policies balance merchant affordability with payment system sustainability, critical for India’s cashless economy. - Measures to Promote Cashless Economy - Definition: - A cashless economy relies on digital payments to reduce physical currency use, enhancing transparency and efficiency. - Initiatives: - RBI’s Vision 2019–2021: - Set goals for increasing digital payment adoption, improving infrastructure, and ensuring security. - Nandan Nilekani Committee (2019): - Recommended measures to promote digital payments, including zero MDR for UPI and RuPay. - Budget Restrictions: - Finance Act 2017 limits cash transactions to Rs. 2 lakh to curb tax​ evasion (refer to Pillar #2: black​ money). - Payments Infrastructure Development Fund (PIDF) - Launched by: - Reserve Bank of India (RBI) - Date: January 2021 - Objective: - To encourage deployment of payment acceptance infrastructure (like PoS, mPoS, QR codes, sound boxes, etc.) in Tier-3 to Tier-6 cities and northeastern states, including J&K and Ladakh - Funding: - Initial corpus of ₹345 crore - ₹250 crore by RBI - ₹95 crore by card networks and banks - Ongoing contributions from card networks and banks - Governance: - Managed by RBI - Oversight by an Advisory Council headed by the Deputy Governor - Extension: - Extended in December 2023 till December 2025 - Now includes newer device types like: - Sound-boxes - Aadhaar biometric devices - Smart PoS - USB-based biometric devices - PIDF and Its Relevance to Government Schemes: - PM SVANidhi (Street Vendor’s AtmaNirbhar Nidhi) - Objective: - Provide collateral-free loans to street vendors - Promote digital transactions among them - Relevance to PIDF: - PIDF subsidizes cost of digital payment infrastructure (e.g., QR code, sound box) - Helps vendors accept UPI or card payments easily - Vendors under PM SVANidhi are often targeted beneficiaries for PIDF support - PM Vishwakarma Yojana - Objective: - Support traditional artisans like carpenter, potter, blacksmith, etc. - Includes training, toolkit incentive, credit support, and digital empowerment - Relevance to PIDF: - PIDF supports digital transaction tools at the artisan/shop level - Helps artisans become part of the digital economy - Makes it easier for them to receive payments from customers electronically - Significance: - Promotes financial inclusion, reduces black​ money, and aligns with India’s Digital India initiative, a key UPSC focus. - Global Money Transfer - Definition: - Global money transfer systems enable cross-border payments for tourism, trade, and investments, reducing reliance on traditional methods like SWIFT. - Initiatives in India: - UPI for Foreign Tourists (2023): - G20 nation tourists can use UPI for shopping from NRE/NRO accounts in India, enhancing tourism. - RuPay Cards International Acceptance (2023): - RBI allows RuPay cards for ATM withdrawals and shopping in countries like Mauritius, Singapore (2024). - UPI in Sri Lanka, Mauritius, etc. (2024): - Available in Bhutan, Oman, UAE, Nepal, France, etc., for Indian tourists’ mobile payments via UPI apps. - India-UAE Local Currency Settlement System (2023): - Enables payments in INR and AED, bypassing US dollars and SWIFT, reducing costs. - UPI-IPP (Instant Payment Platform) linkage under development for seamless settlements. - UAE’s JAYWAN and AANI: - JAYWAN: - National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI) is collaborating with UAE's Al Etihad Payments (AEP) for creation of domestic card scheme JAYWAN in UAE. - It'll help UAE to become self-reliant (presently dependent on MasterCard visa, et cetera, foreign players for running the card system) - AANI: - UAE’s UPI-like payment system, developed with NPCI support. - Project Nexus (2024): - This project connects India’s UPI with fast payment systems in Malaysia, Philippines, Singapore, Thailand. - Coordinated by Bank for International Settlements (BIS) for 60-second cross-border P2P/P2M payments. - Financial Messaging Systems: - SWIFT (Global) - Society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunication (1973, Belgium). - Facilitates international bank transactions via secure messaging. - Banned nations: - Iran - Russia (post-2022 Ukraine invasion) - SFMS (India) - Structured Financial Messaging System by TCS for IDRBT (RBI’s research arm). - Supports NEFT, RTGS, and intra-bank transactions within India. - Significance: - Enhances India’s global financial integration, reduces dependency on Western systems, and supports economic diplomacy. - Payment System Operators (PSOs) - Definition: - PSOs are entities licensed by RBI under the Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007, to operate digital payment systems, ensuring efficiency and security. -
Type of PSO Examples
Umbrella Entity NPCI
Card Payment Gateway Mastercard, Visa, RuPay
Cross-Border Remittance Western Union, MoneyGram

Payment Aggregators Paytm, Google Pay, Razorpay

Financial Market Infrastructure Clearing Corporation of India (CCI)
- Significance: - PSOs drive India’s digital payment ecosystem, ensuring diverse, secure, and accessible transaction platforms. - New Umbrella Entity (NUE, 2019) - Definition: - NUE is a proposed RBI initiative to license new entities competing with NPCI for retail payment systems, fostering innovation. - Objectives: - Increase competition, reduce NPCI’s monopoly, and lower transaction costs. - Promote new technologies for digital payments. - Status (2023): - Applications (e.g., Amazon, Google-led consortiums) rejected - 2023-Jan: RBI has put this license process on halt/suspension due to regulatory concerns. - Significance: - Reflects RBI’s balancing act between innovation and regulatory control, a potential UPSC discussion point. - Third-Party Application Providers (TPAPs) - Definition: - TPAPs are apps (e.g., Google Pay, WhatsApp Pay) that use NPCI’s UPI platform under permission, not statutory licensing. - Process: - TPAPs integrate with UPI via NPCI’s APIs, enabling transactions without direct RBI licensing. - FAQ: How can NPCI give license, it is not a statutory body unlike RBI. - Don't mix the word 'license' with sarkaari type licenses (Gun /vehicle/bank etc.) Here, the word 'license' is in context of getting permission from a company. - Example, If you want to use Photoshop software, you have to buy license-key from Adobe company. - Significance: - Expands UPI’s reach through private sector apps, driving adoption but raising concerns about data privacy and market dominance. - Digital Payment Regulatory Bodies - Definition: - Regulatory bodies oversee digital payment systems to ensure security, efficiency, and consumer protection, critical for India’s financial ecosystem. - Digital Transactions Ombudsman (DTO, 2019) - Definition: - DTOs are senior RBI officials at 21 locations resolving digital payment complaints up to Rs. 20 lakh. - Functions: - Handles issues with PPIs, mobile wallets, NEFT, RTGS, and UPI transactions. - Can order company/bank to fix the issue and provide compensation up to Rs. 1 lakh for customer distress (e.g., fraud losses). - If unsatisfied with the decision, appeal can be made to the RBI Deputy Governor - Process: - Complaints filed via RBI’s online portal or DTO offices; resolved within 30 days. - Significance: - Strengthens consumer trust in digital payments by providing accessible grievance redressal. - Board for Payment and Settlement Systems (BPSS) - Definition: - BPSS is a statutory RBI body under the Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007, regulating all payment system providers (banks, non-banks, PPIs). - Origin: - Recommended by Narsimham-II Committee (1998) to oversee e-banking and digital payments. - Functions: - Formulates policies for payment systems, licenses PSOs, and ensures compliance. - Monitors innovations like UPI, CBDC, and FASTag for stability and security. - Significance: - Central to India’s digital payment governance, ensuring a robust and secure financial ecosystem. - Payment Regulatory Board (PRB) - Government wanted to setup this body few years ago. But, this is not YET setup, and no new developments. - Digital Payment Indices and Initiatives - Definition: - Indices and initiatives measure and promote digital payment adoption, reflecting India’s progress toward a cashless economy. - Indices - Digital Payments Index (DPI) - RBI’s index measuring digital payment adoption across India, based on transaction volume and value. - Financial Inclusion Index - Measures access to banking, loans, and insurance, especially for underserved populations. - Initiatives - RBI Vision 2019–2021 - Outlined goals for enhancing digital payment infrastructure, security, and adoption. - Harbinger Hackathon - RBI’s 2024 hackathon with themes like Zero Financial Frauds and Divyang-Friendly systems, fostering innovation. - Significance: - Tracks India’s progress in digital financial inclusion, a key UPSC topic for economic policy evaluation. - Virtual Currency - Definition: - Unregulated digital money, issued and controlled by private developers. - Used among specific virtual communities or platforms. - Types: - Closed Virtual Currency: - Used only within specific platforms (e.g., in-game coins). - Cannot be converted to real-world currency. - Open Virtual Currency: - Can be traded for real money. - Includes crypto​currencies (e.g., Bitcoin, Ethereum). - Crypto​currency (subset of virtual currency): - Based on blockchain technology. - Decentralized and crypto​graphically secure. - Examples: Bitcoin, Ethereum, Dogecoin. - Risks: - High volatility, speculation. - Prone to hacking, fraud, and scams. - No legal protection or redress. - Regulation in India: - Not legal tender. - RBI issued multiple warnings since 2013. - Budget 2022: 30% tax on gains from Virtual Digital Assets (VDAs). - Budget 2023: 1% TDS on VDA transactions. - Difference from Digital Currency: - Virtual Currency: - Private and unregulated. - Digital Currency (e.g., CBDC): - Issued and regulated by central banks. - Crypto​currency and Blockchain Technology - Definition: - Crypto​currency is a digital or virtual currency secured by crypto​graphy, operating on blockchain technology, a decentralized ledger for tamper-proof transactions. - Blockchain: - A secured decentralized database/ distributed ledger technology (DLT) that maintains a continuously growing list of records/transactions. Old entries can't be deleted, new entries will be visible to all. - Mainly used for running crypto​currency network. But it can also store any type of data - Background: - Anarchist groups lost faith in FIAT MONEY because USA's Subprime Crisis (2007) eroded the purchasing power of US Dollar. - They also dislike Banks & Card Companies because of transaction charges on e-banking, card payments, MDR, interoperability issues. - An anonymous user Satoshi Nakamoto launched bitcoin in 2009. - Examples: - Bitcoin (1 BTC = 10^8 Satoshi), Ethereum, Ripple, Dogecoin (meme coin). - Blockchain Features: - Decentralized, transparent, and secure, used for crypto​currencies, supply chain tracking, and smart contracts. - Types of crypto​currencies: - Utility Tokens: - Used for specific ecosystems (e.g., Ethereum for smart contracts). - Stablecoins: - Pegged to assets like fiat or gold (e.g., Tether, USDC). - Meme Coins: - A meme coin is a crypto​currency named after characters, celebrities, animals, artwork, etc to be humorous, light-hearted - Based on internet trends (e.g., Dogecoin (2013), Trump (2025)). - Significance: - Offers decentralized finance (DeFi) alternatives but poses regulatory challenges for India. - Hot Wallet vs. Cold Wallet - Definition: - Hot wallets are online crypto storage (e.g., mobile apps), while cold wallets are offline (e.g., USB drives). -
Hot Wallet Cold Wallet
  • Connected to internet, convenient for frequent transactions.
  • Vulnerable to hacking (e.g., phishing attacks).
  • Offline storage, highly secure against cyber threats.
  • Less convenient for daily use, suited for long-term storage.
- Crypto-Winter (2021–2022) - Definition: - A period of prolonged low crypto​currency prices, with Bitcoin falling from $70,000 (Nov 2021) to $20,000 (Jun 2022), a 70% drop. - Causes: - Market speculation, regulatory crackdowns, and economic uncertainty. - Impact: - Investor losses and reduced trust in crypto markets. - Bitcoin Price Surge (2024) - Context: - Bitcoin reached $100,000 in December 2024, driven by pro-crypto policies in the US (e.g., Trump administration, Elon Musk’s influence). - Significance: - Highlights crypto’s volatility and global policy impact on markets, relevant for UPSC economic trends. - US Bitcoin Strategic Reserve Fund (2025) - Definition: - A proposed US fund using 200,000 seized Bitcoins to generate profits for welfare and debt reduction without tax hikes. - Over the years, USA federal officers seized ~200,000 bitcoins from criminals and tax evaders. - Background: - 2025-Jan: - Trump set up a committee to create a US bitcoin strategic reserve fund from it. - Goal: - 1. Sell Bitcoins at a profit when its price goes up. - 2. Use the profit money for welfare programs, etc. - 3. Cover the Fiscal debt without raising taxes. (Ref: Pillar#2D for more on Fiscal debt) - Significance: - Reflects global acceptance of crypto​currencies as strategic assets, contrasting India’s cautious approach. - Crypto-Scams - Examples: - Binance: - Binance pled guilty to U.S. anti–money​ laundering violations, paid a $4.3 billion fine - FTX: - FTX owner arrested for scam, 1 million+ investors money got stuck - WazirX: - This Indian crypto​currency portal lost $230+ million in a cyber-attack (2024) - Risks: - Fraud, investor losses, money​ laundering, and lack of traceability. - Significance: - Underlines need for robust regulation, a key UPSC issue for financial governance. - Economic Survey 2023 Observations - Challenges: - Price manipulation by large holders (“whales”). - High electricity consumption for blockchain (e.g., Bitcoin mining). - tax​ evasion, price volatility, fraud risks, and lack of intrinsic value. - Recommendations: - Global coordination for crypto regulation (e.g., G20, OECD frameworks). - Significance: - Highlights India’s cautious stance on crypto​currencies, balancing innovation and risks. - Taxation in India - Budget 2022: - 30% tax on crypto​currency profits, 1% TDS on transfers (refer to Pillar #2A: Direct Taxes). - Significance: - Integrates crypto into India’s tax framework, addressing revenue and black​ money concerns. - Crypto​currency: Global Status - Status: - Banned: - Nepal, Algeria, Egypt, Morocco, Turkey, Iran, China (restricts bank services for crypto). - Legal Tender: - Venezuela: - Bolivar and Petro (crypto​currency). - Marshall Islands: - US Dollar and SOV (2018). - El Salvador: - US Dollar and Bitcoin (2021). - Crypto​currency Permitted in Other Nations/Groups - UNICEF (United Nations International Children's Emergency Fund) - HQ: - New York, USA - Founded: - 1946 (later renamed to UN Children's Fund) - 2019: - Became the first UN organization to accept crypto​currency. - Crypto​currency Fund: - Accepts donations in crypto​currencies like Bitcoin and Ether. - Aimed at funding open-source technology benefiting children and youth. - World Bank - 2018: - Launched the world’s first blockchain bond — iBond — in Australia. - Denomination: - Australian Dollars - Returns: - Public investors earn ~2% interest after 2 years. - Significance: - Blockchain bond reduces cost on server, database, and paperwork compared to traditional bonds. - Further info: - Covered in Pillar#3B (World Bank) and Pillar#1C (Share Market) - EU’s MiCA Regulation (2023) - Definition: - Markets in Crypto Assets (MiCA) is an EU framework to regulate crypto​currencies, excluding CBDCs and NFTs. - Objectives: - Protect against fraud, tax​ evasion, money​ laundering, and terror​ financing. - Standardize crypto regulations across EU nations. - Significance: - Sets a global benchmark for crypto regulation, influencing India’s policy considerations. - OECD’s CARF Framework (2022) - Definition: - Crypto-Asset Reporting Framework (CARF) is an OECD initiative for transparent reporting of crypto transactions to combat tax​ evasion and fraud. - Significance: - Supports global coordination, relevant for India’s alignment with G20 regulatory efforts. - Stablecoins - Definition: - Stablecoins are crypto​currencies pegged to stable assets like fiat (e.g., USD) or gold to reduce volatility (e.g., Tether, USDC). - Challenges: - Risks of money​ laundering, terror​ financing, and lack of consumer protection. - Potential destabilization if backing assets fail (e.g., Tether’s reserve concerns). - Significance: - Offers stability in crypto markets but requires regulation, a key UPSC issue. - World Coin Project (2021–22) - Definition: - A global digital ID system launched by Sam Altman (Co-founder of OpenAI) using blockchain, similar to India’s Aadhaar, for identity verification and access to services. - Key Features: - World ID: - A proof-of-personhood system using iris scans - Verifies humans vs. bots in the age of AI - WLD Token: - Native crypto​currency given to users after verification - Traded on crypto exchanges - Process: - Users visit "Orb" devices to scan their iris - Data converted into a unique code (not storing raw biometrics) - Once verified, users receive WLD tokens - Pros: - Simplifies access to government schemes, education, and passports. - Enhances financial inclusion via blockchain-based identity. - Cons: - Privacy concerns due to biometric data collection (e.g., iris scans). - Risks of misuse by governments or corporations. - Adoption: - Initially rolled out in countries like India, Chile, Kenya, and Germany - Faced resistance over privacy concerns - Significance: - Highlights blockchain’s potential beyond currency, relevant for UPSC’s technology and privacy discussions. - Central Bank Digital Currency (CBDC) - Definition: - Central Bank Digital Currency (CBDC) is a digital form of fiat currency issued and backed by a central bank, using blockchain or distributed ledger technology, recognized as legal tender with the same value as physical currency. - Global Examples: -
Year Nation Paper Currency CBDC
2020 Bahamas Bahamian Dollar (BSD) Digital BSD (“Sand Dollar”)
2021 Nigeria Naira eNaira
2022 Jamaica Jamaican Dollar JAM-DEX
2023 China Renminbi (Yuan) Digital Yuan (e-CNY)
- India’s CBDC (e₹): - Symbol: - e₹ or eINR. - Launched via amendments to the RBI Act, 1934, in December 2022 as a pilot project. - Issued by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) on behalf of the Government of India. - Represents legal tender as a digital token, distributed through banks and financial institutions. - No interest earned, similar to physical cash, ensuring parity with notes and coins. - Features: - Uses blockchain or distributed ledger technology for secure, transparent, and tamper-proof transactions. - Offers trust, safety, and settlement finality, akin to physical currency, backed by RBI’s credibility. - Supports offline transactions (e.g., via NFC) for accessibility in low-connectivity areas. - Interoperable with UPI and other digital payment systems for seamless integration. - Process: - RBI issues e₹ tokens to banks, which distribute them to customers via digital wallets or apps. - Users transact using e₹ for P2P (person-to-person) or P2M (person-to-merchant) payments, authenticated via secure protocols. - Transactions are recorded on a centralized ledger managed by RBI, ensuring traceability and security. - Significance: - Reduces dependency on physical cash, lowering printing and logistics costs (e.g., Rs. 500 note costs ~Rs. 4 to print). - Enhances financial inclusion by enabling digital access in rural areas, aligning with Digital India. - Combats black​ money and counterfeiting through traceable digital transactions. - Positions India as a leader in digital currency innovation, competing with global CBDC initiatives. - CBDC: Wholesale vs. Retail -
Type Purpose
CBDC-Retail (e₹-R)
  • For Person-to-Person (P2P) and Person-to-Merchant (P2M) transactions (e.g., retail purchases, bill payments).
  • Targets individual consumers and small businesses for daily transactions.
  • Example:
    • Paying for groceries or utilities using e₹ via a mobile app.
CBDC-Wholesale (e₹-W)
  • For interbank settlements and large-value transactions (e.g., government bond settlements, corporate transfers).
  • Targets financial institutions, central banks, and large corporations.
  • Example:
    • Banks settling interbank loans using e₹-W for faster, secure transactions.
- Significance: - e₹-R promotes financial inclusion and digital payments among the public, reducing cash reliance. - e₹-W enhances efficiency in financial markets, reducing settlement times and costs for banks. - CBDC Pilot in India - Timeline: - December 2022: - RBI launched e₹-W pilot for wholesale transactions, focusing on government securities settlement. - Government Securities (G-Secs) are debt instruments (e.g., G-Secs, Treasury Bills) issued by the government to borrow money from the public. - When you buy a G-Sec, you are lending money to the government. - In return, the government promises to repay: - The principal amount (at maturity), and - Interest (periodically, usually twice a year). - February 2023: - e₹-R pilot launched for retail transactions in select cities (e.g., Mumbai, Delhi). - 2024–25: - Expanded to more cities, integrated with UPI, and tested for offline transactions. - Participating Banks: - State Bank of India, ICICI Bank, HDFC Bank, and others, distributing e₹ via digital wallets. - Features: - e₹-R pilot supports payments at merchants, peer transfers, and government scheme disbursements. - Offline mode tested for rural areas using NFC or QR codes, ensuring access without internet. - Progress (July 2025): - Over 1 million daily e₹ transactions reported in pilot phase, with growing merchant adoption. - RBI aims for nationwide rollout by 2026, integrating e₹ with existing payment systems. - Significance: - Demonstrates India’s proactive approach to digital currency, balancing innovation with regulatory control, a key UPSC topic. - Benefits of CBDC - Financial Inclusion: - Enables access to digital payments for unbanked populations, especially in rural India, via offline capabilities. - Cost Reduction: - Reduces costs of printing, transporting, and securing physical currency (e.g., RBI spends ~Rs. 6000 crore annually on currency management). - Transparency: - Traceable transactions reduce black​ money and tax​ evasion, supporting India’s economic formalization. - Efficiency: - Faster settlements for wholesale transactions (e.g., e₹-W reduces clearing time in RTGS). - Security: - Blockchain ensures tamper-proof records, reducing fraud and counterfeiting risks. - Global Competitiveness: - Aligns India with global CBDC trends, enhancing cross-border payment efficiency (e.g., integration with Project Nexus). - Challenges of CBDC - Privacy Concerns: - Centralized ledger may allow RBI to track transactions, raising data privacy issues. - Solution: - RBI exploring anonymized tokens for e₹-R to balance privacy and traceability. - Cybersecurity Risks: - Digital currency vulnerable to hacking or system failures, requiring robust security measures. - Infrastructure Gaps: - Limited internet and smartphone penetration in rural areas may hinder e₹ adoption. - Solution: - Offline transaction modes (e.g., NFC, QR codes) being developed. - Public Awareness: - Low awareness of CBDC among masses requires education campaigns, especially in non-urban areas. - Impact on Banks: - Direct CBDC access may reduce bank deposits, affecting lending capacity. - Solution: - RBI’s two-tier model (banks as intermediaries) mitigates disintermediation risks. - Significance: - Addressing these challenges is critical for CBDC’s success, a potential UPSC discussion point on monetary policy. - CBDC vs. crypto​currency -
Feature CBDC (e₹) crypto​currency (e.g., Bitcoin)
Issuer Central Bank (RBI) Decentralized (no single issuer)
Legal Tender Yes No (except in El Salvador)
Value Stability Pegged to INR, stable Volatile (e.g., Bitcoin’s 2021–24 fluctuations)
Regulation Fully regulated by RBI Limited regulation in India
Technology Centralized blockchain/ledger Decentralized blockchain
Use Case Retail and wholesale transactions Investment, DeFi, limited transactions
- Significance: - CBDC offers a regulated, stable alternative to crypto​currencies, addressing RBI’s concerns about volatility and fraud while promoting digital payments. - Fungibility - Meaning: - Fungibility refers to the property of an asset where individual units are identical, interchangeable, and of equal value. - A fungible asset can be subdivided into smaller units, and each unit will have the same value and acceptability as any other unit. - Conditions to test fungibility: - Can the item be subdivided into smaller units? - Can one unit be replaced with another unit of the same value without any difference? - Examples: -
Item Subdivision Interchangeable Fungible
₹500 Note Yes. 5 x ₹100 = ₹500. Yes. Both combinations buy the same goods. Yes
1kg Gold Bar Yes. 10 x 100g biscuits = 1kg. Yes. Same value. Yes
1 Bitcoin Yes. 1 BTC = 10^8 Satoshis. Yes. 1 BTC and 10^8 Satoshis hold same value. Yes
100g Single Diamond Technically possible. No. One large diamond is more valuable than 100 small ones. No
Shares or Bonds No. One share of Tata Motors is not the same as one share of Infosys. No. Different terms and values. No
Non-Fungible Tokens (NFTs) No. Cannot divide a digital painting or song. No. Each NFT is unique in content and value. No
- Fungibility of CBDC (Central Bank Digital Currency) - General Case: - Like physical cash, one unit of CBDC is equal to another of the same denomination. - Hence, it is considered fungible. - Case of Programmable CBDC: - Example: ₹100 given to a student, but programmed to be used only for buying books. - Temporarily becomes non-fungible, as it cannot be used to buy pizza or clothes. - After the student buys the book, the ₹100 goes to the bookshop owner. - For the bookshop owner, the money is now freely usable for anything, so fungibility is restored. - If unused, the money may return to the school, which can spend it for any purpose, again restoring fungibility. - Conclusion: - Programmability limits usage but does not destroy fungibility permanently. - RBI has clarified that CBDC remains fungible even when programmed for specific uses. - Non-Fungible Tokens (NFTs) - Meaning: - NFTs are digital assets stored on a blockchain that represent ownership of unique content. - Common examples include digital art, music files, animated images (GIFs), tweets, or even physical assets like house ownership documents. - Nature: - NFTs cannot be subdivided or exchanged one-to-one like currency. - Each NFT has a different value based on the content and the buyer’s interest. - Examples: - Beeple’s digital artwork sold for $70 million. - Robot Sophia’s digital painting auctioned for over $600,000. - Posters and autographs of celebrities turned into NFTs. - Benefits of NFTs - Empowerment of creators: - Artists, musicians, and content creators can earn directly without middlemen. - NFTs can be coded to provide royalties every time they are resold. - Ownership and verification: - NFTs are secured through blockchain, which makes it easy to prove original ownership. - Reduces the risk of digital plagiarism. - Paperless and tamper-proof records: - NFTs can be used to issue tickets (sports, cinema, travel) with ownership verification. - Promotes transparent and efficient administration. - Global market access: - NFTs can be sold across borders using blockchain technology. - Challenges of NFTs - Price volatility and speculation: - In 2021, many simple NFTs (GIFs, memes) sold for millions of dollars. - Created an investment bubble that may collapse, leading to losses. - Money​ laundering and terror​ financing: - NFTs can be misused to hide or transfer illegal funds. - Regulatory authorities have raised concerns. - Environmental impact: - Some blockchains (especially earlier versions of Ethereum) consumed large amounts of electricity. - This increases carbon emissions. - Legal and regulatory uncertainty: - Most countries lack clear laws about taxation, consumer protection, and dispute resolution in NFT markets. - Lack of liquidity: - An NFT may be difficult to resell unless another buyer is interested. - There is no guaranteed market for every NFT. - Real-world Usage by Governments and Institutions - Ukraine: - Government used NFTs to raise funds during the Russia-Ukraine conflict. - India: - Vishwas NFT Marketplace launched as a pilot project to support Indian digital artists. - China: - Promotes NFTs under the name "digital collectibles" to avoid links to speculative crypto trading. - Uses its own national blockchain infrastructure (BSN). - South Korea: - Seoul city government is working on using NFTs to issue digital tax receipts and official documents. - United Arab Emirates (UAE): - Dubai Culture has launched NFT-based art exhibitions and registries. - Private Sector Examples: - Nike: - Introduced "Crypto​Kicks", sneakers linked to NFTs. - Coca-Cola: - Sold NFT collectibles to raise money for charity on Friendship Day. - Twitter (X): - Allows NFT profile pictures for premium subscribers. - Meta (Instagram): - Integrated NFT sharing and showcasing features in the US. - Pillar 1A2: Money Supply and Monetary Policy - Definition: - Money supply refers to the total stock of money available in an economy at a given time, encompassing currency in circulation and various types of bank deposits. Monetary policy involves actions by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) to regulate money supply, credit availability, and interest rates to achieve economic stability, control inflation, and promote growth. - Context: - Money supply and monetary policy are critical for managing liquidity, stabilizing prices, and fostering economic development in India, aligning with objectives like financial inclusion and sustainable growth. - Significance for India: - Effective monetary policy supports India’s economic goals by controlling inflation, ensuring financial stability, and facilitating credit access, crucial for a developing economy aiming for self-reliance (Atmanirbhar Bharat). - Demand for Money: Liquidity Preference Theory - Definition: - Liquidity Preference Theory, proposed by John Maynard Keynes in his 1936 book *The General Theory of Employment, Interest and Money*, explains why individuals prefer to hold money in liquid form (cash) based on three motives. - Motives for Holding Money: - Transaction Motive: - Individuals hold money for routine transactions, such as purchasing daily necessities like milk or vegetables, facilitating trade as a medium of exchange. - Precautionary Motive: - Money is held to meet unexpected or emergency expenses, such as medical costs or impulsive purchases during travel, ensuring financial preparedness. - Speculative Motive: - Also known as asset demand, individuals hold cash to capitalize on future investment opportunities, such as buying gold or land when prices fall. - Factors Affecting Total Demand for Money: - Total demand = Transaction demand + Precautionary demand + Speculative demand. - Inverse Relationship with Interest Rates: - Higher deposit interest rates encourage people to invest in banks rather than hold cash, reducing liquidity preference, and vice versa. - Other Factors: - Income levels, consumption patterns, and economic uncertainty influence the amount of money held for each motive. - Liquidity of Assets - Definition: - Liquidity refers to the ease with which an asset can be converted into cash without significant loss of value. - Types of Assets: - Highly Liquid Assets: - Cash, gold, demand deposits, government securities (G-Sec/T-Bills), and shares/bonds of reputed companies, easily convertible to cash. - Relatively Illiquid Assets: - Real estate, paintings, or sculptures, which are harder to sell quickly at the desired price due to limited buyer availability. - Liquidity Injection: - Definition - - Liquidity injection refers to the monetary policy action undertaken by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) to increase the availability of money (cash) in the financial system. This is done to ease tight liquidity conditions, promote lending, and stimulate economic activity. - By purchasing these assets, the RBI credits money into the banks' accounts, which: - Increases their cash reserves - Enhances their lending capacity - Mechanism of Liquidity Injection - RBI purchases financial instruments like: - Government Securities (G-Secs) - Treasury Bills (T-Bills) - State Development Loans (SDLs) - These purchases are made from: - Commercial Banks - Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs) - Primary Dealers - This process is typically executed via: - Open Market Operations (OMOs) - OMOs are the buying and selling of government securities (G-Secs) by the RBI in the open market. - Long-Term Repo Operations (LTROs) - LTRO is a tool where RBI provides banks with long-term liquidity (typically 1 to 3 years) at the prevailing repo rate. - Unlike OMOs, no securities are purchased; instead, it’s a loan against eligible collateral. - Targeted Long-Term Repo Operations (TLTROs) - A variation of LTRO with an additional targeting condition. - Banks receiving funds must invest in specific sectors or instruments. - Impact on Economy - Short-term: - Banks have more funds to lend. - Call money rates, repo rates may drop temporarily. - Market sentiment improves (especially in stock and bond markets). - Medium to Long-term: - Boosts aggregate demand. - Helps in reviving stalled credit cycles. - May cause inflationary pressures if overdone, especially if supply doesn’t match rising demand. - Examples of Liquidity Injection in India - COVID-19 Pandemic (2020): - RBI used Targeted Long-Term Repo Operations (TLTROs) to inject over ₹1 lakh crore liquidity. - Banks were mandated to invest the funds in investment-grade bonds, commercial paper, etc. - Global Financial Crisis (2008): - RBI conducted OMOs to infuse liquidity when foreign institutional investors exited and rupee weakened. - Liquidity Trap: - Definition - - A liquidity trap refers to a situation in which monetary policy becomes ineffective in stimulating economic activity despite low or even zero interest rates. - People hoard cash instead of investing or spending, as they expect deflation, low returns, or worsening economic conditions. - Traditional tools like cutting interest rates or injecting liquidity into the system do not revive demand. - Characteristics of a Liquidity Trap - Interest rates are already near zero (Zero Lower Bound). - Despite central bank efforts, money demand remains high and spending remains low. - Consumers and investors prefer holding on to cash. - Inflation remains subdued or deflation persists. - Causes - Deflationary expectations (people delay purchases anticipating further price drops). - Lack of consumer and business confidence. - Weak transmission of monetary policy. - Overleveraging or high debt levels discouraging new borrowing. - Consequences - Stagnant or negative GDP growth. - High unemployment despite monetary stimulus. - Central bank loses control over demand-side management. - May necessitate unconventional tools like quantitative easing or fiscal stimulus. - Historical Examples - Japan (1990s–present): - After the asset price bubble burst, Japan faced prolonged stagnation and deflation. - Despite near-zero interest rates, economic activity remained muted. - USA (Great Depression 1930s and 2008 Global Financial Crisis period): - Federal Reserve faced difficulty reviving credit and demand. - Adopted Quantitative Easing to stimulate economy. - Policy Solutions - Fiscal policy stimulus (increased public spending). - Forward guidance to raise inflation expectations. - Quantitative easing (QE) and asset purchase programs. - Negative interest rate policy (NIRP) in some cases. - Deposit Types - Definition: - Deposits are funds placed in banks, categorized based on accessibility and purpose, forming a significant component of money supply. - Types of Deposits: - Demand Deposits: - Funds in current accounts, savings accounts, and salary accounts, accessible on demand without penalty. - Examples: - Corporate salary accounts (e.g., Infosys opening accounts for employees) and individual savings accounts. - Time Deposits: - Funds in fixed deposits, cumulative/recurring deposits, or staff security deposits, accessible only at maturity or with a penalty for early withdrawal. - Green Deposits: - Introduced by RBI in 2023, these are fixed deposits where funds are loaned to environmentally friendly projects (e.g., renewable energy, waste management). -
Permitted Investments Prohibited Investments
  • Renewable energy
  • Clean transportation
  • Energy efficiency
  • Afforestation
  • Fossil fuels
  • Nuclear power
  • Tobacco-related businesses
- Notes: - Green deposits are issued in rupees only. - Covered by DICGC (Deposit Insurance and Credit Guarantee Corporation), with protection up to ₹5 lakh in case the bank fails. - It is not mandatory for banks or NBFCs to offer green deposits. - If no eligible green borrowers are found, funds may be temporarily parked in liquid instruments like Treasury Bills (T-Bills) with maturity up to 1 year. - Bulk Deposits: - Large fixed deposits, defined by RBI as Rs. 3 crore or higher (post-2024, previously Rs. 2 crore). - Banks may offer different interest rates based on their business strategy. - Inoperative and Unclaimed Deposits: -
Inoperative Accounts Unclaimed Deposits
  • No transactions for 2+ years.
  • Banks must pay interest on savings accounts.
  • Savings/current accounts unoperated for 10 years or fixed deposits unclaimed 10 years post-maturity.
  • Over ₹42,000 crore stuck (2023 data).
- UDGAM Portal (2023): - Launched by RBI as the Unclaimed Deposits Gateway to Access Information, allowing beneficiaries to search unclaimed deposits across banks using Aadhaar or PAN. - Funds unclaimed for 10 years are transferred to RBI’s Depositor Education and Awareness Fund (DEA). - Time and demand liabilities of a bank -
Time Liabilities of a Bank (FDRD) Demand Liabilities of a Bank (CASA)
  • Fixed Deposits (FD)
  • Recurring Deposits (RD)
  • Cumulative Deposits
  • Staff Security Deposits
  • Overdue Fixed Deposits
  • Unclaimed time deposits
  • Current Account
  • Savings Account
  • Demand Drafts
  • Overdue Fixed Deposit balance (if not renewed)
  • Unclaimed savings/current account deposits
  • Bank not legally required to repay before maturity
  • May allow premature withdrawal with penalty
  • Less liquid (not easily convertible to cash)
  • Offers better returns and higher interest rates
  • Bank must repay on demand
  • More liquid (easily accessible cash)
  • Lower interest but high flexibility
  • Public prefers for daily/transactional needs
- Full Reserve vs. Fractional Reserve Banking - Definition: - Banking systems differ in how they manage deposits and lend funds, impacting money creation and economic stability. - Comparison: -
Aspect Full Reserve Banking Fractional Reserve Banking
Lending from Demand Deposits No; demand deposits not used for loans. Yes; banks lend both demand and time deposits.
Lending from Time Deposits Yes Yes
Vulnerability to Bank Runs Low; demand deposits are safe. High; lending both deposit types increases risk.
Economic Growth Contribution Low; limited loan availability. High; more loans fuel economic activity.
- India’s System: - India uses fractional reserve banking, allowing banks to lend a portion of deposits, increasing money supply but requiring careful regulation to prevent bank runs. - A bank run occurs when a large number of customers of a bank withdraw their deposits simultaneously because they believe the bank may become insolvent or go bankrupt. - Measures of Money Supply (M0–M4) - Definition: - Money supply refers to the total stock of currency and liquid instruments in an economy at a specific point in time. It reflects how much purchasing power exists and is crucial for economic planning and monetary policy. - Significance: - Helps the RBI decide interest rates and liquidity adjustments. - Affects inflation (price levels), credit availability, and deposit interest rates. - Understanding different money measures helps evaluate liquidity and monetary health of the economy. - - Narrow Money (M1, M2) - M1 (Most Liquid): - Currency with the public - Demand deposits with banks (Current + Savings accounts) - Other deposits with the RBI - Commonly used to assess liquidity in the short term. - M2: - M1 + Savings deposits with Post Office Savings Banks (excluding time deposits) - Slightly less liquid than M1, but still a narrow form of money. - Broad Money (M3, M4) - M3 (Aggregate Monetary Resources): - M1 + Time deposits with commercial banks (e.g., FDs, RDs) - Most widely used measure by RBI for monetary analysis. - Covers nearly 90% of the public’s money holdings. - M4: - M3 + Total deposits with Post Office Savings Organizations (excluding National Savings Certificates) - Least liquid due to postal restrictions; broader than M3 but not used commonly in policy. - M0 (Reserve Money / Monetary Base): - M0 = Currency in circulation + Bankers’ deposits with RBI + Other deposits with RBI. - Represents the base from which broader money (M1 to M4) is created via credit and deposit multipliers. - Also called high-powered money because: - It forms the foundation of the banking system's money creation. - It reflects RBI’s balance sheet liability. - Why M0 is not used for monetary policy targeting: - RBI uses M3 (Broad Money) as the operational target for monetary policy, not M0. - Reason: - M3 reflects actual money available to public and is more responsive to policy instruments like interest rates, CRR, repo, etc. - M0 is largely determined by: - RBI's own actions (e.g., OMOs, CRR management). - Government’s fiscal operations (e.g., currency issuance). - Since M0 does not include bank credit or time deposits, it does not represent total money in circulation in the economy. - Use cases of M0: - Internal to RBI for: - Analyzing base money expansion. - Studying inflationary pressures via money multiplier effect. - Used in monetary base and reserve requirement calculations. - Conclusion: - M0 is crucial for understanding the source of money creation. - But RBI targets M3 (and sometimes M1) for monetary policy purposes because these are more reflective of the economy’s liquidity and public money supply. - Liquidity Comparison: - M1 > M2 > M3 > M4 in terms of liquidity. - M1 and M2 are narrow money (highly liquid, low volume). - M3 and M4 are broad money (lower liquidity, higher volume). - Formula Summary: - M0 = Currency in circulation + Bankers’ deposits with RBI + Other deposits with RBI - M1 = Currency with public + Demand deposits + Other deposits with RBI - M2 = M1 + Savings deposits with Post Office (excl. time deposits) - M3 = M1 + Time deposits with commercial banks - M4 = M3 + Total deposits with Post Office Savings Orgs (excluding NSCs) - Notes: - Only net public deposits are counted, inter-bank deposits are excluded. - NCERT formulas are simplified and used for prelims-oriented preparation. - RBI focuses on M3 as the core aggregate for monetary policy. - M0 is not always included in “money supply” in NCERT books but remains important for macroeconomic control. - Call Money | Notice Money | Term Money - Definition: - These terms refer to short-term money market instruments used when banks or Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs) lend or borrow funds among themselves for a duration less than 1 year. - Duration Classification: - Call Money: - Loan duration is 1 day - Also referred to as "overnight" borrowing or lending. - Notice Money: - Loan duration is 2 to 14 days - Term Money: - Loan duration is more than 14 days but less than 1 year - Notes: - These are interbank lending instruments, part of the short-term money market. - Should not be confused with: - Broad money or narrow money - Measures like M1, M2, M3, or M4 - Vault Cash: - Refers to cash kept by bankers with themselves for daily operations - Money Multiplier (MM) - Definition: - Measures how many units of bank money (M3) are supported by one unit of central bank money (M0) - Shows how intensively the banking system reuses base money through lending - What it is NOT: - MM does not mean new physical money is created, RBI alone creates M0 (currency + bank reserves). Banks only create deposit balances and loan claims on that M0 - Practical explanation: - When banks lend, they create deposits, these deposits become part of M3 - Multiple deposit holders can simultaneously claim the same underlying M0 - MM measures how many such claims exist on each unit of base money - Economic meaning: - Higher MM: - More lending - More deposit money - Higher spending power in the economy - Greater financial leverage and risk - Lower MM: - Less lending - More money held as reserves - Lower liquidity - Safer but slower banking system - Formula: - \( MM = \frac{M3}{M0} \) - Cases - Theoretical case (no cash leakages, no excess reserves): - \( MM = \frac{1}{CRR} \) - CRR (Cash Reserve Ratio) is the percentage of a bank’s total deposits that it must keep with the RBI in cash form. - I tried to find out why CRR is used in place of M0 in this formula, but could not understand, so better just mug it up and continue. - Example: - If \( CRR = 4\% = 0.04 \) - \( MM = \frac{1}{0.04} = 25 \) - Meaning: - \( 1 \) unit of \( M0 \) can create \( 25 \) units of \( M3 \) - Real world case: - \( MM_{actual} < \frac{1}{CRR} \) - Due to: - Cash held by public - Excess reserves held by banks - Poor banking penetration - Fractional reserve banking & Credit Creation practical example: - Assumptions: - Initial base money (M0) = 100 - Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR) = 10% - All loans are redeposited in the banking system - Only bank can give loan, if an individual gives loan then money is transferred from his account to another and there is no net change -
Stage What Happens Bank Receiving Deposit Deposit Amount CRR Kept (10%) Loan Created Where the Loan Goes Total Deposits So Far (M3)
1 RBI injects base money Bank #1 100 10 90 Borrower spends it, receiver deposits in Bank #2 100
2 First loan returns as a deposit Bank #2 90 9 81 Borrower spends it, receiver deposits in Bank #3 190
3 Second loan returns as a deposit Bank #3 81 8.1 72.9 Spent and redeposited in next bank 271
… Process keeps repeating More banks Smaller each round 10% each round Shrinks each round Loans keep returning as deposits Approaches 1000
Final Total deposits (M3) ≈ 1000 from original M0 = 100 → Money Multiplier = 10 = 1 / CRR
- Characteristics: - In a functional economy, MM is always >1, and CRR is always <100%. - MM directly increases with a reduction in CRR. - MM depends on reserve money and systemic leakages (e.g., cash holdings, CRR). - CRR & Cash are leakages to money multiplier says says Exchange Settlement Account 2025 (ES25) - Factors Affecting Money Multiplier - Cash Holdings - More cash held by individuals reduces bank deposits, acting as a leakage, lowering MM. - Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR) - Higher CRR requires banks to hold more reserves with RBI, reducing lendable funds, lowering MM. - Banking Penetration - Increased financial inclusion and digital economy boost deposits, raising MM. - Economic Conditions - Boom periods with high loan demand or expansionary monetary policy (low CRR) increase MM. - Note: - Statutory Liquidity Ratio (SLR) impact on MM cannot be mathematically quantified. - Historical Trends - 1981–2020 - Source: - Economic Survey 2020. - Trend: - Zigzag pattern, not steadily increasing. - Mid-1990s to 2016-17: - MM increased due to LPG reforms and banking penetration. - Historical Values: - 1960s: - MM < 2x. - 1990s: - MM > 3x. - 2020: - MM > 5x. - 2001–2024 - Source: - Economic Survey 2024. - 2023: - MM > 5x, driven by LPG reforms, increased banking penetration, and digital economy growth. - 2000 Note Withdrawal (2023): - Increased bank deposits as people retained funds in accounts (instead of withdrawing exchanged notes), boosting MM and liquidity. - Factors Increasing Liquidity/MM (2023): - Financial inclusion, digital economy, less-cash economy, high loan demand during economic booms, expansionary monetary policy (e.g., low CRR to combat deflation). - Significance - Higher MM supports economic growth by increasing liquidity but requires careful management to avoid inflation. - Creation of Money (M0) - Definition: - M0 (also called High Powered Money / Reserve Money / Monetary Base) refers to the total currency created by the RBI. - M0 is the base money; everything else (M1–M4) is built upon this - It is the liability side of RBI’s balance sheet, created by the Issue Department under the RBI Act. - Why is it a liablity to RBI? - Your ₹1,000 in a bank is not money in itself it is a liability of the bank a promise that the bank will give you RBI money if you demand it - RBI is a bank for banks. In the same way individuals deposits are liability to a commercial bank, it is the liability of RBI to hold deposits of banks. - So: - RBI money (M0) = liability of the central bank. - Bank deposits (M3) = liability of commercial banks - For every rupee of currency it issues, RBI’s Issue Department must hold an equivalent value of eligible assets (to maintain public confidence). - Why it is called High Powered Money: - Because it forms the base for creation of broader money (M1, M2, M3, M4) via fractional reserve system by commercial banks. - It has a multiplier effect on the economy’s money supply (explained separately under Money Multiplier). - Assets of Issue Department (i.e., backing for currency issued): - Rupee coins and ₹1 notes - RBI does not issue coins and ₹1 notes, RBI only circulates them as Government of India’s agent. - When RBI holds rupee coins and ₹1 notes in its vaults on behalf of Government of India, those coins and and ₹1 notes are a claim on the Government and therefore an asset to RBI’s Issue Department - Why RBI don't issue coins and ₹1 notes? - Because coins and ₹1 notes are not about banking, they are about sovereignty. - The most basic form of money belongs to me, not to my banker. - Gold coin and bullion: - RBI must hold a minimum Rs. 200 crore in gold. - Of this, Rs. 115 crore must be in India in the form of coin or bullion. - Foreign securities (mainly foreign exchange reserves): - No minimum limit post-amendment in 1956 and 1995. - Includes securities of foreign governments, IMF SDRs, etc. - Government securities: - Government Securities (G-Secs) are debt instruments (e.g., G-Secs, Treasury Bills) issued by the government to borrow money from the public. - When you buy a G-Sec, you are lending money to the government. - In return, the government promises to repay: - The principal amount (at maturity), and - Interest (periodically, usually twice a year). - Used to balance the liability of currency issued when needed. - Liabilities of Issue Department (i.e., components of M0): - Currency in circulation: - Because a currency is a promise printed on paper. And promises are always liabilities. - Currency with public. - Cash held by banks (vault cash). - Bankers' deposits with RBI: - Includes CRR (Cash Reserve Ratio) maintained by scheduled commercial banks. - Other deposits with RBI includes: - Deposits of Central and State Governments. - Deposits of international institutions (like IMF). - Deposits of NBFCs or other financial institutions (if allowed). - Relation between M0 and CRR or NFA - When CRR increases, M0 increases - CRR stands for Cash Reserve Ratio, which is the percentage of total deposits that banks are required to keep with the Reserve Bank of India. - When the Reserve Bank of India increases the CRR, commercial banks are required to park more cash with the Reserve Bank. - This cash held by banks with the Reserve Bank is included in Reserve Money or M0. - Therefore, an increase in CRR leads to an increase in M0 because: - A larger portion of money is held in the form of reserves with the Reserve Bank - Although this reduces the banks’ ability to lend, it mechanically raises the monetary base - Note: - This rise in M0 is not due to new note printing, but due to more reserves being maintained with the Reserve Bank - When Net Foreign Assets (NFA) increase, M0 increases - NFA refers to the Net Foreign Assets of the Reserve Bank of India - When the Reserve Bank buys foreign currency (such as United States dollars) from the market (Indian banks and not international entities) to prevent appreciation of the Indian Rupee: - An exporter earns USD from abroad, the bank cannot use USD in India, so it sells those USD to RBI. RBI pays in Indian Rupees to acquire those foreign currencies - This payment injects rupees into the economy, increasing Reserve Money or M0 - RBI is just absorbing foreign currency from Indian banks and issuing rupees in exchange. - Thus, when Net Foreign Assets rise, M0 also rises because: - The Reserve Bank's asset side increases as its foreign exchange holdings go up - The liability side also increases, as more Indian Rupees are released into the system, either as currency in circulation or as reserves of banks - In other words: - The Reserve Bank is expanding base money by acquiring foreign exchange and releasing rupees into the system - Notes: - ₹1 notes and all coins (₹1, ₹2, ₹5...) are liabilities of Government of India, issued by it and circulated by RBI as its agent. - All currency notes of ₹2 and above are liabilities of RBI, issued by the Issue Department. - Why they consider currency as liability? - From a balance sheet perspective: - When RBI issues currency, it receives assets in return (like government securities or foreign currency). - The corresponding liability is the currency it owes the bearer, recorded as "currency in circulation" under liabilities - Hence, currency notes are not "owned" by RBI, but are owed to the public — the ultimate holders of the money, making them liability for it. - What if RBI's Assets (e.g., gold or foreign reserves) are stolen or lost? - Liabilities Do Not Disappear: - The currency already issued (liability) remains in circulation. - The public still holds those notes, and RBI still owes the value they represent. - So the liability stays on the balance sheet. - Loss is Recorded as an Expense or Write-Off: - So RBI's balance sheet still balances, but it now has lower asset backing and weaker financial position. - M0 increases when RBI: - Acquires more foreign securities (e.g., buys dollars). - Buys government securities from the market (OMO). - Prints more currency and injects it into the system. - Money Supply and FRBM Act - Definition: - The Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management (FRBM) Act, enacted in 2003, is a law aimed at ensuring fiscal discipline by limiting the government's borrowing and fiscal deficit. - Key Provisions: - The central government must maintain its fiscal deficit within a specified limit as a percentage of Gross Domestic Product. - Important concepts: - Fiscal deficit - A fiscal deficit occurs when a government's total spending exceeds its total revenue (excluding borrowing) within a specific period, usually a fiscal year - Monetization of Deficit: - This refers to the process of the Reserve Bank of India printing new money to finance the government’s deficit by purchasing government securities directly. - This practice is heavily restricted under the FRBM Act to avoid triggering inflationary pressures in the economy. - It is permitted only under exceptional and clearly defined circumstances. - The FRBM Act bans one very specific thing: - The government is not allowed to borrow directly from the Reserve Bank of India by asking it to buy government securities in the primary market. - Exception to this? - Short-term borrowing through Ways and Means Advances. - Emergency situations such as war, natural disasters, agricultural collapse, or sharp decline in Gross Domestic Product. - What FRBM promotes? - The Reserve Bank of India is allowed to conduct Open Market Operations, which involve buying and selling government securities in the secondary market to manage liquidity. - The secondary market is the market where already issued financial instruments like stocks, bonds, or government securities are bought and sold between investors. - Provided in detail in later part - Currency in Circulation (CIC) - Definition: - Currency in Circulation refers to the total amount of cash in the form of notes and coins actively used by the public for making payments. - It excludes non-cash modes like cheques, debit or credit cards, and UPI transactions. - Trends: - Year 2021: - The growth of CIC slowed down. - This was due to reduced precautionary cash holdings after the COVID-19 pandemic. - More money was being deposited back into the banking system. - Year 2022: - CIC temporarily increased. - The Russia-Ukraine war led to heightened uncertainty, causing people to hold more cash as a precaution. - Significance: - It reflects the public's preference for cash versus digital payments. - A higher CIC suggests continued reliance on cash. - A lower CIC indicates a shift toward digital modes. - The Reserve Bank of India monitors CIC trends to decide on monetary policy adjustments. - Velocity of Money Circulation - Definition: - Velocity of money refers to the average number of times a unit of currency is used to purchase goods and services within a specific time period. - It reflects the pace at which money moves through the economy and indicates the level of economic activity. - Example: - A ten rupee note is used by a person to buy a pen. - The shopkeeper then uses the same ten rupee note to buy a cup of tea. - That single note facilitated transactions worth twenty rupees. - Hence, the velocity in this case is two. - Factors Affecting Velocity of Money: - Income Distribution: - Lower-income individuals tend to spend most of their income quickly on necessities. - Therefore, money in their hands circulates faster than when held by higher-income individuals, who tend to save more. - Borrowing Habits: - In economies where borrowing is easy and culturally common, people spend beyond their income. - This increases the flow of money and raises its velocity. - Economic Cycles: - During economic booms, businesses purchase more raw materials and expand operations. - This increases the frequency of transactions, thereby increasing the velocity of money. - Significance: - High velocity of money indicates strong economic activity, confidence, and demand in the market. - Low velocity suggests sluggish spending, low demand, or economic stagnation. - Central banks monitor velocity trends while designing monetary policy. - Liquidity Overhang - Definition: - - Liquidity Overhang refers to a situation where there is excess liquidity (money supply) in the banking system that is not being absorbed through credit creation or investment. - It implies that banks have more money than they are willing or able to lend. - Causes: - RBI injecting liquidity through: - Open Market Operations (OMO) purchases - Repo operations - Currency depreciation (foreign exchange purchase by RBI, adding rupees to system) - RBI buying foreign currency (adds rupee liquidity) - High government spending without matching absorption capacity - Government increases spending → More money enters the economy. - But economy lacks: - Productive capacity (factories, transport, skilled labor), - Supply chain depth (raw materials, logistics), - Infrastructure or business readiness. - So the extra money isn't converted into real output. - Large capital inflows (Foreign Portfolio Investment / Foreign Direct Investment) - Drop in credit demand (e.g., during economic slowdown or uncertainty) - RBI reducing Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR) or Statutory Liquidity Ratio (SLR) - Consequences: - Surplus funds with banks parked in RBI’s reverse repo window (instead of lending) - Lower interest rates in the money market due to high liquidity - Lower interests -> Cheap loans -> More shopping -> More demand -> Inflation problem worsens - Inflationary pressure if the excess liquidity starts entering the economy through lending - Weakens monetary policy transmission if banks are unwilling to lend - Can distort asset prices (e.g., real estate, stocks) due to easy money - RBI response to liquidity overhang: - Increase CRR or SLR - Conduct Open Market Operations (OMO) sales (sells G-Secs to absorb liquidity) - Conduct Reverse Repo operations (to attract banks to park excess funds with RBI) - Reverse repo is RBI saying “I will borrow money from you for a short time. Park your cash with me.” - Banks give money to RBI, RBI gives them government securities as collateral, RBI pays interest to the banks - This interest given by RBI to banks is called the Reverse Repo Rate, it is opposite of repo rate. - Use Market Stabilisation Scheme (MSS) bonds - Issue Cash Management Bills (CMBs) to mop up excess funds - Cash Management Bills (CMBs) are very short-term government securities issued by the Government of India to meet temporary mismatches in cash flows. - Example: - When ₹500 and ₹1000 notes were demonetized in 2016, and again when ₹2000 was withdrawn in 2023, the following happened: 1. Public deposited huge cash into banks - People returned their invalidated ₹500/₹2000 notes. - Banks suddenly received a flood of deposits (liquidity inflow). 2. But credit demand didn't increase at the same pace - Businesses were cautious, - People were unsure about borrowing. 3. Result: Liquidity Overhang - Too much cash in the banking system, - But not enough demand for loans, - RBI calls this situation "liquidity overhang." - During COVID-19 period, RBI infused large liquidity through LTRO, TLTRO, but credit demand was low, leading to liquidity overhang as banks parked excess funds in reverse repo. - Liquidity Trap vs Liqudity Overhang -
Aspect Liquidity Trap Liquidity Overhang
Definition Excess cash holding due to low interest rates and economic pessimism. Excess liquidity in the system due to high money supply or low loan demand.
Impact Reduces effectiveness of monetary policy; limits economic growth. Increases inflation risk if not absorbed by economic activity.
- Pillar 1A3: Monetary Policy - Definition: - Monetary policy is a macroeconomic strategy designed by a country’s central bank to manage money supply and interest rates, influencing variables such as inflation, consumption, savings, investment, and capital formation. - Context: - Monetary policy has evolved as a vital tool for central banks to stabilize economies, control inflation, and promote sustainable growth, adapting to domestic and global economic challenges. - Significance for India: - The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) employs monetary policy to maintain price stability (2-6% CPI), foster economic growth, generate employment, ensure exchange rate stability, and promote financial inclusion, aligning with India’s vision of becoming a developed economy by 2047. - Objectives of Monetary Policy - Definition: - The objectives of monetary policy are the goals set by the RBI to guide its actions in managing money supply and interest rates to achieve macroeconomic stability. - Milton Friedman: - This American economist's research on monetary policy made this subject more popular, he also won a Nobel in Economics in this regard (1976). - Primary Objectives: - Price Stability: - Maintains inflation within 2-6% Consumer Price Index (CPI) to preserve purchasing power and ensure economic predictability. - Economic Growth: - Encourages investment and consumption through favorable monetary conditions to support sustainable GDP growth. - Employment Generation: - Stimulates economic activity to create jobs, reducing unemployment. - Philip Curve: - - Inflation ↑ = unemployment ↓ (and vice versa). - So, stable & moderate inUation is good for the economy. - So, RBI tries to keep inflation with 2-6% Consumer Price Index (CPI) using its bi-monthly monetary policy made by its 6- member statutory Monetary Policy Committee. - Why unemployment decreases with inflation and vice versa? - 1. Demand-pull Inflation Logic: - When economic activity increases, firms sell more goods/services. - To meet demand, they hire more workers → unemployment falls. - To attract workers and expand production, they increase wages. - Higher wages + strong demand → rise in prices (inflation). - 2. Wage-Price Spiral: - Lower unemployment → workers have more bargaining power (as they less people are looking for job). - Higher wages → higher spending → more demand → firms raise prices. - This continues in a cycle: higher wages → higher prices → more inflation. - 3. Short-Run Trade-Off: - In the short run, policymakers (like RBI or govt) may reduce unemployment by stimulating demand (via spending or rate cuts). - This causes higher inflation as the economy heats up. - 4. But in the Long Run (as per Friedman/Phelps): - The trade-off breaks down. - Economy returns to natural rate of unemployment. - If you keep pushing demand, it only leads to higher inflation, not lower unemployment. - Exchange Rate Stability: - Stabilizes the rupee’s external value to promote trade and attract foreign investment. - Secondary Objectives: - Financial Inclusion: - Enhances access to financial services, supporting initiatives like Jan Dhan Yojana. - Social Justice: - Ensures equitable credit distribution to priority sectors (e.g., agriculture, MSMEs) to reduce economic disparities. - Debt Securities and Market Operations - Definition: - Debt securities are financial instruments representing a loan from an investor to a borrower (government or corporate), entitling the holder to receive principal and interest at specified intervals. They are integral to RBI’s monetary policy for liquidity management (e.g., Open Market Operations) and facilitating government borrowing. - Role in Monetary Policy: - Government Securities (G-Secs) are used in Open Market Operations (OMO) to inject or absorb liquidity, influencing money supply and interest rates. - Bond yields affect monetary transmission by impacting borrowing costs across sectors. - Retail participation (via Retail Direct Scheme) deepens the G-Sec market, enhancing liquidity and supporting RBI’s borrowing and yield management strategies. - Types of Debt Securities: - Government: - Different government securities: - Short-term (< 1 year): - Treasury Bills (T-Bills): - 91 days - 182 days - 364 days - Cash Management Bills: - Issued for temporary cash flow mismatches - Flexible short-term maturity - Long-term (≥ 1 year): - Government Securities (G-Secs): - Dated securities with maturities from 5 to 40 years - State Development Loans (SDLs): - Issued by State Governments - Sovereign Gold Bonds: - Gold-linked, interest-bearing bonds - Inflation Indexed Bonds: - Principal and interest adjusted for inflation - Interest rate characteristics: - Very low default risk - Interest rates act as benchmark for the entire financial system - Corporate: - Different government securities: - Short-term (< 1 year): - Commercial Papers: - Unsecured instruments - Maturity between 7 and 365 days - Bills of Exchange and Promissory Notes: - Used for short-term trade financing - Long-term (≥ 1 year): - Bonds: - Usually secured - Fixed interest - Example: - ₹1000 bond at 9.40% for 15 years - Debentures: - Can be secured or unsecured - Issued for various tenures - Interest rate characteristics: - Higher than government securities - Depend on: - Credit rating - Inflation - Market conditions - Retail Direct Scheme (2021): - Definition: - Launched by RBI in November 2021, the Retail Direct Scheme allows retail investors (Resident Indians and NRIs) to open Retail Direct Gilt (RDG) accounts on the E-Kuber platform to directly purchase T-Bills, G-Secs, SDLs, and Sovereign Gold Bonds without intermediaries. - Features: - Zero application fees, reducing costs compared to mutual fund investments. - Accessible via RBI’s E-Kuber platform, with secondary market trading enabled since 2022. - Supports financial inclusion by encouraging retail participation in government securities. - Impact: - Deepens the G-Sec market, increases liquidity, and reduces reliance on institutional investors. - Supports government borrowing by broadening the investor base, stabilizing yields. - Recent Developments (2025): - As of July 2025, over 1 lakh RDG accounts opened, with retail participation in G-Secs rising by 15% since 2023 (RBI Annual Report 2024–25). - Bond Yield: - Definition: - The annual return an investor earns on a bond, expressed as a percentage of its current market price (e.g., coupon rate ÷ market price × 100). - Inverse Relationship with Price: - Yield is inversely proportional to the bond’s secondary market price. - Example: - An 8% coupon bond bought at ₹100 yields 8%; at ₹90, yields 8.89% (8 ÷ 90 × 100); at ₹110, yields 7.27% (8 ÷ 110 × 100). - Mechanism: - Higher bond demand increases price, reducing yield; lower demand decreases price, increasing yield. - Factors Affecting Bond Yield: - Economic Conditions: - Booming economy: - Investors sell bonds at lower prices to invest in shares for higher dividends, increasing yields (e.g., post-2023 recovery). - Recession: - Investors prefer bonds for fixed returns, increasing demand, raising prices, and lowering yields (e.g., 2020 COVID crisis). - Inflation: - Higher inflation erodes real returns, prompting investors to demand higher yields, lowering bond prices. - Political Instability: - Increases risk perception, reducing bond demand, lowering prices, and raising yields (e.g., Sri Lanka’s 2022 crisis). - Central Bank Actions: - Operation Twist (2019–20): - RBI bought long-term G-Secs and sold short-term ones to flatten the yield curve, reducing long-term yields. - G-Sec Acquisition Programme (G-SAP, 2021): - RBI purchased ₹2.2 lakh crore in G-Secs to lower yields and support borrowing during COVID recovery. - Yield Curve Management: - RBI uses OMO and schemes like Operation Twist to influence the yield curve (short-term vs. long-term yields), ensuring effective monetary transmission. - Corporate Bonds: - Definition: - Debt securities issued by companies to raise funds, offering fixed interest and principal repayment after a specified period (e.g., Reliance Bond: ₹1000, 9.40% p.a., 15 years). - Factors Determining Interest Rates: - Credit Rating: - Lower ratings (e.g., CCC, D) require higher interest rates due to higher default risk (e.g., IL&FS crisis, 2018). - Inflation: - Bonds must offer rates above inflation to attract investors (e.g., Inflation-Indexed Bonds adjust for CPI, Ref: Pillar#1C). - Bank Deposit Rates: - Higher bank fixed deposit rates (e.g., 7% in 2023) force companies to offer competitive bond rates (e.g., 8–10%) to attract household savings. - G-Sec Yield: - If G-Sec yields rise (e.g., 7.5% in 2025), corporate bonds must offer higher rates (e.g., 9–11%) to compete with safer government securities. - Impact on Monetary Policy: - G-Secs facilitate liquidity management (OMO: buying injects money, selling absorbs it). - Bond yields influence borrowing costs, impacting credit availability and inflation control. - Retail Direct Scheme and yield management (e.g., G-SAP, Operation Twist) enhance monetary policy efficacy by stabilizing interest rates and supporting fiscal needs. - Quantitative Tools - Definition: - Quantitative tools, also called general or indirect instruments, are RBI’s macro-level monetary policy instruments used to regulate overall money supply, liquidity and credit conditions in the economy. They include both regulatory ratios (like CRR and SLR) and market-based operations (like repo, OMOs, etc.) and affect all sectors uniformly rather than targeting specific industries. - Types: - 1. Statutory Reserve Requirements - Definition: - Mandates banks to hold a portion of their deposits as reserves, either with the RBI or in liquid assets, to ensure financial stability and control money supply. - Also known as Variable Reserve Ratios / Statutory Reserve Ratios. - CRR-SLR are counted on fortnightly basis. If not maintained, bank will have to pay penalty interest rate to RBI. This Penalty rate is linked with Bank Rate %. - Components: - a. Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR): - Definition and details: - It is the percentage of a bank’s total Net Demand and Time Liabilities (NDTL) that it must maintain as cash reserves with the Reserve Bank of India - RBI doesn't pay interest on this deposit, except in extraordinary circumstances. - RBI can fix any amount of CRR. Legally there is no minimum or maximum limit. Although in real life, RBI will not keep CRR@100% otherwise, no bank can give loans then economy will stop functioning. - Presently it is 4.50% of Net Demand & Time Liabilities of a bank (NDTL) - Net Demand and Time Liabilities = the total deposit base of a bank, after adjusting for what it owes to other banks. - Example: - If CRR is 4 percent and a bank has ₹100 crore in NDTL, it must keep ₹4 crore with the Reserve Bank in the form of cash - Purpose of CRR: - To ensure liquidity and solvency of banks - The Reserve Bank holds this cash to make sure banks have enough money in reserve in case of unexpected withdrawals or financial stress - To control money supply in the economy - By increasing CRR, the Reserve Bank can reduce the amount of money available with banks for lending - By decreasing CRR, the Reserve Bank allows banks to lend more, thus increasing money supply - As a monetary policy tool - It is used by the Reserve Bank to manage inflation, liquidity, and credit growth - Higher CRR reduces inflationary pressure by reducing available money with banks - Lower CRR helps stimulate the economy by allowing more lending - Impact: - Higher CRR reduces lendable funds, contracting money supply;
