lower CRR increases liquidity, expanding money supply. - Case of incremental CRR (iCRR) after ₹2000 withdrawal: - Incremental deposits - Incremental deposits = new deposits received during a specific period − deposits that existed before that period - Incremental CRR (I-CRR): - I-CRR is the temporary measure to absorb excess liquidity and prevent liquidity overhang. - It reduces money supply to control inflation and ensure bank run safety. - e.g., 10% on additional deposits post-₹2000 note withdrawal in 2023. - Context: - After the withdrawal of ₹2000 notes, around 87% of those notes were deposited into bank accounts instead of being exchanged for smaller denomination notes (e.g., 4 × ₹500). - This led to a surge in bank deposits and created a risk of liquidity overhang. - Problem: - When there’s excess liquidity in the banking system beyond productive credit demand, creating "Liquidity Overhang". - May lead to inflationary pressures, asset bubbles, or inefficient allocation of resources. - Solution: - Incremental Cash Reserve Ratio (i-CRR) - Introduced by RBI to absorb excess liquidity post ₹2000 note return. - Normal CRR: - 4.0% of NDTL (Net Demand and Time Liabilities) - i-CRR (Temporary CRR): - RBI directed banks to maintain an additional 10% CRR on the incremental deposits received during May 19, 2023 to July 28, 2023 - Applicable period: - August 12 to October 7, 2023 - Objective of i-CRR: - Reduce excess money supply. - Absorb temporary surge in deposits. - Avoid liquidity overhang risk. - RBI Clarification: - Did not specify whether i-CRR applies only to ₹2000 deposits or also to ₹100-₹500 deposits. So, no need to speculate for exam purposes. - Interest on i-CRR: - No, RBI did not pay interest on the i-CRR parked by banks. - Outcome: - i-CRR temporarily locked ₹1 trillion worth of liquidity. - It was withdrawn after October 7, 2023. - A similar measure was taken after 2016 demonetisation of ₹500 and ₹1000 notes. - b. Statutory Liquidity Ratio (SLR) - Definition and details: - SLR is the minimum percentage of a commercial bank’s Net Demand and Time Liabilities (NDTL) that it must maintain in the form of approved liquid assets before providing credit to customers - Net Demand and Time Liabilities = the total deposit base of a bank, after adjusting for what it owes to other banks. - These assets must be kept with the bank itself, not with the Reserve Bank of India - Legally, SLR can't be made more than 40%. Presently it is 18.00% of NDTL. - All Banks must keep CRR. - However, RBI may prescribe separate % norms/slabs for Regional Rural Banks (RRBs) and Cooperative Banks. - Components allowed under SLR: - Cash - Gold - Approved government securities (such as G-Secs, T-bills) - Market Stabilisation Scheme (MSS) bonds and other RBI-approved securities - Purpose of SLR: - To ensure bank solvency by mandating a cushion of liquid assets - To control credit expansion in the economy - To ensure investment in government securities, helping the government borrow domestically - Acts as a monetary policy tool to regulate liquidity - Impact on economy: - When SLR increases: - Banks must hold more liquid assets, reducing funds available for lending - Leads to reduced credit flow and possibly tighter monetary conditions - When SLR decreases: - Banks can lend more freely, enhancing liquidity and credit flow in the economy - Key points: - SLR is maintained by the bank internally, not deposited with RBI - SLR assets can earn interest (e.g., through G-Secs), unlike CRR - SLR is applicable to all commercial banks, including public, private, and foreign banks operating in India - Statutory backing: - Mandated under the Banking Regulation Act, 1949 - Difference from CRR: - CRR is kept in cash with RBI and earns no interest - SLR is kept in approved liquid assets within the bank and may earn interest - Additional details: - Theoretically, CRR/SLR can be used for inflation control. But RBI primarily relies on REPO Rate (=its Policy Rate) to combat inflation, and not CRR/SLR. - CRR, SLR developments in Recent Years - Green Deposits – SBI wants Lower CRR (2023) - Background: - Green Deposits are funds collected by banks specifically for lending to environmentally sustainable (green) projects. - Issue: - As of February 2024, banks are required to maintain 4.5% CRR on all deposits — including green deposits. - SBI's Request to RBI (2023): - SBI urged the RBI to reduce or exempt CRR on green deposits to: - (A) Increase loanable funds for green projects. - (B) Reduce loan interest rates for green investments. - Status: - RBI has not yet implemented any relaxation in CRR for green deposits. - Significance: - If accepted, this move could: - Boost funding availability for green infrastructure. - Align banking policy with India’s environmental goals. - Faqs: - How can CRR be an asset for a banker? If RBI pays 0% Interest on it? - CRR (Cash Reserve Ratio) is not an income-generating asset, but it is still considered an asset in the balance sheet accounting sense because: - It is cash owned by the bank, just kept with RBI as a regulatory requirement. - In the bank’s balance sheet, CRR appears under "Cash and Balances with RBI", which is an asset. - Even if RBI gives 0% interest, it’s still bank’s own money, not a liability—hence, an asset. - What will be the impact of SLR changes on Money Multiplier? - Impact: - ↑ SLR ⇒ ↓ Money Multiplier - More money locked in government securities. - Less money available for lending → ↓ Credit creation. - ↓ SLR ⇒ ↑ Money Multiplier - Frees up funds for lending. - Increases credit creation capacity of banks. - SLR acts as a leakage in the credit creation process. Higher SLR reduces the money multiplier, and lower SLR increases it. - Does CRR and SLR also apply to the deposits made by foreigners / NRE (Non-Resident External) / FCNR(B) (Foreign Currency Non-Resident - Banks) accounts? - Yes, CRR and SLR do apply, but the treatment varies by type of deposit. - 2. Liquidity Management Framework: - Definition: - The Liquidity Management Framework is the set of monetary policy tools used by RBI to manage short-term liquidity in the banking system. - It ensures that system liquidity remains consistent with RBI’s policy stance. - It helps keep the overnight money market rate aligned with the policy Repo Rate. - Objectives: - Stabilise short-term interest rates. - Ensure smooth functioning of the payment and settlement system. - Absorb excess liquidity or inject liquidity as required. - Components of the Liquidity Management Framework: - i. Liquidity Adjustment Facility (LAF) - Definition and details: - Introduced by RBI in June 2000 based on recommendations of the Narasimham Committee (1998) and later reinforced by the Working Group on Money Market (Y.V. Reddy Committee). - It is the primary instrument used by RBI to manage day-to-day liquidity mismatches in the banking system. - Operates via Repo and Reverse Repo transactions. - Auctions conducted daily on electronic platform (e-Kuber). - Tenure of LAF operations is typically overnight but RBI can modify based on liquidity conditions. - Aims to: - Control short-term interest rates. - Signal stance of monetary policy. - Manage inflation and money supply. - Role in the monetary policy corridor: - Earlier framework (before April 2022): - Reverse Repo → Floor - Repo → Middle - MSF → Ceiling - Current framework (after April 2022): - Standing Deposit Facility (SDF) → Floor - Repo → Middle - Marginal Standing Facility (MSF) → Ceiling - Components: - a. Repo Rate - Definition and details: - Repo (Repurchase Agreement) is the interest rate at which the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) lends short-term funds to commercial banks against the collateral of government securities with an agreement to repurchase them at a predetermined date and price. - Commercial bank includes both public and private - In other words: - Banks sell government securities to the RBI to borrow money. - They agree to repurchase those same securities ("them") after a short period at a slightly higher price (reflecting the repo interest rate). - Participation in repo operations is voluntary, not mandatory. - Banks choose to approach RBI for short-term funds when they face a liquidity shortage. - Repo also called 'Ready Forward Transaction'. - Repo Rate is our Policy Rate to control inflation. - Objective: - Primary tool for liquidity injection into the banking system. - It is the central tool of RBI’s inflation-targeting monetary policy framework under the Monetary Policy Committee (MPC). - Clearing common misconceptions: - Repo operations do not permanently increase money supply. - Are meant for temporary liquidity adjustment. - RBI lends existing money in the system, not newly printed money. - Monetary Policy Role: - Considered the benchmark policy rate for monetary transmission across the economy. - Under the new liquidity framework, RBI seeks to keep money market rates aligned to the repo rate. - Macroeconomic Impact: - Net Impact on System - Liquidity increases temporarily during the repo period. - Once G-Secs are repurchased, the liquidity is withdrawn back. - In the net liquidity sense, we end up with what we started — not more. - Repo rate ↓ (accommodative stance): - Cheaper borrowing for banks → Reduced lending rates for businesses and consumers. - Boosts investment and consumption → Increases aggregate demand. - Useful during economic slowdowns or recessions to stimulate growth. - Impact on Inflation: - May lead to higher inflation if demand rises faster than supply capacity. - Effective when inflation is within target range or disinflationary risks exist. - Repo rate ↑ (tightening stance): - Costlier borrowing → Higher interest rates → Reduction in loans and credit demand. - Slows down investment and consumer spending → Reduces aggregate demand. - Helps control overheating of economy. - Impact on Inflation: - Curbs demand-pull inflation by reducing money flow in the economy. - Essential when inflation breaches RBI's upper tolerance band (e.g., above 6% in India). - Example (2020 Pandemic Period): - Repo rate was slashed to a historic low of 4.00% to support credit expansion and liquidity during COVID-19. - Operational Aspect: - Repo operations usually done via variable rate auctions or fixed rate repos. - Tenor usually ranges from overnight to 14 days. - Under special repo operations: - Long Term Repo Operations (LTRO): - 1–3 year maturity - Targeted Long Term Repo Operations (TLTRO): - Long-term repo linked to lending to specific sectors - LTRO and TLTRO apply only to Repo, not to Reverse Repo or VRRR. - Legal Basis: - Governed under RBI Act, 1934 and Liquidity Adjustment Facility (LAF) guidelines. - Current Repo vs Neutral/Terminal repo rate - Definition: - Current Repo Rate: - Present repo rate announced by RBI. - Example: - 6.25% on 17 Feb 2025. - Neutral or Terminal Repo Rate: - Theoretical interest rate at which inflation is under control and full employment is achieved. - RBI will not increase repo rate beyond this point because it would: - Hurt consumer demand. - Hurt GDP growth. - RBI has not officially declared any number or specific target for this. - Related Concept: - NAIRU (Non-Accelerating Inflation Rate of Unemployment): - Refers to the unemployment rate below which inflation begins to accelerate. - b. Reverse Repo Rate - Note - Fixed-rate Reverse Repo is no longer an active LAF tool after April 2022, RBI replaced it with SDF as the floor of the corridor. - Definition: - The rate at which RBI borrows money from commercial banks by offering government securities as collateral. - Operated via reverse repurchase agreements. - Objective: - Primary tool for absorption of surplus liquidity from the banking system. - Role in Monetary Policy: - Along with Repo Rate, it forms part of the LAF corridor. - Helps RBI manage short-term interest rates and liquidity conditions. - Impact: - Reverse Repo ↑: - Encourages banks to park excess funds with RBI. - Contracts money supply; helps in inflation control. - Reverse Repo ↓: - Banks prefer lending in interbank/market than parking with RBI. - Improves liquidity flow into the economy. - Pandemic Usage: - 2020 Reverse Repo Rate cut - Context: - COVID-19 triggered an economic slowdown → loan demand collapsed. - Banks, facing low credit offtake, began parking excess funds with RBI instead of lending. - This led to a massive build-up in the RBI’s reverse repo book. - RBI’s Response: - To discourage lazy banking (i.e., earning passive interest by parking funds with RBI), RBI reduced the Reverse Repo Rate. - Outcome: - SBI and other banks offering ~5.9% on FDs would incur losses if they parked that money at 3.35%. - Thus, banks were forced to lend more actively (even via telemarketing/SMS campaigns). - Use of VRRR - RBI deployed variable rate reverse repo auctions (VRRR) to mop up excess liquidity infused during the pandemic. - April 2022 onwards, fixed-rate reverse repo window was de-emphasized. - Current Status: - Reverse repo no longer actively used to signal monetary stance; replaced by standing deposit facility (SDF) as floor. - Faq: - Is RBI “spending” money in this as interest for the sole purpose of controlling liquidity? - Yes, RBI does pay interest to banks under reverse repo operations, but it don't take any loss because: - Even if RBI pays interest to banks on reverse repo, it earns from other operations (e.g., interest on G-Secs it holds, forex reserves). - Reverse repo is not permanent — it’s often used in short bouts - So, RBI sees the interest paid as a necessary cost to ensure financial and price stability. - This is one of the reason why reverse repo was discontinued and replaced with SDF, which allows RBI to not offer collatoral against against the money. - c. Variable Rate Reverse Repo (VRRR) - Definition: - An auction-based liquidity absorption tool under the LAF framework where RBI absorbs excess liquidity from banks at a variable interest rate. - Introduced in 2014 to make liquidity absorption more market-oriented compared to the fixed-rate reverse repo. - Objective: - To efficiently absorb durable surplus liquidity from the system, especially during surplus liquidity phases. - To fine-tune short-term interest rates around the policy rate by allowing market-determined rates. - Operational Mechanics: - Conducted through multiple tenor auctions (mostly 14-day VRRR, but may include 3/7/28 days). - Interest rate is determined via auction, unlike fixed reverse repo (which is at a pre-announced rate). - Amount and tenor are pre-announced; banks bid the amount and rate. - Role in Liquidity Framework: - Becomes the main absorption tool during periods of excess liquidity. - Represents the *floor* of the LAF corridor when used actively. - Replaces fixed reverse repo in significance during surplus phases. - Example: - In post-COVID period (2020–22), RBI used weekly 14-day VRRRs to manage massive liquidity surplus (~₹8–9 lakh crore). - RBI gradually increased VRRR size from ₹2 lakh crore to ₹6 lakh crore and above. - Eligibility: - All scheduled commercial banks (excluding RRBs and cooperative banks). - Legal Basis: - Section 45U and 45W of the RBI Act, 1934. - ii Standing facilities - a. Marginal Standing Facility (MSF) - Definition: - Introduced in May 2011 as a safety valve to provide overnight liquidity to banks. - Banks treat it as "My Survival Fund" - Marginal → extra, last-bit, emergency - Standing → always available at RBI’s window - Facility → a borrowing line - Banks can borrow up to 3% of their Net Demand and Time Liabilities (NDTL) by pledging SLR-eligible government securities. - Rate is usually 25 bps above the Repo rate. - Accessible only after banks exhaust their normal LAF limit. - Purpuse: - Helps curb volatility in the overnight money market. - Acts as the upper bound of the monetary policy corridor. - Important during periods of: - Sudden liquidity crunch - Global shocks - Monetary tightening - b. Standing Deposit Facility (SDF) - Definition and details: - It is a facility for banks to park surplus funds with RBI at a fixed rate (below Repo). - Standing → always open - Deposit → banks give money to RBI - Facility → safe parking slot - SDF rate is 25 bps below the Repo rate. - By keeping SDF 25 bps below repo, RBI makes it slightly painful to park money. - That nudges banks to first try lending to others or buy bonds or give loans - Only when they are still swimming in cash do they use SDF. - Allows RBI to absorb liquidity without offering collateral (i.e., without issuing securities). - Introduced in April 2022 Monetary Policy to replace Reverse Repo as the floor of the policy corridor. - Recommended by Urjit Patel Committee (2014) to overcome collateral-related limitations of reverse repo. - Features: - The Standing Deposit Facility (SDF) is not a loan from RBI to banks, but rather like a savings account for banks at the RBI and collateral is mostly required for borrowing/lending - Available at RBI’s discretion - Open to Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs) - Objective: - Strengthen RBI’s liquidity management framework. - Enhance flexibility in sterilization operations especially in surplus liquidity scenarios. - It ensures the liquidity absorption function of RBI continues even when securities availability is limited (unlike Reverse Repo). - System-Level Liquidity: - Definition: - Net liquidity in the banking system, calculated as [(Reverse Repo + SDF) – (Repo + MSF)]. - Impact: - Positive: Surplus liquidity; Negative: Deficit liquidity. - Example: 2023 deficit of ₹0.42–1.61 lakh crore due to inflation control measures. - Policy Corridor (Liquidity Adjustment Facility Corridor) - Definition: - The LAF Corridor is the range (spread) between the Marginal Standing Facility (MSF) Rate (upper bound) and the Standing Deposit Facility (SDF) Rate (lower bound), with the Repo Rate lying in the middle. - “Corridor” here is just RBI’s fancy way of saying a permitted interest-rate range. No tunnels, no mystery, just a band. - The interest-rate fence within which all overnight money market rates are forced to move. - It defines the short-term interest rate band within which the RBI operates to manage liquidity and overnight interest rates in the economy. - Liquidity Adjustment Facility (LAF) corridor is also known as Policy Corridor. - Structure: - Upper Bound: - MSF Rate → RBI lends to banks (last resort) - Middle: - Repo Rate → RBI’s main policy rate for lending to banks - Lower Bound: - SDF Rate → RBI absorbs excess liquidity from banks (without collateral) - Purpose: - To provide a monetary policy transmission mechanism. - To help anchor the overnight call money rate within a predictable range. - To maintain short-term liquidity stability and signal monetary stance. - Width: - As of post-2022 framework (after SDF introduction), the LAF corridor is typically fixed at 50 basis points: - MSF = Repo + 0.25% - SDF = Repo – 0.25% - Historical Note: - Before 2022, the Reverse Repo Rate was the lower bound of the corridor. - After April 2022, RBI introduced the Standing Deposit Facility (SDF) as the new floor. - Example (Assume Repo Rate = 6.50%): - MSF Rate = 6.75% - Repo Rate = 6.50% - SDF Rate = 6.25% - Corridor = 6.25% to 6.75% → 50 bps wide - Significance: - Prevents large volatility in short-term interest rates. - Acts as a signaling tool for the monetary stance of RBI. - Ensures better liquidity management for banks. - Diagrammatic Summary: -
Lower Bound Policy Rate Upper Bound
  • Standing Deposit Facility (SDF)
  • RBI absorbs liquidity
  • No collateral
  • Repo Rate
  • Main policy rate
  • Marginal Standing Facility (MSF)
  • RBI lends to banks
  • Collateral required (G-Secs)
  • Emergency borrowing
- 3. Market-Based Instruments - Definition: - These are indirect monetary policy tools used by the RBI to regulate liquidity and interest rates in the economy through financial market operations. - Unlike direct instruments (like CRR or SLR), they work through influencing market behavior, prices, and yields—particularly in the government securities and money markets. - Their effectiveness depends on the depth and development of financial markets. - Common tools include: - i. Open Market Operations (OMOs) - Definition: - The RBI’s buying or selling of government securities (G-Secs) in the secondary market to manage liquidity in the economy. - Primary market - This is where securities are born. Government issues a new G-Sec, a company launches an IPO, money goes to the issuer. - Secondary market - A secondary market is where already-issued securities get traded between investors. - No new money goes to the government or company there, just ownership changes hands. - It influences the short-term interest rates and overall liquidity conditions. - Types of OMOs: - a. Outright OMOs: - RBI permanently buys or sells G-Secs without any repurchase agreement. - Buying → increases money supply (liquidity injection) - Selling → decreases money supply (liquidity absorption) - b. Special OMOs (time-bound programs): - Operation Twist – RBI's Special OMO - Officially called Special Open Market Operation (OMO) - Mechanism - RBI conducts simultaneous purchase and sale of government securities (Special OMO). - RBI buys long-term G-Secs (e.g., 10-year bonds). - RBI sells short-term G-Secs (e.g., Treasury Bills). - Before: - Short-term rates were low - Long-term rates were high - After Operation Twist: - Short-term rates stay firm - Long-term rates fall - So the yield curve becomes flatter. This is the “twist”. - Background - Post-2019, banks were hesitant to lend due to problem of Non-Performing Assets (NPAs)/Bad loans and credit risks. - Despite repo rate cuts by RBI, lending rates for corporates remained high. - Poor transmission of monetary policy to the real economy. - Private companies continued to face high interest rates for loans and bonds. - Objective - To reduce long-term interest rates, especially on corporate and government borrowings. - To improve transmission of repo rate cuts into the broader economy. - To encourage investments, borrowing, job creation, and economic growth. - How It Works - RBI buys long-term securities - demand ↑ → price ↑ → yield (interest rate) ↓. - Establishing new benchmarks - Long-term government bond yields fall, which act as the benchmark for corporate bonds and long-term bank loans. - Therefore banks and companies are forced to borrow and lend at lower long-term interest rates. - Cheaper borrowing for Private sector Companies. Because they can borrow money by issuing their (long term) Corporate-Bonds at much cheaper interest rate than before. - When a private company meets a bank manager to borrow money, it can negotiate the loan price, If your bank does not lend me money at this cheaper interest % then I will issue my own corporate bonds. - Cheaper borrowing for Government. - Less appealing of long-term G-Sec investment vs long-term borrowing - Investor of long term G-Sec will find investing in long-term G-Secs (10-14 years) less appealing because of low returns, they will try to sell it to another party/RBI and pull out his money, then they may park it in a Corporate Bond/Bungalow/car/Goa-vacation etc. - Thus, it helps boost the consumption → economy grows. - This flattening or downward shift in the yield curve encourages long-term borrowing. - RBI sells short-term securities - demand ↓ → price ↓ → short-term yields ↑ or stable. - Due to high yield of short-term securities, investors are lured to invest in them more, this prevents short-term interest rates from falling too much and keeps liquidity in check. - Example - In December 2019 and multiple times in 2020, RBI conducted Operation Twist. - Bought ₹10,000 crore worth long-term G-Secs while simultaneously selling ₹10,000 crore worth short-term papers. - Objective was to address elevated long-term yields despite sufficient liquidity. - Notes - Called "Operation Twist" after a similar policy used by the US Federal Reserve in the 1960s. - Unlike standard OMO that affects liquidity, Operation Twist alters the maturity structure without changing net liquidity. - Government Securities Acquisition Programme (G-SAP): - RBI committed upfront to buy a fixed amount of G-Secs in a quarter to manage yield volatility and ensure adequate liquidity post-COVID. - G-SAP 1.0 -> ₹1.0 lakh crore in Q1 2021-22 - G-SAP 2.0 -> ₹1.2 lakh crore in Q2 2021-22 - Provided market certainty and improved monetary transmission. - Benefits - Increases money supply in the economy. - Boosts economic activity by injecting liquidity directly into the system. - Investors who sell G-Secs to RBI receive money: - They may spend it (↑ consumption). - Or reinvest in equity, corporate bonds, startups, etc. (↑ investment). - This spending and investing → leads to economic growth. - Helps RBI support the economy without needing to cut Repo Rate: - Not everyone wants to borrow money. - Some investors simply want to unlock liquidity from old G-Secs. - Avoids side effects of Repo Rate cut: - Excessive repo cut → may lead to inflation. - Also reduces interest on savings accounts → hurts middle-class & pensioners. - Impact: - OMO Purchase (buying G-Secs): - Liquidity injection - Used to fight deflation, stimulate growth - OMO Sale (selling G-Secs): - Liquidity absorption - Used to control inflation - ii. Market Stabilization Scheme (MSS) - Definition: - A monetary tool used by the RBI to absorb excess liquidity arising from large capital inflows, especially during times of external shocks like FII inflows or forex interventions. - Unlike normal OMOs, MSS involves issuance of special government securities, specifically for liquidity absorption, not for budgetary spending. - Key Features: - Introduced in 2004 after massive forex inflows following rupee appreciation. - Bonds issued under MSS are kept separate from regular borrowing under the fiscal deficit. - The cost of interest payment is borne by the Government of India (Ministry of Finance), not RBI. - Objective: - Sterilize excess liquidity created by RBI's foreign exchange market operations (especially when RBI buys USD to prevent rupee appreciation). - Prevent inflationary pressures and maintain monetary stability. - Example: - MSS bonds used in 2016 during demonetization when RBI needed to absorb excess liquidity from the banking system. - 4. Other quantitative tools - i. Bank Rate - Definition: - As per the RBI Act, 1934, Bank Rate is the standard rate at which the RBI is willing to buy or rediscount eligible bills of exchange and commercial papers. - It represents RBI’s long-term reference rate for such rediscounting, not a regular liquidity injection tool. - Historical Background: - Before the 1990s, Bank Rate was the primary policy rate used to signal RBI’s monetary stance. - After the introduction of the Liquidity Adjustment Facility (LAF) and Repo framework (post-2000), Bank Rate lost its operational role. - Present-Day Usage: - Bank Rate is no longer used for day-to-day liquidity management. - It is mainly used as a benchmark for: - Penal interest on CRR and SLR shortfalls - Other regulatory and statutory penalties under RBI and Banking Regulation Acts - Current Convention: - Bank Rate is kept significantly higher than Repo and MSF to act as a penalty benchmark. - In practice, it is aligned with RBI’s highest policy rate (currently MSF-linked), and often equals Repo Rate + around 3%. - This is a convention, not a statutory rule. - Significance: - Bank Rate acts as a disciplinary and legal reference rate rather than a monetary policy instrument. - It provides an anchor for penalty calculations in banking regulation. - Not Part of LAF or Corridor: - Bank Rate does not participate in liquidity operations. - It does not form part of the Repo–SDF–MSF policy corridor. - Legal Authority: - Provided under the RBI Act, 1934 (Sections 49 and related provisions). - Why not just change it's name to penalty rate instead? - Since the main goal of bank rate is to act as penalty benchmark, then why don't they change it's name to something like penalty rate? - This is because “Bank Rate” is written into: - RBI Act, 1934 - Banking Regulation Act - Dozens of subordinate laws - Court judgments - Penalty clauses - If RBI renamed it to “Penalty Rate”, Parliament would have to: - Amend multiple Acts - Update rules - Risk legal challenges - Rewrite decades of case law - So RBI does the bureaucratic thing: - Keep the antique name - Quietly change its function - Use it only for penalties - Qualitative Tools - Definition: - Qualitative tools, also known as selective credit controls or direct instruments, are mechanisms used by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) to regulate the distribution and allocation of credit to specific sectors or societal segments (e.g., agriculture, MSMEs, weaker sections), rather than controlling the overall volume of money supply. These tools address sectoral credit imbalances and promote inclusive development. - Role in Monetary Policy: - Complement quantitative tools (e.g., CRR, Repo Rate) by directing credit to priority or under-served sectors, supporting RBI’s objectives of financial inclusion, sectoral stability, and equitable economic growth. - Instruments: - 1. Moral Suasion and Publicity: - Definition: - Non-statutory, persuasive measures by the RBI, typically led by the Governor, to influence banks’ lending or operational behavior through moral authority, reputational pressure, or public opinion, without legal enforcement. - Methods: - Conferences, informal meetings with bank executives, letters, seminars, media statements, or public speeches (e.g., at university convocations, memorial lectures). - Examples: - Post-COVID (2020–21), RBI urged banks to transmit repo rate cuts to MSMEs and contact-intensive sectors (e.g., hospitality, retail) to boost credit flow and economic recovery. - Requesting banks to open rural branches, promote financial literacy, or provide loans to farmers beyond Priority Sector Lending (PSL) quotas. - Urging state Chief Ministers or Finance Ministers to control fiscal deficits and subsidy leakages to enhance the efficacy of RBI’s monetary policy. - Impact: - Encourages voluntary compliance by leveraging banks’ reputational concerns and public pressure, aligning lending with national economic priorities like MSME growth or rural development. - 2. Direct Action: - Definition: - Statutory enforcement measures against banks or non-banks (e.g., NBFCs) for violating RBI directives, prudential norms, or engaging in misconduct, to ensure regulatory compliance and financial system stability. - Examples: - 2019: - RBI mandated clawback provisions for CEO and top executive salaries in cases of fraud or high non-performing assets (NPAs), requiring repayment of bonuses even post-retirement. - Imposing fines, restricting branch expansion, or canceling licenses for non-compliance with regulations under FEMA, PMLA, or RBI guidelines. - Legal Basis: - Section 35A of the Banking Regulation Act, 1949 (for issuing directives); Section 47A of the RBI Act, 1934 (for penalties); also under Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007, and Prevention of money​ laundering Act, 2002. - Impact: - Deters misconduct, enforces regulatory discipline, and ensures banks adhere to RBI’s monetary and financial stability objectives. - 3. Margin Requirements / Loan-to-Value Ratio (LTV): - Definition: - RBI sets limits on the loan amount relative to the value of pledged assets (e.g., gold, property, vehicles) to control credit risk, speculative borrowing, or liquidity in specific sectors. - Examples: - August 2020: - LTV for gold loans increased from 75% to 90% to enhance liquidity during the COVID-19 crisis, allowing borrowers to access more credit against the same quantity of gold. - Reducing LTV for real estate loans during asset bubble risks to curb speculative borrowing (e.g., during housing market overheating). - Impact: - Higher LTV boosts credit access during economic distress, supporting business revival; lower LTV restricts excessive borrowing in overheated sectors, stabilizing credit markets. - 4. Selective Credit Control (SCC): - Definition: - Administrative and regulatory measures to restrict or promote credit to specific sectors based on macroeconomic priorities, such as curbing speculation or supporting economic growth. - Instruments: - a. Credit Rationing: - Definition: - Caps on the total credit banks can extend to specific sectors or borrowers to prevent overexposure or speculative lending. - Examples: - 1960s Credit Authorisation Scheme (CAS): - Required RBI approval for loans above ₹1 crore to control large-scale lending to single borrowers. - Modern: - Limiting credit to real estate or NBFCs during asset bubble risks (e.g., post-2008 housing boom) to ensure financial stability. - Impact: - Redirects credit from non-priority or risky sectors to productive areas, promoting balanced economic growth. - b. Consumer Credit Control: - Definition: - Regulation of terms like down payments, equated monthly installments (EMIs), or margin money for consumer durables to influence demand and consumption. - Examples: - Easing EMI or down payment norms for vehicles, electronics, or appliances during deflation/recession (e.g., post-COVID 2020) to stimulate consumer spending. - Tightening down payment requirements during inflationary periods to curb excessive consumption in non-essential sectors. - Impact: - Stimulates or moderates consumer spending in targeted sectors, aligning with economic conditions (e.g., boosting demand during slowdowns). - Priority Sector Lending (PSL) - Definition: - A mandatory Reserve Bank of India (RBI) directive requiring banks to allocate a fixed percentage of their Adjusted Net Bank Credit (ANBC) or Credit Equivalent Amount of Off-Balance Sheet Exposure (whichever is higher) to under-served sectors to promote inclusive development. - ANBC is a bank’s total loanable funds, adjusted for exemptions like export credit. - Historical Context: - Introduced in 1968, mandating 40% of bank loans to three priority sectors (agriculture, small-scale industries, exporters) by 1985. - Expanded over decades to include additional sectors (e.g., MSMEs, housing, renewable energy). - Key Reforms: 2015 (streamlined categories), 2019 (UK Sinha Committee on MSME loan reforms), 2020 (revised quotas and regional focus). - Norms (Post-2020 Reforms): - Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs) and Foreign Banks with 20+ branches: - 40% of ANBC. - Foreign Banks with <20 branches: - 40% of ANBC. - Regional Rural Banks (RRBs), Small Finance Banks (SFBs): - 75% of ANBC or Credit Equivalent by 2025–26 (phased implementation). - Urban Cooperative Banks (UCBs): - 60% of ANBC. - Rural Cooperative Banks: - PSL norms apply, but under separate cooperative bank guidelines (loans to agriculture, MSMEs, weaker sections etc.) - Note: - Quotas are minimums; banks can exceed (e.g., 60% to weaker sections instead of 12%). - Priority Sectors: -
SectorShare of ANBC (Post-2020)Includes
Weaker Sections 12% (up from 10%)
  • Scheduled Castes/Scheduled Tribes, women, persons with disabilities, minorities, manual scavengers, artisans
  • Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY) overdrafts (up to ₹10,000)
  • Beneficiaries of National Rural Livelihood Mission (NRLM) and National Urban Livelihood Mission (NULM)
Agriculture 10% (up from 8%)
  • Marginal farmers (landholding up to 1 hectare)
  • Small farmers (landholding 1–2 hectares)
  • All farmers (including large farmers, e.g., for farm mechanization)
Micro Enterprises 7.5% (unchanged)
  • Khadi, Village, and Cottage Industries
  • Micro units (investment in plant and machinery up to ₹1 crore, turnover up to ₹5 crore, per MSME definition 2020)
Others 2.5% (down from 4.5%)
  • Small and Medium Enterprises (SMEs, beyond micro enterprises)
  • Affordable housing loans (e.g., under Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana)
  • Food processing, vermicompost, biofertilizer, seed production
  • Export credit
  • Education loans (up to ₹10 lakh)
  • Social infrastructure (schools, drinking water, sanitation, healthcare, COVID-related facilities)
  • Renewable energy (windmills, solar, biomass generators, micro-hydel plants)
- Implementation Mechanisms: - Priority Sector Lending Certificates (PSLCs): - Introduced in 2016, PSLCs are tradable certificates allowing banks exceeding PSL targets to sell credits to underachieving banks, ensuring compliance flexibility. - PSLCs are basically like carbon credits, but for banks. - Types: - General, Agriculture, Small/Marginal Farmer, Micro Enterprises, Weaker Sections. - Example: - In 2024–25, ₹3.5 lakh crore in PSLCs traded (RBI Annual Report). - Shortfall Funds: - Rural Infrastructure Development Fund (RIDF): - Established 1995–96, managed by NABARD. - Funds rural infrastructure (e.g., irrigation, rural roads, schools) when banks miss PSL targets. - Interest earned on deposits linked to Bank Rate, locked in long-term projects. - Urban Infrastructure Development Fund (UIDF): - Established 2023 (Union Budget), managed by National Housing Bank (NHB). - Funds urban infrastructure in Tier-2 (50,000–99,999 population) and Tier-3 (20,000–49,999 population) cities. - Other Funds: - Deposits to SIDBI (for MSMEs) or MUDRA Ltd. as per RBI norms. - Regional Focus (2020 Reforms): - RBI classifies districts based on past PSL lending and assigns higher weightage to backward districts (e.g., aspirational districts under NITI Aayog). - Encourages banks to prioritize credit to these areas for balanced regional development. - Example: In 2024, RBI increased PSL weightage for 117 aspirational districts, boosting credit flow by 10% (RBI data). - Indirect Lending: - Loans by banks to NBFCs for on-lending to PSL sectors (e.g., agriculture, micro enterprises) count toward PSL quotas. - Co-lending or co-origination with NBFCs (e.g., bank-NBFC joint loans to farmers) also qualifies. - Applicability: - PSL norms apply only to banks (SCBs, RRBs, SFBs, UCBs), not NBFCs. - Impact: - Enhances credit access for weaker sections, small farmers, and MSMEs, aligning with UN SDGs (e.g., No Poverty, Zero Hunger, Ref: Pillar#6). - Strengthens rural infrastructure (via RIDF) and urban infrastructure (via UIDF). - Supports financial inclusion and reduces regional disparities. - Recent Developments (2025): - 2024–25: - PSL credit grew by 12% year-on-year, with agriculture and MSME sectors showing significant uptake (RBI Annual Report). - Enhanced monitoring of PSL compliance through digital platforms, reducing shortfall penalties by 5% in 2024. - Non-Monetary Policy Market Instruments - Market Repo (Interbank Repo) - Definition: - A short-term money market transaction in which banks and financial institutions borrow and lend funds among themselves against government securities under a repurchase agreement. - It is conducted between market participants, not with RBI, unlike RBI’s repo under the Liquidity Adjustment Facility (LAF). - Not to be confused with the RBI repo: - Repo (Repurchase Agreement) is a type of financial contract, not an RBI instrument by itself. - When RBI enters into a repo with banks, it is called RBI Repo under LAF. - When banks and financial institutions enter into repo contracts among themselves, it is called Market Repo or Interbank Repo. - Parties Involved: - Lender: - Provides short-term funds against securities. - Borrower: - Sells securities with an agreement to repurchase them at a future date (usually next day). - Both parties can be: - Commercial banks - Mutual funds - Insurance companies - Primary dealers, etc. - Collateral: - Government securities (G-Secs), Treasury Bills, etc. - Ensures safety of lending — backed by sovereign guarantee. - Features: - Tenure: - Usually overnight, can go up to 14 days or more. - Interest rate: - Known as Repo Rate (Market Repo Rate) — decided mutually by parties. - Transaction settled via Clearing Corporation of India (CCIL). - Purpose: - Helps manage day-to-day liquidity needs. - Allows surplus holders to earn safe short-term returns. - Provides access to secured funding for borrowers. - Difference from RBI Repo: -
Parameter RBI Repo (LAF) Market Repo
  • Counterparty
  • RBI
  • Banks, FIs, MFs, PDs
  • Regulation
  • Part of monetary policy
  • Pure market-based
  • Objective
  • Liquidity control by RBI
  • Short-term funding among market players
  • Rate Decided By
  • RBI
  • Market forces
  • Collateral
  • Mandatory (G-Secs)
  • Mandatory (G-Secs, T-Bills)
- Significance: - Indicates short-term interest rate trends in the interbank market. - Acts as a benchmark for pricing other short-term instruments. - Useful for RBI to observe liquidity stress or surplus in the market. - Tri-Party Repo (TPR) - Definition: - A type of repo transaction where a third-party agent (called a Tri-Party Agent) facilitates the repo deal between borrower and lender. - Governed by RBI’s directions under the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934, and regulated by SEBI if in capital market. - Participants: - Borrower: - Sells securities with an agreement to repurchase later (like a bank, mutual fund, NBFC). - Lender: - Buys the security (temporarily) and earns interest (like another bank or financial institution). - Tri-Party Agent: - Usually a clearing corporation (e.g., CCIL or NSCCL) that manages: - Selection of eligible collateral - Valuation - Margining - Settlement and custody - Key Features: - Reduces counterparty risk by acting as a trusted intermediary. - Promotes transparency and efficiency in repo markets. - Supports automatic collateral substitution and margin maintenance. - Collateral is held with the agent, not directly between borrower and lender. - Eligible Collateral: - Government securities - Treasury bills - Corporate bonds (as per RBI/SEBI rules) - Benefits: - For lenders: - Lower risk and operational burden. - For borrowers: - Easier access to liquidity. - Systemic benefit: - Strengthens money market operations and secondary bond market. - Difference from Market Repo: - In Market Repo, repo is bilateral and settled by participants themselves. - In Tri-Party Repo, a third-party agent manages the process, collateral, and margining. - Launch: - Introduced in India by RBI in 2017. - Tri-Party Repo Dealing and Settlement (Reserve Bank) Directions, 2018 define its framework. - Example: - Suppose a mutual fund wants short-term liquidity → enters into a tri-party repo with a bank. - The clearing corporation holds the G-Secs, ensures margins, and manages settlement. - Regulatory Notes: - RBI allows banks, primary dealers, mutual funds, insurance companies, etc. to participate. - Tri-party repo can be used for SLR compliance if collateral is eligible. - Bank Lending and Interest Rates - Banks' Lending Rates - Definition: - Lending rate is the rate at which banks lend money to customers. - It reflects cost of funds, risk premium, operating cost, and policy rate signals. - Determined by: - Internal benchmarks (earlier: BPLR, MCLR) - External benchmarks (since Oct 2019) - External Benchmark System - Introduced: - RBI mandated all new floating rate personal/retail loans and MSME loans to be linked to external benchmarks from 1st Oct 2019. - Examples of External Benchmarks: - RBI’s Repo Rate - 3-Month/6-Month T-Bill yield - Any other benchmark published by FBIL (Financial Benchmarks India Ltd.) - Formula Components: - Interest Rate = External Benchmark + Spread - Spread = Bank's internal cost, operating expenses, risk premium, etc. - Eligible Borrowers: - Retail loans (e.g., home, auto) - MSME loans - NOT applicable to corporate loans - Interest Rate Reset for EMI-Based Floating Loans: - Banks must reset interest rate at least once every 3 months. - Additional terms - Loan Repayment Types: - Equated Monthly Installment (EMI) - Principal and interest repaid in monthly installments - Equal monthly installments over tenure → easier for budgeting. - Bullet Repayment - Principal and interest repaid in a single installment at loan maturity - Entire repayment at the end → interest burden may vary with rate resets. - Fixed vs Floating Interest Loans: - Fixed: - Rate remains constant throughout loan term. - Predictable EMIs. - Costlier than floating. - Floating: - Rate varies with benchmark movement. - Cheaper over long term but volatile. - Floating Interest Rate Reset (2020–2023): - Post-2020 repo hikes (4% to 6.5%), EMI burdens increased for floating-rate loans. - RBI mandates banks to offer borrowers: - Option to switch to fixed-rate loans. - Foreclosure (full repayment in one installment to avoid compound interest). - Limits on resets to prevent misuse (guidelines pending as of 2025). - Teaser Loans: - Initially low rate loans that increase later. - May cause borrower distress. - RBI discouraged post 2008 crisis. - Relationship Between Deposit Rate and Repo Rate: - Repo Rate ↓ → Banks reduce deposit rates → Reduce lending rates. - Lag effect due to fixed deposit lock-ins. - Weighted Average Lending Rate (WALR): - Weighted Average Lending Rate (WALR) is the average interest rate charged by banks on rupee loans, weighted by the size of each loan. - Evolution of Lending Rate Systems - BPLR (Benchmark Prime Lending Rate): - Introduced in 2003. - Lack of transparency; banks lent below BPLR to corporates. - Base Rate System (2010–2016): - Improved transparency but slow transmission. - MCLR (Marginal Cost of Funds based Lending Rate): - Introduced in April 2016. - Partly market-linked. - Improved over Base Rate but still lagged in policy transmission. - External Benchmark Lending Rate (from Oct 2019): - Direct linkage with market rates like repo → faster monetary transmission. - Net Interest Margin (NIM) - Definition: - \( \text{NIM} = \frac{\text{Interest Earned on Loans} - \text{Interest Paid on Deposits}}{\text{Average Earning Assets}} \) - Significance: - Indicator of bank’s profitability and efficiency. - Ideal Range: - 3–4% is considered healthy. - If NIM > 5%: - Could indicate overcharging. - May lead to exploitation of borrowers or underpaying depositors. - Not good for financial system stability. - As per Economic Survey: - Indian banks’ NIM is below 5%, which is within acceptable range. - Monetary Policy Framework and Operations - Strategies for Monetary Policy: - 1. Exchange Rate Stability: - Used by export-oriented economies (e.g., Singapore), aiming to maintain a stable local currency value against the US dollar to boost exports. - How Stability Helps Exports - a. Predictable Pricing - Exporters can set prices in foreign markets with greater confidence. - Example: - If 1 USD = 75 INR consistently, an Indian exporter can sell a shirt for $10 and know they will receive ₹750. - If INR suddenly strengthens to 70, the exporter now earns only ₹700 per shirt — a loss unless they raise prices (which can hurt demand). - b. Contract Security - International buyers prefer stable-currency exporters because it reduces their risk. - Long-term contracts become safer when currency is stable. - c. Lower Hedging Costs - Hedging Cost refers to the money spent to protect oneself against potential financial losses caused by currency exchange rate fluctuations. - Especially relevant for exporters, importers, investors dealing in foreign currencies. - Volatile currencies force exporters to spend on hedging (currency risk insurance via futures/options). - Stability reduces this cost → increases profit margins. - Hedge funds vs hedging - Hedging - Hedging is an activity, it means reducing risk. - Example - If you: - Buy wheat futures because you grow wheat - Buy dollars because you import goods - Buy insurance for your car - It means you are hedging, you are trying to protect yourself from loss. - Hedge fund - A hedge fund is an institution, it is a private investment fund. - The fund: - Takes big risks - Uses leverage - Trades everything - Chases high returns - Ironically, most hedge funds do not hedge. - d. Competitive Pricing - If currency weakens slowly or is kept undervalued intentionally, goods become cheaper in foreign markets. - Stable, weaker currency = long-term price advantage without frequent currency fluctuations. - Country Example (Singapore) - Singapore uses a managed float system, where its central bank intervenes to keep the SGD stable. - It helps maintain global trust in its exports, services, and investments. - Mechanism: - Central banks intervene in forex markets to peg or stabilize currency (Ref: Pillar#3). - Relevance to India: - Limited use due to India’s broader economic goals. - India uses a market-determined exchange rate with intervention when needed. - RBI sometimes sells/buys USD to prevent excessive INR volatility — to protect exporters and importers alike. - 2. Multiple Indicator Approach: - Focuses on balancing economic growth, employment, inflation, and exchange rate stability. - Used by RBI until 2016, assessing multiple economic indicators (e.g., GDP, CPI, forex reserves). - 3. Flexible Inflation Targeting (FIT): - Adopted by RBI in October 2016, following the Urjit Patel Committee Report (2013–14) and amendment to RBI Act (Section 45ZB). - Objective: - Prioritize inflation control (Consumer Price Index, CPI) within 2–6% (target: 4% ± 2%), assuming growth and employment will follow. - Legal Framework: - Established under RBI Act, 1934, with a six-member Monetary Policy Committee (MPC). - Monetary Policy Committee (MPC): - Composition: -
RBI Side (3 Members)Government Side (3 Members)
  • RBI Governor (Ex-officio Chairman, e.g., Sanjay Malhotra, Dec 2024)
  • Deputy Governor (Monetary Policy, e.g., Michael Patra, since Jan 2020)
  • RBI Nominee (e.g., Mridul Saggar, Executive Director)
  • External Members (e.g., Ram Singh, Saugata Bhattacharya, Nagesh Kumar, appointed 2024)
- Tenure and Selection: - RBI Governor/Deputy Governor: - Tied to ex-officio roles, selected by Financial Sector Regulatory Appointment Search Committee (FSRASC, headed by Cabinet Secretary). - External Members: - 4-year tenure, non-renewable, selected by a Search-cum-Selection Committee (headed by Cabinet Secretary). - Operations: - Minimum 4 meetings per year (typically bi-monthly), quorum of 4 members (including Governor). - After every meeting, the RBI's Monetary Policy Committee (MPC) announces its monetary policy stance, which indicates the general direction of interest rates (e.g., accommodative, neutral, or hawkish). - However, the stance is not a specific forecast or commitment about what they will do with the repo rate in the next meeting. - Instead, it reflects their current outlook on inflation, growth, and liquidity, and gives markets a sense of what kind of actions may be expected in the near future — subject to evolving data. - Policy Rate (Repo): - Decided by majority vote; Governor has a casting vote in case of a tie. - Transparency: - MPC publishes meeting minutes on the 14th day and a Monetary Policy Report every 6 months. - Inflation Target: - Set by Union Government in consultation with RBI Governor: - 4% CPI (±2%) from 2016, extended to March 31, 2026. - Failure: - Inflation outside 2–6% for 3 consecutive quarters (9 months) requires a report to the government with reasons and remedies. - Example: - November 2022, inflation exceeded 6% for 9 months; MPC drafted a non-public report (RBI data). - MPC decision scope - MPC decides only the repo rate; other measures (e.g., CRR, SLR, PSL norms, regulatory bans) are decided by the RBI Governor alone. - Example: - 2023, RBI allowed Urban Cooperative Banks meeting PSL targets to offer gold loans up to ₹4 lakh with bullet repayment (up from ₹2 lakh). - Monetary Policy Stances: -
StanceRepo Rate HikeRepo Rate CutRepo Rate Unchanged
Calibrated Tightening Possible Not Possible Possible
Neutral Possible Possible Possible
Accommodative Not Possible Possible Possible
- Purpose: - Signals RBI’s future repo rate intentions to investors, borrowers, and banks. - Flexibility: - Not legally binding (e.g., Dec 2018 Calibrated Tightening followed by Feb 2019 repo cut to combat deflation; May–June 2022 Accommodative stance with repo hikes to fight inflation). - Why Repo Rate Hike Leads to Tightening? - a. Costlier borrowing for banks: - Banks have to pay more interest to borrow from RBI. - b. Pass-through effect: - Banks increase lending rates for consumers and businesses. - c. Credit slows down: - Loans (home, car, corporate) become expensive → demand for credit drops. - d. Consumption and investment slow: - Households postpone borrowing; businesses delay expansion plans. - e. Liquidity tightens: - Less money flows in the economy → money supply shrinks. - f. Inflation control: - Reduced spending helps control inflation (core goal of tightening). - Recent Monetary Policy Trends (2022–2025): -
PeriodRepo RateStanceAdditional Measures
2022 4% to 6.25% Withdrawal of Accommodation Increased to combat inflation
Feb 2023–Oct 2024 6.5% Withdrawal of Accommodation Continued to control inflation
Oct 2024 6.5% Neutral CRR cut from 4.5% to 4% to boost liquidity
Feb 2025 6.25% Neutral Repo cut to support GDP growth; MSF: 6.5%, Bank Rate: 6.5%, SDF: 6%, Reverse Repo: 3.35% (fixed)
- Bank Lending Rates: - Historical Evolution: - 1969: - Government-administered interest rates post-bank nationalization. - 1991: - M. Narasimham Committee recommended deregulation, allowing banks to set rates. - 2003: - Benchmark Prime Lending Rate (BPLR) introduced, but ineffective for monetary transmission. - 2010: - Base Rate system; banks updated rates at their discretion, limiting transmission. - 2016: - Marginal Cost of Funds-based Lending Rate (MCLR) introduced, but slow transmission (e.g., 2019: RBI cut repo by 1.35%, banks cut lending rates by only 0.4%). - External Benchmark System (2019): - Formula: - External Benchmark + Spread (profit margin) + Risk Premium = Loan Interest Rate. - Benchmarks: - RBI repo rate, 91-day/182-day T-Bill yield, or Financial Benchmarks India Ltd. rates. - Update Frequency: - At least every 3 months, ensuring faster transmission. - Eligible Loans: - Personal, retail (home, vehicle), micro/small/medium enterprises (from April 2020). - Benefits: - Faster monetary policy transmission, transparency for borrowers. - Constraints: - Spread is fixed post-loan; risk premium can increase (e.g., if borrower’s job loss raises default risk). - Limitations of Monetary Policy in India: - High Savings Culture: - Unlike Western countries, Indian banks rely on household savings/deposits, not RBI’s repo loans, reducing repo rate impact. - Slow Transmission: - Pre-2019, banks delayed passing repo cuts to borrowers due to NPAs and profitability concerns (e.g., 6–12 months for customer benefit, 24 months for inflation impact). - Public Sector Bank Issues: - Poor management, private bank scams, and high NPAs (Ref: Pillar#1B-2) hinder transmission. - Supply-Side Constraints: - El-Nino, poor monsoons, geopolitical issues (e.g., oil price hikes, US-China protectionism) drive food and input inflation, beyond RBI’s control (Ref: Pillar#3, 4A). - Hawkish Policy Bias: - Governors (e.g., Raghuram Rajan, Urjit Patel) prioritized inflation control over growth, leading to high repo rates and criticism for limiting cheap loans. - Government-Side Issues: - Fiscal deficits, subsidy leakages, and loan waivers disrupt monetary policy efficacy (Ref: Pillar#2D, FRBM Act). - Structural Issues: - Lack of ease of doing business, inadequate infrastructure (roads, electricity) fuel inflation (Ref: Pillar#4B, 5). - Lazy Banking: - Banks’ reluctance to seek loan clients limits money multiplier effects. - Informal Economy: - Rural moneylenders with high interest rates and black​ money reduce banking penetration and financial inclusion (Ref: Pillar#1D). - Additional concepts - Black Swan Events: - Unpredictable events (e.g., 2007 US subprime crisis, 2020 COVID) disrupt markets. - Black Swan theory given by author Nassim Nicholas Taleb in book– The Black Swan. - RBI’s 2025 report warns of potential $100 billion Foreign Portfolio Investment (FPI) pullout, causing liquidity and currency volatility. - Foreign Portfolio Investment - It refers to investments made by foreign investors in the financial assets of a country, such as: - Shares (equity) - Bonds (debt) - Money market instruments - Unlike FDI (Foreign Direct Investment), FPIs do not involve control or management of the enterprise. They are purely for returns and are more liquid and volatile. - In India, FPIs are regulated by SEBI (Securities and Exchange Board of India) and RBI. - Example: - When a US-based mutual fund buys stocks on the Indian stock exchange, it is counted as FPI. - Note: - US Fed Tapering is not a Black Swan event (expected by economists). - Fed Tapering impact on India - - Cantillon Effect: - - Concept given by the 18th century French economist Richard Cantillon. - When money supply increases, early recipients (e.g., banks) gain purchasing power at the expense of others, potentially causing stagflation (high inflation, high unemployment, low growth; Ref: Pillar#4D). - Liquidity Trap: - Occurs when low interest rates fail to stimulate borrowing/investment due to pessimistic economic expectations (e.g., post-2020 lockdown). - Example: - 2020, repo at 4%, yet credit demand remained low due to COVID uncertainty. - Soft landing & Hard Landing - Soft landing - A situation where the central bank raises interest rates or tightens policy, successfully slows down inflation and growth, but avoids a recession. - Hard landing - When the central bank’s tightening results in a sharp economic slowdown or even recession. - Comparison -
Criteria Soft Landing Hard Landing
Inflation
  • Comes down gradually
  • Comes down sharply
GDP Growth
  • Moderate slowdown
  • Severe slowdown or contraction
Unemployment
  • Stable or minor rise
  • Sharp increase
Monetary Policy
  • Calibrated tightening
  • Aggressive tightening
Investor Confidence
  • Relatively intact
  • Low – risk-off sentiment

- RBI’s Approach: - Pursued “soft landing” (moderate rate hikes to control inflation without crashing growth) unlike US’s aggressive hikes, balancing growth and stability. - Pillar 1B1: Financial Intermediaries - Banks - Definition: - Financial intermediaries are entities facilitating financial transactions between lenders and borrowers, investors and entrepreneurs, or households and businesses, channeling funds to promote economic activity. - Context: - Banks, as formal financial intermediaries, are critical to India’s economy, supporting credit creation, financial inclusion, and economic growth under the regulatory oversight of the Reserve Bank of India (RBI). - Significance for India: - Banks drive India’s economic development by mobilizing savings, providing credit, and supporting government initiatives like financial inclusion and infrastructure development, aligning with the vision of a developed economy by 2047. - Reserve Bank of India (RBI) - Definition: - The Reserve Bank of India (RBI), established under the RBI Act, 1934, is India’s central bank, responsible for monetary policy, financial stability, and regulation of the banking and financial sector. - Historical Context: -
Year Event
1913 Commercial banks registered under Companies Act, but lacked stringent oversight (no Cash Reserve Ratio, Statutory Liquidity Ratio, Basel Norms).
1926 Hilton Young Commission recommended establishing Reserve Bank of India as a central bank.
1929 Great Depression led to collapse of 450+ banks in India, prompting urgency for RBI’s creation.
1934 RBI Act enacted; operational from April 1, 1935, with Sir Osborne Smith as first Governor.
1935 Scheduled Commercial Banks listed under RBI Act’s 2nd Schedule; required to maintain Cash Reserve Ratio.
1943–49 C.D. Deshmukh, first Indian Governor, represented India at Bretton Woods Conference (1944, Ref: Pillar#3B).
1948 RBI nationalized under Reserve Bank of India (Transfer of Ownership) Act, making it accountable to Parliament with ~4.4% government ownership initially.
1949 Banking Regulation Act empowered RBI to license banks, regulate branches, prescribe liquidity norms (e.g., Statutory Liquidity Ratio), and protect depositors.
- RBI Offices & Departments -
Category Details
RBI Zonal Offices
  • Northern Region – Delhi
  • Eastern Region – Kolkata
  • Southern Region – Chennai
  • Western Region – Mumbai
Major Departments
  • Department of Banking Regulation (DBR) – Regulates commercial banks
  • Department of Non-Banking Supervision (DNBS) – Supervises Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs)
  • Department of Payment and Settlement Systems (DPSS) – Manages digital payments, RTGS, NEFT, UPI
  • Foreign Exchange Department (FED) – Administers FEMA and forex management
  • Financial Markets Operations Department (FMOD) – Liquidity management via OMOs, Repo, Reverse Repo
  • Department of Currency Management (DCM) – Handles issuance, distribution, and withdrawal of currency
  • Monetary Policy Department (MPD) – Supports the Monetary Policy Committee with data & analytics
  • Financial Inclusion and Development Department (FIDD) – Oversees financial inclusion and Priority Sector Lending
  • Consumer Education and Protection Department (CEPD) – Handles public grievances and promotes financial literacy
Other Important Departments
  • Internal Debt Management Department (IDMD) – Manages Government of India’s domestic borrowings
  • Department of Economic and Policy Research (DEPR) – Conducts research, publishes RBI Bulletin and reports
  • Department of Statistics and Information Management (DSIM) – Maintains RBI databases, surveys, statistics
Offices
  • 31 Regional and Sub-offices across India
  • Central Office: Mumbai
  • Regional Offices implement RBI’s policy, supervise banks, and issue currency
- Governance: - Central Board Composition: -

  • 2 Government officials

  • 4 directors from RBI’s local boards (North: Delhi, East: Kolkata, South: Chennai, West: Mumbai)
- Governor and Deputy Governors: - Selection: - By Financial Sector Regulatory Appointment Search Committee (FSRASC), headed by Cabinet Secretary, with final approval from Appointments Committee of the Cabinet (headed by Prime Minister). - Tenure: - Typically 3 years, re-appointment possible. - As per the RBI Act, 1934, the Governor and Deputy Governors of RBI are appointed by the Government of India for a term not exceeding 5 years, but usually 3 years is practiced. - Why typically 3 years for key roles, as 3 years is very less for such a powerful role: - It allows flexibility for the government to assess performance. - Frequent review aligns with changing economic conditions and political environment. - Avoids over-concentration of power and promotes fresh perspectives. - Notable Governors: -

- Functions: - Controller of Money Supply: - Issues currency (M0) under RBI Act, 1934 (Ref: Pillar#1A2). - Formulates monetary policy via tools like repo rate, CRR, SLR, and Open Market Operations (OMO). - Controller of Foreign Exchange: - Manages forex reserves and exchange rates under Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA), 1999 (Ref: Pillar#3). - Banker to Governments: - Manages Union and State government accounts, public debt, and fiscal operations. - Comparison with Comptroller and Auditor General of India - RBI is government banker, while CAG is like a financial watchdog, ensuring transparency and legality of how government money is spent. - Banker’s Bank: - Acts as lender of last resort, providing liquidity to banks in crisis. - Advises banks on monetary matters (Ref: Pillar#1A1). - Regulator of Financial Institutions: - Regulates Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs), All India Financial Institutions (AIFIs), and deposit-taking NBFCs under Banking Regulation Act, 1949, and Payment Systems Act, 2007. - Licenses banks, approves branch expansions, enforces audits, and mandates mergers of weak banks. - Promotional Roles: - Financial Inclusion: - Enforces PSL norms (Ref: 12.5), mandates 25% of bank branches in rural areas. - Customer Protection: - Operates Banking Ombudsman for grievance redressal. - Data Publication and Awareness: - Publishes Annual Financial Stability Report, monetary policy reports, and economic data. - International Cooperation: - Engages with IMF, G20 Financial Stability Board, and Basel Committee. - 2021: - Joined Network for Greening the Financial System (NGFS, HQ: Paris, est. 2017) to address climate risks. - RBI borrowing and deposts: -
Entity Can Deposit with RBI? Can Borrow from RBI? Explanation
General Public (Individuals)
  • No
  • No
  • RBI does not offer banking services to individuals.
  • You must use commercial or cooperative banks.
Commercial Banks
  • Yes
  • Yes
  • Maintain CRR (Cash Reserve Ratio) with RBI as deposits.
  • Can borrow via Repo, MSF, etc., under monetary policy tools.
NBFCs (Non-Banking Financial Companies)
  • No
  • No (with exceptions)
  • NBFCs do not have direct access to RBI liquidity windows like repo.
  • In rare or emergency cases (e.g., COVID crisis), RBI may offer liquidity support indirectly via banks or special windows.
Government of India
  • Yes (Ways and Means Advances)
  • Yes
  • RBI acts as banker and debt manager to the government.
- Then why RBI allows non-banking bodies to use e-Kuber (direct purchase platform)? - Access to e-Kuber ≠ Access to Liquidity Facilities - Even if NBFCs or mutual funds can access e-Kuber for auctions, they: - Cannot park funds with RBI - Cannot borrow under Repo/MSF/SLF, unless RBI opens a special liquidity window (e.g. COVID-19 special LTRO) - Agency Banks: - Handle tax collection, government transactions, and fund transfers on behalf of the government (e.g., SCBs like SBI, authorized by RBI). - In the world of finance, "agency" simply means one person or business is officially authorized to act for another. - FAQ: - How can a bank collect the tax isn't it the work of the income tax officer - Yes, it is the function of income tax oxcer. But, Tax money is to be transferred from tax payersp bank account to govt account. Agency banks deal with that aspect of money collection/storage/transfer work. - RBI Portals and Initiatives: - UDGAM Portal (2023): - Unclaimed Deposits Gateway to Access Information. - Enables searching unclaimed deposits (unclaimed for 10+ years, transferred to RBI’s Depositor Education and Awareness Fund) using Aadhaar/PAN. - PRAVAAH Portal (2023): - Platform for Regulatory Application, Validation, And Authorization. - Streamlines licensing for banks, NBFCs, and payment companies, enhancing ease of doing business. - Daksh Portal (2022): - Web-based supervisory system for banks/NBFCs to submit compliance reports, enabling RBI to monitor frauds and issue guidelines. - "Daksh" (दक्ष) is a Sanskrit word that literally means efficient, competent, or skillful - E-Kuber Portal: - RBI’s core banking solution for NEFT/RTGS, repo, OMO, and retail G-Sec purchases (Ref: 12.6). - New Domain Names (Effective April 2025): - Banks: - .bank.in (e.g., SBI.bank.in) - NBFCs: - .fin.in (e.g., Bajajfinserve.fin.in). - Managed by RBI’s IDRBT to reduce phishing and cyber frauds. - FREE-AI Framework (Dec 2024): - Framework for Responsible and Ethical Enablement of AI in finance, led by Prof. Pushpak Bhattacharyya (IIT Bombay). - MuleHunter AI (Dec 2024): - Developed by RBI’s Innovation Hub to detect money mule accounts used for laundering or fraud (e.g., digital arrest scams). - What Is a Money Mule? - A money mule is a person who transfers or moves illegally acquired money on behalf of others, often across borders or between accounts. This person might be: - Willing (knowingly assisting a criminal) - Unwitting (tricked into doing it, thinking it’s a job or favor) - It's called a "mule" because it carries a load for someone else — just like the animal mule (which is used for transporting goods). - Digital Payments Intelligence Platform (2024): - Under A.P. Hota Committee, aims to reduce payment frauds using advanced technologies. - RBI-CRIS Portal (Oct 2024): - Reserve Bank Climate Risk Information System, provides climate and pollution data to banks/NBFCs for risk assessment. - Sachet Portal (2024): - For lodging complaints against financial frauds (e.g., investment scams), redirecting to authorities like SEBI. - eConnect 2 Regulate Portal: - Facilitates public feedback to RBI for policy inputs. - RBI Podcast (Dec 2024): - Disseminates financial awareness and policy updates to the public. - Recent Developments (2025): - RBI’s focus on digital transformation: - Enhanced cybersecurity measures and AI integration (e.g., MuleHunter AI, Digital Payments Platform). - Strengthened climate risk management via RBI-CRIS and NGFS membership. - Impact on Monetary Policy: - RBI’s tools (repo, CRR, OMO) and regulations (PSL, licensing) shape liquidity, credit flow, and financial stability. - Portals like E-Kuber and PRAVAAH enhance monetary policy transmission and regulatory efficiency. - Cybersecurity and AI initiatives align with global standards, ensuring robust financial systems. - Mental model of banking system - Banking System - Scheduled - Commercial banks - Cooperative banks - Non-scheduled - Commercial banks - Cooperative banks - Non-Banking Financial Institutions - Fintech and digital platforms - Informal Financial Intermediaries - Why NBFC and other below are not part of Non-scheduled? - “Non-scheduled” is a word that applies only to banks. It has zero meaning outside the banking system. - RBI’s Second Schedule of the RBI Act lists banks. So only institutions that are legally banks can be: - Scheduled banks - Non-scheduled banks - NBFCs, fintechs, MFIs, SHGs, etc. are not banks at all, so they are not even eligible to be scheduled or non-scheduled. - Scheduled Banks - Definition: - Banks listed in the Second Schedule of the RBI Act, 1934, meeting specific criteria for financial stability and depositor safety. - Criteria to meet simultaneously: - 1. Minimum paid-up capital and reserves of ₹5 lakh - What is Paid-up capital? - Portion of a bank's capital that has been funded by shareholders. - Paid-up capital is the portion of share capital for which shareholders have already paid money to the company. - Example - Think of a bank or company raising capital - 1. It first decides how much capital it wants to raise - 2. It issues shares - 3. People buy those shares - 4. They pay money - That money received = paid-up capital. - ₹5 Lakh Capital Sufficiency: - Does this mean every bank with ₹5 lakh deposits becomes a scheduled bank on Day 1? - RBI also checks Condition #2 (bank must not harm depositor interests). - RBI usually observes the bank’s conduct for 1 year after licensing before conferring "Scheduled Bank" status. - So, banks don’t become scheduled immediately upon inception, even if ₹5 lakh criterion is met. - The ₹5 lakh capital requirement, set in 1934, is outdated due to inflation and economic growth. Modern banks require significantly higher capital (e.g., ₹500 crore for new private banks under 2016 on-tap licensing). - The act is not amended, instead ₹5 lakh capital it is interpretated based on inflation for todays scenerio - Remains a historical criterion for scheduled status but is insufficient for operational viability today. - Even if a bank meets these two conditions, it does not become "scheduled" automatically. - The RBI must officially notify and include the bank in the Second Schedule via Gazette notification. - 2. Operations not detrimental to depositors’ interests - A scheduled bank is allowed to do things like: - Accept deposits - Give loans and advances - Invest money safely in assets like Government securities, Treasury bills, Approved bonds, etc - Not crypto, not gambling, not some shady startup run by a cousin. - Payment and settlement services - Foreign exchange and trade finance - What they are NOT allowed to do, for example: - Speculating in stock markets with depositors’ money - Investing in highly risky assets - Giving huge unsecured loans to friends of the promoter - Running casinos, real estate empires, or movie studios - Anything that can recklessly blow up depositor money - Features: - Must maintain Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR) with RBI. - Eligible for RBI’s liquidity facilities (e.g., repo, MSF). - Subject to RBI regulations for depositor protection. - Preferred for government schemes (e.g., PM Jan Dhan Yojana). - Types: - Scheduled - Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs): - Includes public sector banks (e.g., SBI, PNB), private sector banks (e.g., ICICI, Axis), foreign banks, and differential banks (RRBs, SFBs, PBs). - Scheduled Cooperative Banks: - Includes urban and rural cooperative banks (e.g., Haryana Rajya Sahakari Bank). - Non-Scheduled Banks: - Primarily cooperative banks not meeting RBI’s criteria, maintaining CRR internally and ineligible for RBI liquidity facilities. - Comparison: -
Aspect Scheduled Banks Non-Scheduled Banks
Definition Listed in the Second Schedule of the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) Act, 1934. Not listed in the Second Schedule of the RBI Act, 1934.
Subcategories
  • Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs): SBI, ICICI, Axis, etc.
  • Scheduled Cooperative Banks: e.g., Haryana Rajya Sahakari Bank
 Primarily cooperative banks that do not meet RBI’s conditions.
CRR (Cash Reserve Ratio) Must maintain CRR with RBI. Can maintain CRR internally in their own vaults/offices.
Access to RBI Facilities Eligible to borrow or deposit in RBI’s liquidity facilities such as LAF (Liquidity Adjustment Facility), MSF (Marginal Standing Facility), etc. Not eligible by default; may be granted at RBI’s discretion in exceptional cases.
Regulatory Compliance Must follow RBI’s guidelines strictly to protect depositor interests. Also expected to comply, but RBI can shut them down under Banking Regulation Act if they fail.
Institutional Trust More trusted by government departments and large private corporations for salary and business accounts. Less likely to be chosen for major institutional accounts.
Eligibility for Government Schemes Eligible to participate in schemes like PM Jan Dhan Yojana. Usually not eligible.
Recent Example Airtel Payments Bank and Paytm Payments Bank were included in the scheduled bank list in 2021–22. Not applicable.
Can offer loans or fixed deposits? Scheduled Commercial Banks can. Payment Banks (even if scheduled) cannot offer loans or FDs. E.g., Paytm Payments Bank can act only as an agent of other banks like Federal Bank.

- Post-Independence Nationalization: - NEXUS between Banks and Industrialists: - From 1950s to 60- only 188 elite people controlled the economy by being in board of top 20 banks, 1452 companies. - This led to reckless lending to directors and their firms. So, Banks failed frequently, RBI had to close them. - Private Banks unwilling to open in rural areas- this did not help in financial inclusion of poor, farmers, MSME or achievement of Five Year Plan (FYP) targets or reducing regional imbalance. - Nationalization & merger of banks: -
Year Event Details
1948 RBI Ownership Transfer Act RBI nationalized under this act to establish full state control over monetary policy.
1955 Imperial Bank → SBI Imperial Bank nationalized and renamed as State Bank of India (SBI).
1956 LIC Act Life Insurance Corporation of India created by nationalizing all private life insurance companies.
1963 SBI Associates Merger State Bank of Jaipur and State Bank of Bikaner merged into State Bank of Bikaner and Jaipur.
1969 1st Bank Nationalization
  • Banking Companies (Acquisition & Transfer of Undertakings) Ordinance passed.
  • 14 major private banks with deposits > ₹50 crore were nationalized.
  • Examples: Bank of Baroda, PNB, Dena Bank, Canara Bank.
1972-73 GIC Act
  • General Insurance Corporation (GIC) established.
  • 4 Subsidiaries: National Insurance, New India Assurance, Oriental Insurance, United India Insurance.
1980 2nd Bank Nationalization
  • 6 more private banks with deposits > ₹200 crore were nationalized.
  • Examples: Corporation Bank, Vijaya Bank, Oriental Bank of Commerce.
1991 Narasimham Committee-I
  • Recommended reducing CRR, SLR, and phasing out priority sector lending.
  • Introduced concept of NPAs, prudential norms, capital adequacy.
1997 Narasimham Committee-II
  • Suggested bank mergers, recapitalization, and setting up of ARCs.
  • Strengthened governance and autonomy of PSBs.
2007 Raghuram Rajan Committee Focused on financial sector reforms and deepening of financial inclusion.
2014 P J Nayak Committee Suggested reducing government stake below 50% in PSBs and setting up a Bank Investment Company (BIC).
2019 J&K Bank Ownership
  • Earlier: 60% owned by J&K State Government.
  • Post Article 370 abrogation: Union Government took over the stake.
2021 Death of M Narasimham Known as “Father of Indian Banking Reforms”; ex-RBI Governor and key architect of banking sector reforms.
-- Old Private Banks
  • Examples: Catholic Syrian Bank (1920, Kerala), Dhanlaxmi Bank, Ratnakar Bank.
  • Not nationalized due to smaller deposits (< ₹50 cr).
- Public Sector Banks (PSBs): - Definition: - Public Sector Banks are banks in which the majority (more than 50%) of the equity stake is held by the Government of India. - They are established by the government to serve the public interest and economic development goals. - They include nationalized banks, State Bank of India and its associates, and other banks where the Union Government holds controlling stake. - Consolidation of PSBs - Definition: - Public Sector Bank (PSB) consolidation refers to the process where two or more PSBs are merged into a single, stronger bank. This enhances operational efficiency, reduces redundancy, and helps create globally competitive banks. - PSB Consolidation involves two types of reforms: - 1. Merger - Definition: - Merger in banking refers to the combination of two or more banks into one, where one bank absorbs the other(s), and their businesses, assets, and liabilities are consolidated under a single banking entity. - Example: - The merger of Vijaya Bank and Dena Bank into Bank of Baroda in 2019. - Notable PSB Mergers Before April 2019 -
Year Merger Details
2008–2010 State Bank of Saurashtra and State Bank of Indore merged into SBI.
2013 Bharatiya Mahila Bank (BMB) set up as a PSB.
  • Headquarters: Delhi
  • Ownership: 100% by Union Government
  • Board of Directors: All women
  • Customers: Both men and women
  • Loans: Predominantly to women, without collaterals
2017
  • BMB and 5 Associate Banks of SBI (SBBJ, SBH, SBM, SBP, SBT) merged with SBI.
  • Alternative Mechanism (AM) Panel set up under Finance Minister to examine merger proposals and forward to Cabinet for approval.
  • Department of Financial Services coordinated the process.
2019, April 1 Vijaya Bank and Dena Bank merged into Bank of Baroda (BoB).
  • BoB (est. 1908) > Vijaya Bank (1931) > Dena Bank (1938)
