- After this process is over, we'll be left with only 12 PSBs (+1 India Post Payment Bank) - Benefits: - Enhanced geographical and technological synergies (e.g., ATM networks, IT costs). - 2. Privatization - Definition: - Privatization refers to the transfer of ownership and control of a Public Sector Bank from the government to private entities by selling off the majority stake (more than 50%) to non-government players. - The aim is to increase competition, improve governance, and ensure efficient banking operations driven by market forces. - Examples of PSB Privatization: - Axis Bank: - Originally promoted by UTI (a government-owned institution). - Renamed from UTI Bank to Axis Bank. - Now classified as a private sector bank. - IDBI Bank: - Initially a public sector bank. - Majority stake acquired by LIC (a government-owned insurer). - RBI reclassified it as a private bank in 2019. - Challenges in PSB Consolidation: - Political Interference: - Decision-making in PSBs often influenced by political considerations rather than commercial merit. - Can delay or derail merger processes or post-merger reforms. - Low Loan Recovery: - High levels of NPAs (Non-Performing Assets) reduce financial health of merging banks. - Mergers may compound bad loan issues if due diligence is weak. - Union Resistance: - Bank employee unions often oppose mergers due to fear of job losses, transfers, or dilution of benefits. - Can lead to strikes and operational disruptions. - Low Profitability due to Administered Rates: - PSBs have limited flexibility in setting lending/deposit rates due to government mandates. - Reduces their ability to improve margins and compete effectively post-merger. - Banking Investment Company (BIC): - Definition: - A proposed government-owned holding company that would hold the equity stakes of the Government of India in various Public Sector Banks (PSBs). - Objective: - To separate ownership and management in PSBs. - To allow the government to act as a passive investor, thus reducing direct interference in bank operations. - Rationale: - Recommended by the P.J. Nayak Committee (2014) to improve governance in PSBs. - Helps professionalize the boards and improve autonomy of PSBs. - Status: - The idea was removed from Mrunal handouts because the topic became outdated and no substantial progress was made by the Finance Ministry. - Private Sector Banks (PvBs): - Definition: - Banks in which majority stake (>50%) is held by private shareholders rather than the government. They are regulated by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) under the Banking Regulation Act, 1949. - Background: - After nationalization of banks (1969 & 1980), most banks in India were PSBs. - However, PSBs started facing issues like: - Government-administered interest rates → Reduced profitability. - Political interference → Poor loan recovery. - Employee unions → Resistance to reform and innovation. - Result: - Inefficiencies, non-performing assets (NPAs), and stagnation in banking innovation. - Post-1991 Reforms: - Trigger: - 1991 Balance of Payments (BoP) crisis → Urgent need for banking sector reform. - M. Narasimham Committee (1991) Recommendations: - Reduce government shareholding in PSBs. - Lower CRR (Cash Reserve Ratio) and SLR (Statutory Liquidity Ratio). - Liberalize interest rates and branch expansion. - Allow entry of: - New Private Sector Banks. - New Foreign Banks. - Rounds of Private Bank Licensing in India: -

- Note: - These are often termed "New-Generation Private Sector Banks". - Although UTI Bank and IDBI were not initially PvBs, they are now categorized as such due to current ownership structures. - On-Tap Licensing of Private Sector Banks (2016): - Definition: - A system introduced by RBI where eligible entities can apply for a banking license any time (like a driving license), instead of waiting for RBI to invite applications. - If RBI approves, the entity gets a Banking License — becomes a new bank. - Eligibility Conditions: - Minimum ₹500 crore paid-up capital. - Minimum 10 years' experience in banking and finance. - Fit and proper criteria as per RBI norms. - Objective: - Promote competition and innovation in banking. - Improve financial inclusion and credit access. - Scheduled Bank Status and On-Tap - Even after getting a license, RBI must still notify inclusion in Second Schedule separately. - Allowing NBFCs / Large Corporates to Set Up Banks: - This topic was marked as shifted to Mains-QEP in the Mrunal handout. - Key points (for awareness): - RBI’s Internal Working Group (2020) suggested allowing large corporates and NBFCs into banking. - Highly controversial due to concerns over: - Conflict of interest - Crony capitalism - Weak corporate governance - As of now, RBI remains cautious and has not implemented this. - FAQ Highlights: - Why are UTI Bank and IDBI Bank listed under PvBs if they were not private originally? - Because at present, they are private sector banks due to change in ownership. The classification is based on current status, not origin. - Isn't the ₹500 crore capital rule conflicting with Scheduled Commercial Bank definition? - Not really. The licensing and scheduling are two-step processes. - Foreign Banks - Definition: - These are banks that are registered and headquartered outside India and operate in India through a branch or a subsidiary. - Examples of foreign banks in India: - Citibank - Bank of America - HSBC - Historical Context - During Nehruvian Socialist period: - The government was cautious about allowing foreign banks in India. - Only a few foreign banks were given permission to open branches. - After 1991 Economic Reforms - India liberalized its financial sector. - The rules for foreign banks to enter the Indian market became easier. - Rules and Regulations by RBI - These banks must follow Indian regulations like: - CRR (Cash Reserve Ratio) - SLR (Statutory Liquidity Ratio) - Other banking norms issued by the Reserve Bank of India. - Priority Sector Lending (PSL): - Rules vary depending on how many branches a foreign bank has in India. - Full details are available in the Priority Sector Lending section. - Licensing: - RBI offers licenses on a continuous basis (on-tap licensing). - Based on mutual agreement: - If a foreign country allows Indian banks, then India allows their banks too. - RBI Rules for Wholly Owned Subsidiaries of Foreign Banks: - These are banks fully owned by a foreign parent but set up as Indian subsidiaries. - Key requirements: - Minimum 5 billion rupees as capital base. - At least 50% of the board members must be Indian citizens. - Foreign Investment limits in Indian Banks -
Bank Type Foreign Investment Rules Examples
Public Sector Banks (PSBs)
  • Foreign investors can hold up to 20% shares
  • SBI: 14% foreign shareholding
  • PNB: 13% foreign shareholding
Private Sector Banks
  • Up to 49% foreign investment allowed automatically
  • Up to 74% allowed with government approval
  • Proposal under discussion:
    • Allowing up to 100% foreign ownership
    • To help raise capital under BASEL-III norms
    • More info in NPA and BASEL-III section
  • HDFC Bank: 73% foreign ownership
  • ICICI Bank: 59% foreign ownership
  • Axis Bank: 52% foreign ownership
- Differential Banks - Definition: - Differential banks are specialized financial institutions with restricted operational scope (e.g., geographical, functional) to promote financial inclusion, unlike universal banks (e.g., SBI, ICICI) with broader operations. - Differential just means difference, these Banks are different from normal banks in what they are allowed to do. A bank that is limited in powers, targeted in customers, local in focus. - Chronology: -
Year Banking Innovation/Development
1976
  • Regional Rural Banks (RRBs) established under RRB Act, 1976.
1996
  • Local Area Banks (LABs) introduced via Budget 1996.
2015
  • Small Finance Banks (SFBs) and Payment Banks (PBs) launched, per Nachiket Mor Committee (2013–14).
2017
  • Wholesale and Long-Term Finance Banks (WLTF) proposed (not implemented).
2021
  • Digital Banks proposed by NITI Aayog (not approved by RBI as of 2025).
2022
  • Digital Banking Units (DBUs) announced in Budget 2022.
- Universal vs. Differential Banks: - Comparison: -
ParameterUniversal BanksDifferential Banks
Branch Operations Anywhere (25% in unbanked rural areas) Geographical restrictions (e.g., RRBs, LABs in specific districts)
Deposits Accept time and demand deposits (no limit) Restricted (e.g., PBs: demand deposits up to ₹2 lakh)
Loans Anyone (40% PSL, Ref: 12.5) Restricted (e.g., SFBs: 75% PSL; PBs: no loans)
- Types of Differential Banks: - Existing: - Regional Rural Banks (RRBs) - Local Area Banks (LABs) - Are not part of RBI’s current licensing framework. - Small Finance Banks (SFBs) - Payments Banks (PBs) - New proposed differential banks (not much progress): - Wholesale and Long-Term Finance Banks (WLTF) Banks (Proposed by RBI 2017) - Proposed by RBI in 2017 to provide wholesale and long-term financing; not yet implemented. - Full-stack Digital Banks (Proposed by NITI Aayog 2021) - Proposed by NITI Aayog in 2021 as fully online banks under Banking Regulation Act; not approved by RBI yet. - Digital Banks will not have physical branches. They will rely on internet for delivering banking services. - Comparison table with details: -

RRB Act, 1976		 Provide credit to agriculture, rural industries, and weaker sections

  • Operations limited to specific regions
  • Interest rates capped similar to cooperative banks
  • 75% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit (ANBC) must go to Priority Sector Lending (PSL)

Small Finance Banks (SFBs) 2015
Nachiket Mor Committee (2014)		 Provide basic banking to underserved: small businesses, farmers, MSMEs
  • Minimum 75% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit (ANBC) to PSL
  • 50% loans must be below ₹25 lakh
  • Can accept all types of deposits
  • Listed on stock exchange if net worth ≥ ₹500 crore
 Regulated by RBI AU Small Finance Bank, Equitas SFB, Ujjivan SFB
Payments Banks 2015
Nachiket Mor Committee		 Provide small savings accounts and payment/remittance services
  • Cannot lend or issue credit cards
  • Maximum account balance ₹2 lakh
  • Must invest minimum 75% in government securities
  • Can issue ATM/debit cards
 Regulated by RBI under Banking Regulation Act, 1949 Paytm Payments Bank, India Post Payments Bank (IPPB), Airtel Payments Bank
- Cases under Payments Banks: - India Post Payments Bank (IPPB): - Origin: - Established 2018, registered as a Public Limited Company under Companies Act, 2013 (100% owned by Department of Posts, Ministry of Communications). - Objective: - Enhance digital payments and remittances via postal network. - Features: - Reach: - 1.55 lakh post offices, doorstep banking via postmen. - Accounts: - Zero-balance, minor accounts (age 10+), types (Safal, Sugam, Saral). - Services: - Supports UPI, BHIM, NEFT, IMPS, Bharat Bill Payment System (BBPS). - Links with Post Office Savings Bank (POSB) for auto-transfer of excess balance. - Aspires to convert to SFB (requires 5 years’ experience, per RBI guidelines). - Note: - Acts as loan agent (e.g., with HDFC Bank) but cannot lend directly. - Data (2025): - Processed 20% of rural digital transactions in 2024–25 (RBI data). - Paytm Payments Bank (PPB) Issue (2024): - Origin: - Launched 2017, parent: One97 Communications (founder: Vijay Shekhar Sharma). - Issues: - 2024 RBI restrictions due to: - Non-compliance with Know-Your-Customer (KYC) norms (e.g., multiple accounts with same PAN, handling crores in transactions). - Alleged money​ laundering concerns. - Data security violations (no server separation from One97 entities like Paytm Mall, Insurance, Games). - Restrictions: - No new deposits, wallet additions, or Fastag services. - Allowed: - UPI, QR code, card machines, and agency roles (loans, insurance, equity broking). - Impact: - Strengthened RBI’s oversight, with 10% reduction in payment frauds post-restrictions (RBI Annual Report, 2025). - Post Office Savings Bank (POSB) - Overview: - It is not a bank of any kind licensed by RBI, but a government-run savings platform under the National Small Savings Scheme (NSSS). - So it is basically a government savings system, not a commercial bank. - It comes under Postal Savings System and is operated by Department of Posts, Ministry of Communications - It is differential in nature because it has limited scope and specific mandate: - Focused on financial inclusion in rural/postal areas. - It does not engage in lending. - Operates only deposit side schemes, regulated by the Ministry of Finance. - Department of Posts - Ministry of Communications two dept: - 1. Depart of Telecommunications - 2. - Budget 2022 Update: - POSB integrated with Core Banking System (CBS) for e-banking, ATMs, and interoperability, benefiting rural depositors (e.g., farmers, senior citizens). - Digital Banking Initiatives: - Digital Banking Units (DBUs): - Origin: - Announced in Budget 2022, 75 DBUs set up by commercial banks. - Objective: - They are digital service branches of existing banks. - Provide paperless banking (e.g., account opening, credit cards) via digital kiosks. - Features: - Physical offices with automated services, targeting rural digital access. - Data (2025): - DBUs handled 5% of rural account openings in 2024–25 (RBI data). - Neo-Banks/Digital Banks/Full-stack digital banks: - Origin: - Proposed by NITI Aayog (2021) under Banking Regulation Act, 1949. - Objective: - Fully online banks with no physical branches to boost rural financial inclusion. - Status: - Not approved by RBI (2025), as existing banks suffice for online/physical services. - Note: - Term “Neo-Bank” often used in media due to startup PR but lacks regulatory backing. - Difference between DBUs and Neo-Banks: -

- Impact on Monetary Policy: - Contribution: - Differential banks (RRBs, SFBs) meet 75% PSL targets, channeling credit to agriculture, MSMEs, and weaker sections (Ref: 12.5). - PBs and DBUs enhance digital payment penetration, supporting RBI’s digital transformation (Ref: 12.8). - Cooperative Banks - Definition: - Cooperative banks are financial institutions owned and operated by members under cooperative society principles, focusing on community welfare and financial inclusion, unlike profit-driven commercial banks. - Commercial vs. Cooperative Banks: -

- Challenges: - Politicization, casteism, poor loan recovery, scams, and money​ laundering risks. - Types: - Urban Cooperative Banks (UCBs) - Rural Cooperative Banks (RCBs) - Comparison: -

Special Notes Focus on financial inclusion in urban sectors
  • Primary Agricultural Credit Society (PACS) cannot issue chequebooks or provide full banking services
  • DCCBs crucial for short-term rural/agri credit
  • DCCBs overtook RRBs in short-term agricultural credit share (2024–25)
- Banking Regulation (Amendment) Act, 2020: - Origin: - June 2020: Ordinance issued; September 2020: Act passed, amending Banking Regulation Act, 1949. - Objective: - Protect depositors, prevent fraud, and strengthen RBI oversight over cooperative banks. - Changes: -

- Note: - Union Registrar shifted to Ministry of Cooperation (from Agriculture Ministry, post-2020). - National Urban Cooperative Finance and Development Corporation (NUCFDC): - Definition: - NUCFDC is an umbrella organization and Self-Regulatory Organization (SRO) for Urban Cooperative Banks (UCBs), registered as a non-deposit-taking Non-Banking Financial Company (NBFC) under RBI regulation. - Origin: - Launched on March 2, 2024, by Union Home and Cooperation Minister Amit Shah in New Delhi, under the Ministry of Cooperation. - Based on recommendations of the N.S. Vishwanathan Committee (2021) and V.S. Das Working Group (2009) to strengthen UCBs. - Objective: - Modernize and strengthen the UCB sector, ensuring compliance with the Banking Regulation Act, 1949, and promoting financial inclusion under the ‘Sahakar se Samriddhi’ vision for ‘Aatma Nirbhar Bharat’. - Funding: - Initial paid-up capital: - ₹117.95 crore (March 2024). - Capital raised: - ₹242 crore by January 2025, with a target of ₹300 crore by February 2025 to qualify as an SRO. - Funding source: - National Cooperative Development Corporation (NCDC, a statutory body under Ministry of Cooperation) committed ₹200 crore; UCBs can voluntarily subscribe to NUCFDC’s capital. - Functions: - Technology Development: - Develops shared IT platforms for UCBs to reduce costs, enhance digital services (e.g., UPI, mobile banking), and compete with commercial banks. - Self-Regulatory Organization (SRO): - Sets industry standards, ensures compliance with RBI regulations, and promotes professionalism in UCB operations (Ref: NBFC section, Pillar#1B1). - Umbrella Organization: - Provides liquidity and capital support to UCBs, preventing cash flow disruptions. - Advisory Services: - Offers consultancy on fund management, governance, and regulatory compliance. - Capacity Building: - Conducts training programs to enhance UCB staff skills and board decision-making. - Facilitates Dialogue: - Acts as a bridge between UCBs and RBI for streamlined communication and policy inputs. - Significance: - Supports 1,514 UCBs with 11,000 branches, managing ₹5.33 lakh crore in deposits and ₹3.33 lakh crore in loans (2024, RBI Annual Report). - Reduces UCB Non-Performing Assets (NPAs, currently 2.10%) and enhances depositor confidence through governance and digital transformation. - Enables doorstep banking and extended PSL timelines for UCBs, boosting urban financial inclusion. - Leadership: - CEO: Prabhat Chaturvedi (appointed September 2024), succeeding Atul Khirwadkar, focusing on technology adoption and management skills. - Recent Developments (2025): - NUCFDC’s Mumbai corporate office inaugurated on January 24, 2025, by Amit Shah, launching initiatives like the International Year of Cooperatives 2025 Calendar (Ministry of Cooperation press release). - Facilitated 10% growth in UCB digital transactions via shared IT platforms (RBI data, 2025). - Punjab and Maharashtra Cooperative (PMC) Bank Issue (2019): - Context: - Fraudulent activities led to RBI restrictions on withdrawals (6 months, 2019). - Status: - Outdated for 2025–26; resolved via merger with Unity Small Finance Bank (2022, RBI data). - Relevance: - Highlighted need for 2020 amendment to strengthen RBI oversight. - Impact on Monetary Policy: - Contribution: - NUCFDC strengthens UCBs’ PSL compliance (40–75%, Ref: 12.5), enhancing urban credit flow and financial inclusion. - RFIs support RBI’s rural credit goals, channeling 35% of PSL credit to agriculture and MSMEs (Ref: 12.5). - Cooperative banks support RBI’s financial inclusion goals by providing credit to agriculture, MSMEs, and weaker sections (Ref: 12.5). - Urban Cooperative Banks (UCBs) and District Central Cooperative Banks (DCCBs) align with PSL targets, enhancing rural credit flow. - Challenges: - Urban Cooperative Banks (UCBs) face high NPAs (2.10%) and technological lag; NUCFDC mitigates these through liquidity and IT support. - RFIs struggle with regional disparities and loan recovery; NABARD’s oversight addresses these issues. - Politicization and scams limit monetary policy transmission; 2020 amendment mitigates risks by centralizing RBI control. - Non-Banking Financial Institutions - Definition: - Non-Banking Financial Institutions (NBFIs) are financial entities that provide bank-like services such as lending, investment, asset financing, insurance, etc., but do not have a full banking license and cannot accept demand deposits (like savings or current accounts). - Development Financial Institutions (DFIs): - Definition: - DFIs provide medium to long-term loans for industrial and infrastructure projects, focusing on economic growth rather than accepting public deposits. - Post-1991, most DFIs converted into banks (e.g., ICICI, IDBI), so they are no longer a separate category. - DFIs as a distinct class have been phased out or reclassified under AIFIs/NBFCs. The term is still used to describe institutions like NaBFID due to their developmental role.” - Objective: - Boost GDP through financing infrastructure projects, offering financial and technical support (e.g., project feasibility studies, advisory, legal services). - Features: - Do not accept deposits from the public. - Provide services like foreign currency loans, underwriting, bank guarantees, and technical/management consultancy. - Post-1998, M. Narasimham-II Committee reclassified financial intermediaries into banks or non-banks, requiring DFIs to convert (e.g., ICICI, IDBI became banks). - Chronology: -

- All India Financial Institutions (AIFIs): - Definition: - AIFIs are specialized non-banking institutions set up by Acts of Parliament to support specific sectors (e.g., exports, agriculture, housing, MSMEs). - Features: - Do not accept public deposits, hence not classified as banks. - Regulated by RBI, subject to Basel-III norms with customized slabs/deadlines. - Ownership: - Fully government-owned (post-2018/2019 for NABARD, NHB). - Types: - Export-Import Bank of India (EXIM, 1982): - Promotes cross-border trade and investment with loans and foreign currency support. - National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development (NABARD, 1982): - Supervises RRBs and RCBs (Ref: 12.9, 12.10), provides refinance to banks/NBFCs, operates Rural Infrastructure Development Fund (RIDF). - Ownership: - Shifted from RBI to 100% Government of India (2018). - National Housing Bank (NHB, 1988): - Supports housing projects, operates RESIDEX to monitor residential real estate prices. - Ownership: - Shifted from RBI to 100% Government of India (2019). - Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI, 1990): - Supports MSMEs via loans, operates udyamimitra.in for schemes like MUDRA, Stand-Up India (Ref: Pillar#1D). - Ownership: - Initially under IDBI, now with SBI, LIC, and others. - National Bank for Financing Infrastructure and Development (NaBFID, 2021): - Established via NaBFID Act, 2021, with ₹20,000 crore initial capital. - Funding: - From RBI, Government of India, financial institutions, and market bonds. - Objective: - Financial: - Lend or invest (directly/indirectly), attract private sector investment, offer 15–25-year fixed-rate loans. - Developmental: - Coordinate with governments, regulators, and stakeholders for infrastructure projects under National Infrastructure Pipeline (Ref: Pillar#5). - Chairman: - K.V. Kamath; Headquarters: Mumbai. - Status: - Designated as the fifth AIFI in 2022 by RBI. - Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs): - Definition: - NBFCs provide financial services (e.g., loans, investments) without accepting demand deposits, regulated by RBI or other authorities (e.g., SEBI for mutual funds, IRDAI for insurance). - Comparison with Commercial Banks: -

- Credit Card Reforms (2022): - Pre-2022: - NBFCs issued co-branded credit cards with banks (e.g., Bajaj Finserv with RBL Bank). - Could not tie directly with payment gateways (e.g., Visa, Mastercard, Rupay). - Post-2022: - Select licensed NBFCs can issue credit cards independently and tie directly with payment gateways, enhancing market competition (RBI Annual Report, 2025). - Impact on Monetary Policy: - Contribution: - DFIs and AIFIs (e.g., NaBFID, NABARD) support infrastructure and rural credit, aligning with PSL norms (Ref: 12.5). - NBFCs enhance credit access for MSMEs, housing, and microfinance, complementing RBI’s financial inclusion goals (Ref: Pillar#1D). - Challenges: - High NPAs in CICs (e.g., IL&FS) and regulatory gaps in non-deposit-taking NBFCs; RBI’s tightened norms post-2022 mitigate risks. - FinTech and Digital Lending - FinTech Companies: - Definition: - FinTech companies leverage digital technology to provide innovative financial services, including transactions, loans, and investments. - Example: -

- Types (RBI-Regulated): - Peer-to-Peer (P2P) Lenders: - Connect borrowers and lenders via platforms (e.g., Faircent, Cashkumar, CRED Mint). - Function like online marketplaces (similar to OLX), matching loan seekers with investors. - Note: - CRED Mint (launched 2021) gained attention, but detailed business model not critical for UPSC. - Account Aggregators (AA): - Manage and display customers’ financial asset data (e.g., bank accounts, investments) to users or third parties (e.g., loan providers). - Licensed by RBI since 2018; examples include NeSL, Cookiejar. - Loan Aggregators: - Platforms like Loanadda, Moneytap, Paisabazaar act as agents for banks/NBFCs to sell loans. - Challenges: - Customer complaints due to lack of clear grievance redressal mechanisms. - Payment Aggregators: - Provide technological solutions for sellers to accept payments (e.g., credit cards, UPI) via platforms like Razorpay. - Sellers embed payment codes on websites for seamless transactions. - Subtypes (online vs. offline) not critical for UPSC. - Mobile Wallet Apps: - Enable digital payments (e.g., PhonePe, Paytm, MobiKwik). - Covered under digital payments (Ref: Pillar#1A1). - Types (Non-RBI Regulated): - Discount Brokers: - Facilitate online share/bond trading via mobile apps (e.g., Zerodha, Upstox, 5paisa, Groww). - Lower operational costs (fewer physical offices) result in lower commissions than traditional stock brokers (e.g., Motilal Oswal). - Regulated by SEBI. - Insurance Web Aggregators: - Platforms like Policybazaar compare insurance policies. - Regulated by IRDAI (Ref: Pillar#1D). - Self-Regulatory Organisations (SROs) for FinTech: - Definition: - SROs are non-governmental bodies regulating industries to ensure transparency, fair competition, ethical practices, consumer protection, and dispute resolution. - Example: - Fintech Association for Consumer Empowerment (FACE), recognized by RBI in 2024 as an SRO for FinTech. - Benefits: - Reduces RBI’s supervisory workload. - Establishes communication between RBI and FinTech companies. - Sets standards for data privacy, cybersecurity, mis-selling, ethical conduct, and grievance redressal. - Other SROs: - Microfinance: - MFIN (2014), Sa-Dhan (2024) for MFIs (Ref: 12.12). - News Industry: - News Broadcasters & Digital Association (NBDA), a private SRO, vs. Press Council of India (statutory, 1978). - Credit Information Companies (CICs) vs. Credit Rating Agencies (CRAs): -
Parameter Credit Information Companies (CICs) Credit Rating Agencies (CRAs)
Regulator Reserve Bank of India (RBI) Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI)
Examples CIBIL (TransUnion), CRIF High Mark, Equifax, Experian CRISIL, ICRA, CARE Ratings, India Ratings (Fitch), S&P, Moody’s, SMERA
Scope Assess creditworthiness of individuals and MSMEs Assess creditworthiness of corporates, NBFCs, sovereigns
Rating Type Numerical score (e.g., 300–900) Alphabetical ratings (e.g., AAA, AA–, D)
Utility Used by lenders for retail loans: housing, personal, credit cards Used by investors/lenders for bond and debt instrument evaluation
Frequency Updated monthly or in real-time based on repayment behavior Periodic review: quarterly, annual or event-based
- Note: - Brickwork Ratings (CRA) shut down by SEBI in 2022 for malpractices (SEBI press release, 2022). - Digital lenders: - Difinition: - Digital lenders are entities that extend credit through digital platforms such as mobile apps, websites, or other online interfaces, either wholly or partially using digital processes. - They include: - Banks and NBFCs offering loans online. - P2P lending platforms. - Fintech companies partnering with regulated entities. - Unregulated apps offering loans without regulatory approval. - RBI classified them into 3 groups: -

1 Giving loans online and regulated by RBI Banks, NBFCs, P2P Lending Platforms RBI
2 Giving loans online but regulated by other authorities Venture Capital Funds (VCFs) SEBI or other regulators
3 Giving loans online but not regulated by anyone Unauthorized apps, mafia-type lenders Unregulated / Illegal (Apps to be banned, legal action)
- Impact on Monetary Policy: - Contribution: - FinTech enhances credit access via digital lending (e.g., P2P, BNPL), supporting PSL goals (Ref: 12.5). - SROs like FACE ensure compliance, improving monetary policy transmission. - Challenges: - Mis-selling and data privacy issues in loan aggregators; RBI’s 2024–25 guidelines address these. - Informal Financial Intermediaries - Definition: - Informal financial intermediaries provide loans without formal procedures, requiring collateral (e.g., property, gold, crops) and charging high compound interest rates. - Features: - Use muscle power for recovery, unlike banks/NBFCs with legal mechanisms (e.g., SARFAESI Act, Ref: Pillar#1B2). - Regulated by state laws (e.g., Bombay Moneylenders Act 1947, Kerala 1958, Gujarat 2011), requiring registration and capping interest rates. - 2012 Data: - 33% of rural household loans from informal lenders, rest from banks/friends (NSSO Survey, 2012). - Types: -

- Shadow Banking: - Definition: - Shadow banking refers to credit intermediation by non-bank financial institutions that perform bank-like functions but are outside the traditional banking regulatory framework. - Shadow banking does not mean illegal, it does not mean unregulated. It means not regulated like banks. - Key Segments: - Housing Finance Companies (HFCs, e.g., DHFL). - Liquid Debt Mutual Funds (LDMFs, e.g., UTI, Kotak, investing in T-bills, commercial papers). - Retail NBFCs (e.g., gold loan companies, asset finance companies). - Risks: - High-risk assets lead to repayment failures, e.g., IL&FS crisis (2019, Ref: Pillar#1B2). - Impact on Monetary Policy: - Contribution: - Shadow banking extends credit to underserved sectors, complementing PSL (Ref: 12.5). - Challenges: - Shadow banking crises (e.g., IL&FS) disrupt credit flow; RBI’s tightened oversight mitigates risks. - Islamic Banking: - Features: - Interest (riba) prohibited in Islam; operates via Ijara (leasing), Murabaha (cost-plus financing), Musharaka (partnership). - Returns shared as rent/profit, not interest. - Status in India: - RBI opposed Islamic banking (2017) due to regulatory complexities. - 2017: - Kerala permitted an Islamic cooperative society to bypass RBI’s ban, but faced legal challenges (not critical for UPSC). - Pro-Arguments: - Enhances financial inclusion for minorities. - Anti-Arguments: - Requires specialized manpower; PM Jan-Dhan Yojana and Post Office Payments Bank sufficient for inclusion (Ref: Pillar#1D). - Concerns over terror​ financing and money​ laundering raised by international agencies. - Impact on Monetary Policy: - Contribution: - Informal lenders provide credit in rural areas, supplementing formal institutions (Ref: 12.5, PSL). - Challenges: - High interest rates and coercive recovery disrupt monetary policy transmission; state laws aim to regulate these practices. - Additional information - Rural Financial Intermediaries (RFIs): - Definition: - RFIs encompass financial institutions serving rural areas, promoting agricultural and rural development through credit and banking services. - Rural Financial Intermediaries is a functional category, not a legal one. So RFIs don't belong to any specific category. - These can be both scheduled as well as non-scheduled. - Types: - Regional Rural Banks (RRBs, Ref: 12.9). - Rural Cooperative Banks (RCBs, Ref: 12.10). - Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs) operating in rural areas. - Small Finance Banks (SFBs, Ref: 12.9). - Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs). - Microfinance Institutions (MFIs). - Local Area Banks (LABs, Ref: 12.9). - Regulation: - Ultimate Regulator: - RBI, overseeing banking functions under Banking Regulation Act, 1949. - Immediate Regulator: - National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development (NABARD) supervises RRBs and RCBs for performance and compliance. - Objective: - Enhance rural credit access for agriculture, Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs), and weaker sections, aligning with Priority Sector Lending (PSL) norms (Ref: 12.5). - Data (Economic Survey 2025): - RFIs contributed 35% of total agricultural credit in 2024–25, with RCBs and RRBs accounting for 20% (RBI Annual Report). - Digital pene - Primary Dealers (PDs): - Definition: - PDs operate in the primary market, buying fresh Government Securities (G-Secs) from RBI’s E-Kuber platform and selling in the secondary market (Ref: 12.6). - Features: - Licensed by RBI; include banks (e.g., SBI, Kotak) and non-banks (e.g., Standard Chartered, HSBC). - Total: - 20+ PDs (2025, exact numbers not critical). - Related Concept: - Retail investors can buy G-Secs via Retail Direct Gilt Account (RDG Account, Ref: Pillar#1C). - Pillar 1B2: Loans & Other Burning Issues in Banking - Loan - Definition: - A loan is a financial transaction in which one party (the lender, e.g., a bank or NBFC) provides money or credit to another party (the borrower) with the expectation of repayment over time, usually with interest - Loan Types - Depending on Guarantee/Collateral - Secured Loans - Borrower pledges an asset as security (e.g., home loans, gold loans, automobile loans, business loans against fixed deposits, shares, bonds, or property). - Lower risk for bankers, often resulting in lower interest rates. - Typically higher loan amounts and longer repayment tenures (e.g., 15–20 years). - Unsecured Loans - No collateral required (e.g., overdrafts, credit cards, personal loans for wedding/travel, student loans, farmer loans). - Higher risk for bankers, leading to higher interest rates. - Shorter repayment tenures to recover funds quickly. - Exception: - Some unsecured loans (e.g., education loans, micro-enterprise loans, farmer loans) may have lower interest rates due to government-backed credit guarantees (Ref: Pillar#1D). - Based on Interest Rate - Fixed Interest Loan - Interest rate remains constant throughout the loan tenure (e.g., short-term crop loans up to ₹3 lakh for farmers at 7% fixed interest). - Floating Interest Loan - Interest rate varies based on an external benchmark (e.g., repo rate) plus spread and risk premium (Ref: Pillar#1A2-Monetary Policy). - Rate may increase or decrease during the loan tenure. - Teaser Loan: - A subtype with low initial interest rates that increase later. RBI introduced stricter regulations in 2011 but has not banned them. - Why is the offering of "teaser loans" by commercial banks a cause of economic concern? - The teaser loans are considered to be an aspect of sub-prime lending and banks may be exposed to the risk of defaulters in future - Based on arrangement - Syndicated Lending - Multiple lenders share the risk of a borrower’s default, spreading financial exposure. - Can be a fixed lump sum or a credit line (contrary to some misconceptions). - Credit Line - A pre-approved loan amount that borrowers can draw upon as needed, repaying only the used portion with interest. - Additional - Impaired Loan - A loan not performing as per original terms; borrower unlikely to repay in full. - Borrower categories - Prime Borrower - Has the capacity to repay loans, posing lower risk to lenders. - Subprime Borrower (Individual) - A loan given to borrowers with poor credit history or low creditworthiness (i.e., sub-standard or "subprime" borrowers). - Lacks repayment capacity. Offering teaser loans to such borrowers contributed to the 2007-08 US Subprime Crisis, leading to the Global Financial Crisis (Ref: Pillar#3). - Overleveraged Borrower (Company) - Has borrowed beyond repayment capacity, with a high debt-to-equity ratio (Ref: Pillar#1C). - Difference from Subprime borrower: -
Feature Overleveraged Borrower Subprime Loan
Who is the borrower? Typically companies Usually individuals or households
Initial creditworthiness May be good, but later overloaded with debt Poor from the beginning
Risk cause Borrowed beyond repayment capacity Inherently risky borrower (e.g., low income, bad credit history)
Key Indicator High debt-to-equity ratio Low credit score (e.g., CIBIL), past defaults
Typical example IL&FS, DHFL post over-borrowing Subprime housing loans in the US (2008 crisis)
Loan type Corporate loans, infrastructure financing Personal loans, housing loans to low-credit borrowers
Default risk stage Risk increases after taking excess debt Risk is already high at loan approval stage
- Zombie borrower (zombie lending) - Zombie lending refers to the practice where banks continue to lend to unviable, loss-making firms (called zombie firms) just to delay NPA recognition and avoid provisioning. - These firms are often unable to earn enough to even cover interest payments, let alone repay principal. - Financial vs. Non-Financial Debt -
Aspect Financial Debt Non-Financial Debt
Definition Borrowing from banks, NBFCs, or bond markets Borrowing by governments or public institutions (not private loans)
Examples
  • Bank loans
  • NBFC loans
  • Corporate bonds
  • Credit card dues
  • Home loans
  • Treasury Bills (T-Bills)
  • Government Bonds (G-Secs)
  • Sovereign Debt
Who borrows? Individuals, companies, and private sector Central or state governments and public institutions
Purpose For business, housing, consumption, etc. To fund government spending and deficit
In Macroeconomic Data Shown as household or corporate debt Shown as government/public debt
Regulated by RBI, SEBI, and other financial regulators Managed through RBI and government fiscal policy
- Cases related data - Supreme Court: Only RBI can decide Credit Card Interest Rates (2024) - Legal Basis - As per the Banking Regulation Act, 1949, only the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) has the authority to regulate interest rates on loans and advances. - Courts, including consumer courts, cannot interfere in interest rate matters. - Background - Some banks were charging high interest rates on credit card late payments, ranging from 36% to 49% annually. - This led to public complaints and legal scrutiny. - 2008: Consumer Court (NCDRC) Ruling - The National Consumer Disputes Redressal Commission (NCDRC) ruled that banks cannot charge more than 30% interest on credit card late payments. - Appeal to Supreme Court - Banks challenged this ruling in the Supreme Court, arguing that interest regulation is under RBI's jurisdiction. - 2024: Supreme Court Verdict - Supreme Court ruled that only RBI has the legal authority to set interest rates on loans. - Consumer courts cannot impose caps on interest rates. - This reinforces the supremacy of RBI’s regulatory powers under the Banking Regulation Act, 1949. - Side analysis: - It effectively took over 15 years for this matter to be conclusively resolved by the Supreme Court. - Supreme Court cases, especially those involving regulatory authority and constitutional matters, often take years due to legal complexity, procedural delays, and backlog. - In this case, multiple appeals, policy implications, and coordination with RBI likely extended the timeline. - NPA / TBS Problem: Three Stages of Twin Balance Sheet Syndrome - Stage 1: Boom Period (Till mid-2000s) - Global economic boom. - Indian corporates took large loans and became over-leveraged. - Borrowing beyond repayment capacity. - High Debt-to-Equity ratio. - Stage 2: Subprime Crisis and Domestic Delays (From 2007–08 onwards) - Global Financial Crisis (GFC): - Originated from US Subprime Crisis. - Indian exports declined. - Indian exports declined primarily due to the following reasons: - 1. Global Demand Collapse - Major economies like the US, EU, and Japan went into recession. - These countries are key export destinations for India. - As consumers and businesses in those countries cut spending, the demand for imported goods fell — including goods from India. - 2. Slump in Industrial Activity Worldwide - Sectors like automobiles, electronics, textiles, and construction saw major slowdowns. - India exports many intermediate and final goods (e.g., garments, gems, machinery) to these sectors — so demand for Indian exports contracted. - 3. Credit Crunch & Trade Financing Issues - Global banks became risk-averse and tightened lending. - Exporters struggled to get trade credit, letters of credit, or working capital. - Many Indian SMEs couldn't fulfill international orders or take new ones due to this financing bottleneck. - 4. Sharp Drop in Commodity Prices - Global prices of oil, metals, and agricultural products declined. - India exports many of these (e.g., petroleum products, iron ore, rice), so the value of Indian exports fell, even if volumes remained the same. - 5. Reduced Tourism & Services Exports - India earns foreign exchange from tourism, IT services, and BPOs. - With global firms cutting costs and people avoiding travel, services exports from India also took a hit. - Domestic issues in India: - UPA Government's policy paralysis. - Judicial activism and environmental activism led to delays in project approvals. - Result: - Corporates struggled to complete projects and repay loans. - Stage 3: TBS (By 2013) - Around 1/3rd of bank loans were with stressed companies: - These had Interest Coverage Ratio (ICR) < 1. - Meaning: Revenue was not even enough to pay loan interest. - (ICR > 1 is good; ICR < 1 is bad.) - Two sets of entities had weak balance sheets: 1. Large Corporate Companies 2. Public Sector Banks (PSBs) - This situation was called Twin Balance Sheet Syndrome (TBS) by Economic Survey 2015–16. - It was called Twin Balance Sheet Syndrome (TBS) because the above two sectors' balance sheets were simultaneously stressed - Non-Performing Assets (NPAs) - Definition - A loan becomes an NPA if principal or interest remains unpaid for over 90 days from the due date. - For farm loans, NPA status is based on cropping seasons, not days. - NPA Decline (Economic Survey 2024) - Significant reduction in NPAs due to loan recoveries and write-offs. - NPA-Related Definitions in Banking - Standard Assets - Loans where borrowers repay principal and interest on time. - Special Mention Accounts (SMA) - SMA-0: - Principal or interest unpaid for 1–30 days. - SMA-1: - Unpaid for 31–60 days. - SMA-2: - Unpaid for 61–90 days. - Provisioning - RBI mandates banks to set aside funds to cover potential NPA losses, reducing bank profitability. - Gross NPA (GNPA) - Total value of all NPA loans. - Net NPA - GNPA minus provisioning. - Substandard Asset - Loan classified as NPA for 12 months or more. - Doubtful Asset - Substandard asset for an additional 12 months or more. - Loss Asset - Declared by bank, auditor, or RBI as having little to no salvageable value. - One-Time Settlement (OTS) with Haircut - Borrower settles dues at a reduced amount (e.g., pays 60% of dues, bank forgoes 40% as a loss). - Evergreening of Loans - Issuing new loans to pay off old loans, masking NPAs. - Loan Recovery and Write-Off - Loan Recovery - Definition: - Process by which banks recover money from borrowers who default on loan repayments. - Methods of Recovery: - Restructuring / One-Time Settlement (OTS) - Borrower pays part of the loan; rest may be waived. - Used for stressed or unviable borrowers. - SARFAESI Act, 2002 - Enables banks to seize and sell secured assets without court intervention. - Applicable for secured loans above ₹1 lakh. - Debt Recovery Tribunals (DRT) - Special courts for recovering dues > ₹20 lakh. - Banks file recovery suits under the Recovery of Debts Act. - Lok Adalat - Alternative dispute resolution for small-value loans. - Encourages compromise settlement. - Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (IBC), 2016 - For corporate loan defaults. - Creditors take control and either restructure or liquidate the company. - Loan Write-Off - Definition: - When a loan is declared unrecoverable and removed from the bank’s balance sheet. - However, recovery attempts may still continue. - Process - Bank removes NPA from its balance sheet’s asset side to claim tax benefits. - Does not waive the bank’s right to recover the loan; it’s an accounting exercise. - Purpose: - Clean up bank’s books. - Show realistic financials. - Claim tax benefit on losses. - Types: - Technical Write-off: - Written off from books but recovery continues. - Common in India’s banking practice. - Practical Write-off: - No further recovery expected. - Typically for frauds or old NPAs. - Impact - Reduces NPA percentage by removing bad loans from the balance sheet. - 2022: - Banks wrote off ₹11.17 lakh crore in loans over six years (Government to Parliament). - Comparison Table: -
Aspect Loan Recovery Loan Write-Off
Meaning Bank attempts to recover dues from borrower Bank removes loan from its books as unrecoverable
Balance Sheet Impact Loan remains on books until recovered Loan removed from books
Legal Tools SARFAESI, DRT, IBC, etc. None (bookkeeping entry)
Borrower Liability Still liable to repay Still liable, even after write-off
When Used Active efforts to recover defaulted loan Loan deemed unrecoverable (age/fraud)
- Restructured Loans & Stressed Assets - Restructured Loan - Loan terms (principal, interest rate, or tenure) modified to aid borrower repayment. - Stressed Assets - Comprises NPAs, written-off loans, and restructured loans. - Atmanirbhar: Loan / EMI / NPA Relief - Moratorium - Context: - Due to the economic disruption caused by the COVID-19 pandemic, RBI introduced relief measures. - Loan Moratorium Scheme: - If a loan was outstanding as of March 2020, then: - RBI allowed a moratorium (optional suspension) of loan repayments. - Duration: 6 months (March, April, May, June, July, August 2020). - This was part of the broader Atmanirbhar Bharat package. - Purpose: - To provide temporary relief to borrowers whose income was reduced due to COVID. - Relief applied to Equated Monthly Instalments (EMIs). - Beneficiaries: Individuals, MSMEs, and corporates affected by the lockdown. - Key Features: - Not mandatory: - Borrowers could continue paying EMIs if their financial condition allowed. - This preserved their credit history and avoided added interest burden. - Loan was not waived off: - Repayment was only postponed, not cancelled. - Interest continued to accrue on the outstanding amount during moratorium. - Hence, the repayment burden increased after moratorium ended. - Banks had discretion to offer or deny moratorium based on borrower profile. - Implications: - Mixed response: - Many borrowers availed the moratorium to ease immediate pressure. - However, it led to higher EMIs or longer loan tenures after resumption. - For banks: - Created stress on balance sheets due to delayed cash flows. - But it avoided immediate NPAs during the crisis. - Judicial & Policy Developments: - Supreme Court was involved in deliberations on whether interest on interest (compound interest) should be waived. - Government eventually compensated interest-on-interest for loans up to ₹2 crore. - RBI Frameworks for NPAs/Bad Loans (2015–2021) - 3R Framework (2015–19): - Revitalizing Stressed Assets. - Prudential Framework (2019): - For stressed assets. - Resolution Frameworks 1.0 and 2.0 (2020–21): - Allowed extra loans, extended tenures, or reduced interest rates for genuine cases. - Note: - These are outdated for 2025-26, removed from detailed study. - Household Loan Stress (K-Shaped) - Household loans are for consumption (e.g., marriage, iPhone) or asset creation (e.g., home, shop). - Rising defaults in consumption loans (e.g., credit cards, gold loans) but lower in asset creation loans, forming a K-shaped trend (Nomura research, 2024). - ES25 on NPA Data & Observations - NPA reduction attributed to loan recoveries and write-offs. - ES25: Credit-to-GDP Gap - Definition: - Difference between current credit-to-GDP ratio and its long-term trend. - Positive/High Gap: - Indicates looming stress/crisis (e.g., 2006–12 US subprime crisis impacting India’s TBS). - Negative Gap: - Suggests sustainable banking sector with growth potential. - Bank Credit-to-GDP Ratio India vs. Others (ES25): - India’s ratio is lower than the US, UK, and Japan, indicating less risk of an economic crisis compared to these nations. - Budget-2025: On Loans/NPA - NaBFID to establish Partial Credit Enhancement Facility for infrastructure corporate bonds. - PSBs to develop Grameen Credit Score for SHG members and villagers. - Revamped Central KYC Registry rollout in 2025. - SARFAESI Act 2002: - Full form: - Securitisation and Reconstruction of Financial Assets and Enforcement of Security Interest Act - Origin - 1991: - Narasimham-I Committee noted borrowers obtained court stay orders, delaying NPA recovery. Led to Debt Recovery Tribunals (DRTs, 1993). - 1998: - Narasimham-II Committee recommended strengthening DRTs, resulting in the SARFAESI Act 2002. - Objective - Enables lenders to attach mortgaged assets for unpaid loans, change company boards, auction assets, or sell to Asset Reconstruction Companies (ARCs). - Not applicable to farm loans. - Process - Defaulters cannot approach ordinary courts for stay orders; must go to DRT, then Debt Recovery Appellate Tribunal (DRAT), requiring a 50% loan dues deposit. - Appeals escalate to High Court and Supreme Court. - Lenders with SARFAESI Powers - All banks (commercial and cooperative). - Housing Finance Companies (HFCs, e.g., DHFL). - NBFCs with asset size ≥ ₹100 crore and loans ≥ ₹20 lakh (e.g., Bajaj Finserv, Reliance Capital). - Legal Rights of Loan Defaulters -
RightDescription
Right to Ample NoticeLenders must provide 60 days’ notice before auctioning assets.
Right to Be HeardDefaulter can appeal to the lender during the notice period.
Right to Ensure Fair ValueAuction must reflect fair market value (e.g., cannot auction a ₹5 crore bungalow at ₹2 crore).
Right to Balance ProceedsExcess auction proceeds (e.g., ₹1 crore if ₹6 crore fetched for a ₹5 crore loan) returned to defaulter.
Right to Humane TreatmentRecovery agents cannot harass; contact restricted to 7 AM–7 PM.
- Limitations of SARFAESI Act - Understaffed DRTs/DRATs lead to case backlogs (over 1 lakh pending in 2016), delaying recovery. - Asset value erosion (e.g., machinery, vehicles) during prolonged cases. - Auctions may yield low returns in niche sectors (e.g., remote hotels). - Act does not facilitate loan restructuring, necessitating the IBC Code. - Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (IBC) 2016 - Purpose: - Either revive a failing company - Or liquidate it in an orderly way - SARFAESI vs IBC -
Basis SARFAESI Act Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (IBC)
Year 2002 2016
Primary objective Recovery of bank dues by enforcing collateral Resolution or liquidation of insolvent firms
Approach Asset-based recovery Company-wide resolution
Who controls the process Bank or lender NCLT through a Resolution Professional
Court involvement Not required Mandatory (NCLT)
Type of creditors covered Only secured creditors All creditors (secured and unsecured)
Need for collateral Yes No
Can revive a company No Yes, through resolution plan
What happens if recovery fails Bank keeps selling assets Company goes into liquidation
Priority of payments Not clearly defined Clearly defined waterfall mechanism
- Under IBC, creditors are classified into two main categories: - 1. Financial Creditors (FC): - Definition: - Entities that lend money with a return expectation (interest, principal repayment). - Examples: - Banks - NBFCs - Bondholders and other debt security holders - Homebuyers (as per Supreme Court ruling) - Rights under IBC: - Can file a case for recovery of due amount. - Can sit in the Committee of Creditors (CoC) and participate in voting on resolution plans. - 2. Operational Creditors (OC): - Definition: - Entities providing goods/services or employees/salaries, with payments due. - Examples: - Suppliers of raw material or services - Contractors - Salaried employees - Rights under IBC: - Can file complaint under IBC for dues recovery. - Cannot participate in CoC unless minimum dues threshold is met or allowed by court. - Notes: - Both FCs and OCs can initiate insolvency proceedings if payments are not made. - CoC plays a key role in the resolution process, but voting power is primarily with FCs. - Homebuyers' inclusion as FCs was crucial to protect interests in delayed/stalled housing projects. - IBC Appeal Structure -
CriteriaIndividual/Partnership FirmCompany
RegistrationShops and Establishments Act, Indian Partnership Act, 1932Companies Act, 2013
Adjudicating AuthorityDebt Recovery Tribunal (DRT)National Company Law Tribunal (NCLT)
Appellate AuthorityNational Company Law Appellate Tribunal (NCLAT)NCLAT
Next AppealSupreme CourtSupreme Court
- IBC Resolution Process - Insolvency Professional (IP) creates a resolution plan (e.g., reducing interest, extending tenure, finding new investors). - Plan presented to CoC (FCs), with voting power based on loan amounts. - If approved by required FC percentage, the plan is implemented; otherwise, assets are liquidated. - Budget-2024: - IBC resolved over 1,000 companies, recovering ₹3.3 lakh crore for lenders. - Statutory Bodies Related to IBC -
OrganizationFounded UnderHeadquarters
National Company Law Tribunal (NCLT)Companies Act, 2013Delhi (with benches in multiple cities)
National Company Law Appellate Tribunal (NCLAT)Companies Act, 2013Delhi
Insolvency and Bankruptcy Board of India (IBBI)IBC, 2016Delhi
- Insolvent vs. Wilful Defaulter -
CriteriaInsolvent DefaulterWilful Defaulter
DefinitionIncapable of repaying loans due to financial shortage.Has capacity but deliberately avoids repayment.
Loan Restructuring EligibilityEligible under IBC, unless NPA for over a year with no repayment capacity, then assets auctioned under SARFAESI.Not eligible; assets auctioned under SARFAESI.
Contest Elections/Government JobsNot explicitly barred by Constitution.Not allowed (Bankers’ Association proposed to Election Commission, not implemented).

- RBI Proposal (2023) - Banks must declare wilful defaulters within 6 months of NPA classification to avoid delays (e.g., Vijay Mallya case, delayed from 2012 to 2017). - Compromise Settlement (RBI, 2023) - Wilful defaulters can negotiate a compromise settlement with banks. - Wilful Defaulter Characteristics (RBI) - Deliberate non-payment despite adequate cash flow and net worth. - Siphoning funds between companies. - Misusing loan funds or selling pledged assets. - Disposing of collaterals without bank consent. - Falsifying records or engaging in fraudulent transactions. - Guarantor - A person (e.g., friend, relative) legally responsible for repaying a loan if the borrower defaults. - Required for large loans or borrowers with poor credit scores. - Consequences: - Bank can attach guarantor’s assets, downgrade their credit score. - IBC Pre-Packs - A pre-arranged insolvency resolution for MSMEs, introduced via IBC 2021 Ordinance. - Common in the UK and US; faded from current affairs for 2025-26. - Atmanirbhar IBC Ordinance/Act 2020 - Suspended new IBC cases for 6 months (until March 24, 2021) due to COVID-19 challenges. - Increased minimum default threshold for IBC cases from ₹1 lakh to ₹1 crore to protect small businesses. - Insolvency and Bankruptcy Board of India (IBBI) - Statutory body under IBC 2016, under Ministry of Corporate Affairs. - Comprises 1 Chairman (current: Ravi Mittal, 2022), 1 RBI nominee, and 8 government nominees. - Selects Insolvency Professional Agencies (IPAs) like ICAI, ICSI, and Institute of Cost Accountants. - IBBI Information Utility - Maintains borrower database (e.g., National E-Governance Services Ltd., NeSL, 2017). - Aids lenders in assessing creditworthiness and provides evidence for legal proceedings. - Indian Institute of Corporate Affairs (IICA) - Autonomous body under Ministry of Corporate Affairs. - Offers a two-year Graduate Insolvency Programme, allowing graduates to become IPs without 10 years’ experience. - Cross-Border Insolvency - Involves insolvency proceedings across national borders; details not critical for prelims. - IBC Observations from Economic Survey 2025 - Increased Forex Hedging - Pre-IBC: - Borrowers avoided forex hedging due to weak SARFAESI enforcement. - Post-IBC: - Fear of losing company control encourages hedging, improving loan access for exporters. - Reduced Bond Credit Spreads - Bond credit spread = Corporate Bond Yield – Treasury Bond Yield. - IBC reduces spreads by increasing investor confidence in corporate bonds. - Miscellaneous Benefits - Recovered ₹3.6 lakh crore by September 2024. - Improved borrower behavior, reducing need for government bailouts during slowdowns. - Future Suggestions - NCLT should penalize frivolous complaints. - Streamline court procedures to maintain IBC’s non-adversarial approach. - Liquidation of companies - Definition: - Liquidation refers to the process of winding up a company's operations, selling its assets, and distributing the proceeds to creditors and shareholders, ultimately leading to its dissolution. - Types of Liquidation: - Involuntary Liquidation: - Triggered when a company defaults on loans or cannot meet its financial obligations. - Initiated by creditors or regulatory authorities. - Handled under: - Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (IBC), 2016 - SARFAESI Act, 2002 (for secured creditors like banks) - Objective: Recover dues from insolvent or non-performing entities. - Voluntary Liquidation: - Initiated by the company’s owners or shareholders themselves. - No financial default involved; done when the business is no longer viable, strategic, or needed. - Routes available: - Companies Act, 2013 (more commonly used) - Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (IBC), 2016 (Section 59) - Requires: - Declaration of solvency by the directors - Approval by shareholders - Appointment of a liquidator - Clearance of all outstanding liabilities - Final application to the Registrar of Companies (RoC) - Budget 2022 Announcement: - Introduction of C-PACE (Centre for Processing Accelerated Corporate Exit) - A centralized facility to streamline and expedite the voluntary liquidation process. - Objective: - Reduce the time and cost of voluntary exit - Encourage ease of doing and closing business in India - Part of broader Ease of Doing Business (EoDB) reforms. - Notes: - Voluntary liquidation is considered a proactive and cleaner exit strategy. - It avoids litigation, insolvency stigma, and protects the credit profile of promoters. - Credit Rating/Monitoring Initiatives -

- National Financial Information Registry (NFIR, Budget-2023) - Designed with RBI to provide comprehensive borrower data (individuals and companies). - Enhances loan processing speed and accuracy. - RBI's Public-Tech Platform for Frictionless Credit (2023) - Collects data from government (center and state), Credit Information Company (CICs), and aggregators to streamline loan approvals. - RBI's Unified Lending Interface (2024-September) - Extension of Public-Tech Platform, initially for farmers and Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs), managed by RBI’s Innovation Hub. - eBkray Auction Platform/IBAPI (2019) - Also called Indian Banks Auctions Mortgaged Properties Information (IBAPI) - It is a website for e-auction of assets attached by Public Sector banks. - Boss: - Indian Banks Association (IBA) with help of Department of Financial Services (DFS, under FinMin). - Buyers can use eBkray/IBAPI portal to search and get properties details and participate in the auction process. - Baanknet (2025) - PSB e-auction portal for defaulted loan properties, managed by Department of Financial Services under Finance ministry. - CEIB Unified Automated Search Portal (2024) - Assists PSBs in background checks for borrowers seeking over ₹50 crore, managed by Central Economic Intelligence Bureau. - CEIB collects the data related to economic offenses from other regulators/investigating agencies, e.g. SEBI (Securities and Exchange Board of India), ED (Enforcement Directorate), CBI (Central Bureau of Investigation), NIA (National Investigation Agency), SFIO (Serious Fraud Investigation Office), CBDT (Central Board of Direct Taxes), CBIC (Central Board of Indirect Taxes and Customs) etc. - IFTAS Cloud Computing Facility - RBI-owned Indian Financial Technology & Allied Services provides secure cloud storage for banks/NBFCs. - Benefits: - Security and privacy of Indian 6nancial data compared to storing data on some cloud servers owned by foreign companies like Amazon-AWS, Microsoft-Azure, etc. - Reserve Bank Innovation Hub (RBIH) Repository - Reserve Bank Innovation Hub is a company 100% owned by RBI. - 2024: - It will set up a digital repository (=database/knowledge-base) of essential information related to Blockchain (= Distributed Ledger Technology/DLT), Artifcial Intelligence/Machine Learning (AI/ML), and other new technologies developed by Fin-Tech companies. - Benefits to RBI: - 1. RBI to make better policies and - 2. Banks/NBFCs/FinTech companies to come up with new innovative products. - AI and Indian Banking Sector - Definition - Artificial Intelligence (AI) refers to the simulation of human intelligence by machines. - In banking, AI helps automate decision-making, improve customer experience, detect fraud, and enhance risk management. - RBI Initiatives & Observations - RBI has emphasized responsible AI usage in the financial sector. - In 2023, RBI Governor Shaktikanta Das stated that banks must avoid “black-box” models in credit decisions. - RBI launched the “100 Days 100 Pays” campaign using AI to track unclaimed deposits across banks. - RBI’s Innovation Hub is working on AI solutions for better compliance, fraud detection, and credit analytics. - Key AI Applications in Banking - Credit Scoring & Risk Assessment - AI analyzes alternate data (e.g., utility bills, GST returns, social data) to assess MSME/retail borrowers. - Public-Tech Platform for Frictionless Credit (2023) integrates AI to pre-screen loan eligibility. - Fraud Detection & Compliance - AI tools flag unusual transactions in real time (AML/CFT). - ICICI, HDFC, SBI using AI-powered rule engines for suspicious activity reports (SARs). - Customer Service Automation - Chatbots (e.g., SBI’s SIA, HDFC’s EVA) handle 80%+ basic queries instantly. - AI helps with onboarding via video-KYC and voice biometrics. - Predictive Analytics - Banks use AI to predict loan defaults and proactively restructure loans. - Helps in early warning signals for NPA detection. - Recent Trends (2023–25) - Rise of Explainable AI (XAI) - Regulators demand transparency in AI-driven credit decisions to avoid bias. - Use of Generative AI - Banks exploring LLMs for internal document summarization, report writing, and knowledge base automation. - AI-as-a-Service (AIaaS) - Fintechs offer AI tools to co-operative banks and small NBFCs without in-house teams. - RBI’s Regulatory Sandbox - Encourages startups to test AI/ML products in a controlled environment. - Challenges & Risks - Algorithmic Bias - AI systems may discriminate against certain borrower groups due to flawed training data. - Data Privacy & Consent - Use of alternate data must comply with India’s Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023. - Cybersecurity Threats - AI tools can also be exploited for fraud (e.g., deepfake-based frauds). - Legal Entity Identifier (LEI) Number - Objective: - A company blacklisted by Indian banks could still apply for loans overseas. - Overseas bankers may be unaware of the company’s default history. - Hence, a global “Aadhar card” for companies is needed. - Companies must quote this number during every financial transaction. - Origin: - Introduced after USA’s Sub-Prime Crisis and Global Financial Crisis (GFC). - G20 and its Financial Stability Board (FSB) developed the LEI concept. - LEI is a 20-digit alphanumeric code. - Global Authority: - Global Legal Entity Identifier Foundation (GLEIF), Frankfurt, Germany. - Indian Coordinator: - Clearing Corporation of India. - RBI Mandate: - From 1 October 2022, RBI made LEI mandatory for all companies undertaking cross-border transactions of ₹50 crore or more. - Companies must quote LEI during such transactions. - Legal Basis: - RBI has power under: - Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007 - Banking Regulation Act, 1949 - Fugitive Economic Offenders Act, 2018 - Targets economic offenders accused of cheque dishonor, loan fraud, investment scams, chit-fund scams, money​ laundering - Any such offence involving ₹100 crores or more - Offender must have fled India to avoid prosecution/arrest - Legal Framework: - Special courts under the PMLA (Prevention of Money-Laundering Act, 2002) - Court will order appearance within 6 weeks - Failure → Declared "Fugitive Economic Offender" - Consequences: - Indian, overseas, and benami properties attached - Government can administer or liquidate these assets - Judicial Protection: - No ordinary civil court or tribunal can stay the proceedings - Appeal possible only in High Court or Supreme Court - Latest - Bankers Can’t Issue Look Out Circulars (LOCs): Bombay High Court Ruling - LOCs are issued by: - Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA) - Bureau of Immigration - Purpose of LOC: - Prevents person from leaving India via sea, land, or air - Bombay HC Verdict: - Public Sector Banks (PSBs) cannot recommend/request LOCs - Executive cannot curtail fundamental right to travel without legal/court basis - India Joins CARIN Asset Recovery Group (2024) - What is CARIN? - Camden Asset Recovery Inter-Agency Network, named after Camden, London - Informal group of 50+ countries - It is a global network of law-enforcement agencies that cooperate to trace criminal money, freeze assets, confiscate proceeds of crime across borders. - It targets drug trafficking money, terrorism financing, corruption, black money, tax evasion, organized crime assets - What CARIN actually does? - Shares intelligence between countries - Helps track assets hidden abroad - Coordinates freezing and confiscation - Helps governments get stolen money back - It does not arrest people, conduct trials, act like a court - India’s Participation: - Enforcement Directorate (ED) joined in 2024 - Objective: Assist in tracing and recovering: - Movable & immovable assets from criminal activities - Related to offenders like Dawood Ibrahim, Vijay Mallya, Nirav Modi, Mehul Choksi, etc. - Deposit Insurance and Credit Guarantee Corporation (DICGC) - Definition: - DICGC is a wholly-owned subsidiary of the Reserve Bank of India (RBI), established under the Deposit Insurance and Credit Guarantee Corporation Act, 1961, to protect bank depositors. - Objective: - Provide insurance coverage for deposits in all types of banks (commercial, cooperative, RRBs, etc.), ensuring financial stability and depositor confidence. - Features: - Ownership: - 100% owned by RBI; chaired by an RBI Deputy Governor. - Headquarters: - Mumbai. - Coverage: - Insures principal and interest of deposit accounts (savings, fixed, current, recurring). - Premium: - Banks pay a premium to DICGC to insure deposits, historically at ₹0.10 per ₹100 of deposits, adjustable to ₹0.12–₹0.15 (20–50% increase, exact figures not critical for UPSC). - Historical Role: - Previously provided credit guarantees for Priority Sector Lending (PSL) loans, now shared with SIDBI (1990) and NCGTC (2016) (Ref: Pillar#1D). - Reforms (2020–2021): - Pre-2020: - Insurance coverage up to ₹1 lakh per depositor per bank. - No clear repayment timeline; depositors often waited years after bank failure. - Post-2020 (Budget 2020 and DICGC Amendment Bill, 2021): - Coverage increased to ₹5 lakh per depositor per bank (principal and interest). - Repayment timeline: - Within 90 days of RBI imposing a moratorium on a weak bank. - Premium rate flexibility: - DICGC can raise premiums to ₹0.12–₹0.15 per ₹100 of deposits. - Coverage Scope: - All banks (commercial, cooperative, RRBs, LABs) covered. - Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs) and Primary Agricultural Credit Societies (PACS) excluded. - Limitations: - Deposit Cap: - Only ₹5 lakh insured per depositor, even if deposits exceed this amount; remaining claims require bank liquidation by RBI or Cooperative Registrar (Ref: 12.10, Banking Regulation Ordinance 2020). - Exclusions: - NBFCs and PACS not covered; victims must seek court redressal. - Historical Case (2002): - UTI (government-owned mutual fund) losses not covered by DICGC (not a bank); government paid ₹14,500 crore to investors (Economic Survey, 2002–03). - Proposed Solution: - Financial Sector Legislative Reforms Commission (FSLRC, 2011–13, chaired by Justice B.N. Srikrishna) recommended a Resolution Corporation via the Financial Resolution and Deposit Insurance (FRDI) Bill, 2017. - FRDI Bill faced public/media opposition and was withdrawn in 2018 (not critical for UPSC). - Impact on Monetary Policy: - Contribution: - Enhances depositor confidence, supporting RBI’s financial stability goals. - Facilitates PSL compliance (Ref: 12.5) by ensuring bank deposit safety. - Challenges: - Exclusion of NBFCs/PACS limits comprehensive deposit protection. - High premiums strain small banks’ finances; RBI monitors premium hikes to balance stability. - Bad Bank (NARCL, IDRCL-2021) - Definition: - A "Bad Bank" is a financial entity that takes over stressed assets (NPAs) from commercial banks. - It is a specialized Asset Reconstruction or Resolution Company that buys NPAs at a discounted value and attempts recovery or resolution. - It does not function as a conventional bank – it doesn’t accept deposits or give loans. - Its aim is to clean bank balance sheets and enable banks to focus on fresh lending instead of chasing recoveries. - The Indian Bad Bank structure consists of: - NARCL (National Asset Reconstruction Company Ltd.) – acquires bad loans. - IDRCL (India Debt Resolution Company Ltd.) – resolves and recovers value from these loans. - Functioning: - Banks identify large stressed assets (typically ₹500 crore+). - NARCL purchases these assets: - Pays 15% upfront in cash. - Issues 85% in Security Receipts (SRs), guaranteed by Government of India (sovereign guarantee up to ₹30,600 crore). - IDRCL manages legal proceedings, recovery, resolution, or sale of assets to other buyers (e.g., ARCs, investors). - Proceeds from resolution are used to redeem SRs. - Government’s Role: - Provides sovereign guarantee for 5 years to cover the shortfall if recovery is less than Security Receipts (SR) value. - Ensures confidence in the mechanism, as this is not a private Asset Reconstruction Company (ARC) risk. - Benefits: - Improves Bank Balance Sheets: - Removes legacy NPAs from books, reduces provisioning. - Enhances Lending Capacity: - Banks can focus on new lending instead of litigation and recovery. - Improves Credit Flow: - Especially for MSMEs and corporate borrowers. - Professional Recovery Management: - IDRCL staffed by professionals with expertise in asset resolution. - Faster and Centralized Resolution: - Avoids duplication of effort by multiple banks handling same borrower. - Restores Market Confidence: - Especially post banking scandals (e.g., IL&FS, DHFL). - Challenges: - Recovery is still uncertain due to: - Delays in legal system (IBC, DRTs). - Quality of collateral. - Possibility of moral hazard if banks rely excessively on bad banks. - Needs strong cooperation between NARCL, IDRCL, and judiciary for faster turnaround. - Global Examples: - USA – After 2008 crisis, TARP (Troubled Asset Relief Program) bought toxic assets. - Sweden – Formed Securum during 1990s banking crisis. - Malaysia – Formed Danaharta in 1998 to recover bad debts after Asian crisis. - NARCL vs IDRCL -
CriteriaNational Asset Reconstruction Company Ltd (NARCL)India Debt Resolution Company Ltd (IDRCL)
TypeAsset Reconstruction Company (ARC)Asset Management Company (AMC)
Full FormNational Asset Reconstruction Company LimitedIndia Debt Resolution Company Ltd.
FunctionPurchases bad loan assets from banks.Manages, values, and auctions assets acquired by NARCL.
Ownership51% PSBs, 49% private banks/NBFCs49% PSBs/NBFCs, 51% private banks/NBFCs
