- Banking and NBFC Regulatory Frameworks - Prompt Corrective Action (PCA) Framework for Scheduled Commercial Banks: - Definition: - RBI classifies Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs) into Risk Thresholds 1, 2, or 3 based on capital adequacy, non-performing assets (NPAs), and profitability. - Features: - Higher risk threshold (e.g., 3) indicates greater distress, triggering stricter RBI actions. - Corrective Actions: - Issuing warnings, conducting deeper audits, and enhancing supervision. - Restricting directors’ salaries and dividend distributions. - Limiting branch expansion and lending operations. - Enforcing mergers or shutdowns under Banking Regulation Act, 1949. - Exit from PCA: - Banks must reduce NPAs, raise capital, and improve profitability. - Status (2020): - Four Public Sector Banks (PSBs) under PCA: Indian Overseas Bank, Central Bank of India, UCO Bank, United Bank of India (dynamic data, not critical for UPSC). - PCA Framework for NBFCs: - Definition: - Effective October 1, 2022, RBI monitors NBFCs using three indicators: - NPAs, - Basel-III Capital Adequacy Ratio, - Basel-III Tier 1 Capital - Features: - NBFCs classified into Risk Categories 1, 2, or 3, with higher risk leading to stricter measures. - Restrictions: - Halting branch expansion, dividend payouts, and requiring capital infusion by owners. - Applicability: - Covers deposit-taking NBFCs (e.g., Bajaj Finserv) and government-owned NBFCs (e.g., PFC, REC, IRFC, IFCI). - Exclusions: - Primary Dealers, Housing Finance Companies (HFCs), and non-deposit-taking NBFCs (e.g., IL&FS). - Supervisory Action Framework (SAF) for Urban Cooperative Banks (UCBs): - Definition: - RBI's structured approach to monitor and regulate financially weak Urban Cooperative Banks (UCBs). - Aims to restore financial health and protect depositors. - Introduced: - Initially in 2012; revised in December 2019 for enhanced oversight and clarity. - Parameters for Trigger: - CRAR (Capital to Risk-Weighted Assets Ratio) - Net NPA levels - Profitability (e.g., consistent losses) - Risk Threshold Categories: - Category 1 (Moderate Risk): - Initial corrective actions like restrictions on new loans. - Category 2 (High Risk): - Stricter measures including curbs on branch expansion and high-value loans. - Category 3 (Very High Risk): - Severe restrictions such as capital infusion requirements, management changes, and even merger/sale. - Actionable Measures: - Restrict new loan disbursements and investments. - Restrict declaration of dividend or bonuses. - Impose cap on borrowings and deposit growth. - Suggest capital infusion or change in management. - Applicability: - All Urban Cooperative Banks under RBI regulation. - Exclusions: - Not applicable to Rural Cooperative Banks or NBFCs. - Objective: - Early detection of stress and timely corrective action to avoid insolvency or depositor losses. - Comparison with PCA (for NBFCs/Banks): - SAF is specific to UCBs; PCA covers Scheduled Commercial Banks and NBFCs. - Both use tiered triggers and impose escalating restrictions based on risk severity. - 4-Tiered Regulatory Framework for Urban Cooperative Banks: - Definition: - Introduced December 2022, classifies Urban Cooperative Banks (UCBs) into four tiers based on deposit size. - Tiers: - Tier 1: - Up to ₹100 crore. - Tier 2: - ₹100 crore–₹1,000 crore. - Tier 3: - ₹1,000 crore–₹10,000 crore. - Tier 4: - Above ₹10,000 crore. - Features: - Higher tiers face stricter Basel-III norms (e.g., capital, liquidity requirements). - Exact norms not critical for UPSC. - Strategic and Supervisory Initiatives - Regulations Review Authority (RRA 2.0): - Definition: - Established in 2021 (following RRA 1.0 in 1999), chaired by RBI Deputy Governor M. Rajeshwar Rao. - Objective: - Simplify RBI regulations to ease compliance for banks and NBFCs. - Utkarsh-2022 Roadmap: - Definition: - Three-year (2019–2022) framework, based on Viral Acharya Committee recommendations, to align RBI supervision with global best practices. - Digital Lending Regulations: - Digital Lending Norms: - Definition: - RBI guidelines (November 2022) regulate digital lending by Loan Service Providers (LSPs), FinTech firms connecting borrowers to banks/NBFCs (e.g., LazyPay, Paisabazaar, Bank Bazaar). - Features: - LSPs facilitate digital loan access, streamlining borrower-lender interactions. - Prohibit exorbitant interest rates to protect borrowers. - Mandate appointment of grievance redressal officers with fixed resolution timelines (e.g., 30 days for complaints). - Ensure transparency in loan terms, fees, and data privacy. - First Loss Default Guarantee (FLDG): - Definition: - Requires FinTech companies to cover a portion of lenders’ losses (e.g., banks, NBFCs) if borrowers default, reducing lender risk. - Features: - Typically covers 5–10% of defaulted loan value (exact figures not critical for UPSC). - Encourages responsible lending by FinTech firms. - Digital Lending Apps Public Repository: - Definition: - Launched August 2024, an RBI portal listing approved digital lending apps to ensure legitimacy. - Features: - Unlisted apps are deemed illegal, protecting borrowers from fraudulent platforms. - Enhances transparency and borrower awareness. - Basel-III Norms and Systemic Banks - Basel-III Norms: - Definition: - Basel-III norms, set by the Bank for International Settlements (BIS) Committee on Banking Supervision in 2011, aim to enhance global financial stability through improved capital adequacy and risk management. - Features: - Bank for International Settlements (BIS): - International institution of 60 countries’ central banks, headquartered in Basel, Switzerland. - Implementation in India: - Phased by RBI from April 1, 2013. - Key Requirements for Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs): - Capital to Risk-Weighted Assets Ratio (CRAR) - Risk-Weighted Assets (RWA) are a bank’s assets after adjusting them for risk, used to calculate how much capital the bank must hold. - Calculate Risk-Weighted Assets (RWA) based on loan risk profiles. - Maintain minimum Capital to Risk-Weighted Assets Ratio (CRAR) at 9% or higher (effective March 31, 2019). - \( \text{CRAR} = \frac{\text{Total Capital}}{\text{Risk-Weighted Assets}} \times 100 \) - Capital Conservation Buffer (CCB): - Additional capital to absorb losses (specific percentage not critical for UPSC). - Counter Cyclical Capital Buffer (CCCB): - Activated by RBI during economic booms to curb excessive lending. - Leverage Ratio (LR), Net Stable Funding Ratio (NSFR), Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR), and High-Quality Liquid Assets (HQLA): - Ensure liquidity and stability (technical details not critical for UPSC). - Applicability: - Extends to Differential Banks (LABs, RRBs, SFBs, Payments Banks), Cooperative Banks, All India Financial Institutions (e.g., EXIM, NABARD, NHB, SIDBI), and certain NBFCs, with RBI prescribing specific norms. - Objective (UPSC-Prelim-2016): - Improve banking sector’s ability to manage financial and economic stress and enhance risk management. - CRAR Increase for Unsecured Loans (2023): - Definition: - In 2023, RBI increased risk weights for unsecured loans to ensure prudence in lending by banks and NBFCs. - Changes: -
Loan TypeOld Risk WeightNew Risk Weight (2023)
Housing, education, vehicle, gold-secured loans100%100% (no change)
Personal/consumer loans (except above)100%125%
Bankers’ credit card loans125%150%
NBFCs’ credit card loans100%125%
Bankers’ loans to NBFCs (except HFCs, MFIs, PSL)100%125%
- Note: - For UPSC, memorize that risk weights increased for certain unsecured loans; exact figures critical only for RBI Grade B Officer exams. - Implications of Higher CRAR: - Features: - Encourages prudent lending by banks/NBFCs. - Higher risk weights increase capital requirements, raising loan costs (e.g., up to 4% higher interest rates). - Reduces bank/NBFC profitability due to additional capital costs. - Capital Tiers: - Definition: - Basel-III requires banks to maintain capital in tiers against RWAs, including Tier 1 (core capital) and Tier 2 (supplementary capital). - Note: - Detailed tier structures not critical for UPSC; focus on capital adequacy purpose. - Additional Tier 1 (AT1) Bonds: - Definition: - Perpetual bonds with no maturity date, requiring banks to pay interest indefinitely. - AT1 bonds act as a shock absorber, a firewall between losses and depositors. Before depositors lose money, AT1 investors get wiped. - Features: - Can be bought back by banks after a specified period. - In case of significant losses, AT1 bonds may be written off or converted into shares (bail-in process). - Context: - Gained attention due to Yes Bank loan scam (details not critical for UPSC). - Zero Coupon Bonds: - Definition: - Bonds paying no periodic interest, issued at a discount and redeemed at face value (Ref: Pillar#1C Sharemarket). - Note: - Not critical for Basel-III discussion; included for completeness. - Domestic Systemically Important Banks (D-SIBs): - Definition: - Identified by RBI annually as banks “too big to fail,” whose collapse would severely impact the economy. - Features: - Established by G-20’s Financial Stability Board (2010, HQ: Basel). - D-SIBs (e.g., SBI, ICICI, HDFC) must maintain additional equity capital and comply with stricter norms. - Basel-III Deadline Postponements: - Definition: - During COVID-19, RBI postponed Basel-III deadlines to ease banking sector stress. - Note: - Outdated for UPSC; new deadlines not critical as they may be extended further. - Impact on Monetary Policy: - Contribution: - Basel-III norms strengthen banking stability, improving monetary policy transmission (Ref: 12.7). - Higher CRAR and D-SIB norms reduce systemic risks, supporting credit flow to PSL sectors (Ref: 12.5). - Challenges: - Higher capital requirements increase lending costs, potentially reducing credit access for small borrowers. - Non-compliance triggers PCA (Ref: 12.17), affecting bank operations. - Balance sheet / Audit Norms - Key Definitions - Balance Sheet: - A financial statement that provides a snapshot of an entity’s financial position at a specific date. - Shows: - Assets (what the bank owns), - Liabilities (what it owes), - and Shareholder’s Equity (capital). - Equation: - Assets = Liabilities + Equity - For banks, this includes: - Deposits, loans, investments, borrowings, CRR/SLR holdings, capital reserves, etc. - Example: - SBI's balance sheet will show: - ₹40 lakh crore in deposits (liability), - ₹30 lakh crore in loans (asset), - ₹3 lakh crore in capital (equity). - Audit Norms: - Rules and guidelines for independent examination of financial records. - Objective: - Ensure true & fair view, - Detect frauds, - Comply with RBI norms, Companies Act, SEBI rules, etc. - Types of audit applicable to banks: - Statutory Audit (mandated by RBI and Companies Act) - Concurrent Audit (real-time checking of transactions) - Internal Audit - RBI Inspection (Annual Financial Inspection) - Norms derived from: - RBI Master Circulars - Institute of Chartered Accountants of India (ICAI) - IndAS/IFRS for presentation - IndAS Accounting Norms Deferred - What is IndAS? - IndAS = Indian Accounting Standards. - Issued by Ministry of Corporate Affairs (MCA), converged with IFRS (International Financial Reporting Standards). - Intended to replace old Indian Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP). - Used to prepare financial statements for companies, including banks (eventually). - Key IndAS Examples: - IndAS 109: - Financial Instruments (Expected Credit Loss model) - IndAS 115: - Revenue from Contracts - IndAS 116: - Lease Accounting - Benefits of IndAS: - Better transparency in financial reporting. - Global comparability of Indian companies. - Helps foreign investors assess true position. - Example: - Under IndAS 109, banks must provide for expected losses on loans in advance, not after default. - RBI’s Stand: - Implementation for banks has been deferred multiple times. - Reason: - Complex provisioning under Expected Credit Loss model. - May raise provisioning & lower profits/capital adequacy. - Need to upgrade banking IT systems (CBS). - As of 2024: - IndAS not yet implemented for commercial banks. - Recapitalization of PSBs - Definition: - Recapitalization refers to injecting fresh capital into banks to help them meet regulatory norms (like BASEL-III), absorb losses, and continue lending to the economy. - Particularly crucial for Public Sector Banks (PSBs), which often suffer from high NPAs and capital erosion. - Why Recapitalization? - Banks need to maintain a Capital Adequacy Ratio (CAR) under BASEL-III norms. - If capital falls below threshold: - Banks may not be allowed to lend further. - Risk triggering RBI's Prompt Corrective Action (PCA). - Undermines depositor confidence and financial stability. - Methods: - Government (owner) injects capital directly. - Banks issue: - Debt instruments (bonds) - Equity shares (to public or institutional investors) - Timeline and Initiatives - 2015: - Estimation: - ₹1.8 lakh crore required by PSBs by March 2019 for BASEL-III compliance. - Govt launches Indradhanush Plan: - ₹70,000 crore allocated (2015–2018). - Allocation based on performance (e.g., RoA, NPA reduction). - Goal: - Encourage efficiency, reduce political interference. - 2017: - Realization: - ₹70,000 crore insufficient. - Govt announces additional capital infusion. - Starts issuing Bank Recapitalization Bonds (RcBs): - Features: - Interest ~7%, maturity: 2028–2033. - Non-transferable, non-convertible (not traded like regular bonds). - Helps recapitalize banks without burdening fiscal deficit immediately. - 2017–2019: Consolidation of weaker banks - Investors hesitant to invest in weak PSBs. - Govt merges weak banks to create stronger entities: - SBI + BMB + 5 Associates (2017) - BoB + Dena + Vijaya (April 2019) - Budget 2021–22: - ₹20,000 crore earmarked for recapitalization. - Budget 2022 onwards: - No allocation made. - Justification: - PSBs' profitability improving. - Now capable of raising capital on their own (via bonds/shares). - Broader Impact and Observations - PSBs strengthened capital buffers. - Improved ability to meet BASEL norms and expand lending. - Reduced dependence on taxpayer money. - Encouraged market discipline. - Examples: - SBI raised over ₹15,000 crore via QIP (Qualified Institutional Placement) in 2020. - PNB and BoB issued Additional Tier 1 Bonds to improve their CAR. - Banking Sector Governance and Reforms - Gyan-Sangam (2015): - Definition: - Workshop organized by the Finance Ministry’s Department of Financial Services in Pune (2015) for financial regulators, PSBs, and public sector insurance companies. - Outcomes: - Bifurcation of PSB Chairman and Managing Director & CEO roles to enhance professionalism and accountability. - Indradhanush Plan: - ₹70,000 crore for PSB recapitalization to meet Basel-III norms (Ref: 12.19). - Establishment of Bank Board Bureau (BBB) for personnel selection and governance reforms. - Bank Board Bureau (BBB): - Definition: - Non-constitutional, non-statutory body established in 2016 to select top officials for PSBs, public sector insurance companies, and AIFIs (e.g., NABARD, SIDBI). - Features: - Interviews and recommends candidates for MD, CEO, Chairman, and full-time directors. - Actual appointments by Finance Ministry’s Department of Financial Services (analogous to UPSC-DoPT for IAS). - Assists PSBs in governance reforms and Basel-III capital raising (Ref: 12.19). - Chaired by a retired IAS officer with additional members (exact members not critical for UPSC). - Financial Services Institutions Bureau (FSIB): - Definition: - Established in 2022 to replace BBB after Delhi High Court ruled BBB cannot select public sector insurance company heads due to legal conflicts. - Features: - Functions identical to BBB; selects top officials for PSBs, AIFIs, and public sector insurance companies, and supports governance/capital raising. - Complies with court order; no ban on FSIB as of 2025. - PSB Banking Personnel Reforms (ES20): - Note: - Shifted to Mains per raw text; involves personnel management reforms (details not critical for Prelims). - Enhanced Access and Service Excellence (EASE) Agenda: - Definition: - Launched in 2018 by Finance Ministry’s Department of Financial Services to improve PSB responsiveness and responsibility. - Features (Six Pillars): - Customer Responsiveness: - Enhance service quality for customers. - Responsible Banking: - Reduce NPAs and prevent frauds (Ref: Pillar#1B2). - Credit Off-Take: - Proactively reach potential borrowers (e.g., home, auto, education loans). - MSME Support: - Faster loan approvals (within 15 days) via SIDBI’s Udyamimitra.com (Ref: 12.5). - Financial Inclusion and Digitalization: - Expand access and digital services. - Human Resource Management: - Improve training, performance-linked promotions, and transfers. - Implementation: - Each PSB whole-time director oversees one pillar, evaluated by the bank’s board. - Independent agency assesses public perception; PSBs ranked (e.g., PNB ranked #1 in 2018). - Updates: - EASE 6.0 (2023–24): - Focused on customer service, digital enablement, analytics-driven business, and HR development. - EASE 7.0 (2024–25): - Emphasizes risk assessment, NPA management, financial inclusion, customer service, and digital transformation. - Staff Accountability Framework for PSB-NPA: - Definition: - Introduced by Finance Ministry in 2022 for PSB NPA accounts up to ₹50 crore. - Features: - Protects PSB bankers from undue inquiries by CBI, CVC, and CAG for bona fide business decisions leading to NPAs. - Addresses fear of “3Cs” (CBI, CVC, CAG), which previously discouraged big loan sanctions post-scams (e.g., Vijay Mallya, Nirav Modi). - B.P. Kanungo Committee (Customer Service Reforms): - Definition: - Formed by RBI in 2022 to recommend reforms against loan recovery agent harassment and other customer service issues. - Features: - Suggested measures to curb bullying by recovery agents and improve transparency in customer interactions. - Key Terms (Bancassurance, Cross-Selling, Mis-Selling): - Definitions: - Bancassurance: - Bankers selling insurance products (e.g., IDBI selling LIC policies) to earn commissions; legal. - Cross-Selling: - Offering additional services (e.g., credit cards, mutual funds) to customers; legal. - Mis-Selling: - Misleading customers about products (e.g., falsely claiming credit cards are free for life); illegal/unethical. - Impact on Monetary Policy: - Contribution: - Governance reforms enhance PSB efficiency, supporting monetary policy transmission (Ref: 12.7). - EASE and Staff Accountability Framework boost credit flow to PSL sectors (Ref: 12.5) and reduce NPAs (Ref: Pillar#1B2). - Challenges: - Balancing accountability with banker autonomy to avoid risk-averse lending. - Ensuring customer service reforms are uniformly implemented across PSBs. - Shadow Banking and Financial Frauds - Shadow Banking: - Definition: - Shadow banking involves financial activities and institutions (e.g., NBFCs) operating partially or fully outside traditional banking regulations, not fully overseen by RBI. - Features: - Mobilizes funds via bank loans, Commercial Papers (CPs), and Non-Convertible Debentures (NCDs). - Key Segments of shadow banking in India: - Housing Finance Companies (HFCs): - e.g., Dewan Housing Finance Limited (DHFL). - Liquid Debt Mutual Funds (LDMFs): - Invest in short-term debt like T-bills and CPs (e.g., UTI, Kotak, L&T, Tata schemes). - Retail NBFCs: - Include gold loan and asset finance companies. - Risks: - Assets are risky and illiquid, vulnerable to “bank run” situations where investors demand funds, leading to potential defaults. - IL&FS and NBFC Liquidity Crisis: - Definition: - Infrastructure Leasing & Financial Services (IL&FS), a Systemically Important Non-Deposit-Taking NBFC (NBFC-ND-SI), triggered a liquidity crisis in 2019. - Features: - Established: - 1987, HQ: Mumbai. - Shareholders: - LIC, Japan’s Orix Group, Abu Dhabi Group, Central Bank of India, SBI. - Crisis Causes: - Loans to unworthy borrowers with forged documents and bribes to IL&FS directors/auditors. - LDMFs (e.g., UTI, Kotak) invested in IL&FS and DHFL, with ₹4,000 crore stuck, sparking NBFC crisis. - RBI Remedies for NBFCs: - Features: - Targeted Long-Term Repo Operations (TLTRO): - Provides liquidity to NBFCs (Ref: Pillar#1A2). - Special Liquidity Window: - Temporary funding support for NBFCs. - Tightened Asset-Liability Management (ALM) norms for better NBFC financial health. - 2021 Proposal: - 4-tier NBFC regulatory structure (Base, Middle, Upper, Top Layer) for tighter oversight, with Top Layer facing strictest rules (implementation details not critical for UPSC). - Partial Credit Guarantee Scheme (PCGS) 2.0: - Definition: - Announced in Budget 2019, government guarantees 20% of losses for PSBs investing in debt securities (bonds, CPs) issued by NBFCs, HFCs, or MFIs if borrowers default. - Additional Government and SEBI Reforms: - Note: - Shifted to Mains per raw text; includes SEBI’s tightened regulations for NBFCs (details not critical for Prelims). - PNB Scam (LoU, SWIFT, Nirav Modi): - Definition: - Punjab National Bank (PNB) scam (2018) involved fraudulent Letters of Undertaking (LoUs) issued for Nirav Modi and Mehul Choksi, worth ₹12,000 crore. - Features: - LoUs: - Bank guarantees for foreign currency loans from overseas banks, earning PNB commissions. - Scam Details: - Corrupt PNB Mumbai branch managers issued LoUs without due diligence or collateral for 7 years. - SWIFT Issue: - LoUs processed via SWIFT platform, not integrated with PNB’s Core Banking Solution (CBS), delaying detection (Ref: Pillar#1A1 Digital Payments). - Outcome: - Nirav Modi and Mehul Choksi fled India in January 2018. - International Banking Crises (Silicon Valley Bank, Credit Suisse): - Silicon Valley Bank (SVB) Collapse (2023): - Background: - SVB was a key lender to tech startups and venture capital firms in the U.S. - Held large amounts of long-term government bonds. - Trigger: - U.S. Federal Reserve hiked interest rates rapidly. - Bond portfolio value fell; bank booked losses on asset sales. - Sudden withdrawal by depositors (tech startups), causing a bank run. - Outcome: - SVB collapsed in March 2023. - Taken over by FDIC (Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation). - Signaled interest rate risk in banking sector. - Credit Suisse Crisis (2023): - Background: - One of Switzerland’s largest and oldest banks. - Already weakened by previous scandals and losses (e.g., Archegos, Greensill). - Trigger: - Global banking fears post-SVB collapse led to panic. - Credit Suisse's largest investor ruled out further capital infusion. - Sharp drop in share prices and deposit withdrawals. - Outcome: - Swiss government facilitated emergency takeover by UBS (another major Swiss bank). - Aimed to avoid systemic risk in European banking. - Impact on Monetary Policy: - Contribution: - RBI’s remedies (TLTRO, ALM, 4-tier structure) stabilize NBFCs, supporting monetary policy transmission (Ref: 12.7). - PCGS 2.0 enhances credit flow to PSL sectors (Ref: 12.5). - Challenges: - Shadow banking’s partial regulation increases systemic risks, requiring tighter RBI oversight. - Fraud prevention (e.g., PNB scam) demands better technology integration (Ref: Pillar#1A1). - RBI Financial Framework and Governance - RBI Income Sources and Reserve Allocations: - Definition: - RBI generates income from various sources and allocates funds to reserves and dividends under the RBI Act, 1935 (Section 47). - - Remaining profit transferred as dividend to the Union Government. - Bimal Jalan Panel (Economic Capital Framework): - Definition: - Formed in 2018 to establish principles for RBI’s dividend transfers to the government, following a 2017 dispute (government demanded ₹3.6 lakh crore vs. RBI’s ₹50,000 crore). - Features: - Approved by RBI board in August 2019. - Updated norms to enable higher dividend transfers while maintaining adequate reserves for financial stability. - Record Dividend to Government (2023-24): - Definition: - RBI transferred a record ₹2.1 lakh crore dividend to the government in 2023–24. - Note: - Details of reasons not critical for UPSC; reflects RBI’s robust income and Bimal Jalan norms. - RBI Financial Year Alignment: - Definition: - RBI aligned its financial year to April–March from July–June, effective April 1, 2021, per Bimal Jalan panel recommendations (2019). - Features: - Historical Financial Years: - January–December (1934), July–June (1940). - Pre-2021: - RBI provided interim dividends due to misalignment with government’s April–March cycle. - Post-2021: - Alignment eliminated need for interim dividends, streamlining financial reporting. - Section 7 of RBI Act, 1934 and 2018 Controversy: - Background: - Section 7 of the RBI Act, 1934 empowers the Central Government to issue directions to the RBI in public interest, after consultation with the Governor. - Historically, this section had never been invoked since RBI's establishment in 1935. - Features: - In October 2018, the Central Government initiated formal consultations under Section 7(1) with then RBI Governor Urjit Patel. - Key points of contention: - PCA (Prompt Corrective Action) Norms: - Government wanted relaxation in PCA restrictions for 11 public sector banks to improve credit flow to MSMEs. - Basel-III Deadlines: - Government sought delayed implementation to ease capital stress on banks. - Higher Dividend Transfer: - Government demanded higher surplus transfer from RBI’s reserves to support fiscal spending. - Relaxed Monetary Policy: - Government pushed for a more accommodative stance to boost growth amid slowdown. - Conflict Dynamics: - RBI argued for financial stability, autonomy, and adherence to prudential norms. - Deputy Governor Viral Acharya gave a strong speech warning that undermining central bank independence could lead to economic disaster. - Outcome: - Despite the Section 7(1) consultation, no directive was issued under Section 7(2)—which would have been binding on the RBI. - Issuing such a directive would have been politically controversial and could trigger investor concerns over institutional autonomy. - Urjit Patel resigned on 10 December 2018, citing personal reasons, before completing his 3-year term. - Widely interpreted as fallout from the escalating friction between RBI and the Government. - Significance: - Marked one of the rare instances of open confrontation between the RBI and the Centre. - Sparked debate on central bank autonomy, fiscal prudence, and governance of monetary institutions. - RBI later transferred ₹1.76 lakh crore to the government in FY2019–20 after accepting Bimal Jalan Committee recommendations on capital reserves. - Impact on Monetary Policy: - Contribution: - Dividend transfers support government fiscal policy, indirectly aiding monetary policy (Ref: 12.7). - Robust reserves (e.g., ₹7 lakh crore revaluation reserve) ensure exchange rate stability, enhancing RBI’s credibility (Ref: 12.8). - Challenges: - Balancing high dividend demands with RBI’s reserve adequacy to maintain financial stability. - Avoiding government overreach via Section-7 to preserve RBI autonomy. - Pillar 1C1: Bonds and Debt Instruments - Definition: - Debt instruments are financial securities representing a loan made by an investor to a borrower, typically a government or corporation, entitling the holder to periodic interest payments and principal repayment at maturity, playing a critical role in capital markets. - Context: - In India, debt instruments like bonds and debentures facilitate government and corporate financing, supporting infrastructure, economic growth, and financial inclusion, with the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) and Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) ensuring regulatory oversight. - Capital - Definition: - Capital refers to financial resources used by businesses or governments to fund operations, investments, or projects, categorized based on purpose and duration. - Types of Capital: - Fixed Capital: - Funds allocated for long-term assets like land, machinery, or buildings, essential for establishing or expanding business infrastructure (e.g., purchasing factory equipment). - Working Capital: - Funds used for day-to-day operations, such as raw materials, salaries, rent, and marketing, ensuring smooth business functioning (e.g., paying employee wages). - Capital Funding / Arrangement Classifications: - 1. On the Basis of Period - (a) Long-term Sources - Definition: - Finance required for a period exceeding 1 year. - Used for: - Fixed assets (plant, building), expansion. - Examples: - Equity Shares - Debentures - Long-term Loans from banks - Retained Earnings - (b) Short-term Sources - Definition: - Finance required for less than 1 year. - Used for: - Working capital needs (day-to-day operations). - Examples: - Trade Credit - Bank Overdraft - Short-term Loans - Bills Discounting - 2. On the Basis of Ownership - (a) Owners’ Funds - Definition: - Funds provided by the owners/promoters. - Features: - Permanent in nature - Not to be returned during business lifetime - Involves risk-bearing - Examples: - Equity Share Capital - Retained Earnings - (b) Borrowed Funds - Definition: - Funds borrowed from outsiders that must be repaid with interest. - Features: - Fixed obligation - Interest is paid regardless of profit - Examples: - Debentures - Loans from banks/financial institutions - Public Deposits - 3. On the Basis of Source of Generation - (a) Internal Sources - Definition: - Funds generated from within the business. - Features: - No cost of capital - Increases self-dependence - Examples: - Retained Earnings - Depreciation Funds - Sale of Assets - (b) External Sources - Definition: - Funds arranged from outside the business. - Features: - May involve cost and legal formalities - Examples: - Issue of Shares/Debentures - Loans from banks/institutions - Public Deposits - Capital Funding Instruments / Examples: - Trade Credit: - Short-term credit extended by suppliers allowing deferred payment for goods or services (e.g., a retailer paying a supplier 30 days after delivery). - Accounts Receivable and Payable: - Receivables are amounts owed to a company by its customers; payables are amounts a company owes to suppliers, both critical for managing cash flow. - Lease Financing (Medium-Term): - A company rents assets (e.g., machinery) instead of purchasing, reducing upfront costs and preserving capital for other uses, typically for 1-5 years. - Retained Earnings (Long-Term): - Profits reinvested into the business after distributing dividends, decided by the board of directors, used for expansion or debt repayment (e.g., reinvesting profits to open new branches). - Significance: - Efficient capital management enhances business sustainability and growth, critical for India’s economic development and job creation. - Securities - Definition: - Securities are financial instruments that are tradable in financial markets and represent either ownership (equity) or a creditor relationship (debt). - Used by governments, corporations, and other entities to raise capital from investors. - Recognized and regulated under laws such as the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 and SEBI Act, 1992 in India. - Types of Securities: -
Equity Securities Debt Securities
  • Represent ownership in a company (e.g., equity shares).
  • Shareholders are part-owners and may vote in key decisions.
  • Returns come as dividends, which depend on profits.
  • Higher risk, but potential for capital appreciation.
  • Residual claimants — paid only after all liabilities during liquidation.
  • Traded on stock exchanges (e.g., BSE, NSE).
  • Preferred in bullish/expansion phases of the economy.
  • Represent borrowed funds (e.g., bonds, debentures).
  • Investors are creditors, not owners.
  • Fixed returns in the form of interest, irrespective of profit.
  • Lower risk, hence preferred for stable income.
  • Priority over equity holders in case of liquidation.
  • May be secured (against assets) or unsecured.
  • Often used by governments and companies to finance debt.
  • Attractive during recessionary or low-growth periods.
- Subcategories (Optional for advanced learners): - Hybrid Securities: - Instruments combining features of both equity and debt. - Example: - Convertible debentures (debt that can be converted into equity). - Derivative Securities: - Value derived from underlying assets like stocks, indices, or commodities. - Examples: - Options, Futures — not direct capital-raising instruments but used for hedging/speculation. - Bonds and debentures differ technically: - Bonds are typically issued by government or large institutions and are often secured. - Debentures are more common with corporations and may or may not be secured. - Regulatory Authority in India: - Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) regulates securities markets. - Ensures transparency, protects investors, and curbs malpractices. - Financial / Securities Market - Definition: - A financial market is a structured platform (physical or digital) where buyers and sellers engage in the trade of financial instruments such as equity shares, bonds, treasury bills, derivatives, currencies, and commodities. - It enables the efficient flow of capital from surplus units (investors/savers) to deficit units (governments/corporates), supporting investment, production, and economic growth. - Types of Financial Markets: - 1. Based on Tenure of Instruments: - (a) Money Market: - Deals with short-term instruments (maturity < 1 year). - Highly liquid and low-risk. - Participants: - Banks, RBI, financial institutions, mutual funds. - Examples: - Treasury Bills (T-Bills), Commercial Papers (CP), Certificates of Deposit (CD), Call/Notice Money. - (b) Capital Market: - Deals with medium- to long-term instruments (maturity ≥ 1 year). - Facilitates capital formation for long-term projects. - Subdivided into: - Equity Market: - Shares - Debt Market: - Bonds, Debentures - 2. Based on Stage of Trading (Freshness): - (a) Primary Market: - New securities are issued for the first time to raise capital. - Investors buy directly from the issuer. - Methods: - Initial Public Offerings (IPOs), Rights Issues, Private Placements. - Enables resource mobilization for companies and governments. - (b) Secondary Market: - Existing securities are traded among investors. - No fresh capital is raised; provides liquidity and price discovery. - Regulated by SEBI; Examples: BSE, NSE. - 3. Based on Settlement Timing: - (a) Spot Market: - Transactions are settled immediately or within T+1/T+2 days. - Example: - Buying stocks with current prices and instant delivery. - (b) Futures / Derivatives Market: - Contracts are made to buy/sell assets at a future date at a predetermined price. - Used for hedging, speculation, and risk management. - Instruments: - Futures, Options, Swaps. - 4. Based on Nature of Assets Traded: - Major Segments: - Equity / Share Market: - Trading of company shares. - Debt / Bond Market: - Trading of bonds, debentures. - Government Securities (G-Sec) Market: - RBI auctions sovereign debt. - Foreign Exchange (Forex) Market: - Currencies are traded (INR, USD, EUR etc.). - Commodity Market: - Trading in goods like gold, crude oil, agricultural produce. - Universal Exchange: - Integrated platforms (e.g., NSE, BSE since 2018) that offer multi-asset trading (equity, debt, commodity, derivatives) under a single roof. - Significance: - Financial markets play a vital role in economic development by: - Mobilizing idle savings and directing them into productive investments. - Enhancing liquidity and reducing transaction costs. - Facilitating price discovery through demand-supply forces. - Supporting infrastructure growth via long-term capital availability. - Enabling monetary transmission through interest rate signals. - Providing platforms for risk management via derivatives. - Regulatory Framework in India: - SEBI (Securities and Exchange Board of India): - Regulates capital markets. - RBI (Reserve Bank of India): - Regulates money market and G-Secs. - IRDAI, PFRDA, FMC (merged into SEBI): - Other sector-specific oversight. - Debt Instruments - Definition: - Debt instruments are financial contracts where the borrower promises to repay the lender principal and interest, traded in money or capital markets. - Characteristics: - Bondholders are creditors with first claim during liquidation. - Assured interest payments, regardless of issuer’s profitability. - Classified into short-term (<1 year) and long-term (≥1 year) based on tenure. - Short-Term Debt Instruments: - Definition: - Debt instruments with tenure less than 1 year, typically unsecured (no collateral) and negotiable/transferable in the money market. - Features: - Sold at a discount and repurchased at face/par value; difference is investor’s interest (process called “discounting the bills”). - Near Money: - Highly liquid assets, easily convertible to cash. - Government Short-Term Debt Instruments: - Definition: - Short-term debt instruments issued by the Union Government; states stopped issuing T-Bills since 2001. - Features: -

- RBI manages and services Union G-Secs but not SDLs. - T-Bills issued at a discount from par value. - Government Short-Term Loans (WMA, SDF, Overdraft): - Definition: - Short-term loans by RBI to address temporary mismatches in government receipts and expenditures. - Includes: -

- WMA: - 3-month loans, not counted in fiscal deficit (Ref: Pillar#2D FRBM). - SDF: - For states/UTs, against Union G-Secs/T-Bills as collateral. - 2020–2024: - RBI increased WMA limits under Atmanirbhar Bharat to address COVID-19 cash flow mismatches (exact figures not critical for UPSC). - Certificates of Deposit (CDs): - Definition: - Certificates of Deposit are negotiable short term money market instruments issued by banks and financial institutions to raise funds for a fixed period at a fixed interest rate. - These are unsecured, short-term, and negotiable - 2023: - Surge in CD issuance due to high loan demand, insufficient deposits, and costly repo loans. - Other Short-Term Debt Instruments: -

- Negotiable Instruments: - Signed documents promising payment to a specified person at a specified time. - Unsecured: - No collateral pledged. - Tri-Party Repo: - Definition: - Short-term borrowing where a tri-party agent (e.g., NSE, BSE) facilitates collateral custody and repayment, earning a commission. - Features: - Differs from bi-party repo (borrower and RBI) by involving a third-party agent. - Not a monetary policy tool. - Factoring and TReDS: - Factoring: - A financial arrangement where a business sells its accounts receivable (invoices) to a third party called a factor - The factor pays cash immediately (after a discount), the factor then collects the payment from the customers later - Think of it as: - “Here are my unpaid bills. You deal with them. I want money now.” - Factoring Regulation (Amendment) Act, 2021: - Before: - Only banks and seven NBFC-Factoring Companies allowed. - After: - All RBI-registered NBFCs (over 9,000) can factor, increasing MSME loan access. - Central registry under SARFAESI Act prevents dual financing frauds. - TReDS: - Online platforms connecting MSMEs, buyers, and factors (banks/NBFCs). - TReDS Platforms: - M1xchange, RXIL (owned by SIDBI, NSE, SBI, ICICI, Yes Bank), A.TReDS; registered with RBI. - Bill Discounting: - Bill discounting is the process by which a bank purchases a bill of exchange before its maturity at a discount and pays immediate funds to the holder. - Long-Term Debt Instruments - Definition: - Long-term debt instruments have a tenure of one year or more, issued by governments or companies to raise funds for extended periods. - Features: - Used for large-scale projects, infrastructure, or corporate financing, with varying risk and return profiles based on the issuer and instrument type. - Colonial-Era Government Bonds: - Definition: - Historical government borrowing instruments used during colonial times. - Includes: - Coupon Bonds: - Coupon bonds are debt instruments that provide periodic interest payments to the holder and repay the face value at maturity. - Zero Coupon Bonds/Zero Interest Debentures (ZID): - Zero coupon bonds are debt instruments issued at a discount that pay no periodic interest and repay the full face value at maturity.” - Bearer Bonds: - Bearer bonds are debt instruments that are payable to the person who holds the physical bond certificate, without any registered ownership record. - Modern Government Long-Term Debt: - Definition: - Instruments issued by modern governments to finance deficits or development, known as gilt-edged securities due to assured repayment. - Includes: - Government Securities (G-Secs), Dated Securities, Sovereign Bonds. - Government small savings schemes Kisan Vikas Patra, etc - Low risk, hence lower interest rates compared to corporate bonds. - Global Credit Rating Agencies (e.g., Fitch, Moody’s, Standard & Poor’s) rate sovereign bonds; India’s rating is ~BBB (lowest investment grade) vs. AAA for US Treasury Bonds. - Criticism: - Agencies (Fitch, Moody’s, S&P) allegedly favor US/EU bonds, under-rating India, China, and Russia despite economic growth. - Global Bond Indices: - Definition: - Indices tracking bond market performance, enhancing investor confidence and liquidity. - Includes: -

- Benefits: - Attracts foreign investors, improves secondary market liquidity. - Special Purpose Bonds by Government/RBI: - Definition: - Bonds issued for specific economic or social objectives, often with unique features. - Includes: - Inflation-Indexed Bonds (IIBs): - Inflation-Indexed Bonds are government bonds whose returns rise with inflation, so the investor earns a positive real interest rate. - Launched in 1997, 2004, 2013, 2018 to provide positive real interest rate (nominal interest minus inflation). -

- IINSS-C: - Inflation-Indexed National Saving Securities - Cumulative. - In deflation (inflation < 0%), RBI guarantees minimum interest rate. - Capital Indexed Bonds - Capital Indexed Bonds are special bonds where the amount you invested, the principal, does not stay fixed. It is automatically increased or adjusted according to inflation. Because of this, both your invested amount and the interest you earn are protected from losing value when prices rise. - Issued by the Government of India, managed by the Reserve Bank of India. - How the increase actually works: - You invest ₹1000 in a capital indexed bond. - The bond is linked to an inflation index (like CPI). - Suppose inflation rises 10% over the year. - The bond contract says: principal will be adjusted by inflation. - So on paper: - Original principal = ₹1000 - Inflation adjustment = +10% - Adjusted principal = ₹1100 - Capital Indexed Bonds (CIB) vs Inflation Indexed Bonds (IIB) -
Feature CIB – 1997 CIB – 2004 IIB – 2013 IINSS-C – 2013
Principal protected against inflation? Yes Yes Yes Yes
Interest protected against inflation? No Yes Yes Yes
Inflation Index Used WPI (Wholesale Price Index) WPI CPI (Consumer Price Index) CPI
- Utility: - These bonds are useful for investors looking for inflation-hedged returns. - Also reduce government borrowing costs when inflation is stable. - FAQ: - CPI vs WPI - CPI includes prices of goods and services consumed by households (more accurate reflection of real inflation). - WPI includes wholesale prices — less reflective of end-user inflation. - What happens in case of deflation (inflation < 0%)? - Investor does not lose money. - RBI guarantees a minimum fixed interest rate, even if inflation becomes negative. - Sovereign Gold Bonds (SGBs): - Issued from 2015, denominated in gold grams, repay equivalent to prevailing gold prices after 8 years. - Annual interest: - 2.5–2.75%. - Eligible investors: - Indian residents, HUFs, trusts, universities, charitable institutions. - Purchase limits: - 1 gram to 20 kg, based on investor category. - Benefit: - Reduces physical gold imports, shifts savings to financial assets (Ref: Pillar#3A). - Sovereign Green Bonds: - Sold via RBI’s E-Kuber platform, funds to Consolidated Fund of India (CFI) for green projects (Ref: Pillar#2A). - Uses: - Renewable energy, energy efficiency, clean transport, water/waste management, pollution control, green buildings. - Exclusions: - Nuclear power, large hydropower (>25 MW), alcohol, weapons, tobacco, gaming, palm oil. - Reviewed by Finance Ministry’s Green Finance Working Committee (GFWC). - Greenium: - Investors accept lower interest (e.g., 2.5–2.7% vs. 3%) for environmental impact. - Long-Term Debt Instruments by Companies: - Definition: - Bonds or debentures issued by companies for long-term financing. - Includes: - Bonds (British term) vs. Debentures (American term): - No significant difference. - Junk Bonds: - High default risk (e.g., IL&FS, BB to D rating), requiring higher interest rates. - Redeemable Bonds: - Pay interest and principal on maturity. - Irredeemable Bonds: - Pay only interest, often by PSBs for Basel-III (Ref: 12.19, AT1 Bonds), with redemption option after 5–10 years. - Secured: - Backed by assets - Unsecured: - No collateral - Non-Convertible Debentures (NCDs): - Cannot convert to shares. - Hybrid Instruments: - Optionally Fully Convertible Debentures (OFCDs): - Convertible to shares, used in mezzanine financing by venture capitalists. - Foreign Currency Convertible Bonds (FCCBs): - Issued in foreign currency (e.g., Tata’s $1 billion FCCB in 2010), convertible to shares at a pre-set exchange rate, lower interest than NCDs. - Contingent Convertible Bonds (CoCos)/Enhanced Capital Notes (ECNs): - Hybrid of AT1 Bonds and OFCDs; convert to shares or write off principal/interest under specific conditions. - Bonds by Issuer (ULBs and Global): - Urban Local Bodies (ULBs): -

- Global Issuers: - BRICS Bonds: - Issued by New Development Bank (2014, HQ: Shanghai) in US dollars for infrastructure. - World Bank Bond-i (2018): - First blockchain-based bond, 2-year tenure, ~2% interest, in Australian dollars (Kangaroo Bonds). - Evergrande Crisis: - Chinese developer’s bond repayment issues caused market panic (not critical for UPSC). - Masala Bonds - Definition: - Rupee-denominated bonds issued outside India to raise funds for Indian companies. - Buyers bear the currency risk (since repayment is in ₹, not in foreign currency). - Helps avoid foreign exchange rate fluctuation risk for Indian issuer. - Named “Masala” to reflect Indian culture, just as “Samurai Bonds” (Japan), “Dim-sum Bonds” (China), etc. - Purpose: - Designed to help Indian public and private sector companies raise funds internationally. - International Finance Corporation (IFC), a sister agency of the World Bank, pioneered them to support Indian entities. - RBI Guidelines: - In 2015, RBI allowed Indian entities to issue Masala Bonds abroad. - Key Issuers & Events: - 2017: - NHAI (National Highways Authority of India) issued Masala Bonds at London Stock Exchange to raise funds for Indian highway projects. - 2019: - Kerala became the first Indian state to issue Masala Bonds. - Kerala Infrastructure Investment Fund Board (KIIFB) issued the bond at London Stock Exchange. - Details: - Denomination: - ₹ (rupee-denominated) - Total Size: - ₹2,150 crore (₹21.5 billion) - Tenure: - 5 years - Rating: - KIIFB had BB rating (below investment grade), so it offered higher interest rate. - Coupon Rate: - ~9.7% - Other Notes: - Masala bonds are often issued by lower-rated issuers because foreign investors demand higher yield for INR risk. - Because of currency risk for investors, these bonds offer attractive interest rates. -

- Miscellaneous Bond Types: - Market-Linked Debentures/Bonds: - These are bonds where the interest payout is linked to performance of a specified market benchmark. - Examples of linked benchmarks include SENSEX, G-Sec yields, gold price, Nifty50, etc. - The principal is typically protected, but returns depend on market movement. - Suitable for investors seeking market exposure with limited downside risk. - Covered in detail under Pillar#1C2. - Strip Bonds (Zero Coupon Bonds): - Strip Bonds (STRIPS) are zero-coupon securities created by separating the interest (coupon) and principal components of an existing government bond and trading each component independently. - STRIP stands for: - Separate Trading of Registered Interest and Principal of Securities. - Example: - A 10-year bond with annual ₹100 interest can be stripped into 10 zero-coupon instruments (for each ₹100 coupon) + 1 zero-coupon instrument for the principal. - These stripped components are sold at deep discount and redeemed at face value. - No periodic interest is paid — investor earns through price appreciation. - Useful for: - Long-term investors wanting fixed maturity values. - Institutions managing predictable liabilities (e.g., pension funds). - Issued/created by: - RBI or government-designated institutions in India. - STRIPS are common in US Treasury markets. - Special Purpose Bonds: - These are debt instruments issued for targeted purposes (infrastructure, environment, catastrophe relief, etc.). - Surety Bonds: - Introduced in Budget 2022. - Alternative to traditional bank guarantees. - Used in construction/infra projects. - Regulated by IRDAI (Insurance Regulator). - Sovereign Green Bonds: - Issued to fund environment-friendly public sector projects. - India’s first Sovereign Green Bond issued in 2023. - Previous issuers include: - World Bank (2007) - Yes Bank (2015) - BRICS-NDB (2016) - IREDA (Masala Green Bond, 2018) - PFC (Euro Green Bond, 2021) - Blue Bonds: - Sub-type of Green Bonds. - Focused specifically on marine conservation and sustainable fisheries. - Example: - Seychelles (2018) issued world’s first sovereign Blue Bond. - ESG Bonds: - Stands for Environmental, Social, and Governance Bonds. - Issued by companies with strong ESG credentials. - Gain popularity among ethical/sustainable investors. - May be green, social, or sustainability-linked. - Catastrophe Bonds (CAT Bonds): - High-yield bonds issued by insurance companies. - Used to transfer risk from insurer to investors. - If a specified catastrophe (earthquake, cyclone, etc.) occurs, investor loses principal. - Otherwise, they earn high returns. - Example: - Used heavily in US hurricane insurance markets. - Oil Bonds: - Special bonds issued by Government of India to Oil Marketing Companies (OMCs). - Used to compensate them for selling petrol/diesel at subsidized prices. - Interest-bearing, but deferred cash flow. - Explained further in Pillar#2 under petroleum pricing. - Consol Bonds: - Short for "Consolidated Bonds". - Perpetual (no maturity date), irredeemable. - Government pays fixed interest indefinitely. - Proposed for India post-COVID to raise long-term cheap funds. - Historically used: - USA (1870s for Civil War debt) - UK (1917, War Bonds consolidated into Consols) - Elephant Bonds: - Proposed by Surjit S. Bhalla Committee (2019). - 25-year long-term bond scheme. - They were designed to absorb very large volumes of black money in one shot - Voluntary disclosure scheme: - Black​ money holders to declare and invest in these bonds. - Proceeds earmarked for infrastructure development. - Social Impact Bonds (SIBs): - Outcome-based investment instruments. - Private investors fund a social program. - Government repays principal + return only if pre-agreed outcomes are achieved. - Examples: - 2019: - SIDBI Women’s Livelihood Bonds (₹300 crore, 3%, 5 years) - 2021: - NSDC Skill Impact Bond ($14.4 million for skill training) - 2023: - NABARD Social Bonds (7.##%, 5 years, for Jal Jeevan Mission) - Sukuk Bonds: - Sharia-compliant bonds (Islamic finance). - Do not pay interest (haram); instead, structured around asset ownership/profit sharing. - Rare in India; not core for UPSC but relevant internationally. - Bhutan Nation Building Bonds: - 2024–25: - Bhutan raised $100 million via 10-year bonds. - Purpose: - Funding for Gelephu Mindfulness City near Indian border. - India closely observes due to geopolitical implications. - Zero Coupon Zero Principal (ZCZP) Bonds: - No periodic interest. - No repayment of principal either. - Acts as philanthropic/donation instruments issued by NGOs/NPOs. - Listed on NSE Social Stock Exchange (SSE). - 2023: - “Unnati” NGO issued ZCZP bonds for youth skill development. - Buffer Funds for Bond Repayment: - Debenture Redemption Reserve (DRR): - Companies issuing bonds create a buffer fund from profits to repay principal/interest, mandated by Companies Act. -

Other Companies25% buffer10% buffer
- 2019: Rules relaxed for ease of doing business; banks, NBFCs, listed companies exempted. - Consolidated Sinking Fund (CSF): - State governments’ fund for loan repayment; no clear data on Union government equivalent. - Electoral Bonds: - Definition: - Introduced in Budget 2017, issued by SBI in denominations of ₹1,000 to ₹1 crore, for political funding. - Features: - Purchase Window: - 10 days each quarter (Jan, Apr, Jul, Oct); 30 extra days in Lok Sabha election years. - Eligibility: - Indian citizens/companies, KYC-compliant, non-anonymous. - Donation: - Only to political parties under Representation of People Act, 1951, with ≥1% votes in recent elections. - Validity: - 15 days, interest-free, bearer instrument, non-transferable. - Benefits: - Enhances transparency, reduces black​ money, ensures donor confidentiality. - Supreme Court Ban (Feb 2024): - Ruled unconstitutional for promoting corruption, violating RPA, Companies Act, Income Tax Act, and voters’ right to information (Article 19(1)(a)). - Donor privacy secondary to voters’ right to know. - SBI ordered to disclose 2019–24 donor/recipient data to Election Commission, publicized by March 2024. - Companies Act Amendments (2017): - Removed 7.5% profit cap on donations, disclosure of recipient names; ruled unconstitutional in 2024. - Electoral Trusts (2013): - Allow anonymous donations via trusts (e.g., Prudent Electoral Trust); not banned as of 2024. - Interest Rate Benchmarks (LIBOR, MIBOR, MIFOR, SORR) - Definition: - Benchmarks used for pricing loans, bonds, and derivatives. - Used in floating rate instruments to determine interest payments. - Includes: - LIBOR (London Interbank Offered Rate): - Average rate at which major global banks lend to one another in London. - Once widely used globally; now phased out due to manipulation controversy. - MIBOR (Mumbai Interbank Offered Rate): - Indian equivalent of LIBOR. - Daily reference rate based on unsecured lending between Indian banks. - MIFOR (Mumbai Interbank Forward Outright Rate): - Combines USD LIBOR and forward premium of USD-INR. - Previously used for hedging external commercial borrowings (now less relevant). - SORR (Secured Overnight Rupee Rate): - Proposed by RBI’s Ramanathan Subramanian Committee (2024) as a replacement for MIBOR. - Based on secured overnight call rates. - Calculated by Financial Benchmarks India Limited (FBIL). - Pillar 1C2: Share Market and SEBI Regulations - Definition: - The share market is a regulated platform where securities like equity shares, bonds, derivatives, and other financial instruments are traded, enabling companies to raise capital and investors to gain ownership or returns. The Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) oversees these markets to ensure transparency, investor protection, and financial stability. - Context: - India’s share market, primarily operated through the Bombay Stock Exchange (BSE) and National Stock Exchange (NSE), is a cornerstone of the economy, facilitating capital formation, wealth creation, and economic growth, with SEBI ensuring alignment with global standards. - BSE is Asia's oldest stock exchange with more listed companies, while NSE is newer but more liquid and preferred for derivatives trading. - India has both BSE and NSE to promote competition, improve efficiency, provide wider access to investors, and avoid monopoly in stock trading infrastructure. - Significance for India: - SEBI’s regulatory framework fosters investor confidence, curbs malpractices, and supports initiatives like Make in India and financial inclusion, positioning India as the 4th-largest equity market globally by January 2024 (ES25). - Equity Instruments - Definition: - Equity instruments represent ownership in a company, entitling holders to a share of profits (dividends) and residual assets during liquidation, primarily in the form of shares. - Characteristics: - Ownership: - Equity holders are proprietors with voting rights on corporate decisions (e.g., electing board members). - Dividends: - A dividend is the money a company pays to its shareholders out of its profits. - Receive dividends if the company earns profits, though not guaranteed. - Liquidation: - Last claim on assets after creditors and preference shareholders. - Types of Equity Investments: - Private Equity (PE): - Investments in unlisted companies by wealthy individuals or firms for growth or restructuring (e.g., PE funding in Byju’s). - Venture Capital (VC): - A subtype of PE focusing on early-stage startups with high growth potential (e.g., Ratan Tata’s investment in Urban Ladder). - Angel Investment: - A type of direct equity investment made by wealthy individuals (Angel Investors) into early-stage startups. - Example: - Anupam Mittal investing in a tech startup. - Angel Investors: - High-net-worth individuals investing in startups for profit or passion, often in seed stages (e.g., angel funding in Ola). - Corporate Strategic Investment: - A type of direct equity investment made by corporations into startups or other companies. - Purpose: - To gain technology, market access, or explore future buyouts. - Investor: - The corporation (called a Corporate Strategic Investor). - Example: - Google investing in Indian startups for strategic synergies. - Share Types: - Based on Risk/Quality: - Blue-Chip Shares: - Named after blue poker chips, which are the most valuable. - Low-risk, high-quality shares of established companies (e.g., TCS, Reliance). - Growth Shares: - High-risk shares of companies with growth potential (e.g., tech startups). - Value Shares: - Undervalued shares with strong fundamentals (e.g., underperforming PSUs). - Penny Stocks: - High-risk, low-priced shares of small companies, prone to speculation. - The term "penny stocks" originated in the United States, where such stocks were typically priced below one dollar (i.e., one penny)—hence the name. - Based on Floating vs Restricted Stocks: - Floating Stocks: - Shares available for public trading, enhancing market liquidity. - Restricted Stocks: - Shares held by insiders (e.g., promoters) with trading restrictions, often under SEBI-mandated lock-in periods post-IPO. - Based on Preference - Preference Shares: - Preference shares are shares that provide preferential rights over equity shares regarding dividend payment and return of capital, usually with fixed dividends and limited voting rights. - Ordinary Shares (Equity shares): - Equity shares are ownership shares of a company that carry voting rights and variable dividends, with residual claim over profits and assets. - Comparison: -
Feature Preference Shares Ordinary Shares
Dividend Fixed rate (e.g., 10% on ₹100 = ₹10 annually) Variable, based on remaining profits
Liquidation Priority Paid before ordinary shareholders Paid after creditors and preference shareholders
Voting Rights Generally none, except in specific cases Full voting rights
Convertibility Convertible to ordinary shares if specified Non-convertible
Issuance Optional Mandatory for registered companies
Risk Lower, due to fixed returns Higher, due to variable returns
- Shares for Employees and VIPs - Definition: - Special shares or options issued to employees and key personnel to incentivize performance and retention. - Types: - Sweat Equity: - Shares issued by a company to employees or directors in exchange for non-cash contributions like know-how, IP, or value addition. - Employee Stock Option Plan (ESOP): - Allows employees to buy shares at a predetermined price (e.g., Infosys ESOPs). - Equity Warrants: - Options to buy shares at a future date, often issued to VIPs or strategic investors. - Significance: - Aligns employee and company goals, common in startups; SEBI regulates ESOP disclosures. - Accounting Account Types - "Current account," "savings account," etc., belong to the banking domain, not accounting. - Types of Accounts (As per traditional accounting classification): - Personal Account - Definition: - Accounts related to individuals, firms, companies, and other legal entities. - Example: - You pay ₹5,000 to Raj. - Raj (receiver) = Debit - Cash (your asset going out) = Credit - Subtypes: - a. Natural Person Account: - Refers to accounts of actual human beings. - Example: - Mark Zuckerberg's personal account. - b. Artificial Legal Person Account: - Refers to organizations or entities treated as persons in the eyes of law. - Example: - Meta (Facebook), Google Inc., etc. - Real Account - Definition: - Accounts of tangible and intangible assets (non-living things) owned by the business. - Example: - You buy machinery for ₹1,00,000 - Machinery (comes in) = Debit - Cash (goes out) = Credit - Subtypes: - a. Tangible Real Accounts: - Tangible means touchable, indirectly saying physical/real - Physical, touchable assets. - Examples: - Building, Land, Furniture, Machinery, Vehicles. - b. Intangible Real Accounts: - Non-physical assets with financial value. - Examples: - Goodwill, Patents, Copyrights, Trademarks, Software. - Nominal Account - Definition: - Accounts related to business incomes, gains, expenses, and losses. - Example: - You pay ₹10,000 salary. - Salary (expense) = Debit - Cash (goes out) = Credit - You earn ₹15,000 interest. - Cash (comes in) = Debit - Interest income = Credit - Subtypes: - a. Income or Gain Accounts: - Revenue generated by business operations. - Examples: - Sales revenue, Interest income, Dividend income. - b. Expense or Loss Accounts: - Costs incurred to earn revenue. - Examples: - Salary, Rent, Raw Material Cost, Utilities. - Golden Rules: - Personal Account → Debit the receiver, Credit the giver. - Real Account → Debit what comes in, Credit what goes out. - Nominal Account → Debit all expenses & losses, Credit all incomes & gains. - Goodwill – While Buying Another Company or Brand - Concept: - Goodwill is an intangible asset that arises when one company acquires another for a price higher than the fair value of its net identifiable assets. - It reflects the premium paid for the target's brand reputation, customer base, employee loyalty, technology, etc. - Example: -
Item Price (in million $)
Value of Whitehat Jr.'s tangible + intangible assets 250
Amount Byju’s paid to acquire Whitehat Jr. 300
Goodwill (Excess amount paid) 50
- Interpretation: - Byju’s paid $50 million over and above the fair value of Whitehat Jr.’s assets. - This $50 million is recorded as Goodwill in Byju’s balance sheet. - It signifies Whitehat’s: - Brand equity - Market - Valuation of Companies - Definition: - Valuation is the process of determining the current worth of a company using quantitative metrics. - It is essential for investment decisions, IPO pricing, M&A (mergers and acquisitions), regulatory scrutiny, and VC funding rounds. - Classification of companies based on company market capitalization (in India): -
