- Methods of Valuation: - Price-to-Earnings (P/E) Ratio: - Formula: - \( \text{P/E Ratio} = \frac{\text{Market Price per Share}}{\text{Earnings per Share (EPS)}} \) - Indicates investor expectations about the company’s future earnings. - Higher P/E may signal growth optimism or overvaluation. - Discounted Cash Flow (DCF) Method: - Projects the company’s future cash flows and discounts them to present value using a discount rate (often WACC). - Assumes time value of money (₹100 today > ₹100 next year). - Used in fundamental analysis and for startup/investor negotiations. - Book Value Method: - Book Value = Total assets - Total liabilities (as per balance sheet). - Per share Book Value = Book Value ÷ Total outstanding shares. - Shows liquidation value; often used for manufacturing and heavy asset-based companies. - Market-Based Valuation: - Relies on current market price of shares (i.e., market capitalization). - Reflects real-time investor sentiment and supply-demand forces. - May deviate from intrinsic value due to speculation or herd behavior. - Startup Valuation (Unicorn Valuation): - Unicorns are private startups with a valuation of $1 billion or more. - Valuation is based not on revenue but on: - Future potential, user base, scalability, disruptive models. - Comparable company analysis, VC negotiation, market sentiment. - Indian unicorns: - Byju’s, Paytm, Flipkart, etc. - Market Froth & Asset Bubbles: - "Market froth" occurs when stock prices are driven by speculation rather than fundamentals. - Often seen in small or mid-cap stocks due to retail investor frenzy. - SEBI (ES25) warned of possible correction due to high valuations and irrational exuberance. - Leads to risk of "asset bubble" where prices inflate beyond sustainable levels and then crash. - Case Study: Tulip Mania (Netherlands, 1634–1637) - Definition: - One of the earliest recorded speculative bubbles in history. - Tulip bulb prices rose exponentially (e.g., Semper Augustus bulb sold for 10,000 guilders). - Collapse: - Prices crashed suddenly in 1637, leading to panic selling and economic fallout. - Mechanism: - Futures contracts, herd behavior, greed. - Relevance: - Analogous to modern bubbles (e.g., dot-com, real estate, Bitcoin). - Used by economists and regulators as a cautionary tale. - Indian Context: - SEBI uses "circuit breakers" to control extreme volatility and prevent such bubbles in equity markets. - What are Circuit Breakers? - They are temporary halts (pauses) in trading on stock exchanges when a stock index (like Nifty or Sensex) moves beyond a specified limit in a single day. - The goal is to give investors time to assess information, avoid panic selling or buying, and restore market stability. - Volatility & ViX Index: - Volatility: - Measure of the degree of variation in stock prices over time. - High volatility = high risk and uncertainty. - India VIX (Volatility Index): - Published by NSE. - Indicates expected volatility over the next 30 days. - Helps investors and SEBI gauge market mood and apply regulatory controls like circuit breakers. - Other Valuation & Financial Metrics: - EPS (Earnings Per Share): - Formula: - \( \text{EPS} = \frac{\text{Net Profit}}{\text{Total Number of Equity Shares}} \) - Key metric for profitability per share. - Leverage Ratio: - Indicates how much of a company’s operations are financed through debt. - High leverage = high risk in downturns. - Debt-to-Equity Ratio: - Formula: - \( \text{Debt-to-Equity Ratio} = \frac{\text{Total Liabilities}}{\text{Shareholders' Equity}} \) - Measures financial risk and capital structure stability. - ROCE (Return on Capital Employed): - Formula: - \( \text{ROCE} = \frac{\text{EBIT}}{\text{Total Assets} - \text{Current Liabilities}} \) - Shows how efficiently a company generates profit from capital employed. - Net Worth: - Total assets minus total liabilities. - Represents shareholder equity or the company’s intrinsic value. - EBITDA: - Earnings Before Interest, Taxes, Depreciation, and Amortization. - Proxy for operational profitability without accounting distortions. - Depreciation: - Gradual reduction in value of tangible assets (e.g., machinery) due to wear and tear. - Amortization: - Similar to depreciation, but applies to intangible assets like goodwill, patents, etc. - Significance of Valuation Transparency: - SEBI mandates listed companies to disclose valuation-related data in financial reports. - Enables investors to assess company fundamentals and avoid investing based on hype. - Promotes trust, accountability, and long-term financial stability in capital markets. - Methods of Issuing Shares - Definition: - The issuance of shares is a primary method for companies to raise long-term capital from the public or institutional investors. - It signifies the process through which ownership in a company is distributed in exchange for funds. - Regulated by SEBI under the Companies Act and the SEBI (Issue of Capital and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, ensuring transparency, investor protection, and orderly development of the capital market. - A share represents a fractional ownership in a company. Shareholders gain specific rights such as: - Voting rights in shareholder meetings (primarily for equity holders) - Right to receive declared dividends - Claim on residual assets upon liquidation (after creditors and preference shareholders) - Methods of Equity Fundraising and Share Transactions: - IPO (Initial Public Offering): - First-time public issue of shares by an unlisted company. - Purpose: - To get listed on a stock exchange and raise capital. - Used for business expansion, loan repayment, or working capital. - Example: - LIC IPO in 2022. - FPO (Follow-on Public Offering): - Additional share issue by a company already listed on the stock exchange. - Purpose: - To raise further capital from public investors. - Example: - ONGC FPO. - QIP (Qualified Institutional Placement): - Private issue of shares to Qualified Institutional Buyers (QIBs). - Benefits: - Faster execution, limited paperwork, avoids retail market volatility. - Used by large-cap firms for quicker fundraising. - OFS (Offer for Sale): - Promoters or large shareholders (e.g., government) sell their stake via the exchange. - Common in PSU disinvestment. - Proceeds go to the seller, not the company. - Share Pledging: - Promoters keep their shares as collateral to secure loans. - High promoter pledging = red flag for investors (signals liquidity stress or over-leverage). - Risk: - If promoters default, lenders can sell pledged shares, crashing prices. - Open Offer: - An open offer is a mandatory public offer made by an acquirer to purchase at least 26% additional shares from public shareholders after crossing the prescribed ownership or control threshold in a listed company. - Purpose: - To ensure fair exit opportunity to existing investors and prevent coercive takeovers. - Trigger: - When an acquirer acquires substantial control (≥25% stake). - Governed by SEBI Takeover Code. - Equity Dilution: - Occurs when a company issues additional shares. - Impact: - Reduces existing shareholders' percentage ownership. - Happens via: - Rights issue (pro-rata offer to existing shareholders) - ESOPs (Employee Stock Option Plans) - Preferential allotments to selected investors. - Bonus Shares: - Free shares issued to existing shareholders by converting reserves. - No funds raised; just internal accounting adjustment. - Signals strong financial position and increases share liquidity. - Stock Split (Share Splitting): - Each existing share is divided into multiple shares. - Face value reduces; total capital remains unchanged. - Purpose: - Increase affordability, boost trading activity. - Example: - 1 share of ₹100 split into 2 shares of ₹50 each. - Share Buyback: - Company repurchases its own shares from the market. - Reduces floating supply and boosts earnings per share (EPS). - Indicates undervaluation or excess cash. - Can be done via: - Tender offer - Open market purchases. - SPAC (Special Purpose Acquisition Company): - Shell company listed via IPO solely to acquire a private firm. - Enables reverse merger, bypassing traditional IPO route for target. - Globally trending; India exploring regulations. - Direct Listing: - Company lists its shares directly on the exchange without raising fresh capital. - No underwriters, no IPO process. - Useful for already well-funded or popular companies. - India enabled direct overseas listing (2023) via IFSC at GIFT City. - ICO (Initial Coin Offering): - Fundraising via issuing crypto​currency tokens. - Used by blockchain startups. - Highly speculative and unregulated in India. - Not recognised under Indian securities laws. - Other Relevant Terms: - Underwriters / Lead Managers: - Investment banks or merchant bankers. - Ensure successful IPO subscription. - Responsibilities: - Pricing, marketing, compliance, due diligence. - Grey Market: - Informal trading before IPO listing. - Includes GMP (Grey Market Premium): - Difference between expected market price and issue price. - Unofficial but gives early sentiment signals. - Share-Swapping: - In M&A, shareholders of target company are given shares of acquiring firm instead of cash. - Used to avoid large cash payouts and to align ownership. - De-listing: - Company voluntarily or compulsorily removed from exchange. - Reasons: - Restructuring, promoter buyout, regulatory issues. - Shareholders offered exit at SEBI-calculated price (fair value based). - Market capitalization - - Market capitalization is just the total market value of a company based on its share price. - Market capitalization = current share price × total number of outstanding shares - Example - If a company has 1 crore shares and each share trades at ₹50, then market cap = ₹50 crore. - Free float market capitalization - Free float market capitalization is market cap, but only counting the shares that are actually available for public trading. Not the locked-up ones sitting with promoters, governments, or insiders like family silver. - Free float market capitalization = share price × number of shares available for public trading - Example - For a company having: - Total shares = 10 crore - Promoter + locked shares = 4 crore - Public (free float) shares = 6 crore - Share price = ₹100 - Then: - Free float market cap = ₹100 × 6 crore = ₹600 crore - Significance: - Ensures resource mobilization for private and public sector growth. - Broadens public participation in corporate ownership. - SEBI plays a crucial role in ensuring transparency, efficiency, and investor protection. - Recent reforms like UPI-ASBA integration, faster T+1 settlement, and GIFT IFSC framework aim to make Indian capital markets globally competitive. - Stock Exchanges and Secondary Market - Definition: - Stock exchanges, also known as share markets or bourses, are platforms where securities like shares and bonds, initially issued in the primary market through Initial Public Offerings (IPOs), are bought and sold in the secondary market. These exchanges facilitate the trading of financial assets, contributing to liquidity and economic growth. - Features: - Historical Context: - World’s Oldest: - Amsterdam Stock Exchange, Netherlands (1602). - Asia’s Oldest: - Bombay Stock Exchange (BSE, 1875). - India’s Stock - Exchange Chronology: - BSE (1875), Ahmedabad, Kolkata, National Stock Exchange (NSE, early 1990s). - Trading Platforms: - Stock exchanges use electronic platforms for efficient trading, similar to Core Banking Solutions for banks. - Examples include: - BOLT: - BSE’s On-Line Trading System. - NEAT: - National Exchange for Automated Trading (NSE). - Communication occurs via VSAT (Very Small Aperture Terminal) satellite technology. - Separate Platforms: - Stock exchanges offer distinct platforms for trading debt instruments (e.g., bonds) and equities (e.g., shares). - Social Stock Exchange: - Definition: - A specialized segment of the stock exchange for trading securities (shares, bonds, mutual funds) issued by organizations focused on social objectives like eradicating hunger, malnutrition, poverty, promoting gender equality, LGBTQIA+ welfare, rural sports, slum area development, and affordable housing. - Features: - Background: - Announced in Budget 2019, regulated by SEBI to help social enterprises and voluntary organizations raise capital. - SEBI formed the Ishaat Hussain panel and later the Harsh Bhanwala panel to provide recommendations (details not critical for UPSC). - Listing Requirements: - Non-Profit Organizations (NPOs) and Non-Governmental Organizations (NGOs) must disclose past and present audit reports and have at least three years of operational experience to list on the Social Stock Exchange (SSE). - Performance: - As per Economic Survey 2024, 51 NPOs and NGOs raised ₹12 crore through the SSE for social projects in education, livelihood generation, and skill development. - Zero-Coupon, Zero-Principal Instrument: - These are bonds issued by NPOs/NGOs for social causes, offering neither interest nor principal repayment, akin to donations. - Listed on the NSE Social Stock Exchange, they enhance transparency by requiring issuers to disclose financial details to SEBI and investors. - Example: - In 2023, Unnati (an NPO) issued Zero-Coupon, Zero-Principal (ZCZP) bonds for youth skill development. - Secondary Market significance: - Cost Efficiency: - Reduces transaction costs by eliminating the need to manually search for buyers or sellers, streamlining trading processes. - Liquidity: - Provides liquidity to financial assets, enabling investors to convert securities into cash quickly. - Price Discovery: - Facilitates transparent pricing of shares and bonds through market-driven supply and demand. - Economic Growth: - Funds from selling securities in the secondary market can be reinvested in new IPOs in the primary market, supporting capital formation and economic growth. - DEMAT Account and Depositories: - Definition: - A DEMAT (Dematerialized) account holds securities like shares and bonds in electronic form, managed by depositories to facilitate online trading. Depositories are organizations regulated by SEBI under the Depositories Act, 1996. - Features: - Purpose: - Eliminates risks associated with physical securities (e.g., theft, forgery, fire), enabling faster and safer transactions. - Depositories: - Central Depository Services Limited (CDSL): - Owned by BSE, HDFC, Canara Bank, etc. - National Securities Depository Limited (NSDL): - Owned by SBI, IDBI, UTI, NSE, etc.; also holds an RBI license to operate as a Payment Bank. - Rematerialization: - Depositories can convert electronic securities back to physical form upon client request. - DEMAT Account: - Investors open DEMAT accounts through Depository Participants (DPs), which can be banks or NBFCs. - Market Reach: - As of March 2024, India had 9.2 crore registered investors, with nearly 20% of households channeling savings into financial markets (Economic Survey 2024). - ASBA Reforms in IPO/Primary Market: - Background: - In 2005, the Rupal Panchal scam involved fake DEMAT accounts to manipulate IPO share allocations, followed by selling at higher prices in the secondary market. - SEBI Reforms: - Made PAN Card mandatory for DEMAT accounts to prevent fraud. - Introduced ASBA (Application Supported by Blocked Amount), where funds are blocked in the investor’s bank account during IPO application but deducted only if shares are allotted. - ASBA Benefits: - Ensures only serious investors with sufficient funds apply. - Allows investors to earn bank interest on blocked funds until IPO allotment is complete. - UPI-ASBA in Secondary Market: - Definition: - A SEBI reform introduced in January 2024, integrating Unified Payments Interface (UPI) with ASBA for secondary market transactions, enhancing efficiency and accessibility. - Features: - Allows investors to use UPI for blocking funds in secondary market trades, similar to ASBA in IPOs, ensuring seamless transactions. - ISIN Number: - International Securities Identification Number (ISIN) is a unique 12-character alphanumeric code identifying securities. - Examples: - Reliance Industries Limited Shares (ISIN: INE002A01018), Infosys Shares (ISIN: INE009A01021). - CAS System: - Definition: - Consolidated Account Statement (CAS) is a monthly statement emailed by depositories (NSDL, CDSL) to investors, detailing transactions across all dematerialized securities (mutual funds, shares, bonds). - Features: - Alerts investors to unauthorized transactions. - Provides the total value of investments for tracking and planning. - Central Counter Parties (CCPs) - Definition: - Central Counterparties (CCPs) are financial institutions that act as intermediaries between buyers and sellers in financial markets, facilitating trades in securities such as Government Securities (G-Secs), Treasury Bills (T-Bills), shares, bonds, derivatives, forex, and commodities. - Features: - Role in Trades: - CCPs serve as the central counterparty to every trade through a process called novation. In novation, the CCP becomes the seller to the buyer and simultaneously the buyer to the seller, ensuring both parties fulfill their obligations. - Risk Mitigation: - If one party fails to honor the trade agreement (e.g., due to default), the CCP steps in to settle the transaction, preventing systemic failure in the financial market. - ESMA Controversy: - Indian CCPs, which handle international financial trades including in Europe, face challenges as the European Securities and Markets Authority (ESMA) plans to cancel their licenses or restrict their operations. Specific details are not critical for UPSC, but it’s sufficient to note that Indian CCPs encounter regulatory hurdles in Europe. - CCP Examples in India: - List of CCPs: - Clearing Corporation of India Limited (CCIL) - Indian Clearing Corporation Limited (ICCL) - NSE Clearing Limited (NSCCL) - Multi Commodity Exchange Clearing Corporation (MCXCCL) - India International Clearing Corporation (IFSC) - NSE IFSC Clearing Corporation Limited (NICCL) - Functions: - These CCPs clear and settle trades across various asset classes, ensuring stability and efficiency in India’s financial markets. - CCP Regulation in India: - Regulatory Framework: - CCPs are regulated by multiple authorities based on the type of transaction: -
MatterRegulatorLegal Framework
Money transfer between buyer and sellerReserve Bank of India (RBI)Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007
Foreign currency transactionsRBIForeign Exchange Management Act, 1999
Shares, bonds, commoditiesSecurities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI)SEBI Act, 1992; Securities Contract (Regulation) Act, 1956
- Coordination: - RBI and SEBI collaborate to ensure robust oversight, aligning CCP operations with monetary policy and market stability goals. - Investors - Definition: - Investors are individuals or entities who allocate capital to financial securities (e.g., shares, bonds) with the expectation of generating returns. They are classified based on buying capacity, behavior, trading style, and objectives, influencing market dynamics and monetary policy transmission. - Types: - Depending on Buying Capacity: - Qualified Institutional Buyers (QIBs): - Institutional investors with expertise and significant financial resources to make large investments in capital markets. - Examples: - Mutual Funds, Insurance Companies, Foreign Venture Capital Funds. - Regulated by SEBI with specific registration norms due to their market influence. - Anchor Investors: - A sub-type of QIBs offered shares before an Initial Public Offering (IPO) launch. - Their participation signals confidence, encouraging other investors to subscribe to the IPO. - Retail Investors: - Individual investors who are not QIBs, typically investing smaller amounts. - SEBI mandates quotas for QIBs, anchor investors, and retail investors in IPOs to ensure fair allocation. - Table: Investor Types by Buying Capacity: -
Investor TypeDescriptionExamples
Qualified Institutional Buyers (QIBs)Institutional investors with large financial capacityMutual Funds, Insurance Companies
Anchor InvestorsQIBs offered shares pre-IPOLarge Mutual Funds, FVCIs
Retail InvestorsIndividual investors with smaller investmentsGeneral public
- Depending on Buying Behavior: - Jobbers: - Full-time traders who buy and sell securities using their own capital, unlike brokers who use client funds. - Stag: - Investors who buy newly issued securities in the primary market (e.g., IPOs) and sell them in the secondary market for quick profits. - Stag (animal) is known for being alert, quick, and opportunistic - Bull: - Optimistic speculators who buy securities expecting price increases, aiming to sell at higher prices later. Their actions stimulate price rises, akin to a bull tossing its target upward. - Bear: - Pessimistic speculators who sell securities anticipating price declines, exerting downward pressure on prices, similar to a bear pressing its target downward. - Disposition Effect: - Definition: - A behavioral bias where investors sell securities at a profit quickly but hold onto losing investments longer, hoping for recovery, often leading to greater losses. - - - SEBI Observation: - SEBI used this term to describe Indian investors’ behavior in IPOs, where profitable shares are sold immediately, but loss-making shares are retained. - Style of Trading / Investing Based on Duration and Risk: - Margin Trading: - - Buying more shares than one can afford by borrowing from a broker. High risk due to potential debt if investments fail. - Day Trading / Intra-Day Trading: - - Buying and selling shares within a single trading day to profit from small price fluctuations, conducted online. - Swing Trading: - Holding shares for a few days or weeks to capitalize on short-term price movements. - Long-Term Investing: - Holding shares for over a year, focusing on long-term growth and stability. - Block Deal: - Large-value transactions (e.g., ₹5 crore or more), typically by institutional investors like Mutual Funds or Insurance Companies. - One big buyer, one big seller, large chunk of shares changes hands at once - Contra Trading / Investing: - Investing against market trends, e.g., buying undervalued securities (like cinema shares during a lockdown) expecting future gains. - Note: - Additional trading styles with animal/bird names exist (e.g., on Investopedia), but they offer low cost-benefit for UPSC exams. - Table: -
StyleDescriptionRisk LevelDuration
Margin TradingBorrowing to buy excess sharesHighShort-term
Day TradingTrading within a single dayHighIntra-day
Swing TradingHolding for days/weeksModerateShort-term
Long-Term InvestingHolding for over a yearLowLong-term
Block DealLarge-value institutional tradesLowShort-term
Contra TradingInvesting against market trendsHighVariable
- Trader Types - Retail Traders: - Individuals trading with personal capital for profit. - Example: - Solo investors with small amounts. - Mutual Fund Traders: - Manage client funds, earning commissions. - Examples: - Tata Mutual Fund, Birla Mutual Fund. - Proprietary Traders: - Companies trading with their own capital for profit, operating systematically with large resources. - Examples: - Jaypee Capital, Edelweiss Capital. - Table: -

- Stock Market Indices - Definition: - Stock market indices are weighted averages of the market performance of selected companies, reflecting the overall health of the stock market and economy. They serve as benchmarks for investors and influence monetary policy decisions. - Features: - SENSEX: - SENSEX (Sensitive Index) is calculated by the Bombay Stock Exchange (BSE) and represents a weighted average of the Free Float Market Capitalization (FFMC) of 30 leading companies across sectors such as finance, IT, energy, and FMCG. FFMC refers to the portion of a company's market capitalization that is available for trading by the general public, excluding shares held by promoters or the government. The SENSEX acts as a key barometer of the Indian equity market and uses the year 1978–79 as its base, with a base value of 100. - NIFTY: - NIFTY 50, calculated by the National Stock Exchange (NSE), is an index comprising 50 major companies that collectively represent 13 key sectors of the Indian economy. Like the SENSEX, it is based on Free Float Market Capitalization, providing a more realistic picture of market movements by excluding non-tradable shares. NIFTY serves as a benchmark for broader market performance and uses the year 1995 as its base, with a base value of 1000. - Nikkei - The Nikkei 225, maintained by Japan’s business daily Nihon Keizai Shimbun, is a price-weighted index tracking the performance of 225 prominent companies listed on the Tokyo Stock Exchange. Unlike SENSEX and NIFTY, the Nikkei gives more weight to stocks with higher prices, which may skew its representation. It covers major Japanese sectors such as automobiles, electronics, finance, and pharmaceuticals, and uses 1950 as its base year with an initial value of 176.21. - Note: - While SENSEX and NIFTY use the Free Float Market Capitalization method to ensure more accurate reflection of tradable market value, the Nikkei’s price-weighted method can sometimes overemphasize the impact of high-priced stocks, making it structurally different from Indian indices. - SENSEX Goes Up and Down When: - Factors Influencing SENSEX: -
Goes Up WhenGoes Down When
RBI’s soft/easy monetary policy (e.g., low repo rates, cheap loans, credit cards) → increased consumer spending → higher company profits → higher dividends → bullish marketTight monetary policy (e.g., high repo rates, reduced liquidity, see Pillar#1A2)
Peace, economic boom, political stability → bullish marketWar, recession, political instability → bearish market
Government hikes Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) limitsGovernment reduces FDI limits
Company events: mergers, acquisitions, new product launches, environmental clearancesNegative company events: CEO/MD arrests, FIRs, court fines, media-exposed scandals
- Explanation: - Bullish markets, driven by optimism, increase share prices, while bearish markets, driven by pessimism, depress prices. - Force Majeure: - Definition: - A French term meaning “superior force,” referring to unexpected external events (e.g., natural disasters, epidemics, wars, terror attacks) that prevent a party from fulfilling contractual obligations. - - Features: - Courts may exempt parties from penalties for failing to honor contracts due to Force Majeure events. - Example: - During the COVID-19 pandemic (2020), Singapore imposed entry restrictions on Chinese workers, causing labor shortages for construction firms. These firms invoked Force Majeure to delay project timelines without penalties. - Global Impact: - By October 2020, COVID-19 infected over 55 million people worldwide, disrupting financial and contractual obligations. - Relevance to Stock Markets: - Force Majeure events (e.g., pandemics, geopolitical crises) can depress stock indices like SENSEX due to market uncertainty. - Sharemarket: 2020-2025 - Overview: - The Indian share market from 2020 to 2025 marked a transformative phase, achieving global prominence as the fourth-largest equity market in 2024. Driven by domestic resilience, foreign portfolio investment (FPI) dynamics, and global market interlinkages, it influences monetary policy and investor sentiment, as highlighted by the Economic Survey 2025 (ES25). - India Becomes 4th-Largest Equity Market (2024-Jan): - Global Ranking: - In January 2024, India overtook Hong Kong to become the world’s fourth-largest equity market, with a combined stock exchange value of $4.33 trillion, trailing the US, China, and Japan (Hong Kong: $4.29 trillion). - Reasons for Rise: - Stricter regulations in China prompted foreign investors to redirect capital to India’s share market. - Stable political environment in India bolstered investor confidence. - Strong GDP growth, positioning India as a leading emerging market economy. - Foreign Portfolio Investment (FPI) Exit and Resilience (ES25): - FPI Exit Impact: -
YearFPI Exit AmountNIFTY Index Impact
March 2020$8 billion23% fall
October 2024$11 billion6.2% fall
- Resilience: - Despite higher FPI outflows in October 2024 ($11 billion) compared to March 2020 ($8 billion), the NIFTY index fell only 6.2% in 2024 versus 23% in 2020, as Indian retail investors and mutual funds absorbed the sell-off. - Domestic investors, including mutual funds, confidently purchased shares sold by FPIs, enhancing market stability. - Implication: - The Indian stock market has grown more resilient to external shocks due to increased domestic investor participation. - Potential US Share Market Crisis (ES25): - Concerns: - US company share prices (e.g., Apple, Tesla) rose by 20% annually in 2023 and 2024, but stagnant consumer income levels raise concerns about sustaining sales growth. - Complex investment products issued by US data centres, music companies, and solar panel manufacturers risk triggering a financial crisis similar to the 2007 Subprime Crisis. - Relation Between Indian and American Share Markets (ES25): - Historical Performance: - From 2014–2024, Indian markets (NIFTY) delivered an average annual return of 8.8%, but only 4.6% from April to December 2024, compared to the US NASDAQ’s 20% returns in 2023 and 2024. - Correlation Patterns (2000–2024): -
ScenarioFrequencyDetails
US market falls, Indian market falls21 out of 22 timesStrong correlation; Indian markets closely follow US downturns.
Indian market falls, US market falls13 out of 51 timesWeak correlation; US markets often rise when FPIs shift funds from India.
- Granger Causality: - ES25 applied Granger causality, a statistical tool, to assess whether past values of one market index predict another. It found that Indian markets (NIFTY 50) react strongly to US market (S&P 500) trends, but the reverse is less significant due to higher US investment in India. - Impact on Investors: - Many young Indian investors, entering the market post-COVID, lack experience with major corrections. A potential US market crash in 2025 could significantly affect their sentiments and spending behavior, impacting domestic consumption. - Market Theories - Overview: - Market theories help explain how financial markets behave, how securities are priced, and how investors make decisions. These theories form the foundation of investment strategy and financial regulation. Among the most important are the Efficient Market Hypothesis (EMH) and the Random Walk Theory, both of which emphasize that it's extremely difficult to consistently beat the market using available information or past data. - Efficient Market Hypothesis (EMH): - Definition: - The Efficient Market Hypothesis states that financial markets are so efficient in absorbing and reflecting all publicly available information that the prices of securities at any given time are fair and accurate. This means that no investor can consistently earn returns higher than average by using publicly known information or standard analysis techniques. - - Example: - Suppose a company reports higher-than-expected earnings. According to EMH, this information is immediately reflected in the stock price as soon as it's released, making it too late for an investor to act and profit abnormally. Even if you have read the earnings report just minutes after its release, others already acted, and the price has adjusted accordingly. - Key Points: - All relevant public information—such as financial statements, economic news, or political developments—is instantly factored into stock prices. - Neither technical analysis (studying chart patterns) nor fundamental analysis (studying company balance sheets) can give investors a consistent edge. - As a result, outperforming the market consistently is more about chance than skill. - Criticism: - Critics argue that markets are not always perfectly efficient. For example, during speculative bubbles—like the Dot-com Bubble or the 2008 Housing Crisis—prices can deviate widely from a company's actual value. Similarly, overreactions to news or irrational investor behavior can lead to mispricing, contradicting EMH. - Random Walk Theory: - Definition: - The Random Walk Theory claims that stock prices move in a completely unpredictable manner because they are influenced by new, unforeseen information that cannot be predicted by analyzing past data. Therefore, trying to forecast future prices using charts or trends is not reliable. - - Example: - Suppose a stock’s price increased for the past 10 days. According to the Random Walk Theory, there’s no logical reason to expect it to rise again tomorrow just based on that pattern. Tomorrow’s price will depend on new information—such as government policy changes or sudden global events—not on yesterday’s trend. - Key Points: - Price changes are random and not linked to previous movements. - No consistent pattern exists that investors can exploit to earn above-average returns. - Supports the EMH by reinforcing the idea that markets already reflect all known information. - Alpha, Beta, and Portfolio Analysis - Overview: - Alpha and Beta values are critical financial metrics used to assess investment performance and risk in share markets. Combined with fundamental and technical analysis, they guide investors in managing portfolios, which are collections of assets held in DEMAT accounts. These concepts influence monetary policy by shaping investor behavior and market stability. - Beta Value to Monitor Price Volatility or Risk of an Asset: - Definition: - Beta (β) is an indicator that measures the volatility or risk of an asset’s price relative to the overall stock market, typically benchmarked against an index like SENSEX. - - Values and Meaning: -
Beta ValueMeaningExamples
β > 1Asset moves faster than the market in both directions; high reward and high risk (e.g., if SENSEX rises/falls at 1x speed, asset moves at 1.5x speed)Tech, startup, small-cap companies
β < 1Asset moves slower than the market; low risk, low returnLarge-cap, stable companies
β = 1Asset moves parallel to the marketIndex-tracking stocks
β = 0Asset is uncorrelated to the market; value changes independentlyNon-financial assets like art (e.g., Mona Lisa painting)
β < 0Asset moves opposite to the market (e.g., if SENSEX rises, asset falls); not always true in practiceBonds, gold
- Alpha Value to Compare Performance of Mutual Funds: - Definition: - Alpha is an indicator used to evaluate a Mutual Fund or Hedge Fund manager’s performance by comparing its returns against a benchmark index like SENSEX. - Values and Meaning: -
Alpha ValueMeaningExample
Alpha > 0Fund outperforms the benchmark (e.g., if SENSEX returns 10%, a 2% Alpha fund returns 12%)HDFC Mutual Fund
Alpha = 0Fund performs the same as the benchmarkIndex-tracking funds
Alpha < 0Fund underperforms the benchmarkPoorly managed funds
- Alpha vs. Beta Similarity/Difference: - Comparison: -
TermUsage

- Note: - Detailed analysis beyond this comparison is not critical for UPSC, focusing on their primary roles as one-line indicators. - Types of Analysis – Fundamental vs. Technical: - Definition: - Fundamental and technical analysis are methods investors use to evaluate securities and make investment decisions. - - Comparison: - Fundamental Analysis: - Focus: - Examines a company’s financial health, including profit, loss, balance sheet, and new product launches. - Suitable for: - Long-term investors (holding assets for over one year). - Technical Analysis: - Focus: - Analyzes share price charts, graphs, and mathematical indicators (e.g., 200-day Simple Moving Average, Relative Strength Index, Stochastic Oscillator, Moving Average Convergence Divergence). - Suitable for: - Intraday traders (buy/sell within a single day) and swing traders (buy/sell over days or weeks). - Note: - Detailed indicator lists (e.g., RSI, MACD) are not critical for UPSC. - DEMAT Portfolio - Definition: - A portfolio is the collection of financial assets (e.g., shares, bonds, Exchange-Traded Funds) held by an investor in a DEMAT account. - Types: - Equity portfolios: - Focused on shares. - Debt portfolios: - Focused on bonds. - Hybrid portfolios: - Mix of shares, bonds, and ETFs. - Portfolio Diversification: - Spreading investments across different asset classes to reduce risk, aligning with the principle of not putting all eggs in one basket. - Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) - Overview: - The Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI), headquartered in Mumbai, is India’s apex regulator for securities and capital markets. Established to protect investors, promote market growth, and ensure financial stability, SEBI shapes monetary policy by regulating securities, trading platforms, and market participants. - Establishment and Powers: - Formation: - Formed in 1988 via executive order, became a statutory body under the SEBI Act, 1992. - Powers expanded in 1999 and 2014, enabling search and seizure, property attachment, arrest, and detention to combat market violations. - Board Composition: -
PositionNumberAppointed By
Chairman1Union Government
RBI Officer1Reserve Bank of India
Union Government Officers2Union Government
Other Members5Union Government
- Chairman Tenure: - Appointed for up to 5 years or until age 65, with reappointment possible. - Selected by the Financial Sector Regulatory Appointments Search Committee (FSRASC), chaired by the Cabinet Secretary (IAS). - Regulatory Scope: - Securities Issuance: - Regulates issuance of securities (e.g., bonds, shares, IPOs, ETFs, REITs, InvITs) under the Securities Contracts Regulation Act, 1956 (SCRA). - Market Platforms: - Oversees depositories, stock exchanges, and commodity exchanges for transparent operations. - Market Participants: - Regulates investors, brokers, fund managers, and public limited companies to ensure fair practices. - Collective Investment Schemes (CIS): - Supervises CIS with assets over ₹100 crore, introduced post-Sahara and chit fund scams. - Appeals Process: - Appeals against SEBI orders go to the Securities Appellate Tribunal (SAT), then Supreme Court. - SAT also hears appeals against IRDAI and PFRDA orders. - Investor Support Initiatives: - SCORES Portal: - Online platform for investor complaints, with version 2.0 launched in 2024, targeting resolution within 21 days. - Securities Market Trainers (SMARTs) Program: - Promotes investor education and financial literacy. - Saarthi App: - SEBI’s mobile app for investor awareness, offering market insights and rights education. - SEBI Leadership: - Recent Chairpersons: -

- Securities Market Code: - Proposal: - Announced in Budget 2021, the Securities Market Code will merge: 1. SEBI Act, 1992 2. Depositories Act, 1996 3. Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 4. Government Securities Act, 2007 - Benefits: - Simplifies regulations, reduces compliance costs, enhances investor protection, and promotes transparency, aligning with SEBI’s Investor Charter. - SEBI Investor Charter: - Definition: - A citizens’ charter outlining SEBI’s commitments to investors, specifying services, timelines, and grievance redressal mechanisms (e.g., contact details for unresolved issues). - Origin: - Announced in Budget 2021, notified by SEBI in November 2021, covering all financial products (shares, bonds, derivatives, commodities). - Scope: - Applies to entities (e.g., investors, mutual funds, brokers, stock exchanges, depositories, REITs, InvITs, CIS) and events (e.g., IPOs, FPOs). - Investor Protection and Participation: - Need for Protection: - Share market scams erode savings, leading to social issues like alcoholism, depression, and suicide. - Scams deter investors, diverting funds to gold or real estate, increasing import bills and black​ money. - Limited household participation forces companies to rely on bank loans, constrained by CRR, SLR, PSL, NPA, and PCA (Pillar#1B2). - Benefits of Participation: - Increased retail investor participation deepens capital markets, enabling factory expansion, job creation, and economic growth. - Measures to Boost Retail Investor Participation: - Public Shareholding Norms: - Budget 2019: - Mandated minimum 25% public shareholding for listed government companies. - SEBI raised minimum public shareholding for private sector companies to 35%. - Atmanirbhar Bharat: - Deferred norms during COVID-19 crisis. - Capital Market Growth (ES25): -

- Systematic Investment Plans (SIPs) drove demand, pushing SENSEX and attracting more investors. - Indian household investments in share markets grew by over ₹45 lakh crore in 2020–2025. - Debt/Bond Market Challenges (ES25): - Development Status: -
ParameterIndiaChinaKorea
Corporate Bond Market (% of GDP)452%
- India’s corporate bond market lags due to regulatory constraints and low liquidity. - Challenges: - IRDAI restricts insurance companies from investing in private sector bonds. - IRDAI and PFRDA limit investments to AA-rated or higher bonds, excluding most manufacturing firms. - 60% of bonds are issued by banks and NBFCs, as other companies struggle to attract investors. - Underdeveloped secondary bond market reduces liquidity, deterring investors. - SEBI Reforms to Boost Investor Confidence: - Circuit Breaker: - Introduced post-1992 (Harshad Mehta) and 2001 (Ketan Parekh) scams, where manipulated stock price surges led to retail investor losses. - Halts trading if share price fluctuates beyond a set percentage (e.g., 10%) from the previous day’s close, for a specified duration (e.g., 15 minutes). - T+1 and T+2 Settlement: - Discontinued Badla System (2001), which allowed delayed settlements up to 72 days, prone to misuse by scamsters. - Introduced T+2 rolling settlement (payment and delivery within two working days) in 2001, upgraded to T+1 in 2022. - T+1 Hour and T+0 Hour Settlement: -
TermMeaningSEBI Launch Target
T+1 HourPayment and delivery within 1 hourMarch 2024
T+0 HourInstantaneous settlementOctober 2024
- Illegal Trading: - Dabba Trading: - Unofficial trades recorded in brokers’ ledgers, bypassing DEMAT accounts, leading to investor scams and tax​ evasion. Declared illegal by SEBI. - - Insider Trading: - Using confidential company information (e.g., product launches, mergers) for personal trading gains, banned by SEBI. - - Front Running: - Brokers or fund managers trading based on advance knowledge of large orders (e.g., LIC Mutual Fund manager buying shares before fund’s purchase), prohibited by SEBI. - - Note: - Detailed comparisons (e.g., insider trading vs. front running) are not critical for UPSC; focus on one-line definitions. - Algo Trading and Co-Location: - Refers to Trading Access Point (TAP) System, where high-frequency trades are executed via algorithms or co-located servers near exchanges for speed advantages. - SEBI regulates to prevent unfair advantages, but details are not critical for UPSC. - Investor Protection Fund (IPF): - Mandated for stock and commodity exchanges (e.g., BSE, NSE, NSEL, MCX) to cover non-speculative investor losses (e.g., counterparty defaults due to court cases). - Maximum protection: ₹25 lakh per investor. - Also funds investor education initiatives. - Settlement Guarantee Fund (SGF): - Similar to IPF, ensures trade settlements, funded by exchanges, clearing corporations, and brokers. - Detailed analysis not required for UPSC. - Server Crash Protection: - SEBI’s BCP Plan and DRS Sites: - Business Continuity Plan (BCP) for Market Infrastructure Institutions (MIIs: stock exchanges, depositories, clearing corporations). - Requires Disaster Recovery Sites (DRS) to shift operations during primary server failures. - March 2024: - BSE and NSE conducted mock drills and special trading sessions to test BCP/DRS. - SEBI LAMA System: - Log Analytics and Monitoring Application (LAMA) enables real-time IT system integrity checks for exchanges and depositories. - IRRA Portal: - Investor Risk Reduction Access (IRRA) platform, a safety net for investors during technical glitches, allowing trade cancellations to protect funds. - Developed jointly by BSE, NSE, NCDEX, MCX, and Metropolitan Stock Exchange of India (MSE). - Adani-Hindenburg, Flash Crash, and Short Selling (2023): - Hindenburg Allegations: - In 2023, US-based Hindenburg Research accused Adani Group of stock manipulation, accounting fraud, money​ laundering, and excessive borrowing. - Triggered a flash crash, with Adani shares falling from ₹3,500 (January 2023) to ₹1,500 (February 2023). - Supporters’ Claims: - Alleged Hindenburg spread negative news to profit from short selling Adani shares. - Supreme Court Action: - Formed Justice A.M. Sapre panel to investigate the crash and recommend regulatory improvements (details in Mains-handout). - Short Selling: - Investors borrow shares, sell them at a high price, and repurchase at a lower price to profit from falling prices. - Example: - Borrowing Adani shares at ₹3,500, selling them, then repurchasing at ₹1,500, pocketing the difference. - Adani US Case and Hague Service Convention: - US Allegations: - In 2024, a US court initiated a case against Adani under the Foreign Corrupt Practices Act (FCPA), alleging bribes to Indian officials for solar projects funded by American investors. - Developments: - In 2025, US President Trump paused FCPA enforcement for 180 days, providing temporary relief to Adani. - Hague Service Convention (1965) enables judicial summons delivery across countries; US may use it to summon Adani in India. - Full name: - Hague Convention on the Service Abroad of Judicial and Extrajudicial Documents in Civil or Commercial Matters - It is an international treaty to create a standard procedure for serving legal documents (like summons, notices, court papers) from one country to people or entities in another country. - Finfluencers: - Definition: - Financial influencers providing investment advice via social media, sometimes misleading investors on behalf of scammers. - SEBI Action: - Punishes finfluencers for fraudulent advice to protect retail investors. - Government Securities Trading and Market Dynamics - Overview: - Government Securities (G-Sec) trading platforms, facilitated by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI), enable retail and institutional investors to participate in secure, low-risk investments. Concepts like bid-ask spread, arbitrage, and global financial strategies (e.g., yen carry trade) influence market dynamics, impacting monetary policy and economic stability. - G-Sec Trading for Retail Investors: RBI’s Retail Direct Scheme: - Retail Direct Scheme (2021): - Launched by RBI in 2021 to allow retail investors to directly purchase G-Secs via the Retail Direct Gilt (RDG) account on the E-Kuber platform. - Open to resident Indians and non-resident Indians (NRIs) with zero application fees. - Securities available: - Treasury Bills (T-Bills), Union Government G-Secs, State Development Loans (SDLs), and Sovereign Gold Bonds. - Note: - Retail investors can also invest indirectly via mutual funds, but direct purchases require an RDG account on E-Kuber. - Mobile App: - In 2024, RBI announced plans to launch a mobile app for RDG account management, enhancing accessibility. - Benefits: - Eliminates mutual fund manager commissions, reducing costs for retail investors. - Deepens the G-Sec market through increased retail participation, supporting economic growth. - G-Sec Trading: - Negotiated Dealing System (NDS): - Owned and regulated by RBI, maintained by the Clearing Corporation of India Limited (CCIL), founded by SBI, IDBI, ICICI, LIC, and others. - Records G-Sec buy-sell transactions through multiple channels. - Components: - Telephonic - Buyers/sellers negotiate via phone, then report the deal to NDS. - Over-the-Counter (OTC) - Buyers/sellers meet physically to finalize deals, later reported to NDS. - NDS-OM - Electronic, anonymous, order-driven trading system for G-Sec secondary market, where buyers/sellers place orders online without revealing identities. - E-Kuber: - RBI’s core banking solution platform, handling operations like NEFT, RTGS, repo, MSF, CRR, PSLC trading, G-Sec, Sovereign Gold Bonds, and RDG. - NDS is a sub-component within E-Kuber, managing G-Sec transaction databases. - Bid-Ask Spread: - Definition: - The difference between the price a buyer is willing to pay (bid) and the price a seller demands (ask) for an asset. - - Example: -
AssetBid Price (Jetha)Ask Price (Bhide)Spread
WIPRO Share₹600₹800₹200
- High spread (e.g., ₹200) prevents deals unless one party compromises on price. - Implication: - High bid-ask spreads reduce asset liquidity, making it harder to sell. - Arbitrage: - Definition: - Profiting from price differences for the same asset in different markets or locations. - - Example: -
LocationGold Price (10gm)Arbitrage Profit
Delhi₹60,900₹100
Mumbai₹60,800
- A dealer buys gold in Mumbai (₹60,800) and sells in Delhi (₹60,900), earning ₹100 per 10gm. - Modern Context: - Arbitrage opportunities in financial assets (e.g., shares, bonds) are minimal due to online trading platforms synchronizing prices. - Carry trade and Fall of Indian Share Market (2024): - Carry trade: - A strategy where investors borrow money in a country with low interest rates and invest it in assets in a country with higher interest rates. - Currency carry trade: - - A strategy where investors borrow in a low-interest-rate currency and invest in a high-interest-rate currency to earn the interest difference. - Yen carry trade: - A carry trade where investors borrow in Japanese Yen at very low interest rates and invest in higher-interest assets in other countries. - 2024 Impact: - Japan’s central bank raised loan interest rates in 2024, reducing profit margins for yen carry traders. - Foreign investors withdrew funds, triggering a heavy sell-off in global markets. - Market Declines: -
IndexDecline (%)
Japan’s Nikkei 22512%
SENSEX2%
South Korea’s Kospi8%
- Investment Funds - Overview: - Investment funds, such as mutual funds, hedge funds, REITs, InvITs, ETFs, and AIFs, pool investor money for diversified investments, regulated by SEBI. Corporate governance ensures transparency and accountability in companies, overseen by the Ministry of Corporate Affairs (MCA) and SEBI, impacting monetary policy through market stability and investor confidence. - Mutual Funds (MF) for Aam-Aadmi: - Definition: - A Mutual Fund is an Asset Management Company (AMC-NBFC) that pools retail investors’ savings, issues units, and invests in securities to build a portfolio. - Dividend/interest from the portfolio is distributed proportionally based on units held. - Investors pay entry load (joining fee) and exit load (exit fee), regulated by SEBI. - Market Trends: - Post-IL&FS and COVID-19 crises, mutual fund popularity declined due to market risks, but low bank deposit rates drove renewed interest by 2024. - Side Pocketing: - SEBI’s mechanism to segregate stressed/toxic assets (e.g., IL&FS) from standard assets, protecting investors (technical details not critical for UPSC). - - Types of Mutual Funds: - Based on assets: -

- SIP, ELSS, ULIP: - Systematic Investment Plan (SIP): - Regular small investments at fixed intervals (e.g., monthly). - Equity Linked Savings Scheme (ELSS): - Equity-linked Savings Scheme with a 3-year lock-in, offering income tax benefits. - Unit Linked Insurance Plan (ULIP): - Combines mutual fund investment and insurance, with funds split between the two. - Risk-o-Meter: - SEBI mandates monthly risk disclosure via a digital dashboard (risk-o-meter) with six categories: - Low - Low to moderate - Moderate - Moderately high - High - Very high (added in October 2020). - Helps investors choose funds based on risk appetite. - Hedge Funds for Rich-Aadmi: - Specialized mutual funds for High Net-Worth Individuals (HNIs) with a minimum investment of ₹1 crore, targeting high risk for high returns. - Managers invest in junk bonds, risky assets, and engage in arbitrage, leverage, short selling, futures & options (F&O), and derivatives trading. - Real Estate and Infrastructure Investment Trusts (REITs/InvITs): - Overview: -
TypeMinimum InvestmentInvests InExamples
REITs₹50,000 (reduced from ₹2 lakh in 2019)Real estate projects (rent/sale income)Blackstone-Embassy Group
InvITs₹1 lakh (reduced from ₹10 lakh in 2019)Infrastructure (toll, service fees)IRB, India-Grid
- Benefits: - Provides financing for stressed developers and investment opportunities for HNIs. - Units can be sold on stock exchanges. - SEBI introduced REITs/InvITs in 2014, relaxing norms in 2019 for accessibility. - Sovereign Wealth Funds (SWFs): - Definition: - State-owned funds pooling surplus from central banks, finance ministries, or public sector intermediaries for investments. - Example: - Abu Dhabi Investment Authority (ADIA) bought 1.16% of Reliance Jio for ₹5,600+ crore in June 2020; Singapore’s GIC and Qatar Investment Authority (QIA) also active. - Exchange Traded Funds (ETFs): - Definition: - Investment funds that are traded on stock exchanges like shares. They usually track an index, commodity, or basket of assets. - How they work: - ETF units are bought and sold during market hours. - Price changes throughout the day based on demand and supply. - What they track: - Stock indices (like broad market indices) - Bonds - Gold / commodities - Sectors or themes - Features: - Passive investing (mostly index-tracking). - Lower expense ratio compared to many active mutual funds. - Requires a demat and trading account to buy/sell. - Price vs NAV: - Market price may be slightly above or below NAV due to trading. - Benefit: - Diversification + liquidity + lower cost. - Various ETFs: - CPSE Exchange Traded Funds (ETFs): - Definition: - Facilitate government disinvestment in Central Public Sector Enterprises (CPSEs) while retaining ≥51% ownership (disinvestment, not privatization). - Fund managers (e.g., Goldman Sachs) create ETFs from CPSE shares, offered via New Fund Offer (NFO) at ₹10 per unit. - Investors earn dividends from CPSEs and can trade ETFs on stock exchanges. - Bharat-22 ETF: - Launched in 2017–18 by ICICI Prudential, covering 22 companies (CPSEs, PSBs, UTI). - Initially faced low returns due to PSB-NPA issues, but ELSS-like tax benefits introduced to attract investors. - Bharat Bond ETF (2019): - Similar to CPSE-ETF but invests in bonds of government-owned companies. - CPSE-ETF vs. Mutual Fund: -

- Bitcoin ETF (USA, 2024): - Overview: - Approved by the US SEC in 2024, not yet approved in India. - Investors buy ETF units instead of direct Bitcoin, bypassing complexities. - Pros: - No anonymity, easing tax compliance. - Avoids risks of direct Bitcoin purchases from unknown platforms. - Easier password recovery compared to Bitcoin wallets. - Reduces price volatility due to institutional investor stability. - Cons: - Investors pay stock exchange fees for buying/selling. - Gold ETF: - Investors’ money is used to buy gold, managed by a fund manager, with returns distributed to unit-holders. Tradeable on stock exchanges. - RBI Overseas ETF Restrictions (2024): - Under FEMA 1999, RBI caps Indian mutual funds’ overseas ETF investments at $1 billion to prevent currency exchange crises. - April 2024: SEBI halted new investments as funds neared 95% of the quota. - Alternative Investment Funds (AIFs): - Categories: -

- Forward/Future Contracts and Options: - Definition: - Forward/Future Contract: - Agreement to buy/sell an asset (e.g., 100 Infosys shares at ₹1,000 on 1/5/2025) at a future date with today’s price. - Options: - Insurance-like instruments (call/put) to hedge against non-fulfillment risks, bought from third parties for a fee. - Hedging: - Purchasing options to minimize losses in forward/future contracts. - Over-Financialisation and Sachetisation: - Sachetisation of F&O Market: - Making F&O trading accessible to low-income investors via small investments (e.g., ₹100–250), enabled by discount brokers like Zerodha. - ES24: 9/10 F&O traders lose money due to speculative gambling without understanding risks, necessitating reduced sachetisation. - Over-Financialisation: - Financial sector growth outpacing real economy (GDP), with India’s market capitalization at 140% of GDP (₹415 lakh crore) by May 2024. -
Financial YearMarket Cap to GDP Ratio
2018–1977%
March 2024124%
May 2024140%
- Risks: - Economic crises (e.g., 1997–98 Asian, 2007–08 Subprime), inequality, speculative trading losses. - Productivity Impact (ES25): - Talent shifts to financial sector jobs (e.g., investment banking) over real sector (e.g., engineering). - Capital flows to complex, risky products (e.g., F&O) or low-productivity projects (e.g., real estate bonds). - Bell-Shaped Relationship (ES25): - Financial sector growth boosts GDP in developing nations (e.g., Gambia, Ecuador), but over-financialisation harms growth if poorly regulated. - Derivatives: - Definition: - Contracts deriving value from underlying assets (e.g., shares, bonds, commodities, currencies), often created via securitization (e.g., NHB’s Mortgage-Backed Securities). - Derivative instruments: - Swaps: - Derivative instruments to exchange assets, reducing risk (e.g., Currency Swap between RBI and Japan’s central bank for $75 billion to counter dollar volatility). - Credit Default Swap (CDS): - Protects against loan default (e.g., Credit Suisse scam, not detailed for UPSC). - Participatory Notes (P-Notes): - Definition: - Offshore derivative instruments issued by SEBI-registered FPIs (e.g., Morgan Stanley) to foreign investors avoiding SEBI registration, PAN, or DEMAT accounts. - Investors receive dividends/interest and can sell P-Notes to third parties. - Issues: - Lack of investor identity transparency risks tax​ evasion, money​ laundering, and terror​ financing. - Tax losses when P-Notes are sold abroad vs. domestic share sales (linked to PAN and DEMAT). - SEBI tightened norms (e.g., restricting certain FPI categories, $1,000 deposit). - Comparison: -

- Commodity Market and SEBI Merger: - Overview: - Commodity markets trade bulk goods (e.g., food grains, cotton, precious metals, energy). - Commodity Futures: Contracts for future delivery (e.g., 500 quintals of wheat on 1/5/2021 at X price). - FMC Merger: - Forward Market Commission (FMC) under Ministry of Consumer Affairs failed to prevent scams (e.g., Jignesh Shah’s NSEL fake receipts). - FMC transferred to Finance Ministry, merged with SEBI in 2015. - Gold Exchanges (Budget-2021): - SEBI regulates gold exchanges; Warehousing Development and Regulatory Authority (WDRA) oversees warehousing, vaulting, assaying, and logistics. - Electronic Gold Receipts (EGRs): - Gold companies deposit gold in WDRA-authorized warehouses. - Warehouses issue EGRs, listed on SEBI-regulated gold exchanges. - Buyers trade EGRs or collect physical gold, ensuring quality and reducing tax​ evasion. - India International Bullion Exchange (IIBX): - Launched in 2022 at GIFT City, Gujarat, for 24/7 gold and silver trading (coins, biscuits, ingots). - Financial Stability and Development Council (FSDC, 2010): - Functions: - Supervises economy, coordinates financial regulators (RBI, SEBI, IRDAI, PFRDA, IFSCA), promotes financial literacy and inclusion. - Composition: - Chairman: - Finance Minister. - Members: - Minister of State (Finance), heads of RBI, SEBI, IRDAI, PFRDA, IFSCA, Secretaries of Economic Affairs, Expenditure, Financial Services, Revenue, Corporate Affairs, MeitY, and Chief Economic Adviser. - Secretariat: - Department of Economic Affairs. - Related Financial Bodies: - Financial Stability Board (FSB, 2009): - HQ: - Basel, G20 initiative. - Functions: - Global financial monitoring, regulator coordination. - India’s representation: - Secretary (Economic Affairs), RBI Deputy Governor, SEBI Chairman. - Financial Action Task Force (FATF, 1989): - HQ: - Paris, G7 initiative, India joined in 2010. - Functions: - Combats money​ laundering and terror​ financing. - International Organization of Securities Commissions (IOSCO): - Global body for securities regulators, SEBI is a member. - Known for investor protection and risk prevention guidelines. - Financial Sector Assessment Program (FSAP): - Quinquennial survey by IMF and World Bank, first for India in 2011–12. - Covers Basel-III, compensation, OTC derivatives, resolution, and NBFI. - National Institute of Securities Markets (NISM): - SEBI-established (2006, Mumbai) for training financial market professionals. - Budget-2023: Expanded courses with degrees, diplomas, and certificates. - Corporate Governance - Provisions: - Companies Act mandates term limits, independent directors, women directors, whistleblower protection, and company secretaries. -     - RGESS: - Rajiv Gandhi Equity Savings Scheme (2012) offered tax benefits for first-time share market investors, discontinued in 2017. - Pillar 1D1: Insurance - Definition: - Insurance is a legal contract where an insurer compensates an insured party for losses due to specified risks (e.g., death, damage, illness) in exchange for premiums, promoting financial stability and inclusion. - Significance: - Provides security to households against death, disability, or property damage, and to entrepreneurs against risks like fire or natural disasters, supporting economic resilience. - Insurance companies channel premiums into public and private sector investments, fostering capital formation and economic growth, aligning with India’s financial inclusion goals (e.g., Jan Dhan Yojana). - Insurance Principles - - Uberrima Fides: - Principle of utmost good faith; insured must disclose all material facts (e.g., HIV status) to avoid policy invalidation. - Indemnity: - Compensation limited to actual financial loss, not imaginary losses (e.g., cannot claim for missed opportunities like IIM admission due to fire-damaged books). - Subrogation: - Insurer can recover compensation paid from a negligent third party (e.g., recovering from a boiler manufacturer for a factory fire). - Causa Proxima: - Covers losses directly linked to the insured event (e.g., cannot insure against emotional distress from a celebrity’s death). - Insurable Interest: - Insured must have a financial stake; loss impacts their financial position (e.g., cannot insure a lottery ticket’s failure). - History of Insurance in India - 1818: - European insurers charged higher premiums for Indian clients, reflecting colonial biases. - 1870: - Bombay Mutual Life Insurance, the first Swadeshi life insurance company, offered equitable premiums. - 1912: - Life Insurance Companies Act introduced lax regulations, inadequate during the Great Depression. - 1938: - Insurance Act enforced stricter norms to address financial instability. - Post-Independence: - Nationalization addressed scams and supported financial inclusion: - 1956: - LIC Act nationalized ~245 private life insurance companies. - 1972: - GIC Act nationalized ~107 private general insurance companies, forming GIC and four subsidiaries (National Insurance, New India Assurance, United India, Oriental). - Reforms: - 1993: - Malhotra Committee recommended private sector entry and FDI liberalization. - 1999: - IRDAI established as a statutory regulator. - 2025: - Dinesh Khara Committee proposed reforms to the Insurance Act, 1938. - Safeguards: - Investment norms mandate minimum investment in G-Secs and limit exposure to low-rated private companies. - Rural and social obligation norms require selling policies to rural/marginalized groups and investing in affordable housing. - Delivery Channels: - Agents, brokers, bancassurance, surveyors/loss assessors, third-party administrators (e.g., hospitals). - Life Insurance Types - Definition: - Life insurance provides financial protection against death or disability, with premiums varying by policy type. - Types: -
    Type Duration Return at Maturity Return at Death
    Whole Life Long-term (e.g., 35–40 years) Savings with interest Yes
    Endowment Short-term (e.g., 10–20 years) Savings with interest Yes
    Term Life Short-term, low premium (e.g., PMJJB) No Yes
    Unit Linked Insurance Plan (ULIP) Variable Savings with mutual fund profits Yes
    - Note: - ULIP details: - Combines life insurance with investment in mutual funds, offering dual benefits but with higher risk due to market linkage (ES25 notes insurance gap risk). - IRDAI Reform - Surrender Value - Definition: - Surrender value is the refund paid if a policyholder discontinues a whole-life or endowment policy after 1–2 years. - Reform (2024): -
    Period Surrender Value
    Before 2024 Zero or minimal
    After 2024 80–85% of premium refunded
    - Life Insurance and Post Office Services - Overview: - Life insurance, particularly through public sector entities like Postal Life Insurance (PLI), provides financial security to diverse groups, including rural and urban populations. Post offices also serve as critical intermediaries for government-to-citizen (G2C) and business-to-citizen (B2C) services, facilitating financial inclusion and public service delivery, which align with monetary policy goals of economic stability and access to financial services. - Postal Life Insurance (PLI): - Overview: - Managed by the Department of Posts, PLI offers life insurance schemes for public and private sector employees, professionals, and rural populations. - Developed the DARPAN (Digital Advancement of Rural Post Office for a New India) PLI App to enhance accessibility and digital management. - PLI vs. Rural PLI: -

These initiatives support monetary policy by enhancing financial security and inclusion, contributing to economic stability. - Life Insurance Corporation of India (LIC, 1956): - Establishment: - Created under the LIC Act, 1956, to nationalize private life insurance companies, making LIC a statutory corporation. - Headquarters: - Mumbai, named "Yogakshema" (from Rigveda, meaning well-being). - Motto: - "Yogakshemam Vahamyaham" (from Bhagavad Gita, meaning "I ensure safety and well-being"). - Publishes "Yogakshema" magazine. - Regulatory Framework: - Governed by: - LIC Act, 1956. - Insurance Act, 1938. - Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority (IRDAI) Act, 1999. - Aam Aadmi Bima Yojana (AABY, 2007): - Beneficiaries: - Below Poverty Line (BPL) and marginally Above Poverty Line (APL) citizens in occupations like carpentry, cobbling, blacksmithing (BPL/APL defined in Pillar#6: Poverty). - Benefits: -

    - General insurance - Overview: - General insurance, encompassing non-life policies like accident, health, crop, fire, marine, and vehicle insurance, provides financial protection to workers, farmers, and businesses through public sector entities like ESIC, ECGC, and GIC. These mechanisms support monetary policy by promoting economic stability and financial inclusion, complementing life insurance services. - General Insurance: - Definition: - Non-life insurance policies covering risks such as accidents, health, crops, fire, marine, and vehicles, distinct from life insurance. - Public Sector General Insurance Entities: -

    - Arguments: - Pro: - Privatization enhances efficiency, profitability, and customer service. - Anti: - Risks public welfare focus (details shifted to mains handout). - Employees’ State Insurance Corporation (ESIC, 1948): - Establishment: - Created under Employees’ State Insurance Act, 1948; operationalized in 1952 under Labour Ministry. - Applicability (Pre-Atmanirbhar 2020): -

    - Hazardous industries (e.g., firecrackers, toxic chemicals) with <10 workers require compulsory registration for health insurance purposes. - Subscribers: - Employees in covered establishments with monthly salary <₹21,000, including permanent and casual/temporary workers (employed for days/weeks/months). - Premium: - Shared between employee (X%) and employer (Y%), exact percentages not critical for UPSC. - Benefits: - Medical insurance for worker and family from day one. - Maternity benefits for women employees. - Monthly pension for family if worker dies from employment-related injuries (extended to COVID-19 deaths in 2020–21, originally for factory accidents). - Sickness benefits: - Partial wages during medical leave. - Disability payments. - Unemployment allowance via Atal Bimit Vyakti Kalyan Yojana (formerly Rajiv Gandhi Shramik Kalyan Yojana) for involuntary job loss. - One IP-Two Dispensaries: - Migrant workers (e.g., in Surat) and families (e.g., in Bhubaneswar) access benefits at separate hospitals. - E-Pehchan: - Digital ID cards for beneficiaries. - Abhiyan Indradhanush: - Daily bed sheet changes in ESIC hospitals following a rainbow pattern. - Nirman Se Shakti/ESIC 2.0: - Modernized hospital infrastructure. - Project Panchdeep: - Digitization and automation by WIPRO (2017). - Note: - Project Arrow (2008) relates to India Post modernization, not ESIC, but included in raw text context. - Investment Reform (2022): -

    - Health Insurance Schemes: - Standalone Health Insurers (SAHI): - Companies like Star Health, Care Insurance, Niva Bupa, Aditya Birla Health, and ManipalCigna offer only health insurance, excluding vehicle or property policies. - Types of Health Insurance: - Individual vs Floater -
    TypeIndividualFloater
    Coverage1 person onlyPerson and family (e.g., PMJAY)
    - Fixed vs Indemnity-based -
    TypeFixed BenefitIndemnity-Based
    PayoutFixed amount (e.g., ₹50 lakh for cancer, regardless of ₹10 lakh spent)Actual hospitalization cost up to insured limit (e.g., ₹2 lakh for ₹2 lakh cost, ₹5 lakh max for ₹7 lakh cost)
    Subtypes-Cashless (free at empaneled hospitals); Non-Cashless (reimbursed after payment)
    ExamplesSBI Corona Rakshak (₹700 premium, ₹50,000 fixed)SBI Corona Kavach (₹4,848 premium, ₹5 lakh coverage), PMJAY
    - Corona Kavach vs. Rakshak: - Kavach: - Indemnity-based, higher premium (₹4,848), up to ₹5 lakh coverage. - Rakshak: - Fixed benefit, lower premium (₹700), ₹50,000 payout. - Note: - Faded topic for UPSC 2023/24 exams. - Arogya Sanjeevani & Saral Jeevan Bima: - Standardized policies by public/private insurers, details faded for 2023/24 exams. - Niramya Health Insurance: - For persons with disabilities, managed by Oriental Insurance under Department of Empowerment of Persons with Disabilities. - Coverage: - Up to ₹1 lakh. - Premium: - ₹250–500, based on poverty level. - Note: - Old scheme, low priority for UPSC. - Ayushman Bharat / Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (PMJAY, 2018): - Overview: - Ayushman Bharat, launched in 2018, transforms healthcare delivery through Health & Wellness Centres and the Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (PMJAY), providing free health insurance to vulnerable populations. By ensuring access to secondary and tertiary care, it supports monetary policy goals of financial inclusion and economic stability, complementing life and general insurance frameworks. - Components: - Health & Wellness Centres: - Transform 1.5 lakh Primary Health Centres (PHCs) into Health & Wellness Centres offering free drugs, checkups, mother-child care, and other services. - National Health Protection Scheme (AB-NHPS): - Renamed PM Jan Arogya Yojana (PMJAY) with the motto "Swasthya Aapka, Saath Hamara" (Your Health, Our Support). - Subsumed Rashtriya Swasthya Bima Yojana (RSBY) and Senior Citizen Health Insurance Scheme (SCHIS). - PMJAY Features: - Free insurance cover up to ₹5 lakh per family per year for secondary and tertiary hospitalization. - Covers all pre-existing diseases from day one. - Includes 3 days of pre-hospitalization and 15 days of post-hospitalization expenses. - Cashless and paperless access via NITI Aayog’s web portal with privacy protection. - Beneficiaries: - ~50 crore people (10 crore families, including 8 crore rural and 2 crore urban) based on Socio-Economic Caste Census (SECC) data. - No cap on family size or age. - Treatment available at all public hospitals and empaneled private hospitals (by respective states). - Hospitals employ Pradhan Mantri Aarogya Mitras (PMAMs), trained by the Ministry of Skill Development, to assist patients. - Sub-schemes: - PM SEHAT (Social Endeavour for Health and Telemedicine, 2020): - Pre-2020: - Covered 6 lakh poor J&K families (per SECC 2011). - Post-2020: - Extended ₹5 lakh PMJAY coverage to all 21 lakh J&K residents (rich and poor), accessible nationwide at PMJAY-empaneled hospitals. - Ayushman CAPF Scheme (January 2021): - Joint initiative by Home Ministry and National Health Authority (NHA). - Covers Central Armed Police Forces (CAPF) personnel, including Assam Rifles, Border Security Force (BSF), Sashastra Seema Bal (SSB), Central Reserve Police Force (CRPF), Central Industrial Security Force (CISF), National Security Guard (NSG), and Indo-Tibetan Border Police (ITBP), along with their families. - Clarification: - Despite debates, NSG is included in the CAPF list for this scheme. - Senior Citizens Coverage (September 2024): -
    CategoryPre-September 2024Post-September 2024

    - National Health Authority (NHA): - Established as an agency, restructured as an authority in 2019. - Oversees PMJAY implementation, sets operational guidelines, collaborates with insurance companies and IRDAI, and manages the web platform. - Attached office of the Health Ministry, handling parliamentary matters (e.g., question hour, annual reports) to allow NHA operational autonomy. - Led by a Chief Executive Officer (CEO) with Secretary-level status. - Governed by a board chaired by the Minister of Health & Family Welfare, with members including NITI Aayog CEO, NHA CEO, government officials, experts, and state representatives (on a rotational basis). - Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission: - Managed by NHA, digitizes health records (e.g., X-ray reports) for accessibility (details in Pillar#6: Health Schemes). - State Health Agency (SHA): - Each state forms an SHA to implement PMJAY locally. - Funding / Cost-Sharing: -
    CategoryStates/UTsUnion:State Cost Sharing
    Special Category StatesNorth-Eastern States, Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand90:10
    Other States/UTs with LegislatureUP, Bihar, Delhi, Puducherry, Jammu & Kashmir60:40
    UTs without LegislatureLadakh, Andaman & Nicobar100:0
    - Notes: - Pre-2019, Jammu & Kashmir (as a special category state) received 90:10 funding. - Post-Article 370 revocation (2019), J&K, now a UT with legislature, receives 60:40 funding. - A “Hill Union Territory” category was proposed in August 2019 to restore 90:10 funding for J&K, but it has not been implemented by 2025. - Challenges: - Identification: - Outdated SECC-2011 database excludes newly poor families - Infrastructure: - Limited empanelled hospitals in rural/remote areas - Private hospitals reluctant due to low package rates - Quality of Care: - Risk of over-treatment or unnecessary procedures to maximize claims - Lack of standardization in service delivery - Fraud: - Fake hospitalizations and inflated billing in some private institutions - Weak monitoring mechanisms - Inter-State Portability: - Though allowed, not effectively implemented in practice - Human Resource Constraints: - Shortage of doctors and paramedics, especially in public hospitals - Awareness: - Many eligible beneficiaries unaware of scheme benefits - Fiscal Sustainability: - Risk of rising claims burden on state and central finances - Hospital Construction: - Government to establish hospitals in aspirational (backward) districts for PMJAY beneficiaries. - Funding: - Public-Private Partnership (PPP) model, with public funding from health cess on imported medical devices (details in Pillar#2: Taxation). - Non-health General Insurance - Pradhan Mantri Fasal Bima Yojana (PMFBY, 2016): - Nodal: - Agriculture Ministry; implemented by public/private insurers. - Covers natural calamities, pests, diseases, pre/post-harvest losses. - Premium: - Rabi (1.5%), Kharif (2%), Horticulture/Commercial (5%); balance by Union/State. - Reforms (2020): - Voluntary for farmers (previously mandatory with crop loans). - Union contribution reduced (25–30%), states’ burden increased; NE states retain 90:10. - Updated crop loss assessment methodology. - Tech Solutions: - WINDS Portal: - Gram Panchayat-level weather data. - AIDE/Sahayak App: - Door-to-door farmer enrollment. - YES-Tech Manual: - Crop damage assessment procedure. - Other Agriculture Schemes: - Restructured Weather-Based Crop Insurance Scheme (RWBCIS, 2016): - Covers weather risks only. - National Agricultural Insurance Scheme: - Subsumed by PMFBY. - NIRVIK Scheme (2019): - By ECGC (Commerce Ministry); covers 90% of exporters’ loan defaults (previously 60%). - Third-Party Motor Insurance: - Mandatory under Motor Vehicles Act, 1988; covers victim compensation; IRDAI regulates premiums. - Own Damage (OD) Insurance: - Protects vehicle owners against theft, vandalism, accidents, fire. - Pilot project for road accident victims (2024): - Pilot in Chandigarh; cashless treatment up to ₹1.5 lakh/accident. - Chandigarh was chosen due to its robust health infrastructure and high density of CCTV/Police surveillance, making it easier to track accidents and verify claims for the pilot phase. - Nodal: - Ministry of Road Transport; implemented by NHA. - Tech Add-Ons for Motor OD: - IRDAI allows “pay as you drive,” “pay how you drive” for two-wheelers/cars. - De-tariffication (China, ES23): -
    System Description
    Pre-De-tariffication Fixed premiums based on vehicle type, set by regulator
    Post-De-tariffication Premiums based on driver risk profile (e.g., age, accident history)
    - Title Insurance: - Protects property buyers from title disputes; mandatory for builders under RERA, 2016. - Clinical Trial Liability Insurance: - Covers pharma companies’ legal costs for clinical trial damages. - Bharat Griha Raksha, Sookshma/Laghu Udyam Suraksha: - Standardized fire and perils policies (from 2021) for homes/MSMEs, covering fire, riots, terrorism up to ₹50 crore. - Property Insurance: -
    Type Philosophy Claims Process
