    - Catastrophe Insurance (Proposed): - Covers natural/manmade disasters; aims to reduce government compensation burden. - Catastrophe/Surety Bonds: - See Pillar 1C2 for details. - Cyber Insurance: - Covers malware, phishing, data theft, ransomware; demand surged post-COVID (e.g., Bajaj Allianz, HDFC ERGO). - e-Insurance Account (eIA): - Digital storage of policies (e.g., NSDL’s NIR), similar to DEMAT for shares. - Re-insurance - Definition: - Re-insurance = Insurance for insurance companies. - Insurance companies transfer part of their risk portfolio to another (re-insurance) company to reduce the likelihood of paying a large obligation resulting from an insurance claim. - Legal Mandate: - As per Insurance Act, 1938 → Every insurer must re-insure part of their risks. - Analogy: - Just like banks must insure deposits under Deposit Insurance and Credit Guarantee Corporation Act, insurance companies must reinsure their risks. - Evolution in India: - Earlier Scenario: - GIC (General Insurance Corporation of India) had a monopoly as the sole national reinsurer. - Reforms in 2015: - IRDAI liberalized reinsurance norms. - Entry allowed for: - Indian private re-insurers: - e.g. ITI Reinsurance Ltd. - Foreign re-insurers: - Swiss Re (Switzerland) - Munich Re (Germany) - General Reinsurance (USA, owned by Warren Buffett’s Berkshire Hathaway) - Lloyd’s of London (UK) - Significance of Reinsurance Market Liberalization: - Ends GIC monopoly → More competition. - Benefits: - Insurance companies can negotiate better reinsurance premiums. - Reduced cost of operation for insurance companies. - Encourages: - Business expansion. - Launch of innovative insurance products. - Pandemic risk pool (Proposed by IRDAI) - Background: - COVID-19 pandemic → Massive insurance claims due to deaths, hospitalizations, etc. - Insurers and reinsurers faced huge financial strain. - Proposal: - Creation of a dedicated Pandemic Risk Pool. - Structure: - Contributions from: - Insurance companies - Government - Purpose: - To share pandemic-related risks. - Prevent future collapse of insurance/reinsurance sector during large-scale health disasters. - Status: - IRDAI has proposed; Government support awaited for implementation. - Significance: - Risk mitigation tool for systemic health crises. - Enhances financial resilience of insurance ecosystem in pandemics. - Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India (IRDAI): - Overview: - The Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India (IRDAI) oversees the insurance sector, ensuring financial stability and consumer protection through licensing, norms, and reforms. Initiatives like Bima Sakhi, Bima Vahak, and Bima Vistaar enhance rural insurance penetration, while metrics like insurance density and penetration highlight progress and gaps, supporting monetary policy goals of economic stability and financial inclusion. - Establishment: - Set up in 1996, granted statutory status under the IRDAI Act, 1999. - Renamed in 2014 from IRDA to IRDAI to emphasize development focus. - Headquarters: - Hyderabad, Telangana (unlike RBI and SEBI in Mumbai). - Structure: - Comprises 1 Chairman (5-year term or until age 65) and 9 members (5-year term or until age 62), all eligible for re-appointment. - Functions: - Issues separate licenses for life, general, and reinsurance companies. - Prescribes norms for accounting, solvency, audits, and agent commissions. - Can penalize companies, suspend, or cancel registrations; appeals go to the Securities Appellate Tribunal (SAT, details in Pillar#1C: Sharemarket). - Regulates agents, brokers, bancassurance (banks selling insurance), surveyors/loss assessors, and third-party administrators (e.g., hospitals). - Handles consumer grievances through the Insurance Ombudsman. - Member of the Financial Stability and Development Council (FSDC, details in Pillar#1C). - Domestic Systemically Important Insurers (D-SIIs): - Identifies insurers deemed “too big to fail” due to potential economic distress if they collapse. - D-SIIs face stricter rules, monitoring, and supervision. - Current List: -
    InsurerType
    Life Insurance Corporation of India (LIC)Life Insurance
    General Insurance Corporation of India (GIC)Reinsurance
    New India Assurance Co. Ltd. (NIACL)General Insurance

    - Premium Hikes and Senior Citizen Protection (2024–25): - 2024: - Life and health insurers charged 10–15% extra premiums for Delhi residents due to poor air quality. - 2024: - Hospital/medicine prices rose by 14%, prompting insurers to seek premium hikes. - 2025: - IRDAI capped premium increases for senior citizens at 10% per year to protect affordability. - 30-Day Free-Look Period Rule: - Allows policyholders to review insurance policies for 30 days. - Full premium refund if canceled within this period, without penalties. - Bima Sakhi Agents: - Objective: - Recruit women to sell LIC policies, especially in rural areas. - Eligibility: - Women aged 18–70, minimum 10th standard education. - Salary: - Monthly stipend: - ₹7,000 (Year 1), ₹6,000 (Year 2), ₹5,000 (Year 3). - Additional commission based on policies sold. - Career Progression: - Post-3 years, potential to become LIC Development Officer (at LIC’s discretion, not a fundamental right). - Bima Vahak (2023–24): - Village-level salespersons (individuals or companies) selling insurance at gram panchayat level, focusing on women’s involvement. - Can represent multiple insurers (e.g., LIC and ICICI). - Assist with KYC document collection and claims processing. - Bima Vistaar (Proposed, 2024): - All-in-one standard insurance product combining life, health, accident, and property coverage. - As of March 2024, guidelines under development, not yet launched. - Bima Sugam (Proposed): - A government-run digital platform to compare, buy, renew policies, and file claims online. - Features paperless processes and Aadhaar-based online KYC, eliminating branch visits. - Aims to compete with private platforms like Policybazaar.com with enhanced features. - National Health Claim Exchange (NHCX): - Launched by Health Ministry with IRDAI support. - Objective: - Faster health insurance claim processing by connecting hospitals with insurers. - Enables digital exchange of data, documents, and images between hospitals/labs/clinics and payers (insurers or government schemes like PMJAY’s NHA). - Claims settled within three hours of patient discharge, improving customer satisfaction and fraud detection. - Insurance for All at Gram Panchayat Level (2024): - IRDAI’s Rural, Social Sector, Motor Third Party Obligations Regulations mandate coverage targets. - Applies to life, health, and motor vehicle insurance. -     YearMin. % People Covered in Gram PanchayatsMin. Gram Panchayats Targeted
    Year 130%25,000
    Year 240%50,000
    Year 350%75,000
    - Insurance Progress Indicators: - Insurance penetration: - Insurance penetration is a measure of the development of the insurance sector relative to the size of the economy. - It is the ratio of total insurance premiums collected in a year to the country's Gross Domestic Product (GDP). - Insurance density: - Insurance density is a measure of the intensity or the average consumption of insurance per person in the country. - It is the ratio of total insurance premiums collected to the total population of the country. - Data: -
    MetricFY2001–02FY2021–22FY2022–23FY2023–24
    Insurance Penetration (%: Premium/GDP)2.7%4.2%4.0%3.7%
    Insurance Density ($: Premium/Population)$11$91$92$95
    - Notes: - Penetration declined from 4.2% (2021–22) to 3.7% (2023–24), below global average (7%). - Density increased from $11 (2001–02) to $95 (2023–24), but remains low compared to developing countries like Malaysia. - ULIP Savings-Linked Policies and Insurance Gap: - Insurance gap: - Inadequate insurance coverage, leaving households vulnerable. - Unit Linked Insurance Plans (ULIPs): - Combine life insurance with savings/investment components. - Issue: - ULIPs have minimal protection components, exposing households to significant financing gaps upon premature death of the primary breadwinner. - Implication: - Metrics like insurance density don’t fully reflect coverage adequacy due to savings-focused products. - Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) in Insurance Sector - Definition of FDI: - FDI = Foreign Direct Investment. - Occurs when a foreign entity invests in an Indian company’s equity. - Ownership >10% typically qualifies as FDI (as per global standards). - Investor gets ownership + management control. - Example: - Walmart (USA) bought 77% stake in Flipkart (India) in 2018 for $16 billion. - FDI Limits (Before vs After) -
    Category Before After Year
    LIC (State-owned) No provision 20% 2022
    Insurance Companies (non-LIC) 49% 74% 2021
    Insurance Companies (non-LIC) - 100% (if all funds invested in India) 2025
    Insurance Intermediaries (agents, brokers etc.) 49% 100% 2020
    - Budget 2021 Safeguards for 74% FDI: - Majority of Board and Key Management Persons (KMP) must be Resident Indians. - At least 50% of directors must be Independent Directors. - A certain percentage of profits must be kept in general reserve. - Budget 2025 Provision: - 100% FDI allowed only if entire insurance premium collections are invested in India. - Notes: - Intermediaries = Insurance agents, surveyors, third-party administrators (TPAs). - All under IRDAI regulation. - FDI Debates: - Political: - Concerns over loss of sovereignty in policy-making. - Opposition by political parties and farmer/trader lobbies to sectors like retail FDI. - Debate over foreign influence on domestic policymaking. - Social: - Impact on small retailers, employment, and traditional businesses. - FDI in retail and pharma can disrupt livelihoods in informal sectors. - Consumer benefits via better quality and lower prices. - Economic: - Brings in capital, technology, and global best practices. - Helps bridge the current account deficit. - May lead to profit repatriation and domination by foreign players. - Can crowd out local startups or MSMEs. - Recommendations by Parliamentary Committee (2023): - FDI in LIC should be raised to 74% or 100% to improve capital inflow. - Raise 49% limit for Indian promoters in foreign reinsurers (like Lloyd’s). - IRDAI should clarify whether reinsurers need Indian partner or not. - Recommend 100% FDI in insurance brokers to attract global talent. - Insurance Intermediaries: Bankers, Agents, Brokers - Definition: - Insurance intermediaries act as the middlemen between the insurer (company) and the insured (customer). - Regulated by IRDAI. - Insurance Agent vs Insurance Broker -
    Insurance Agent Insurance Broker
    - Sells policies of a single insurance company.
    - Works as a representative of the insurer.
    - Example: LIC life insurance agent.     		- Sells policies of multiple insurance companies.
    - Works in the interest of the customer.
    - Example: Policybazaar.com.
    - Types of Brokers & Bancassurance - 1. Direct Broker: - Sells life, general, or health insurance policies of multiple companies. - Services: - advice, claims assistance, risk assessment. - 2. Reinsurance Broker: - Specializes in selling reinsurance contracts of multiple companies. - Works between insurance company and reinsurer. - 3. Composite Broker: - Performs functions of both Direct + Reinsurance Broker. - 4. Bancassurance: - Derived from French term meaning “bank + insurance.” - Refers to banks selling insurance products to their customers. - Benefits: - Banks earn commission. - Insurance companies access a large customer base. - Customers get one-stop access to banking + insurance. - Notes: - All brokers must register with IRDAI. - Composite brokers must fulfill stricter capital & compliance norms. - Bancassurance allowed under RBI & IRDAI frameworks. - Micro Insurance: - Low-sum (≤ ₹50,000) insurance (life/general) for rural poor, farmers, labourers. - Designed to reduce vulnerability to death, illness, crop failure, etc. - Delivered through SHGs, NGOs, MFIs in vernacular languages. - Examples: - LIC’s Jeevan Madhur, Jeevan Mangal. - Pillar 1D2: Pension - Definition: - Pension: - A pension is a fixed monthly payment provided to an individual after retirement, typically starting at the age of 58 or 60. It continues until the person's death, and in most cases, a reduced amount is paid to the surviving spouse thereafter. - Unlike insurance, which offers a lump-sum compensation for specific events like death or damage (e.g., ₹2 lakh under Pradhan Mantri Jeevan Jyoti Bima Yojana), a pension is a long-term income stream meant for sustenance in old age. - Financial Inclusion: - Financial inclusion refers to efforts aimed at ensuring that vulnerable and unorganized sections of society have access to essential financial services such as pensions, insurance, and banking. - It aligns with the objectives of the Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY), which seeks to bring the poor into the formal financial system by offering them zero-balance bank accounts, credit, and security. - Significance: - Social Security: - Pensions act as a safety net for the elderly by providing a regular income after retirement, helping them maintain a minimum standard of living and preventing old-age poverty. - Economic Impact: - Pension funds mobilize long-term household savings and channel them into productive investments such as government securities and bonds. - This not only deepens the domestic financial markets but also contributes to the overall capital formation and infrastructure development of the country. - Employee Provident Fund Organisation (EPFO) - Establishment: - Formed in 1951–52 through an ordinance, and formalized under the Employees’ Provident Fund & Miscellaneous Provisions Act, 1952. - It functions under the Ministry of Labour and Employment, with headquarters located in New Delhi. - Governance Structure: - Managed by the Central Board of Trustees, a tripartite body comprising: - 15 members nominated by the Central and State Governments, - 10 members representing employers, and - 10 members representing employees. - The Board decides how EPFO funds are to be invested (e.g., allocation in government securities, corporate bonds, and equities within limits) and also recommends annual interest rates for subscribers. - Coverage and Applicability: - Mandatory for establishments employing 20 or more workers. - Certain exceptions apply, such as cooperatives with fewer than 50 workers not using electricity (e.g., handloom, beedi). - Employee Identification: - Universal Account Number (UAN): - A unique, portable account number allotted to each EPFO subscriber. - Remains constant even if the employee switches jobs, ensuring continuity in EPF contributions. - 2019 Reform: - Employees can now self-generate their UAN using Aadhaar and mobile number, removing the earlier dependency on employers for UAN issuance. - Employer Identification: - Labour Identification Number (LIN): - A unique code issued to employers for accessing EPFO and other labour law compliance systems via the Shram Suvidha Portal (maintained by the Labour Ministry). - Transaction & Access Platforms: - Financial transactions can be managed through partnered public and private sector banks. - The UMANG App (Unified Mobile Application for New-age Governance), developed by the Ministry of Electronics and IT (MeitY), allows users to check balances, track claims, and file grievances from mobile devices. - EPFO Schemes - Employee Provident Fund (EPF, 1952): - A compulsory retirement savings scheme in which both employer and employee contribute a fixed percentage of the employee’s salary every month. Upon retirement at age 58 or earlier in case of death, the employee or nominee receives a lump sum consisting of principal plus accumulated interest. - Interest Rates: - The interest is declared annually by EPFO. It was 8.5% in 2020–21, dipped to a 40-year low of 8.1% in 2022–23, and slightly improved to 8.25% in both 2023–24 and 2024–25. - Partial Withdrawals: - Allowed under specific conditions such as for higher education, marriage, medical emergencies, or house purchase/construction. The withdrawal limit varies based on the purpose and service tenure. - Employees Deposit Linked Insurance Scheme (EDLI, 1976): - Offers life insurance coverage to EPF subscribers without the need for individual premium contributions by employees. The employer pays the premium. If the employee dies during service, the nominee/family receives a lump-sum insurance payout. - 2020 Reform: - The scheme was extended to cover COVID-related deaths with enhanced insurance benefits, increasing coverage between ₹2.5 lakh and ₹7 lakh depending on salary and service conditions. - Employee Pension Scheme (EPS, 1995): - Provides a fixed monthly pension to employees after retirement (age 58) or in case of permanent disability. It is not linked to stock market performance, unlike the National Pension System (NPS). - If the employee dies before retirement, the spouse becomes eligible for a minimum monthly pension of ₹1,000. The actual pension depends on pensionable salary and years of service. - EPF Commutation (2020): - Allows employees to withdraw a portion of their pension corpus before reaching age 58. However, this reduces the monthly pension amount they would receive after retirement. - Centralized Pension Payments System (CITES-CPPS): - An IT modernization initiative that centralizes the pension disbursal mechanism under EPFO. - Pre-CITES: - Pensioners had to physically visit banks for verification. Zonal EPFO offices were linked with only 3–4 banks, causing issues for retirees without accounts in those banks. - Post-CITES: - All banks are digitally integrated with EPFO, enabling seamless pension processing. Retirees no longer need to visit banks or EPFO offices for PPO (Pension Payment Order) updates or verification. - EPFO 3.0 (2025): - A major upgrade to the EPFO’s digital interface, designed to resemble modern e-banking platforms. - Key Features: - Enables ATM withdrawals from EPF accounts, online updates such as address change, and lodging complaints or service requests without physically visiting EPFO offices. - Government Schemes for EPFO Contribution - Pradhan Mantri Rojgar Protsahan Yojana (PMRPY, 2016): - Aimed at incentivizing employers to generate new employment by reimbursing EPF contributions for new employees. - Applicable to employees earning ≤ ₹15,000/month and registered with EPFO. - Government paid 8.33% (Employer’s EPS share), later extended to cover 12% (full employer share). - Valid for new employees added on or after April 1, 2016. - Discontinued and deleted from the 2023 PIB handbook due to overlap with newer schemes. - Atmanirbhar Bharat Rozgar Yojana (ABRY, 2020): - Launched as part of Atmanirbhar Bharat Package 3.0 in November 2020. - Nodal Ministry: - Ministry of Labour and Employment. - Objective: - Promote job creation during post-pandemic recovery. - Eligibility: - Employees earning less than ₹15,000/month. - Either newly registered with EPFO or those who lost jobs during Mar–Sep 2020 and rejoined after Oct 1, 2020. - Applicable for establishments registered with EPFO and having up to 1,000 employees. - Government Contribution: -
    Firm Size Worker Contribution Employer Contribution
    Up to 1,000 employees 12% of wages (Govt pays to EPFO) 12% of wages (Govt pays to EPFO)
    More than 1,000 employees 12% of wages (Govt pays to EPFO) 12% of wages (Paid by Employer)
    - Benefits Duration: - Two years from the date of registration under the scheme. - Outcomes: - Promoted formal job creation. - Helped boost disposable income and social security during COVID-19 recovery. - Reduced compliance burden on MSMEs. - Registration Deadline: - Extended till June 30, 2021. - Employment Linked Incentive Scheme (ELI, Budget 2024): - Announced in Union Budget 2024–25. - Nodal Ministry: - Ministry of Labour and Employment. - Target: - Generate over 2 crore formal jobs in two years. - ELI-Scheme A (First-Time Employees): - Direct Benefit Transfer of ₹15,000 to first-time EPFO-registered employees. - Paid in 3 equal installments. - Exclusion: Employees earning more than ₹1 lakh/month not eligible. - ELI-Scheme B (Manufacturing Sector Boost): - Government to contribute both worker and employer shares to EPFO. - Applicable for 4 years to new jobs created in the manufacturing sector. - Estimated 30 lakh jobs targeted. - Implementation details pending. - ELI-Scheme C (Wage Subsidy to Employers): - Applies to jobs paying up to ₹1 lakh/month. - Government will contribute up to ₹3,000/month per employee to EPFO for 2 years. - Estimated to support creation of 50 lakh jobs. - Coal Miners’ Pension (CMPFO): - Governed by the Coal Mines Provident Fund & Miscellaneous Provisions Act, 1948. - Nodal Ministry: - Ministry of Coal. - Coal Mines Provident Fund Organisation (CMPFO): - A statutory body distinct from EPFO. - Administers provident fund, pension, and deposit-linked insurance for coal sector employees. - C-CARES Portal: - Digital platform to simplify pension application, grievance redressal, and status tracking for retired coal miners and their families. - FAQ: Why Not Merge EPFO with ESIC? - EPFO and ESIC are governed by distinct legislations: - EPFO: - Employees’ Provident Funds and Miscellaneous Provisions Act, 1952. - ESIC: - Employees’ State Insurance Act, 1948. - Merging requires complex legal and structural changes. - Functional domains are different: - EPFO: - Provident fund, pension, insurance. - ESIC: - Healthcare and cash benefits for sickness, maternity, disability. - Pension for Government Employees - Types: - Old Pension Scheme (OPS): - A defined benefit scheme offering a fixed pension post-retirement, applicable to employees who joined before 01/01/2004. Discontinued for new entrants due to long-term fiscal burden. - National Pension System (NPS): - Introduced in 2004 as a defined contribution and market-linked scheme for central government employees joining after 01/01/2004 (excluding armed forces). Later extended to all citizens aged 18–55 in 2009. - Unified Pension Scheme (UPS): - Introduced in 2024 as a hybrid scheme aiming to balance fiscal sustainability with adequate retirement benefits. Applicable from 01/04/2025. - National Pension System (NPS): - Employee contributes 10% of basic salary; Government contributes 14% (raised from 10% in 2019 Interim Budget). - Investments are made in a mix of government securities, corporate bonds, and equities as per subscriber's choice under: - Tier 1 (mandatory; has withdrawal restrictions), - Tier 2 (voluntary; flexible withdrawals). - Each subscriber receives a PRAN (Permanent Retirement Account Number), similar to EPFO’s UAN. - Regulated by PFRDA (Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority); managed through NPS Trust. - In 2023, the T V Somanathan Committee was constituted to review NPS and suggest improvements, leading to the recommendation of the Unified Pension Scheme (UPS). - Unified Pension Scheme (UPS): - Officially notified in August 2024; effective from 01/04/2025. - Government employees may opt to stay with NPS or shift to UPS. - Contribution comparison: -
    Contributor NPS UPS
    Government 14% 18.5%
    Employee 10% 10%
    - UPS provides: - Fixed pension of ₹10,000/month after minimum 10 years of service. - 50% of average last 12-month salary as pension after 25 years of service. - Family pension of 60% in case of pensioner's death (to spouse). - Pension indexed to inflation via CPI-IW. - Lump sum benefit at retirement (not relevant for UPSC exams). - OPS Revival in Non-BJP States: - States like Rajasthan have revived OPS, citing demands from employee unions. - Union Government opposes OPS revival, warning of fiscal unsustainability. - Centre advocates targeted welfare schemes over universal pension promises like OPS. - Andhra Pradesh’s Guaranteed Pension Scheme (GPS): - AP introduced GPS as a hybrid between OPS and NPS to address concerns of state employees. - Contributions and pension under GPS: -
    Contributor Contribution Pension
    Employee 10% 33% of last salary
    State Government 14% 40% of last salary
    - Penny Drop Verification (2023): - Introduced in NPS pension disbursal to ensure account validity. - A token amount of ₹1 is credited to verify correct bank account and IFSC before full pension transfer. - Aimed at reducing transaction errors and preventing fraud. - NPS for Non-Government Employees: - NPS-Lite (Swavalamban Scheme, 2010): - Aimed at economically vulnerable individuals in the unorganized sector who were excluded from formal pension coverage. - Under this scheme, the government contributed ₹1,000 per year to each eligible account holder for a period of 5 years to incentivize pension savings. - Managed by the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority (PFRDA), this scheme laid the groundwork for future inclusive pension models. - NPS-Vatsalya (Announced in Union Budget 2024): - Introduced as a child-focused pension savings mechanism allowing minors (under 18 years) to have NPS accounts. - Parents or legal guardians are responsible for making the contributions on behalf of the minor. - The minimum annual contribution is ₹1,000, with no upper cap, making it flexible for different income groups. - Once the minor turns 18, the account is seamlessly converted into a regular NPS account under their name. - Minimum Assured Return Scheme (MARS): - Proposed by PFRDA to offer a guaranteed minimum return (e.g., 8%) for NPS subscribers from the private or unorganized sector. - If the actual market-based returns fall below the assured threshold, the shortfall would be covered by PFRDA or a designated financial institution. - This is intended to reduce the perceived risk of market-linked pension instruments and boost voluntary NPS enrollment among non-government workers. - Jeevan Pramaan (Launched in 2014): - An Aadhaar-enabled Digital Life Certificate developed by the Ministry of Electronics and IT (MeitY), eliminating the need for physical submission of life certificates by pensioners. - The pensioner authenticates their identity using Aadhaar and biometric inputs like fingerprint or iris scan. In 2022, facial recognition was added to ease access for the elderly. - This digital certification is then automatically transmitted to the Pension Disbursing Agency, ensuring uninterrupted pension payments. - In 2020, the scope of Jeevan Pramaan was expanded to include EPFO pensioners as well. - Pension for Senior Citizens with Capacity to Invest: - Pradhan Mantri Vaya Vandana Yojana (PMVVY, 2017): - A pension scheme designed for senior citizens aged 60 years and above, aimed at providing them with a fixed and secure return on investment to ensure financial stability during retirement. - Nodal Authority: - Department of Financial Services, Ministry of Finance. - Implemented and managed exclusively by the Life Insurance Corporation of India (LIC). - The scheme offered a guaranteed pension at a specified rate (initially 8%, then revised periodically) for a policy term of 10 years. - Senior citizens could invest a lump sum (up to ₹15 lakh limit per individual) and receive regular monthly, quarterly, half-yearly, or annual pension payments. - The scheme was closed for new subscriptions after 31 March 2023. - Related / Similar Schemes: - Senior Citizen Savings Scheme (SCSS): - A government-backed savings option offered via banks and post offices. - Meant for individuals aged 60 years and above, with investment capped at ₹30 lakh (as per recent Budget revisions). - Offers higher interest rates than typical savings instruments, with quarterly interest payout. - Tenure is 5 years, extendable by 3 years. - LIC Varistha Pension Bima Yojana (VPBY): - Launched in 2003 and relaunched in 2014 (short-term window). - Also targeted at 60+ citizens with lump-sum investment and fixed pension benefits. - Now discontinued; holds minimal current relevance for UPSC except historical reference. - Pension for Poor with Capacity to Invest - Maan Dhan Yojanas: -

    Type Description Examples
    Defined Contribution
    • Amount of pension depends on market returns and accumulated corpus
    • More risk; no fixed post-retirement payout
    • National Pension System (NPS)
    • Private Pension Plans
    Defined Benefit
    • Fixed monthly pension irrespective of market returns
    • Government or insurer guarantees payout
    • Employees' Pension Scheme (EPS)
    • Old Pension Scheme (OPS)
    • Unorganised Pension Schemes: UPS, APY, PMSYM, NSAP
    - ES25 Observations on pension sector - Pension sector challenges as per ES25: - Globally, the elderly population has surpassed children. - Growing elderly population and increasing life expectancy → pension companies must pay for more years. - Pensioners save less than active workers, leading to lower national savings in countries with a larger elderly population. - Countries like Japan, France, and China raised retirement ages, causing worker resentment. - Rising inflation increases the need for higher pension amounts. - Pension market not yet fully developed in India says ES25: -
    Nation Pension assets as a % of GDP
    OECD Nations 80%
    India EPFO (17%) & NPS (4.5%)
    - OECD = Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development - Out of the total number of pensioners in India: 80% are Atal Pension Yojana (APY) subscribers. - APY gives hardly ₹1,000–₹5,000 pension. - Micro pension scheme problems as per ES25: - Micro-pension schemes like Atal Pension and Shram Yogi Maan-Dhan provide small pensions to poor families. - Poor people often prioritize immediate consumption over saving for pensions or insurance. - Consequently, 93% of Atal Pension subscribers joined the ₹1,000 plan because the ₹5,000 plan requires more contributions. - Behavioral interventions needed: - Simplifying enrollment - Timely reminders for renewal - To increase active subscribers in micro-pensions - Britain’s PAYGO schemes & demographic changes - ES25: - Pay-As-You-Go (PAYGO) schemes: - Young salaried employees bear the cost of pensions for the older generation. - Worker’s money is NOT invested in shares/bonds (unlike EPFO/NPS). - Current worker’s money is directly paid to the pensioners. - Britain and several OECD nations follow PAYGO. - For such schemes to be sustainable: (i) Outflows must be adequately linked to the inflows → more young workers must keep joining to pay for retiring workers. (ii) Inflows must increase with rising inflation index → Indexation = formula to increase salary & pension with inflation. - Otherwise: - Asset-liability mismatch occurs. - Government ends up borrowing to pay pensions (as seen in Greece & Italy). - Britain may face similar issues in the next 2 decades. - Globally, the number of pensioners exceeds children → reduces active workers → increases pensioners in future. - Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority (PFRDA) - Establishment: - Initially set up in 2003 through an executive order by the Government of India. - Later granted statutory status via the PFRDA Act, 2013 (came into effect from 1st February 2014). - Headquarters: - New Delhi. - Composition & Tenure: - 1 Chairperson: - 5 years or until age 65 (whichever is earlier); eligible for reappointment. - 5 Whole-time Members: - 5 years or until age 62 (whichever is earlier); re-appointable. - Appointed by: - Government of India. - Functions & Powers: - Administers and oversees the National Pension System (NPS) for government and non-government sectors. - Selects and licenses Pension Fund Managers (PFMs) who manage subscriber investments. - Regulates various types of pension schemes and intermediaries (e.g., CRA, POP, Annuity Service Providers). - Exclusions: - Does not regulate pension funds under: - EPFO (Employees' Provident Fund Organisation). - Seamen’s Provident Fund (under Merchant Shipping Act). - Coal Miners’ Provident Fund (CMPF Act). - Assam Tea Plantation Workers' Provident Fund (specific Act). - Investor Protection: - Ensures transparency, fair practices, grievance redressal for pension subscribers. - Prescribes rules for asset allocation, minimum returns (like MARS), fund liquidity, and auditing standards. - Quasi-judicial Powers: - Empowered to conduct inquiries, summon witnesses, and enforce penalties similar to a civil court under the PFRDA Act. - Promotional Activities: - Conducts outreach and pension literacy via: - Official site - Public portal - FDI Norms: - Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) in Pension Sector: - Capped at 49% (same as Insurance Sector). - FDI policy is governed by Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade (DPIIT), not PFRDA itself. - Social Security for Overseas Indians - Pravasi Bharatiya Bima Yojana (PBBY, 2017) - Nodal Ministry: - Ministry of External Affairs (MEA), Government of India. - Applicability: - Mandatory insurance scheme for Indian workers going to Emigration Check Required (ECR) countries. - ECR countries include Gulf Cooperation Council (GCC) nations like: - Saudi Arabia, United Arab Emirates (UAE), Qatar, Oman, Kuwait, Bahrain, and others. - Purpose: - To provide social security cover to vulnerable Indian emigrant workers working abroad, especially in blue-collar jobs. - Key Benefits (Insurance Coverage): - ₹10 lakh in case of accidental death or permanent disability. - Maternity benefits for female workers. - Hospitalization expenses for family members in India (coverage subject to limits). - Repatriation costs in case of death or serious injury. - Legal expenses for litigation related to employment abroad. - Funding: - Premium is paid by the emigrant worker (mandatory before departure). - Implementation: - Carried out through empanelled insurance companies in India. - Validity of insurance is typically linked with the employment contract period (usually up to 2 or 3 years). - Totalisation Pact (India–USA) - Concept: - A bilateral agreement intended to prevent dual payment of social security taxes by expatriates. - Especially important for Indian professionals working in the US on H-1B and L-1 visas. - Issue: - Indian workers temporarily employed in the US pay into the US Social Security system but are not eligible for benefits unless they stay for 10 years. - Upon returning to India, they forfeit their contributions, leading to financial loss. - Purpose of the Pact: - To enable portability of pension/social security benefits. - To allow short-term workers to be exempted from contributing to host country's social security if already covered in home country. - Current Status: - India has signed totalisation agreements with countries like Germany, Canada, Australia, Japan, South Korea, etc. - However, negotiations with the United States are ongoing. - No agreement has been finalized as of now. - Relevance: - Important for safeguarding financial rights of NRIs and improving cross-border social protection. - Although not finalised, the issue is relevant for policy and international labour rights. - Pillar 1D3: Financial Inclusion - Definition: - Financial Inclusion (FI): - Refers to the process of ensuring access to useful and affordable financial products and services—such as banking, savings, credit, insurance, and pension—delivered in a responsible and sustainable way to every individual, particularly the marginalized and unorganized sectors. - Aims to provide S-E-T security: - Social security - Economic security - Transactional security - Enablers: - PM Jan Dhan Yojana, RuPay cards, Direct Benefit Transfers (DBT), mobile banking, etc. - Social Security (SS): - Refers to state-sponsored protection against economic and social distress, ensuring financial support for citizens in cases such as illness, disability, unemployment, old age, or maternity. - Constitutional roots in: - Article 41: - Right to work, education, and public assistance in cases of unemployment, old age, sickness, and disablement. - Article 42: - Provision for just and humane conditions of work and maternity relief. - Examples: - PM Atmanirbhar Bharat Rozgar Yojana - Employees’ State Insurance Scheme (ESI) - National Social Assistance Programme (NSAP) - Social Justice (SJ): - A political and legal philosophy aiming at the fair and equitable distribution of wealth, opportunities, and privileges within a society. - Ensures protection and empowerment of vulnerable groups through: - Affirmative action (reservations in jobs, education) - Legal safeguards (e.g., SC/ST Prevention of Atrocities Act) - Welfare schemes (e.g., Stand Up India, SC/ST sub-plan) - Fundamental to achieving equality and dignity as enshrined in the Preamble of the Constitution. - Significance: - Together, FI, SS, and SJ form the triad for equitable development and democratic deepening. - Foster human development by ensuring economic access and social dignity. - Enhance financial literacy and reduce dependence on informal financial systems. - Empower marginalized communities, especially women, SCs, STs, minorities, and rural poor. - Support efficient targeting of government subsidies and entitlements via: - JAM Trinity (Jan Dhan + Aadhaar + Mobile) - DBT (Direct Benefit Transfer) architecture - Promote inclusive growth in line with Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), especially: - Goal 1 (No Poverty) - Goal 5 (Gender Equality) - Goal 8 (Decent Work and Economic Growth) - Goal 10 (Reduced Inequalities) - Reduces leakages, corruption, and cash-based shadow economy. - Contributes to social cohesion, nation-building, and trust in governance. - Example Use Case: - A woman in a rural village opens a Jan Dhan account → receives maternity assistance via DBT → uses RuPay card at a local kirana shop → also covered under PM Jeevan Jyoti Bima Yojana and Atal Pension Yojana → this integration ensures FI, SS, and contributes toward SJ. - Bank Accounts for Everyone - Historical Context: -
    Year Milestone in Financial Inclusion
    1955 Establishment of State Bank of India to extend banking to rural areas.
    1961 Deposit Insurance and Credit Guarantee Corporation (DICGC) Act enacted to protect small depositors.
    1966 Bringing Cooperative Banks under RBI regulation for unified oversight.
    1969 & 1980 Nationalization of 14 (1969) and 6 (1980) major banks to enhance outreach in underserved regions.
    1969 Launch of Lead Bank Scheme:
    • Each district assigned to a lead commercial bank.
    • Banks to prepare district credit plans using the Service Area Approach.
    • Coordinated planning with Government, NBFCs, and cooperative institutions.
    1971 Formation of State-Level Bankers’ Committees (SLBCs) to monitor state-level FI implementation.
    1976 Regional Rural Banks (RRBs) established under RRB Act to bridge rural credit gaps.
    2005 RBI allows 'No-frills accounts' (zero-balance) to bring the unbanked population into the formal system.
    2006 Business Correspondent (BC) model permitted to deliver banking services in remote areas through intermediaries.
    2011 Launch of Swabhiman Scheme to promote banking penetration in rural India using BCAs and mobile technology.
    2013 Introduction of e-KYC using Aadhaar and biometrics—no need for paper documents for identity verification.
    2014
    • Launch of Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY) for universal banking access.
    • Approval of two new private sector banks—Bandhan and IDFC First Bank.
    2015 Launch of Small Finance Banks and Payments Banks to target underserved segments and enhance last-mile delivery.
    2017–18 India Post Payments Bank (IPPB) launched, leveraging postal network for financial inclusion (see Pillar#1B1).
    - RBI Norms: - Commercial banks must open 25% of branches in unbanked rural areas. - Similar norms for White Label ATM companies. - Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY, 2014) - Nodal: - Finance Ministry, Dept. of Financial Services (DFS) - Motto: - “Mera Khaata, Bhagya Vidhaata.” - Phases and Objectives: - Phase I (2014–15): - Financial literacy programs. - Banking access within 5 km radius. - One account per family: - Overdraft facility. - RuPay ATM-cum-debit card. - Phase II (2015–18): - Creation of Credit Guarantee Fund for overdraft risks. - Seamless Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) of subsidies, scholarships. - Promotion of micro-insurance and pension products via banking channel. - Features: - Account Eligibility and Nature: - Accounts opened in Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs) and Cooperative Banks. - Banks must be on Core Banking System (CBS) and use RuPay Payment Gateway. - Basic Savings Bank Deposit Account (BSBDA): - For individuals aged 10 and above. - Zero-balance account; no penalties for non-maintenance. - Chequebook optional but requires minimum balance. - Overdraft Facility: - Up to ₹10,000 (earlier ₹5,000). - Conditions: - Based on satisfactory operation for 6 months. - One account per household (preferably woman). - Repayable with interest over 3 years. - Interest rate set by the bank. - Insurance Benefits: -
    Type Amount Conditions
    Accidental Insurance ₹1 lakh
    • Funded by NPCI
    • Regular RuPay card usage (e.g., balance inquiry)
    • Not available for income taxpayers or Union Govt employees
    - Withdrawal Limits: - Monthly withdrawal limits imposed to ensure account discipline and curb misuse. - Significance: - Forms the JAM Trinity (Jan Dhan – Aadhaar – Mobile) for financial inclusion. - Enables leakage-free delivery of welfare via DBT. - Emergency Relief Examples: - 2020: PM Garib Kalyan Yojana: - ₹500/month for 3 months transferred to 20 crore women’s PMJDY accounts. - 2023: - PMJDY deposits crossed ₹2 lakh crore (₹2 trillion) mark. - Criticism: - Many dormant or zero-balance accounts. - Used as money mules during demonetization (2016). - Jan Dhan Darshak App (2018): - Developed by Department of Financial Services and NIC. - Purpose: - Geo-location of nearest financial touchpoints: - Bank branches - ATMs - Post Offices - Business Correspondents (BCs) - Investments Other Than Bank - Overview: - Small savings schemes offer higher returns than bank deposits, especially for poor/lower-middle-class individuals. - Funds are routed to National Small Savings Fund (NSSF), which is used for: - Lending to Union Government. - Lending to select state governments. - Interest rates are set quarterly by the Finance Ministry’s Department of Economic Affairs (DEA). - Schemes: -
    Scheme Act Launch
    Post Office Savings Govt Savings Bank Act, 1873 -

    Kisan Vikas Patra Govt Savings Certificate Act, 1959 1988, relaunched 2014
    Public Provident Fund (PPF) PPF Act, 1968 -
    Sukanya Samriddhi Yojana No Act 2015
    - Mahila Samman Bachat Patra (2023) - Purpose: - To promote savings and financial inclusion among women and girls. - Announced In: - Budget 2023; available till 31 Mar 2025. - Eligibility: - Women and girl children; guardian can open account for minor. - Tenure: - Fixed for 2 years from date of deposit. - Deposit Limit: - Minimum ₹1,000; maximum ₹2 lakh (only one account allowed per eligible individual). - Interest Rate: - 7.5% per annum, compounded quarterly. - Withdrawal: - 40% of eligible balance can be withdrawn after 1 year. - Taxation: - Interest taxable; no deduction under Section 80C. - Operated By: - Department of Posts; available at post offices and select banks. - Senior Citizen Savings Scheme (SCSS) - Purpose: - To provide regular income to senior citizens post-retirement. - Introduced In: - 2004; revised via Budget 2023. - Eligibility: - Citizens aged 60+; or 55–60 if retired under VRS/superannuation with proof. - Tenure: - 5 years, extendable by 3 years once. - Deposit Limit: - ₹30 lakh (raised from ₹15 lakh in Budget 2023); in multiples of ₹1,000. - Interest Rate: - 8.2% per annum for Q1 FY25; paid quarterly. - Premature Withdrawal: - Allowed with penalty (1%–1.5% depending on tenure completed). - Taxation: - Interest fully taxable; principal eligible under Section 80C (within ₹1.5 lakh limit). - Operated By: - Banks and post offices; one of the most preferred schemes for seniors. - Sukanya Samriddhi Yojana (2015): - Fixed deposit scheme for girl child (0–10 years age). - Annual Deposit: - Minimum: - ₹250 - Maximum: - ₹1.5 lakh - Until girl turns 14. - Interest: - Originally 9.1%, now ~8.5%. - Set by DEA quarterly. - Withdrawal: - Allowed at age 18–21. - Based on marriage status. - Purpose: - Prevent child marriage, promote girl’s education/business. - Limits: - One account per daughter. - Maximum: - 2 daughters per family. - Five Star Village Scheme (2020): - Nodal Ministry: - Ministry of Communications, Department of Posts. - Purpose: - Post offices to promote: - Small Savings Schemes: - PPF, NSC, Sukanya Samriddhi. - Insurance Schemes: - RPLI, PM Jeevan Jyoti Bima Yojana (PMJJBY), PM Suraksha Bima Yojana (PMSBY). - Focus: - Rural outreach. - Sampoorna Bima Gram Yojana (2017): - Nodal Agency: - Department of Posts. - Aim: - Cover at least one RPLI (Rural Postal Life Insurance) policy for every household in one village per district. - Model Insurance Villages (MIV, 2021): - Proposed by: - Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India (IRDAI). - Objective: - Ensure full insurance coverage in villages: - Life insurance - Health insurance - Crop insurance - Livestock insurance - Vehicle insurance - Funding: - Sources: - Union Government - State Governments - Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) - NABARD - Other All India Financial Institutions (AIFIs) - Chit Funds: - Description: - Collective investment scheme. - Members contribute monthly; one member gets loan from the pooled fund. - Legal Status: - Item in Concurrent List (both Union & States can make laws). - Regulators: - RBI - SEBI - State Governments - Issues: - Scam-prone due to weak/lax regulation. - Banning of Unregulated Deposit Schemes Act, 2019: - Purpose: - Prohibits unregistered entities from soliciting deposits or investments from public. - Example: - Builders, jewelers, chit-fund scams. - Regulators: - RBI → Banks, NBFCs - SEBI → Mutual Funds, REITs - IRDAI → Insurance - PFRDA → Pensions - MCA → NIDHI companies - State Govts → Chit Funds - EPFO - Penalties: - Fine up to ₹50 crore. - Imprisonment up to 10 years. - Power to attach properties for refunding investors. - Online Database: - Union Government to build an online database of all legal deposit-taking activities. - Credit (Loans) - Context: - Links to Pillar#1A2 (Priority Sector Lending) and Pillar#1B1 (Microfinance). - Refinance: - AIFIs (e.g., NABARD, SIDBI) or MUDRA provide new finance to banks/NBFCs based on loans already given to end-borrowers, often via securitization. - Credit Guarantee: - Covers bank/NBFC losses if borrowers default, enabling collateral-free lending. - Earlier by DICGC; now by specialized organizations (e.g., CGTMSE, NCGTC). - Credit Guarantee Organizations: -
    Organization Fund Loans Covered

    NCGTC Credit Guarantee Fund Scheme for Skill Development (CGFSSD) Skill development loans

    - Credit Guarantee Fund for Education Loans (CGFEL): - Covers education loans up to ₹7.5 lakh, managed by NCGTC. - MSME Definition (Atmanirbhar 2020, Revised 2025): - MSMED Act, 2006; updated 2020 and Budget 2025. -

    - Budget 2025: - Investment and turnover limits enhanced 2.5x and 2x, respectively (exact figures pending). - Emergency Credit Line Guarantee Scheme (ECLGS 1.0, 2020): - For MSMEs hurt by COVID lockdown; Eligibility: Non-NPA, outstanding loans ≤₹25 crore. - Loan: - 20% of outstanding amount; Tenure: 4 years; Interest: 9.25% (banks), 14% (NBFCs). - Collateral-free; defaults covered by NCGTC. - ECLGS 2.0 (Atmanirbhar 3.0, 2020): - For MSMEs and large firms in healthcare or 26 RBI-identified stressed sectors (e.g., automobiles, tourism). - Eligibility: - Outstanding loans ₹50–500 crore (as of 29/02/2020). - Loan: - 20% of outstanding amount; Tenure: 5 years (1-year principal moratorium). - Collateral-free; defaults covered by NCGTC. - Subordinate Debt for Stressed MSMEs: - For MSMEs with NPA/stressed loans; Loan: Up to ₹75 lakh. - Collateral-free; defaults covered by CGTMSE. - Equity Infusion via Fund of Funds: - Government’s ₹10,000 crore Fund of Funds invests in daughter funds, mobilizing ₹40,000 crore. - Total ₹50,000 crore equity for MSMEs. - Budget 2024: MSME Loan Announcements - Faster Loan Approval via Digital Footprint: -

    - Credit Guarantee for MSMEs in Manufacturing: - Details pending; enhances collateral-free loans for manufacturing MSMEs. - Interest Equalisation Scheme: - Nodal: - Commerce Ministry (DGFT); 2–3% interest subsidy for MSME exporters, disbursed via RBI to banks. - Various government initiatives - MUDRA (2015) - Full Form: - Micro Units Development & Refinance Agency Ltd. - Status: - 100% subsidiary of SIDBI. - Objectives: - Promote micro-entrepreneurship via indirect lending. - Refinance support to: - SCBs, RRBs, Cooperative Banks, MFIs, NBFCs. - Funding Sources: - PSL shortfalls (directed by RBI). - Budgetary allocations from Department of Financial Services (DFS). - Target Beneficiaries: - Non-corporate, non-farm small enterprises (manufacturing, trading, service, allied agri). - Product Segments: -
    CategoryLoan Amount (₹)
    ShishuUp to 50,000
    Kishor50,001 – 5 lakh
    Tarun5,00,001 – 10 lakh (Raised to 20 lakh for successful Tarun borrowers in Budget 2024)
    - Key Features: - Collateral-free loans. - Credit risk covered by CGFMU (operated by NCGTC). - MUDRA RuPay Card: ATM/debit card linked to loan account for working capital usage. - Challenges: - High interest rates by intermediaries. - Growing risk of NPAs. - Stand Up India (2016) - Nodal Ministry: - Department of Financial Services (DFS). - Timeline: - Extended till 31 March 2025 (via Budget 2019). - Objective: - Promote SC/ST and women entrepreneurship. - Each SCB branch to provide ₹10 lakh – ₹1 crore loan to at least: - 1 SC/ST borrower and 1 woman borrower. - Loan Tenure: - Up to 7 years; moratorium up to 18 months. - Collateral: - Optional; covered by CGFSI (Credit Guarantee Fund for Stand-Up India). - Budget 2021 Update: - Margin money reduced from 25% to 15%. - Agri-allied activities made eligible. - Self Help Groups (SHGs) – Credit Linkage - Informal groups (10–20 members); women-dominated. - Activities: - Cottage industries, agarbatti making, weaving, food processing. - Formation: - Under schemes like NRLM (see Pillar#6). - SHG–Bank Linkage Programme (1992): - Launched by NABARD. - Provides collateral-free credit via formal banking. - India: - Largest microfinance movement. - PM SVANidhi (PM Street Vendor’s AtmaNirbhar Nidhi, 2020) - Nodal Ministry: - Ministry of Housing & Urban Affairs (MoHUA). - Objective: - Empower street vendors affected by COVID-19 through micro-credit. - Loan Amounts: - 1st cycle: - ₹10,000 | 2nd: ₹20,000 | 3rd: ₹50,000. - Tenure: - 1 year, monthly EMI basis. - Interest Rate: - Market-linked (~7.25%); 7% interest subsidy for timely repayments. - Budget 2025 Update: - ₹30,000 credit card facility announced (integration with loan cycles pending clarity). - SVANidhi – Main Bhi Digital (2021) - MoHUA-led digital literacy initiative under SVANidhi. - ULBs (Urban Local Bodies) funded to train vendors in: - UPI, BHIM, NetBanking, QR Payments. - Vendor Onboarding: - Food vendors connected to Swiggy, Zomato, etc. - SVANidhi – Rashtriya Udyamita Vikas Pariyojana (2024) - Nodal Ministry: - Ministry of Skill Development and Entrepreneurship. - Aim: - Entrepreneurship training for street vendors. - Format: - 22-week blended training (offline/online/hybrid). - Support: - Stipend and official certification. - Key Partners: - Flipkart, IIE (Indian Institute of Entrepreneurship), NIESBUD. - PM Vishwakarma Scheme (2023) - Nodal: - MSME Ministry; Targets 18 trades (e.g., carpenter, blacksmith, tailor). - Entitlements: - Recognition: - PM Vishwakarma certificate, ID card; registration on Udyam Assist Portal. - Skill Upgradation: - 5–7 days basic training, 15+ days advanced training; ₹500/day stipend. - Toolkit: - ₹15,000 e-voucher for purchase. - Digital Incentive: - ₹1 per digital transaction (max ₹100/month). - Marketing and loan support (see below). - PM Vishwakarma Loans: - Collateral-free Enterprise Development Loans up to ₹3 lakh. - Interest: - 5% paid by beneficiary, 8% subsidized by government (total 13% for banker). - Rationale: - Subsidies ensure banks lend without losses, given typical 13–16% rates. - Agricultural Credit - Kisan Credit Card (KCC, 1998) - Launched by RBI & NABARD; issued by PSBs, RRBs, Coop. Banks. - Purpose: - Farm inputs, investment credit, working capital, post-harvest expenses, household needs, cash withdrawal. - Features: - Repayable loans with interest. - Accidental insurance cover. - Budget 2018: - Extended KCC to Animal Husbandry & Fisheries. - Interim Budget 2019: - Simplified application + interest subvention for allied sectors. - Collateral-Free Farm Loans (RBI Guidelines) - W.e.f. 01 Jan 2025: Revised limits: -
    ActivityEarlierFrom Jan 2025
    Farming + Allied (animal, dairy, fisheries)₹1.6 lakh₹2 lakh
    - Beyond this: - Collateral like land, gold, or NWR required. - Modified Interest Subvention Scheme (MISS / KCC Loans) - Budgetary interest subsidy on short-term agri-loans (up to ₹3L / ₹5L). -

    - Farm Loan Waivers - Economic Survey 2024-25 (ES25) opposes blanket waivers: - Waivers incentivize willful default, especially during elections. - Lead to fiscal burden & stress on state finances. - Disrupt credit culture and hurt punctual borrowers. - Suggested alternatives: - Expand credit guarantee schemes. - Encourage crop insurance and targeted income support (e.g., PM-KISAN). - Social Security Code, 2020: Coverage of Gig and Platform Workers: - Categories: - Informal Workers: - No formal contract (e.g., domestic help, construction). - Gig Workers: - Temporary, task-based (e.g., Uber, Zomato). - Platform Workers: - Use online platforms for jobs (e.g., Swiggy, Ola). - Social Security Code Provisions: - Coverage under life, disability, health insurance. - Maternity benefits, old age pensions, education support for children. - Funding through Central/State + aggregator contributions. - e-Shram Portal: - National database of unorganised workers. - Registration mandatory to claim benefits. - Aadhaar-linked, portable benefits. - Budget 2025: Focused Measures for Financial Inclusion & Social Security: -
    Target Group Budget 2025 Announcements
    Gig Workers Health insurance of ₹5 lakh via PM-JAY for those registered on e-Shram.
    Farmers Kisan Credit Card (KCC) & MISS (Modified Interest Subvention Scheme) limit raised from ₹3 lakh to ₹5 lakh.
    Dak Sevaks / Postmen More responsibilities in financial inclusion outreach in rural areas.
    MSME Sector Investment & turnover limits increased by 2.5x and 2x respectively; Credit Guarantee expanded.
    New Entrepreneurs Loans up to ₹2 crore for women, SC/ST under new targeted scheme.
    Micro Vendors PM-SVANidhi: Credit card of ₹30,000; Micro enterprises: ₹5 lakh credit access.
    - Unorganised Workers’ Social Security Act, 2008: - Setup: - National Social Security Board (chaired by Union Labour Minister). - States to set up corresponding boards. - National Social Security Fund: - Initial corpus of ₹1,000 crore. - Funding for welfare schemes for unorganised workers. - Key Schemes: -
    Scheme Details
    Indira Gandhi National Old Age Pension Monthly pension under National Social Assistance Programme (NSAP).
    National Family Benefit Scheme ₹20,000 lump sum to BPL family on death of primary breadwinner.
    Janani Suraksha Yojana Cash incentive for institutional delivery to BPL women (linked to Pillar#6A).
    Aam Aadmi Bima Yojana Jointly run by LIC and Central Govt for unorganised sector workers.
    Rashtriya Swasthya Bima Yojana Earlier scheme merged with PM-JAY.
    - Notes: - Many older schemes now merged or obsolete. - Social Security Code 2020 intended to replace the 2008 Act, pending full implementation. - Customer Protection - Purpose: - Ensures grievance redressal across various financial sectors (banking, insurance, securities, pensions). - Protects consumers from mis-selling, service deficiencies, fraud, delays, and unethical practices. - Promotes transparency, trust, and financial inclusion. - Redressal Mechanisms by Sector: -
    Sector Mechanism Handled By Notes
    Bank / NBFC / Payment Systems RBI Integrated Ombudsman Scheme (2021) RBI “One Nation, One Ombudsman” — merged banking, NBFC, and digital transaction ombudsmen
    Insurance Insurance Ombudsman for claims ≤ ₹30 lakh IRDAI Above ₹30 lakh → appeal to SAT
    Securities / Commodities SCORES Portal + Appellate Tribunal SEBI SCORES = SEBI Complaints Redress System
    Pensions Internal: EPFO; External: PFRDA, NSDL EPFO / PFRDA NPS grievances → NSDL & PFRDA; Private pensions → PFRDA
    - RBI’s Integrated Ombudsman Scheme (2021): - Launched under Section 35A of the Banking Regulation Act. - Replaces 3 earlier schemes: - Banking Ombudsman Scheme (2006) - NBFC Ombudsman Scheme (2018) - Digital Transaction Ombudsman Scheme (2019) - Key Features: - Single portal - No need to identify the correct scheme/authority. - No fee for filing complaints. - “Deficiency in Service” replaced with broad “Grounds for Complaint”. - Covers Scheduled Commercial Banks, NBFCs, and non-bank payment system providers. - Exception: - Cooperative banks not covered (unless brought under scheduled list). - SEBI SCORES Portal: - Online grievance mechanism for securities investors. - Mandatory for all listed companies and SEBI-registered intermediaries to be registered on SCORES. - Timeline: - 30 days for companies to respond. - After escalation: - SEBI intervenes if unsatisfied. - IRDAI Grievance Redress: - 17 Insurance Ombudsman Offices across India. - Handles: - Delays in claim settlement, partial or total denial, misrepresentation, non-issuance of policy documents. - Above ₹30 lakh: - Appeals go to SAT. - Pension Sector Grievance Handling: - EPFO (for EPF-related grievances): - via internal officers & CPGRAMS. - NPS (for government/private subscribers): - CRA (NSDL), then escalation to PFRDA. - Private Pensions: - Regulated by PFRDA, with SAT as appellate body. - Cross-Cutting Initiatives: - CPGRAMS: - Centralized Public Grievance Redress and Monitoring System by Govt of India — available across sectors. - Financial Literacy Programs by RBI and other regulators aim to empower consumers to file complaints confidently. - Web Portals - PaiSA Portal (2018): - "Portal for Affordable Credit and Interest Subvention Access". - Initially launched by Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs (MoHUA) to provide centralized tracking of interest subvention to SHG & PM SVANidhi beneficiaries. - Status: - Discontinued in 2024–25 due to low relevance and integration with newer platforms. - psbloansin59minutes.com (2018): - Developed by SIDBI in collaboration with 5 Public Sector Banks. - Objective: - Provide MSMEs quick access to working capital/term loans within 59 minutes. - Eligibility: - GST-registered MSMEs, income tax return filed. - Features: - Loan amount: - ₹10 lakh to ₹1 crore. - Collateral-free under CGTMSE scheme. - Integrated checks via Aadhaar, GSTN, IT data, and CIBIL score. - Fee: - ₹1,000 charged only if loan is sanctioned. - Jan Samarth Portal (2022): - Nodal Ministry: - Ministry of Finance. - Objective: - Unified national digital platform for providing access to various credit-linked government schemes. - Coverage: - Sectors: - Education, Agriculture, Business, Livelihood. - Banks: - Connects with 125+ banks and NBFCs. - Features: - Auto-verification of Aadhaar, GST, UDYAM Registration, CIBIL. - Scheme suggestion engine based on borrower profile and needs. - Tracks application and disbursement status. - SARTHI Portal (2024): - Full Form: - Smart Agriculture Resource & Technology Hub for Insurance. - Nodal Ministry: - Ministry of Agriculture & Farmers’ Welfare, with technical support from UNDP India. - Target Users: - Farmers and rural households. - Objective: Facilitate access to multiple types of insurance: - Crop insurance (PMFBY), life, health, tractor, livestock, shop, and dwelling insurance. - Features: - Integrated claims tracking. - Premium subsidy calculators. - KYC through Aadhaar and land record linkage. - PM-SURAJ Portal (2024): - Full Form: - PM Social Upliftment through Rationalised Assistance and Justice. - Nodal Ministry: - Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment. - Target: - SC, ST, OBC, de-notified, semi-nomadic, and nomadic tribe entrepreneurs. - Features: - Facilitates enterprise loans up to ₹15 lakh. - Integrated interest subsidy and credit guarantee modules. - Initially targets 1 lakh beneficiaries in FY2024–25. - Status: - Detailed operational guidelines pending. - Financial Inclusion Reports - Global Microscope Report: - Published by Economist Intelligence Unit in collaboration with Accion and Gates Foundation. - Assesses financial inclusion enabling environment across 50+ countries. - India ranked high due to digitization, regulatory frameworks, and inclusive policies. - Global Findex Database: - Released by World Bank & Gates Foundation every 3 years. - Tracks financial account ownership, savings, borrowings, digital payments. - India’s 2021 edition showed 78% adults had bank accounts (up from 53% in 2014). - Global Pension Index: - Prepared by Mercer CFA Institute. - Measures pension systems across adequacy, sustainability, and integrity. - India’s score declined from 45.9 (2023) to 44.0 (2024, per Economic Survey 2024–25). - India’s FI Index (by DFS): - Released by Department of Financial Services. - Measures progress on three dimensions: access, usage, and quality of financial services. - RBI’s FI Index (launched 2021): - Scored on scale of 0 to 100 (higher = better financial inclusion). - Index rose from 60.1 (Mar 2023) to 64.2 (Mar 2024). - Sub-components include banking penetration, insurance coverage, digital usage, grievance redress. - National Strategy for Financial Inclusion (NSFI): - Published by RBI in 2020 (vision: 2020–2024). - Goals: - Provide universal access to financial services, strengthen digital infrastructure, promote financial literacy, and protect consumers.
